Kleshchev multipartitions, affine Mirković-Vilonen polytopes, and representations of KLR algebras in type 𝙰 1 ( 1 ) {\tt A}^{(1)}_{1}

 Abstract.

 
 We construct explicit isomorphisms between three models for the ℬ ⁡ ( ∞ ) \mathcal{B}(\infty) crystal in type 𝙰 1 ( 1 ) {\tt A}_{1}^{(1)} : affine Mirković–Vilonen
polytopes, Kleshchev multipartitions, and a new model we call upper ledge diagrams . We also present some clarifying results on these crystals, giving a direct method for completing an affine MV polytope from the data of one of its boundary root partitions, and a non-iterative recognition theorem which characterizes Kleshchev multipartitions in type 𝙰 1 ( 1 ) {\tt A}_{1}^{(1)} . We apply these results to the representation theory of KLR algebras, where they yield a combinatorial dictionary between cuspidal- and cellular-theoretic frameworks, along with some augmented branching rules for real root functors of induction and restriction.

 1. Introduction


 
 Crystal bases are a powerful combinatorial framework for studying representations of quantum groups, and the crystal ℬ ⁡ ( ∞ ) \mathcal{B}(\infty) plays a central organizing role; it encodes the combinatorial skeleton of highest weight representations and arises naturally in the categorification program via Khovanov–Lauda–Rouquier (KLR) algebras. Herein our focus is on the fundamental type 𝙰 1 ( 1 ) {\tt A}_{1}^{(1)} crystals for the quantum group U q ​ ( 𝔰 ​ 𝔩 ^ 2 ) U_{q}(\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}) , where ℬ ⁡ ( ∞ ) \mathcal{B}(\infty) admits a number of well-studied but still rather mysterious combinatorial realizations. Under consideration in this paper are two key examples:

 
 • 
 
 Kleshchev multipartitions (§ 5 ), whose crystal rules originate
in work of Kleshchev [ 35 , 36 ] on modular branching rules for symmetric groups
and Hecke algebras, and work of Lascoux–Leclerc–Thibon [ 40 ] 
and Misra–Miwa [ 46 ] on the basic representation of U q ​ ( 𝔰 ​ 𝔩 ^ e ) U_{q}(\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{e}) , extended to higher levels by Ariki–Mathas [ 4 ] .




 • 
 
 Rank 2 affine Mirković–Vilonen (MV) polytopes (§ 4 ), defined in work of Baumann–Dunlap–Kamnitzer–Tingley [ 6 ] 
(see also [ 7 , 23 , 50 , 51 ] ), which encode PBW data for the quantum group. These polytopes are fundamental building blocks (2-faces) in the characterization of affine MV polytopes in arbitrary symmetric affine type.




 


 
 Motivating our interest in these two crystals is the deep representation-theoretic content they carry in the world of categorification via KLR algebras (see § 10 ).
Simple KLR modules are indexed by affine MV polytopes via the cuspidal system framework of [ 37 , 45 , 57 ] . On the other hand, Kleshchev multipartitions index simple modules for cyclotomic KLR
algebras through the graded cellular structure of [ 18 ] . From each point of view we capture complementary structural information; the
former offers stratification, filtrations, and BGG-type reciprocity [ 33 , 47 ] , the latter offers
ties to modular representation theory
of Hecke algebras and attendant results on Specht homomorphisms and decomposition numbers [ 10 , 9 , 31 ] .



 
 Creating a direct combinatorial bridge between these perspectives would allow these tool kits to be brought to bear in tandem, yet translating directly between crystals has remained heretofore a difficult task. Happily, our main result (Theorems  Theorem E and Theorem G and Corollary  Corollary I ) constructs such a bridge by way of explicit bijections between these crystals and a new, third, nicely transparent (in both characterization and crystal operation) combinatorial model for ℬ ⁡ ( ∞ ) \mathcal{B}(\infty) which we call upper ledge diagrams (see § 6 ).



 
 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 Figure 1. 
The affine Mirković-Vilonen polytopes (left, § 4 ), upper ledge diagrams (center, § 6 ), and Kleshchev multipartitions (right, § 5 ) are models for ℬ ⁡ ( ∞ ) \mathcal{B}(\infty) in type 𝙰 1 ( 1 ) {\tt A}_{1}^{(1)} . We present direct isomorphisms 𝒳 , 𝒴 , 𝒵 𝜿 , 𝒲 𝜿 \mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Z}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}},\mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}} between these crystals in § 7 . 
 
 
 Along the way, we establish several results of independent interest, the first two of which address significant practical difficulties in working with these crystals:

 
 • 
 
 There are two convex preorders on the root system of type 𝙰 1 ( 1 ) {\tt A}_{1}^{(1)} , and root partitions in either order correspond to northwest/southeast boundaries of polytopes (as seen in Figure  1 ). Working with affine MV polytopes and their crystal operators requires one to generate, from a given root partition in one order, the root partition in the other order which uniquely completes an affine MV polytope—a rather delicate iterative balancing act featuring the satisfaction of many tropical-flavored inequalities. We simplify matters by providing an explicit tableau-theoretic polytope completion algorithm in type 𝙰 1 ( 1 ) {\tt A}_{1}^{(1)} (Theorem  Theorem B ).




 • 
 
 Recognizing whether a given multipartition is Kleshchev is also an iterative process which in general requires a number of steps roughly equal to the rank of the multipartition itself. We provide a new non-iterative recognition theorem for Kleshchev multipartitions in type 𝙰 1 ( 1 ) {\tt A}^{(1)}_{1} for arbitrary level and multicharge (Theorem  Theorem C ), the first of its kind in affine type A .




 • 
 
 A key ingredient in our combinatorial setup is a bijection between partitions and 2-regular partitions (Theorem  Theorem A ) with interesting properties; it can be seen for instance that it restricts to a bijection between partitions of n n with k k parts and 2-regular partitions whose odd-numbered parts sum to n n and even-numbered parts sum to n − k n-k . That the cardinalities of these sets are equal is known thanks to Euler and refinements by Sylvester and Bessenrodt (see for instance [ 2 , 8 , 52 ] ) but our bijection is apparently new.




 


 
 Next we consider in § 10 applications of these results to type 𝙰 1 ( 1 ) {\tt A}_{1}^{(1)} KLR representation theory. Corollary  Corollary I ’s crystal isomorphisms lift to isomorphisms of simple modules in the associated cuspidal/cellular labeling regimes mentioned above, as shown in § 10.10 . Exploiting this new connection allows us to freely share representation-theoretic information between regimes, and in this direction we establish several related results and pose some new conjectures:

 
 • 
 
 We give a remarkably simple “glue-then-split” procedure for translating multipartition labels of simple modules between any two choices of multicharge, allowing us to pass between distinct cellular regimes for cyclotomic KLR algebras (§ 10.11 ).




 • 
 
 We provide a direct means of calculating the jump statistic on simple KLR modules via upper ledge diagram combinatorics (§ 10.12 ).




 • 
 
 We present some ‘augmented’ branching rules (§ 10.13 ) for Kleshchev multipartitions describing heads/socles of simple modules under real root functors of induction/restriction (generalizing the usual notion of branching by simple roots , see § 10.6 ).




 • 
 
 We describe a combinatorial ‘tile-factor’ theorem (§ 10.14 ) which identifies some multiplicity-one simple factors of quasirestricted Specht modules in level one.




 • 
 
 Inspired by the crystal combinatorics, we pose some new conjectures: a level-two analogue of James’s famous regularization theorem (§ 10.15 ), and an alternate construction of simple KLR modules closely tied to the upper ledge diagram crystal (§ 10.16 ).




 


 
 1.1. Future directions


 
 It would be interesting to construct explicit crystal isomorphisms between upper ledge diagrams and other combinatorial realizations of ℬ ⁡ ( ∞ ) \mathcal{B}(\infty) in type 𝙰 1 ( 1 ) {\tt A}_{1}^{(1)} using the upper ledge diagram crystal as a combinatorial hub. Natural candidates include Young walls [ 25 , 29 ] ; Nakajima monomials [ 30 ] ; and rigged configurations [ 55 ] . At the level of
highest weight crystals ℬ ⁡ ( Λ ) \mathcal{B}(\Lambda) , it would also be interesting to connect upper ledge diagrams to Uglov/FLOTW multipartitions [ 16 , 17 , 19 , 58 ] , which provide alternatives to the Kleshchev labeling in higher levels.



 
 Another important direction would be to extend this work to types 𝙰 > 1 ( 1 ) {\tt A}_{>1}^{(1)} ,
constructing analogous notions of upper ledge diagrams, associated crystal isomorphisms, and dictionaries between KLR representation theories. This work is a crucial first step in that direction, since the 𝙰 1 ( 1 ) {\tt A}_{1}^{(1)} polytopes (and their rank 2 finite-type kin) govern the key 2-face conditions in the characterization of higher-rank affine MV polytopes [ 7 ] .
There are new difficulties that arise in higher ranks—for instance, the associated Weyl group combinatorics become more complex and there are infinitely many convex preorders to contend with (as opposed to two). Certain aspects of Kleshchev multipartition combinatorics also become considerably thornier—for instance, the Mullineux map is nontrivial in higher ranks (see e.g. the increase in complexity in characterizing Kleshchev bipartitions in [ 3 , Propositions 9.7, 9.8] ).





 
 1.2. On the structure of the paper


 
 In order to maximize readability/usability, we adopt a slightly idiosyncratic structure. In § 2 –§ 7 we define the objects under study, present the key combinatorial algorithms and functions, and state the main (lettered) theorems. The combinatorial heavy lifting is then done below decks in the long and technical § 8 –§ 9 , where more ancillary objects and functions are defined, and proofs of the lettered theorems may be found. In § 10 we devote our attention to applying the main combinatorial theorems to KLR algebras.





 
 1.3. ArXiv Version


 
 We relegate some of the more routine/repetitive calculations to the arXiv version of the paper. The reader interested in seeing these additional details can download the L a T e X  source file from the arXiv . Near the beginning of the file is a toggle which allows one to compile the paper with these calculations included.

 2. Partition combinatorics


 
 We will freely associate integer partitions λ = ( λ 1 , … , λ m ) \lambda=(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{m}) with their Young diagrams, displayed in the English convention as top-left aligned box arrangements, where the k k th row consists of λ k \lambda_{k} boxes. We write

 
 • 
 
 | λ | := λ 1 + ⋯ + λ m |\lambda|:=\lambda_{1}+\cdots+\lambda_{m} for the number of boxes in λ \lambda ;




 • 
 
 p ⁡ ( λ ) := m p(\lambda):=m for the number of nonzero rows (or parts ) in λ \lambda ;




 • 
 
 c ⁡ ( λ ) c(\lambda) for the number of nonzero columns in λ \lambda ;




 • 
 
 c t ​ ( λ ) c_{t}(\lambda) for the length of the t t th column in λ \lambda ;




 • 
 
 n k ​ ( λ ) := # ⁡ { t ∣ λ t = k } n_{k}(\lambda):=\#\{t\mid\lambda_{t}=k\} for the number of rows of length k k in λ \lambda ;




 • 
 
 λ ′ \lambda^{\prime} for the conjugate of λ \lambda , given by λ t ′ = c t ​ ( λ ) \lambda^{\prime}_{t}=c_{t}(\lambda) for all t t ;




 • 
 
 min ⁡ ( λ ) := λ p ⁡ ( λ ) {\rm min}(\lambda):=\lambda_{p(\lambda)} for the minimal row length in λ \lambda if λ \lambda is nonempty, and min ⁡ ( λ ) = ∞ {\rm min}(\lambda)=\infty if λ = ∅ \lambda=\varnothing ;




 • 
 
 double ​ ( λ ) \textup{double}(\lambda) for the partition given by doubling all column lengths in λ \lambda ;




 • 
 
 halve ​ ( λ ) \textup{halve}(\lambda) for the partition given by halving all column lengths (assuming the column lengths of λ \lambda are even of course).




 
 We say that λ \lambda is 2-regular (resp. 2-restricted ) provided that λ \lambda has no two rows (resp. columns) of equal length. We write 𝒫 , 𝒫 2 ​ r ​ e ​ g , 𝒫 2 ​ r ​ e ​ s \mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{2reg}},\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{2res}} for the set of partitions, 2-regular partitions, and 2-restricted partitions respectively.
In some settings a partition λ \lambda will come equipped with a distinguished charge κ ∈ ℤ 2 \kappa\in\mathbbm{Z}_{2} which defines residues associated to each box in λ \lambda : if λ \lambda has charge κ \kappa then the residue of a box u u in the r r th row and c c th column of λ \lambda is given by res ​ ( u ) := κ + c ¯ − r ¯ ∈ ℤ 2 \textup{res}(u):=\kappa+\bar{c}-\bar{r}\in\mathbbm{Z}_{2} . We write 𝒫 κ \mathcal{P}^{\kappa} for the set of partitions equipped with charge κ \kappa .



 
 2.1. Spar tableaux


 
 Given a partition λ \lambda , we define the spar tableau 𝚜𝚙𝚊𝚛 ⁡ ( λ ) {\tt spar}(\lambda) to be the labeling of boxes in the Young diagram for λ \lambda with positive integers as follows. Traversing through columns from right to left, and within each column from bottom to top, label each box with the smallest positive integer which exceeds the label of any boxes directly below and to the right, and appears nowhere in the column immediately to the right. See Figure  2 for an example. We set 𝚠 ⁡ ( λ ) {\tt w}(\lambda) to be the label in the upper left corner of 𝚜𝚙𝚊𝚛 ⁡ ( λ ) {\tt spar}(\lambda) (or 0 if λ = ∅ \lambda=\varnothing ). We now define a pair of functions which refract out of spar tableau combinatorics.



 
 2.1.1. Ordinary/extraordinary functions


 
 Given λ ∈ 𝒫 \lambda\in\mathcal{P} , we define μ = ord ⁡ ( λ ) ∈ 𝒫 2 ​ r ​ e ​ g \mu=\mathrm{ord}(\lambda)\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{2reg}} to be the unique 2-regular partition such that c t ​ ( μ ) c_{t}(\mu) is odd if and only if t t appears in the first column of 𝚜𝚙𝚊𝚛 ⁡ ( λ ) {\tt spar}(\lambda) . More concretely, we have c 𝚠 ⁡ ( λ ) ​ ( μ ) = 1 c_{{\tt w}(\lambda)}(\mu)=1 , and moving to the left, the t t th column length in μ \mu increases by one precisely when exactly one of t , t + 1 t,t+1 is in the first column of 𝚜𝚙𝚊𝚛 ⁡ ( λ ) {\tt spar}(\lambda) .



 
 For w ≥ 𝚠 ⁡ ( λ ) w\geq{\tt w}(\lambda) , we then define ext w ​ ( λ ) ∈ 𝒫 2 ​ r ​ e ​ g \mathrm{ext}^{w}(\lambda)\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{2reg}} by adding a row of length w w to the top of ord ⁡ ( λ ) \mathrm{ord}(\lambda) if w > 𝚠 ⁡ ( λ ) w>{\tt w}(\lambda) , or deleting the top row of ord ⁡ ( λ ) \mathrm{ord}(\lambda) if w = 𝚠 ⁡ ( λ ) w={\tt w}(\lambda) . It follows from definitions that ord ⁡ ( λ ) \mathrm{ord}(\lambda) and ext w ​ ( λ ) \mathrm{ext}^{w}(\lambda) are ‘orthogonal’ in a particular sense; for each t ∈ [ 1 , w ] t\in[1,w] , the t t th columns in each are of opposite parity.
See Figure  2 for an example.



 
 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 Figure 2. At top, a partition λ = ( 4 , 2 2 , 1 ) \lambda=(4,2^{2},1) and its spar tableau. Below are shown the 2-regular partitions ord ⁡ ( λ ) = ( 7 , 4 , 2 , 1 ) \mathrm{ord}(\lambda)=(7,4,2,1) , ext 7 ​ ( λ ) = ( 4 , 2 , 1 ) \mathrm{ext}^{7}(\lambda)=(4,2,1) , and ext 9 ​ ( λ ) = ( 9 , 7 , 4 , 2 , 1 ) \mathrm{ext}^{9}(\lambda)=(9,7,4,2,1) . Note that calc ∘ ord ⁡ ( λ ) = calc ⁡ ( 7 , 4 , 2 , 1 ) = ( 4 , 2 2 , 1 ) = λ \mathrm{calc}\circ\mathrm{ord}(\lambda)=\mathrm{calc}(7,4,2,1)=(4,2^{2},1)=\lambda . 
 
 
 Conjecture 2.1 . 

 
 If λ , ν ∈ 𝒫 \lambda,\nu\in\mathcal{P} are such that ν \nu is obtained by deleting the last row of λ \lambda , then ord ⁡ ( ν ) ⊆ ord ⁡ ( λ ) \mathrm{ord}(\nu)\subseteq\mathrm{ord}(\lambda) .







 
 2.1.2. The calc function


 
 Given μ ∈ 𝒫 2 ​ r ​ e ​ g \mu\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{2reg}} , we construct a (tableau for a) partition λ = calc ⁡ ( μ ) \lambda=\mathrm{calc}(\mu) as follows. Set the first column of λ \lambda to consist of boxes labeled by { t ∈ [ 1 , c ⁡ ( μ ) ] ∣ c t ​ ( μ ) ​  is odd } \{t\in[1,c(\mu)]\mid c_{t}(\mu)\text{ is odd}\} in decreasing order from top to bottom. Then, beginning from the second column in λ \lambda , traverse through columns from left to right, and within each column from top to bottom, placing labeled boxes according to the following rule. For each new box, label it with the largest positive integer which is strictly less than any entry directly above or to the left of the new box, and appears nowhere in the column immediately to the left. If no such integer exists—or if there is no box immediately to its left—terminate this column without placing this box and move on to the next column to the right. The construction halts when a column receives no new boxes, and the resulting partition is λ \lambda . See Figure  2 for an example.



 
 The following result is proved in Theorem  8.6 .



 
 Theorem A . 

 
 The functions ord : 𝒫 → 𝒫 2 ​ r ​ e ​ g \mathrm{ord}\colon\mathcal{P}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{2reg}} and calc : 𝒫 2 ​ r ​ e ​ g → 𝒫 \mathrm{calc}\colon\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{2reg}}\rightarrow\mathcal{P} are mutually inverse bijections. Moreover, the tableau generated in the construction of calc ⁡ ( μ ) \mathrm{calc}(\mu) is exactly 𝚜𝚙𝚊𝚛 ⁡ ( calc ⁡ ( μ ) ) {\tt spar}(\mathrm{calc}(\mu)) . 









 
 2.2. The involution orth w \mathrm{orth}^{w} 


 
 For λ ∈ 𝒫 \lambda\in\mathcal{P} and w ≥ 𝚠 ⁡ ( λ ) w\geq{\tt w}(\lambda) , we set:

 

 
 
 orth w ​ ( λ ) = calc ⁡ ( ext w ​ ( λ ) ) ∈ 𝒫 . \displaystyle\mathrm{orth}^{w}(\lambda)=\mathrm{calc}(\mathrm{ext}^{w}(\lambda))\in\mathcal{P}. 
 
 
 
 It follows that orth w ​ ( λ ) \mathrm{orth}^{w}(\lambda) is the (unique, thanks to Theorem  Theorem A ) partition such that [ 1 , w ] [1,w] is the disjoint union of the labels in the first columns of 𝚜𝚙𝚊𝚛 ⁡ ( λ ) {\tt spar}(\lambda) and 𝚜𝚙𝚊𝚛 ​ ( orth w ​ ( λ ) ) {\tt spar}(\mathrm{orth}^{w}(\lambda)) . It also follows that orth w \mathrm{orth}^{w} is an involution with no fixed points on the set of (the 2 w 2^{w} many) partitions λ \lambda with 𝚠 ⁡ ( λ ) ≤ w {\tt w}(\lambda)\leq w .







 
 3. The type 𝙰 1 ( 1 ) {\tt A}^{(1)}_{1} root system


 
 Throughout the paper, it will be convenient to write i ^ = i + 1 ¯ \widehat{i}=i+\bar{1} for i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in\mathbbm{Z}_{2} .
We work with the root system of type 𝙰 1 ( 1 ) {\tt A}_{1}^{(1)} for the Kac-Moody Lie algebra 𝔰 ​ 𝔩 ^ 2 ​ ( ℂ ) \widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}({\mathbb{C}}) (see [ 22 ] for a thorough exposition). We write I = { α 0 , α 1 } I=\{\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1}\} for the simple roots, ℤ ≥ 0 ​ I \mathbbm{Z}_{\geq 0}I for the root lattice, and define

 

 
 
 α 1 : k := k α 1 + ( k − 1 ) α 0 ; α 0 : k := ( k − 1 ) α 1 + k α 0 ; δ = α 1 + α 0 , \displaystyle\alpha_{1:k}:=k\alpha_{1}+(k-1)\alpha_{0};\qquad\alpha_{0:k}:=(k-1)\alpha_{1}+k\alpha_{0};\qquad\delta=\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{0}, 
 
 
 
 in ℤ ≥ 0 ​ I \mathbbm{Z}_{\geq 0}I for all k ∈ ℤ > 0 k\in\mathbbm{Z}_{>0} , where δ \delta is the null root. For θ = a 1 ​ α 1 + a 0 ​ α 0 ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 ​ I \theta=a_{1}\alpha_{1}+a_{0}\alpha_{0}\in\mathbbm{Z}_{\geq 0}I we write ht ⁡ ( θ ) = a 1 + a 0 {\operatorname{ht}}(\theta)=a_{1}+a_{0} for the height of θ \theta . We have

 

 
 
 Φ + re = { α 1 : k , α 0 : k ∣ k ∈ ℤ > 0 } ; Φ + im = { k δ ∣ k ∈ ℤ > 0 } ; Φ + = Φ + re ⊔ Φ + im ; Ψ + = Φ + re ⊔ { δ } , \displaystyle\Phi_{+}^{\mathrm{re}}=\{\alpha_{1:k},\;\alpha_{0:k}\mid k\in\mathbbm{Z}_{>0}\};\qquad\Phi_{+}^{\mathrm{im}}=\{k\delta\mid k\in\mathbbm{Z}_{>0}\};\qquad\Phi_{+}=\Phi_{+}^{\mathrm{re}}\sqcup\Phi_{+}^{\textup{im}};\qquad\Psi_{+}=\Phi_{+}^{\mathrm{re}}\sqcup\{\delta\}, 
 
 
 
 for the sets of real, imaginary, positive, and indivisible roots respectively. We write P = ℤ ⁡ { Λ 1 , Λ 0 , δ } P=\mathbbm{Z}\{\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{0},\delta\} for the weight lattice.



 
 3.1. Root partitions


 
 Given θ ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 ​ I \theta\in\mathbbm{Z}_{\geq 0}I , a root partition π \pi of θ \theta is a triple of integer partitions π = { π 1 , π δ , π 0 } \pi=\{\pi^{1},\pi^{\delta},\pi^{0}\} such that

 

 
 (3.1) 
 
 θ = ∑ k = 1 ∞ n k ( π 1 ) α 1 : k + | π δ | δ + ∑ k = 1 ∞ n k ( π 0 ) α 0 : k . \displaystyle\theta=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}n_{k}(\pi^{1})\alpha_{1:k}+|\pi^{\delta}|\delta+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}n_{k}(\pi^{0})\alpha_{0:k}. 
 
 
 
 We write Π ⁡ ( θ ) \Pi(\theta) for the set of root partitions of θ \theta and set Π = ⨆ θ ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 ​ I Π ⁡ ( θ ) \Pi=\bigsqcup_{\theta\in\mathbbm{Z}_{\geq 0}I}\Pi(\theta) .





 
 3.2. Crystals of type 𝙰 1 ( 1 ) {\tt A}_{1}^{(1)} 


 
 Definition 3.1 . 

 
 A highest weight 𝙰 1 ( 1 ) {\tt A}_{1}^{(1)} -crystal is the data of a set B B equipped with a highest weight element b + ∈ B b^{+}\in B and functions wt : B → P \textup{wt}:B\rightarrow P , ε i , φ i : B → ℤ \varepsilon_{i},\varphi_{i}:B\rightarrow\mathbbm{Z} , f i , e i : B → B ⊔ { 0 } f_{i},e_{i}:B\rightarrow B\sqcup\{0\} for i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in\mathbbm{Z}_{2} , such that for b , b ′ ∈ B b,b^{\prime}\in B ,

 
 (1) 
 
 φ i ​ ( b ) = ε i ​ ( b ) + ⟨ wt ​ ( b ) , α i ∨ ⟩ \varphi_{i}(b)=\varepsilon_{i}(b)+\langle\textup{wt}(b),\alpha_{i}^{\vee}\rangle ;




 (2) 
 
 e i e_{i} increases φ i \varphi_{i} by one, decreases ε i \varepsilon_{i} by one, and increases wt by α i \alpha_{i} (when e i ​ b ≠ 0 e_{i}b\neq 0 );




 (3) 
 
 f i ​ b = b ′ f_{i}b=b^{\prime} if and only if e i ​ b ′ = b e_{i}b^{\prime}=b ;




 (4) 
 
 ε i ​ ( b ) = max ⁡ { m ∣ e i m ​ b ≠ 0 } \varepsilon_{i}(b)=\max\{m\mid e_{i}^{m}b\neq 0\} ;




 (5) 
 
 e i ​ b + = 0 e_{i}b_{+}=0 ;




 (6) 
 
 b + b_{+} can be reached from any b ∈ B b\in B by applying a sequence of e 1 e_{1} ’s and e 0 e_{0} ’s.




 




 
 Definition 3.2 . 

 
 An 𝙰 1 ( 1 ) {\tt A}_{1}^{(1)} -bicrystal is a set B B equipped with two distinct crystal structures whose weight functions agree. We indicate the secondary structure with stars, writing e i ∗ , f i ∗ , ε i ∗ , φ i ∗ e_{i}^{*},f_{i}^{*},\varepsilon_{i}^{*},\varphi_{i}^{*} .





 
 Kashiwara’s theory of crystals [ 26 ] attaches to each integrable highest weight representation V ⁡ ( Λ ) V(\Lambda) 
of the quantum group U q ​ ( 𝔰 ​ 𝔩 ^ 2 ) U_{q}(\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}) 
a highest weight crystal ℬ ⁡ ( Λ ) \mathcal{B}(\Lambda) 
which records the leading-order ( q → 0 ) (q\rightarrow 0) 
combinatorial behavior of V ⁡ ( Λ ) V(\Lambda) .
The negative half of the quantum group U q − ​ ( 𝔰 ​ 𝔩 ^ 2 ) U_{q}^{-}(\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}) has a crystal basis ℬ ⁡ ( ∞ ) \mathcal{B}(\infty) given as the direct limit of (weight shifted) ℬ ⁡ ( Λ ) \mathcal{B}(\Lambda) , and ℬ ⁡ ( Λ ) \mathcal{B}(\Lambda) can be obtained from cutting out a certain part of ℬ ⁡ ( ∞ ) \mathcal{B}(\infty) .







 
 4. Affine Mirković-Vilonen polytopes


 
 4.1. Convex orders and polytopes


 
 We work with two total orders on the indivisible roots Ψ \Psi which are restrictions of the two possible convex preorders on Φ + \Phi_{+} :

 

 
 (4.1) 
 
 α 1 : 1 ≻ 1 α 1 : 2 ≻ 1 α 1 : 3 ≻ 1 ⋯ ≻ 1 δ ≻ 1 ⋯ ≻ 1 α 0 : 3 ≻ 1 α 0 : 2 ≻ 1 α 0 : 1 , \displaystyle\alpha_{1:1}\hskip 2.84526pt\begin{subarray}{c}{}\\

 and

 

 
 (4.2) 
 
 α 0 : 1 ≻ 0 α 0 : 2 ≻ 0 α 0 : 3 ≻ 0 ⋯ ≻ 0 δ ≻ 0 ⋯ ≻ 0 α 1 : 3 ≻ 0 α 1 : 2 ≻ 0 α 1 : 1 . \displaystyle\alpha_{0:1}\hskip 2.84526pt\begin{subarray}{c}{}\\

 We may associate a root partition π ∈ Π ⁡ ( θ ) \pi\in\Pi(\theta) with finite sets of points in the root lattice ℤ ≥ 0 ​ I {\mathbbm{Z}}_{\geq 0}I , letting S ⁡ ( π , ≻ 1 ) S(\pi,\begin{subarray}{c}{}\\

Then, for π , ϕ ∈ Π ⁡ ( θ ) \pi,\phi\in\Pi(\theta) we may visualize the ordered pair ( π | ϕ ) (\pi|\phi) as the (marked) convex hull of the points S ⁡ ( π , ≻ 1 ) ⊔ S ⁡ ( ϕ , ≻ 0 ) S(\pi,\begin{subarray}{c}{}\\

\textstyle\succ\\
\scriptscriptstyle{}^{0}\end{subarray}) , see for instance Example  4.1 .
For this reason we abuse terminology somewhat and refer to ordered pairs of root partitions ( π | ϕ ) (\pi|\phi) as ‘polytopes’. While we will make use of the geometric visualization of ( π | ϕ ) (\pi|\phi) for aid of intuition occasionally, we will work with polytopes in this paper from a primarily combinatorial and tableau-theoretic standpoint.



 
 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 Figure 3. The polytope ( π | ϕ ) ∈ Π ⁡ ( θ ) (\pi|\phi)\in\Pi(\theta) , for θ = 17 ​ α 1 + 20 ​ α 0 \theta=17\alpha_{1}+20\alpha_{0} , as discussed in Example  4.1 . 
 
 
 Example 4.1 .

 n 1 ​ ( π 1 ) = 4 ; n 2 ​ ( π 1 ) = 2 ; | π δ | = 3 ; n 4 ​ ( π 0 ) = 1 ; n 2 ​ ( π 0 ) = 3 ; n 1 ​ ( π 0 ) = 5 , \displaystyle n_{1}(\pi^{1})=4;\quad n_{2}(\pi^{1})=2;\quad|\pi^{\delta}|=3;\quad n_{4}(\pi^{0})=1;\quad n_{2}(\pi^{0})=3;\quad n_{1}(\pi^{0})=5, 
 
 
 
 with n k ​ ( π i ) = 0 n_{k}(\pi^{i})=0 for all other k ∈ ℤ > 0 , i ∈ ℤ 2 k\in\mathbbm{Z}_{>0},i\in\mathbbm{Z}_{2} , we have that S ⁡ ( π , ≻ 1 ) S(\pi,\begin{subarray}{c}{}\\

\scriptscriptstyle{}^{1}\end{subarray}) consists of the points

 

 
 
 0 → + 4 α 1 : 1 4 ​ α 1 → + 2 α 1 : 2 8 ​ α 1 + 2 ​ α 0 → + 3 ​ δ 11 ​ α 1 + 5 ​ α 0 → + α 0 : 4 14 ​ α 1 + 9 ​ α 0 → + 3 α 0 : 2 17 ​ α 1 + 15 ​ α 0 → + 5 α 0 : 1 17 ​ α 1 + 20 ​ α 0 . \displaystyle 0\xrightarrow{+4\alpha_{1:1}}4\alpha_{1}\xrightarrow{+2\alpha_{1:2}}8\alpha_{1}\hskip-1.70717pt+\hskip-1.70717pt2\alpha_{0}\xrightarrow{+3\delta}11\alpha_{1}\hskip-1.70717pt+\hskip-1.70717pt5\alpha_{0}\xrightarrow{+\alpha_{0:4}}14\alpha_{1}\hskip-1.70717pt+\hskip-1.70717pt9\alpha_{0}\xrightarrow{+3\alpha_{0:2}}17\alpha_{1}\hskip-1.70717pt+\hskip-1.70717pt15\alpha_{0}\xrightarrow{+5\alpha_{0:1}}17\alpha_{1}\hskip-1.70717pt+\hskip-1.70717pt20\alpha_{0}. 
 
 
 
 By convention we will represent the simple roots α 1 , α 0 \alpha_{1},\alpha_{0}

We use white dots to subdivide the edge parallel to δ \delta into segments π 1 δ ​ δ , π 2 δ ​ δ , … \pi_{1}^{\delta}\delta,\pi_{2}^{\delta}\delta,\ldots , so that the root partition π \pi can be completely recovered from the shape together with these markings.
The points S ⁡ ( ϕ , ≻ 0 ) S(\phi,\begin{subarray}{c}{}\\

 4.2. Affine Mirković-Vilonen polytopes


 
 Following [ 6 ] , we will only be interested in those polytopes which satisfy some rather delicate tropical-flavored conditions.
For π , ϕ ∈ Π ⁡ ( β ) \pi,\phi\in\Pi(\beta) and m ∈ ℤ > 0 m\in\mathbbm{Z}_{>0} we define integers

 

 
 
 
 c m ↑ ​ ( π | ϕ ) = ∑ k = 1 m k ⁡ ( n k + 1 ​ ( ϕ 0 ) − n k ​ ( π 1 ) ) ; \displaystyle c_{m}^{\uparrow}(\pi|\phi)=\sum_{k=1}^{m}k(n_{k+1}(\phi^{0})-n_{k}(\pi^{1})); 
 c m → ​ ( π | ϕ ) = ∑ k = 1 m k ⁡ ( n k + 1 ​ ( π 1 ) − n k ​ ( ϕ 0 ) ) ; \displaystyle c_{m}^{\rightarrow}(\pi|\phi)=\sum_{k=1}^{m}k(n_{k+1}(\pi^{1})-n_{k}(\phi^{0})); 
 
 
 
 
 
 c m ↓ ​ ( π | ϕ ) = ∑ k = 1 m k ⁡ ( n k + 1 ​ ( π 0 ) − n k ​ ( ϕ 1 ) ) ; \displaystyle c_{m}^{\downarrow}(\pi|\phi)=\sum_{k=1}^{m}k(n_{k+1}(\pi^{0})-n_{k}(\phi^{1})); 
 c m ← ​ ( π | ϕ ) = ∑ k = 1 m k ⁡ ( n k + 1 ​ ( ϕ 1 ) − n k ​ ( π 0 ) ) . \displaystyle c_{m}^{\leftarrow}(\pi|\phi)=\sum_{k=1}^{m}k(n_{k+1}(\phi^{1})-n_{k}(\pi^{0})). 
 
 
 


 
 Definition 4.2 . 

 
 Let π , ϕ ∈ Π ⁡ ( θ ) \pi,\phi\in\Pi(\theta) .
Then ( π | ϕ ) (\pi|\phi) is an affine Mirković-Vilonen (MV) polytope (of content θ \theta ) provided that both of the following conditions are satisfied:

 
 (1) 
 
 For every m ∈ ℤ > 0 m\in\mathbbm{Z}_{>0} , we have

 

 
 
 0 ∈ { c m ↑ ​ ( π | ϕ ) , c m → ​ ( π | ϕ ) } ⊆ ℤ ≤ 0 and 0 ∈ { c m ↓ ​ ( π | ϕ ) , c m ← ​ ( π | ϕ ) } ⊆ ℤ ≤ 0 \displaystyle 0\in\{c_{m}^{\uparrow}(\pi|\phi),c_{m}^{\rightarrow}(\pi|\phi)\}\subseteq\mathbbm{Z}_{\leq 0}\quad\textup{and}\quad 0\in\{c_{m}^{\downarrow}(\pi|\phi),c_{m}^{\leftarrow}(\pi|\phi)\}\subseteq\mathbbm{Z}_{\leq 0} 
 
 
 



 (2) 
 
 We have π 1 δ ≤ p ⁡ ( π 1 ) + p ⁡ ( ϕ 0 ) = p ⁡ ( π 0 ) + p ⁡ ( ϕ 1 ) \pi_{1}^{\delta}\leq p(\pi^{1})+p(\phi^{0})=p(\pi^{0})+p(\phi^{1}) and

 

 
 
 ϕ δ = { π δ if  ​ | π δ | = | ϕ δ | ; ( π 2 δ , π 3 δ , … , π p ⁡ ( π δ ) δ ) if  ​ | π δ | > | ϕ δ | ; ( p ⁡ ( π 1 ) + p ⁡ ( ϕ 0 ) , π 1 δ , … , π p ⁡ ( π δ ) δ ) if  ​ | π δ | < | ϕ δ | . \displaystyle\phi^{\delta}=\begin{cases}\pi^{\delta}&\textup{if }|\pi^{\delta}|=|\phi^{\delta}|;\\
(\pi^{\delta}_{2},\pi^{\delta}_{3},\ldots,\pi^{\delta}_{p(\pi^{\delta})})&\textup{if }|\pi^{\delta}|>|\phi^{\delta}|;\\
(p(\pi^{1})+p(\phi^{0}),\pi^{\delta}_{1},\ldots,\pi^{\delta}_{p(\pi^{\delta})})&\textup{if }|\pi^{\delta}|<|\phi^{\delta}|.\end{cases} 
 
 
 



 




 
 The above definition is a rephrasing of [ 6 , Definition 3.4] adapted to our notational choices—to translate between our polytope ( π | ϕ ) (\pi|\phi) notation and the Lusztig data ( a t , λ , a t , a ¯ t , λ ¯ , a ¯ t ) (a_{t},\lambda,a^{t},\bar{a}_{t},\bar{\lambda},\bar{a}^{t}) of [ 6 ] , one takes:

 

 
 
 n t ​ ( π 1 ) = a t ; π δ = λ ; n t ​ ( π 0 ) = a t ; n t ​ ( ϕ 0 ) = a ¯ t ; ϕ δ = λ ¯ ; n t ​ ( ϕ 1 ) = a ¯ t . \displaystyle n_{t}(\pi^{1})=a_{t};\quad\pi^{\delta}=\lambda;\quad n_{t}(\pi^{0})=a^{t};\quad\quad n_{t}(\phi^{0})=\bar{a}_{t};\quad\phi^{\delta}=\bar{\lambda};\quad n_{t}(\phi^{1})=\bar{a}^{t}. 
 
 
 
 We write ℳ ​ 𝒱 ​ ( θ ) \mathcal{MV}(\theta) for the set of affine MV polytopes of content θ \theta :

 

 
 
 ℳ 𝒱 ( θ ) = { ( π | ϕ ) ∣ π , ϕ ∈ Π ( θ )  and  ( π | ϕ )  is an affine MV polytope } , \displaystyle\mathcal{MV}(\theta)=\{(\pi|\phi)\mid\pi,\phi\in\Pi(\theta)\textup{ and }(\pi|\phi)\textup{ is an affine MV polytope}\}, 
 
 
 
 and set ℳ ​ 𝒱 = ⨆ θ ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 ​ I ℳ ​ 𝒱 ​ ( θ ) \mathcal{MV}=\bigsqcup_{\theta\in\mathbbm{Z}_{\geq 0}I}\mathcal{MV}(\theta) .



 
 Example 4.3 . 

 
 Consider the polytope ( π | ϕ ) (\pi|\phi) as in Figure 1. We have

 

 
 
 n 1 ​ ( π 1 ) = 4 n 2 ​ ( π 1 ) = 2 n 3 ​ ( π 1 ) = 0 n m ​ ( π 1 ) = 0 n 1 ​ ( ϕ 0 ) = 2 n 2 ​ ( ϕ 0 ) = 2 n 3 ​ ( ϕ 0 ) = 3 n m ​ ( ϕ 0 ) = 0 \displaystyle\begin{array}[]{lllllllll}n_{1}(\pi^{1})=4&&n_{2}(\pi^{1})=2&&n_{3}(\pi^{1})=0&&n_{m}(\pi^{1})=0\\
n_{1}(\phi^{0})=2&&n_{2}(\phi^{0})=2&&n_{3}(\phi^{0})=3&&n_{m}(\phi^{0})=0\end{array} 
 
 
 
 for m > 3 m>3 , and thus

 

 
 
 c 1 ↑ ​ ( π | ϕ ) = − 2 c 2 ↑ ​ ( π | ϕ ) = 0 c m ↑ ​ ( π | ϕ ) = 0 c 1 → ​ ( π | ϕ ) = 0 c 2 → ​ ( π | ϕ ) = − 4 c m → ​ ( π | ϕ ) = − 13 \displaystyle\begin{array}[]{lllllll}c_{1}^{\uparrow}(\pi|\phi)=-2&&c_{2}^{\uparrow}(\pi|\phi)=0&&c_{m}^{\uparrow}(\pi|\phi)=0\\
c_{1}^{\rightarrow}(\pi|\phi)=0&&c_{2}^{\rightarrow}(\pi|\phi)=-4&&c_{m}^{\rightarrow}(\pi|\phi)=-13\end{array} 
 
 
 
 for all m ≥ 3 m\geq 3 , and so 0 ∈ { c m ↑ ​ ( π | ϕ ) , c m → ​ ( π | ϕ ) } ⊆ ℤ ≤ 0 0\in\{c_{m}^{\uparrow}(\pi|\phi),c_{m}^{\rightarrow}(\pi|\phi)\}\subseteq\mathbbm{Z}_{\leq 0} . A similar check shows that 0 ∈ { c m ↓ ​ ( π | ϕ ) , c m ← ​ ( π | ϕ ) } ⊆ ℤ ≤ 0 0\in\{c_{m}^{\downarrow}(\pi|\phi),c_{m}^{\leftarrow}(\pi|\phi)\}\subseteq\mathbbm{Z}_{\leq 0} , and so Definition  4.2 (1) is satisfied. Since π 1 δ = 2 < 13 = p ⁡ ( π 1 ) + p ⁡ ( ϕ 0 ) \pi^{\delta}_{1}=2<13=p(\pi^{1})+p(\phi^{0}) and π δ = ϕ δ \pi^{\delta}=\phi^{\delta} , we have that Definition  4.2 (2) is satisfied as well, and therefore ( π | ϕ ) (\pi|\phi) is an affine MV polytope.





 
 Theorem 4.4 . 

 
 [ 6 , Theorem 3.11] 
Let π ∈ Π ⁡ ( θ ) \pi\in\Pi(\theta) . Then there exists a unique ϕ ∈ Π ⁡ ( θ ) \phi\in\Pi(\theta) such that ( π | ϕ ) (\pi|\phi) is an affine Mirković-Vilonen polytope. 





 
 Given π ∈ Π ⁡ ( θ ) \pi\in\Pi(\theta) , we will write π ◊ \pi_{\lozenge} for the unique root partition of θ \theta such that ( π | π ◊ ) (\pi|\pi_{\lozenge}) is an affine MV polytope. It follows from the symmetricity of Definition  4.2 then that ( π ◊ | π ) (\pi_{\lozenge}|\pi) is an affine MV polytope as well, so the function ◊ : π ↦ π ◊ \lozenge:\pi\mapsto\pi_{\lozenge} is an involution on Π ⁡ ( θ ) \Pi(\theta) .





 
 4.3. The affine Mirković-Vilonen polytope crystal


 
 Let π ∈ Π ⁡ ( θ ) \pi\in\Pi(\theta) and i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in\mathbbm{Z}_{2} . We let π + i : k ∈ Π ( θ + α i : k ) \pi_{+i:k}\in\Pi(\theta+\alpha_{i:k}) be defined by inserting a row of length k k into π i \pi^{i} . If n k ​ ( π i ) > 0 n_{k}(\pi^{i})>0 , we let π − i : k ∈ Π ( θ − α i : k ) \pi_{-i:k}\in\Pi(\theta-\alpha_{i:k}) be defined by deleting a row of length k k from π i \pi^{i} .
For i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in\mathbbm{Z}_{2} , k ∈ ℤ > 0 k\in\mathbbm{Z}_{>0} we define operators

 

 
 
 f i : k , f i : k ∗ : ℳ 𝒱 ( θ ) → ℳ 𝒱 ( θ + α i : k ) ; e i : k , e i : k ∗ : ℳ 𝒱 ( θ ) → ℳ 𝒱 ( θ − α i : k ) ⊔ { 0 } \displaystyle f_{i:k},f_{i:k}^{*}\colon\mathcal{MV}(\theta)\rightarrow\mathcal{MV}(\theta+\alpha_{i:k});\qquad e_{i:k},e_{i:k}^{*}\colon\mathcal{MV}(\theta)\rightarrow\mathcal{MV}(\theta-\alpha_{i:k})\sqcup\{0\} 
 
 
 
 as follows.

 
 (1) 
 
 The action of the operators f i : k , f i : k ∗ f_{i:k},f_{i:k}^{*} is given by setting

 

 
 
 ( π | ϕ ) → f 1 : k ( ( ϕ + 1 : k ) ◊ | ϕ + 1 : k ) ; ( π | ϕ ) → f 1 : k ∗ ( π + 1 : k | ( π + 1 : k ) ◊ ) ; ( π | ϕ ) → f 0 : k ( π + 0 : k | ( π + 0 : k ) ◊ ) ; ( π | ϕ ) → f ∗ 0 : k ( ( ϕ + 0 : k ) ◊ | ϕ + 0 : k ) . \displaystyle\begin{array}[]{llllll}(\pi|\phi)\xrightarrow{f_{1:k}}((\phi_{+1:k})_{\lozenge}\,|\,\phi_{+1:k});&&(\pi|\phi)\xrightarrow{f_{1:k}^{*}}(\pi_{+1:k}\,|\,(\pi_{+1:k})_{\lozenge});\\
(\pi|\phi)\xrightarrow{f_{0:k}}(\pi_{+0:k}\,|\,(\pi_{+0:k})_{\lozenge});&&(\pi|\phi)\xrightarrow{f^{*}_{0:k}}((\phi_{+0:k})_{\lozenge}\,|\,\phi_{+0:k}).\end{array} 
 
 
 



 (2) 
 
 The action of the operators e i : k , e i : k ∗ e_{i:k},e_{i:k}^{*} is given by setting

 

 
 
 ( π | ϕ ) → e 1 : k ( ( ϕ − 1 : k ) ◊ | ϕ − 1 : k ) ; ( π | ϕ ) → e 1 : k ∗ ( π − 1 : k | ( π − 1 : k ) ◊ ) ; ( π | ϕ ) → e 0 : k ( π − 0 : k | ( π − 0 : k ) ◊ ) ; ( π | ϕ ) → e ∗ 0 : k ( ( ϕ − 0 : k ) ◊ | ϕ − 0 : k ) . \displaystyle\begin{array}[]{llllll}(\pi|\phi)\xrightarrow{e_{1:k}}((\phi_{-1:k})_{\lozenge}\,|\,\phi_{-1:k});&&(\pi|\phi)\xrightarrow{e_{1:k}^{*}}(\pi_{-1:k}\,|\,(\pi_{-1:k})_{\lozenge});\\
(\pi|\phi)\xrightarrow{e_{0:k}}(\pi_{-0:k}\,|\,(\pi_{-0:k})_{\lozenge});&&(\pi|\phi)\xrightarrow{e^{*}_{0:k}}((\phi_{-0:k})_{\lozenge}\,|\,\phi_{-0:k}).\end{array} 
 
 
 
 if n k ​ ( ϕ 1 ) , n k ​ ( π 1 ) , n k ​ ( π 0 ) , n k ​ ( ϕ 0 ) n_{k}(\phi^{1}),n_{k}(\pi^{1}),n_{k}(\pi^{0}),n_{k}(\phi^{0}) are nonzero, respectively. Otherwise we set
 ( π | ϕ ) → 0 (\pi|\phi)\rightarrow 0 .




 
 We abbreviate e i = e i : 1 e_{i}=e_{i:1} , f i = f i : 1 f_{i}=f_{i:1} , e i ∗ = e ∗ i : 1 e_{i}^{*}=e^{*}_{i:1} , f i ∗ = f ∗ i : 1 f_{i}^{*}=f^{*}_{i:1} .



 
 Following [ 6 ] we consider ℳ ​ 𝒱 \mathcal{MV} as an 𝙰 1 ( 1 ) {\tt A}_{1}^{(1)} -bicrystal with operators e i , f i , e i ∗ , f i ∗ e_{i},f_{i},e_{i}^{*},f_{i}^{*} as defined above, and statistics defined by wt ​ ( π | ϕ ) = − θ \textup{wt}(\pi|\phi)=-\theta , and

 

 
 (4.3) 
 
 ε 1 ​ ( π | ϕ ) = n 1 ​ ( ϕ 1 ) ; ε 0 ​ ( π | ϕ ) = n 1 ​ ( π 0 ) ; ε 1 ∗ ​ ( π | ϕ ) = n 1 ​ ( π 1 ) ; ε 0 ∗ ​ ( π | ϕ ) = n 1 ​ ( ϕ 0 ) , \displaystyle\varepsilon_{1}(\pi|\phi)=n_{1}(\phi^{1});\quad\varepsilon_{0}(\pi|\phi)=n_{1}(\pi^{0});\quad\varepsilon_{1}^{*}(\pi|\phi)=n_{1}(\pi^{1});\quad\varepsilon_{0}^{*}(\pi|\phi)=n_{1}(\phi^{0}), 
 
 
 
 for all ( π | ϕ ) ∈ ℳ ​ 𝒱 ​ ( θ ) (\pi|\phi)\in\mathcal{MV}(\theta) . The statistics φ i , φ i ∗ \varphi_{i},\varphi_{i}^{*} are implicitly defined by Definition  3.1 (1).



 
 We include the starred operators for completeness; we will work primarily with ℳ ​ 𝒱 \mathcal{MV} as a highest weight crystal (rather than bicrystal) with highest weight element ( ∅ | ∅ ) ∈ ℳ ​ 𝒱 ​ ( 0 ) (\varnothing|\varnothing)\in\mathcal{MV}(0) using the unstarred operators.
The initial layers of the ℳ ​ 𝒱 \mathcal{MV} crystal are shown in Figure  4 . The key result on affine MV polytopes is the following



 
 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 Figure 4. Initial layers of the ℳ ​ 𝒱 \mathcal{MV} crystal, with elements displayed as marked polytopes (see Example  4.3 for an explanation of the notation). The operators e 1 / f 1 e_{1}/f_{1} appear in red as upward/downward segments respectively. The operators e 0 / f 0 e_{0}/f_{0} appear in blue. 
 
 
 Theorem 4.5 . 

 
 [ 6 , Theorem 4.5] 
The crystal ℳ ​ 𝒱 \mathcal{MV} is isomorphic to the crystal ℬ ⁡ ( ∞ ) \mathcal{B}(\infty) . 





 
 Remark 4.6 . 

 
 Under the isomorphism of Theorem  4.5 , Kashiwara’s ∗ * -involution on ℬ ⁡ ( ∞ ) \mathcal{B}(\infty) corresponds to negation at the level of the geometric polytope, and to the exchange ( π | ϕ ) ↦ ( ϕ | π ) = ( π ◊ | ϕ ◊ ) (\pi|\phi)\mapsto(\phi|\pi)=(\pi_{\lozenge}|\phi_{\lozenge}) in our combinatorial notation.







 
 4.4. The highest weight crystal ℳ ​ 𝒱 Λ \mathcal{MV}^{\Lambda} 


 
 Let t 1 , t 0 ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 t_{1},t_{0}\in\mathbbm{Z}_{\geq 0} , so Λ = t 1 ​ Λ 1 + t 0 ​ Λ 0 \Lambda=t_{1}\Lambda_{1}+t_{0}\Lambda_{0} is a dominant integral weight. Let ℳ ​ 𝒱 Λ ⊂ ℳ ​ 𝒱 \mathcal{MV}^{\Lambda}\subset\mathcal{MV} be the subset of polytopes ( π | ϕ ) (\pi|\phi) such that ε i ∗ ​ ( π | ϕ ) ≤ t i \varepsilon_{i}^{*}(\pi|\phi)\leq t_{i} for i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in\mathbbm{Z}_{2} . Then ( ℳ ​ 𝒱 Λ , e i Λ , f i Λ , wt Λ , ε i Λ , φ i Λ ) (\mathcal{MV}^{\Lambda},e_{i}^{\Lambda},f_{i}^{\Lambda},\textup{wt}^{\Lambda},\varepsilon_{i}^{\Lambda},\varphi_{i}^{\Lambda}) is a highest-weight combinatorial 𝙰 1 ( 1 ) {\tt A}_{1}^{(1)} -crystal, where e i Λ , f i Λ e_{i}^{\Lambda},f_{i}^{\Lambda} are the operators inherited from ℳ ​ 𝒱 \mathcal{MV} by setting all ( π | ϕ ) ∉ ℳ ​ 𝒱 Λ (\pi|\phi)\notin\mathcal{MV}^{\Lambda} equal to 0 0 , and we have wt Λ = Λ + wt \textup{wt}^{\Lambda}=\Lambda+\textup{wt} , ε i Λ = ε i \varepsilon_{i}^{\Lambda}=\varepsilon_{i} , φ i Λ = φ i + t i \varphi_{i}^{\Lambda}=\varphi_{i}+t_{i} .
The following is [ 6 , Corollary 4.6] .



 
 Theorem 4.7 . 

 
 The crystal ℳ ​ 𝒱 Λ \mathcal{MV}^{\Lambda} is isomorphic to the highest weight crystal ℬ ⁡ ( Λ ) \mathcal{B}(\Lambda) . 







 
 4.5. Working with the crystal ℳ ​ 𝒱 \mathcal{MV} 


 
 The main inconvenience in working with the crystal ℳ ​ 𝒱 \mathcal{MV} (or OPEN ℳ ​ 𝒱 Λ ) \mathcal{MV}^{\Lambda}) is the cumbersome definition of the involution ◊ : π ↦ π ◊ \lozenge:\pi\mapsto\pi_{\lozenge} . The existence/uniqueness definition of ◊ \lozenge implied by Theorem  4.4 does not yield a very practical way to work with affine MV polytopes, particularly since each application of an operator e i / f i e_{i}/f_{i} in § 4.3 involves recalculating ◊ \lozenge anew. Currently, the best approach to constructing π ◊ \pi_{\lozenge} involves iteratively tweaking/mending polytope shapes ‘one trapezoid at a time’ until the myriad ℳ ​ 𝒱 \mathcal{MV} conditions are satisfied (see [ 6 , Remark 3.22] ). We now rectify this issue.



 
 4.5.1. A combinatorial description of the ◊ \lozenge involution


 
 The tableau combinatorics of § 2.1 , 2.2 can be used to directly describe the key involution ◊ : Π ⁡ ( θ ) → Π ⁡ ( θ ) \lozenge:\Pi(\theta)\rightarrow\Pi(\theta) . This appears as Theorem  9.3 .



 
 Theorem B . 

 
 Let π = { π 1 , π δ , π 0 } ∈ Π ⁡ ( β ) \pi=\{\pi^{1},\pi^{\delta},\pi^{0}\}\in\Pi(\beta) be a root partition in type 𝙰 1 ( 1 ) {\tt A}_{1}^{(1)} . Then π ◊ = { π ◊ 1 , π ◊ δ , π ◊ 0 } \pi_{\lozenge}=\{\pi_{\lozenge}^{1},\pi_{\lozenge}^{\delta},\pi_{\lozenge}^{0}\} may be directly constructed as follows. Set 𝚠 ⁡ ( π ) = max ⁡ { 𝚠 ⁡ ( π 0 ) , π 1 δ , 𝚠 ⁡ ( π 1 ) } {\tt w}(\pi)=\max\{{\tt w}(\pi^{0}),\pi_{1}^{\delta},{\tt w}(\pi^{1})\} . Then we have 

 

 
 
 π ◊ 1 \displaystyle\pi_{\lozenge}^{1} 
 = orth 𝚠 ⁡ ( π ) ​ ( π 0 ) ; \displaystyle=\mathrm{orth}^{{\tt w}(\pi)}(\pi^{0}); 
 
 
 
 
 π ◊ 0 \displaystyle\pi_{\lozenge}^{0} 
 = orth 𝚠 ⁡ ( π ) ​ ( π 1 ) ; \displaystyle=\mathrm{orth}^{{\tt w}(\pi)}(\pi^{1}); 
 
 
 
 
 π ◊ δ \displaystyle\pi_{\lozenge}^{\delta} 
 = { ( 𝚠 ⁡ ( π ) , π 1 δ , … , π p ⁡ ( π δ ) δ ) if  ​ 𝚠 ​ ( π ) = 𝚠 ⁡ ( π 0 ) = 𝚠 ⁡ ( π 1 ) ; ( π 2 δ , … , π p ⁡ ( π δ ) δ ) if  ​ 𝚠 ​ ( π ) > 𝚠 ⁡ ( π 0 ) , 𝚠 ⁡ ( π 1 ) ; π δ otherwise . \displaystyle=\begin{cases}({\tt w}(\pi),\pi_{1}^{\delta},\ldots,\pi_{p(\pi^{\delta})}^{\delta})&\textup{if }{\tt w}(\pi)={\tt w}(\pi^{0})={\tt w}(\pi^{1});\\
(\pi^{\delta}_{2},\ldots,\pi^{\delta}_{p(\pi^{\delta})})&\textup{if }{\tt w}(\pi)>{\tt w}(\pi^{0}),{\tt w}(\pi^{1});\\
\pi^{\delta}&\textup{otherwise}.\end{cases} 
 
 
 










 
 5. Kleshchev multipartitions


 
 5.1. Multicharges and multipartitions


 
 A (semi-infinite) multicharge 𝜿 = ( κ 1 , κ 2 , … ) \boldsymbol{\kappa}=(\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2},\ldots) is a sequence of elements of ℤ 2 \mathbbm{Z}_{2} containing infinitely many 0’s and infinitely many 1’s. A 𝜿 \boldsymbol{\kappa} -multipartition 𝝀 = ( λ ( 1 ) , λ ( 2 ) , … ) \boldsymbol{\lambda}=(\lambda^{(1)},\lambda^{(2)},\ldots) is a sequence of partitions λ ( t ) ∈ 𝒫 κ t \lambda^{(t)}\in\mathcal{P}^{\kappa_{t}} , with λ ( u ) = ∅ \lambda^{(u)}=\varnothing for u ≫ 0 u\gg 0 .
If λ ( m ) = ∅ \lambda^{(m)}=\varnothing for m > ℓ m>\ell then we say that 𝝀 \boldsymbol{\lambda} is of level ℓ \ell .
We write | 𝝀 | = ∑ t = 1 ∞ | λ ( t ) | |\boldsymbol{\lambda}|=\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}|\lambda^{(t)}| . The content of 𝝀 \boldsymbol{\lambda} is given by

 

 
 
 cont ​ ( 𝝀 ) = # ⁡ { boxes of residue  ​ 0 ​  in  ​ 𝝀 } ⋅ α 0 + # ⁡ { boxes of residue  ​ 1 ​  in  ​ 𝝀 } ⋅ α 1 ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 ​ I . \displaystyle\textup{cont}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})=\#\{\textup{boxes of residue }0\textup{ in }\boldsymbol{\lambda}\}\cdot\alpha_{0}+\#\{\textup{boxes of residue }1\textup{ in }\boldsymbol{\lambda}\}\cdot\alpha_{1}\in\mathbbm{Z}_{\geq 0}I. 
 
 
 
 We write ℳ ​ 𝒫 𝜿 ​ ( θ ) \mathcal{MP}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\theta) for the set of 𝜿 \boldsymbol{\kappa} -multipartitions of content θ ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 ​ I \theta\in\mathbbm{Z}_{\geq 0}I , and set ℳ ​ 𝒫 𝜿 = ⨆ θ ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 ​ I ℳ ​ 𝒫 𝜿 ​ ( θ ) \mathcal{MP}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}=\bigsqcup_{\theta\in\mathbbm{Z}_{\geq 0}I}\mathcal{MP}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\theta) .
We depict 𝝀 ∈ ℳ ​ 𝒫 𝜿 \boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{MP}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}} as a vertical array of partitions with λ ( 1 ) \lambda^{(1)} at the bottom and with residues shown within each box; see Figure  5 for examples.
The dominance order ⊵ \trianglerighteq on ℳ ​ 𝒫 𝜿 \mathcal{MP}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}} is defined by setting 𝝁 ​ ⊵ ​ 𝝀 \boldsymbol{\mu}\trianglerighteq\boldsymbol{\lambda} provided

 

 
 
 ∑ s = t + 1 ∞ | μ ( s ) | + ∑ r = 1 m μ r ( t ) ≥ ∑ s = t + 1 ∞ | λ ( s ) | + ∑ r = 1 m λ r ( t ) for all  ​ t ∈ ℤ > 0 , m ∈ [ 1 , p ⁡ ( λ ( t ) ) ] . \displaystyle\sum^{\infty}_{s=t+1}|\mu^{(s)}|+\sum_{r=1}^{m}\mu_{r}^{(t)}\geq\sum^{\infty}_{s=t+1}|\lambda^{(s)}|+\sum_{r=1}^{m}\lambda_{r}^{(t)}\qquad\textup{for all }t\in\mathbbm{Z}_{>0},m\in[1,p(\lambda^{(t)})]. 
 
 
 


 
 Remark 5.1 . 

 
 We use semi-infinite multicharges in this paper because our primary focus is the ℬ ⁡ ( ∞ ) \mathcal{B}(\infty) crystal; one easily recovers the more familiar finite multicharge ( κ 1 , … , κ ℓ ) (\kappa_{1},\ldots,\kappa_{\ell}) setting by selecting any semi-infinite extension 𝜿 = ( κ 1 , … , κ ℓ , … ) \boldsymbol{\kappa}=(\kappa_{1},\ldots,\kappa_{\ell},\ldots) and restricting to multipartitions of level ℓ \ell , see § 5.3 . Because we choose to index our multipartition components vertically with the first component at the bottom, readers accustomed to indexing beginning from the top component will also need to reorder to match our convention.





 
 For i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in\mathbbm{Z}_{2} , a box u ∈ 𝝀 u\in\boldsymbol{\lambda} is said to be i i -removable in 𝝀 \boldsymbol{\lambda} if res ⁡ ( u ) = i \mathrm{res}(u)=i and 𝝀 \ u \boldsymbol{\lambda}\backslash u is a 𝜿 \boldsymbol{\kappa} -multipartition. A box u ∉ 𝝀 u\notin\boldsymbol{\lambda} is said to be i i -addable in 𝝀 \boldsymbol{\lambda} if res ⁡ ( u ) = i \mathrm{res}(u)=i and 𝝀 ⊔ u \boldsymbol{\lambda}\sqcup u is a 𝜿 \boldsymbol{\kappa} -multipartition. We note that by the conditions on the semi-infinite multicharge 𝜿 \boldsymbol{\kappa} , there will always be infinitely many i i -addable boxes for 𝝀 \boldsymbol{\lambda} , and finitely many (perhaps zero) i i -removable boxes.



 
 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 Figure 5. Take the multicharge 𝜿 \boldsymbol{\kappa} with κ 1 = κ 2 = 0 , κ 3 = 1 \kappa_{1}=\kappa_{2}=0,\kappa_{3}=1 , and θ = 7 ​ α 1 + 8 ​ α 0 \theta=7\alpha_{1}+8\alpha_{0} . A level 3 multipartition 𝝀 ∈ ℳ ​ 𝒫 𝜿 ​ ( θ ) \boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{MP}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\theta) is shown in the center, where λ ( 1 ) = ( 3 , 2 2 , 1 ) \lambda^{(1)}=(3,2^{2},1) , λ ( 2 ) = ( 2 2 , 1 ) \lambda^{(2)}=(2^{2},1) , λ ( 3 ) = ( 1 2 ) \lambda^{(3)}=(1^{2}) . The 0 0 -addable boxes in the first three components are highlighted, as is the 0 0 -arc arrangement 𝝀 ⁡ [ 0 ] \boldsymbol{\lambda}[0] for 𝝀 \boldsymbol{\lambda} . As 𝝀 \boldsymbol{\lambda} has one unarced 0 0 -removable box, we have ε 0 ​ ( 𝝀 ) = 1 \varepsilon_{0}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})=1 . The multipartitions e 0 ​ 𝝀 e_{0}\boldsymbol{\lambda} and f 0 ​ 𝝀 f_{0}\boldsymbol{\lambda} are shown to the left and right respectively. 
 


 
 5.2. The Kleshchev multipartition crystal


 
 Let 𝝀 ∈ ℳ ​ 𝒫 𝜿 \boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{MP}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}} and i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in\mathbbm{Z}_{2} . We define the i i -arc arrangement 𝛌 ⁡ [ i ] \boldsymbol{\lambda}[i] for 𝛌 \boldsymbol{\lambda} as follows. Reading top to bottom, whenever an i i -removable box is followed by an i i -addable box with no unarced i i -removable/ i i -addable boxes between them, draw an arc connecting them, and repeat until no more such arcs are possible.



 
 For i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in\mathbbm{Z}_{2} , we define operators

 

 
 
 f i : ℳ ​ 𝒫 𝜿 ​ ( θ ) → ℳ ​ 𝒫 𝜿 ​ ( θ + α i ) ; e i : ℳ ​ 𝒫 𝜿 ​ ( θ ) → ℳ ​ 𝒫 𝜿 ​ ( θ − α i ) ⊔ { 0 } \displaystyle f_{i}:\mathcal{MP}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\theta)\rightarrow\mathcal{MP}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\theta+\alpha_{i});\qquad e_{i}:\mathcal{MP}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\theta)\rightarrow\mathcal{MP}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\theta-\alpha_{i})\sqcup\{0\} 
 
 
 
 as follows.

 
 (1) 
 
 Set f i ​ 𝝀 f_{i}\boldsymbol{\lambda} to be the 𝜿 \boldsymbol{\kappa} -multipartition given by adding the bottommost unarced i i -addable box in 𝝀 ⁡ [ i ] \boldsymbol{\lambda}[i] .




 (2) 
 
 Set e i ​ 𝝀 e_{i}\boldsymbol{\lambda} to be the 𝜿 \boldsymbol{\kappa} -multipartition given by deleting the topmost unarced i i -removable box in 𝝀 ⁡ [ i ] \boldsymbol{\lambda}[i] . If no such box exists, set e i ​ 𝝀 = 0 e_{i}\boldsymbol{\lambda}=0 .




 
 We show an example application of the operators e 0 / f 0 e_{0}/f_{0} in Figure  5 .



 
 We say that 𝝀 ∈ ℳ ​ 𝒫 𝜿 \boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{MP}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}} is Kleshchev provided that 𝝀 = f i m ⋯ f i 2 f i 1 ∅ \boldsymbol{\lambda}=f_{i_{m}}\cdots f_{i_{2}}f_{i_{1}}\varnothing (or equivalently ∅ = e i 1 e i 2 ⋯ e i m 𝝀 \varnothing=e_{i_{1}}e_{i_{2}}\cdots e_{i_{m}}\boldsymbol{\lambda} ) for some sequence ( i 1 , i 2 , … , i m ) ∈ ℤ 2 m (i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{m})\in\mathbbm{Z}_{2}^{m} .
We write ℳ ​ 𝒫 K 𝜿 \mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}} for the set of Kleshchev multipartitions. Then ℳ ​ 𝒫 K 𝜿 \mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}} is a highest weight combinatorial 𝙰 1 ( 1 ) {\tt A}^{(1)}_{1} -crystal, where e i , f i e_{i},f_{i} are simply the restrictions to ℳ ​ 𝒫 K 𝜿 \mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}} of the operators defined above, the weight function is given by wt ​ ( 𝝀 ) = − cont ​ ( 𝝀 ) \textup{wt}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})=-\textup{cont}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}) , the statistic ε i ​ ( 𝝀 ) \varepsilon_{i}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}) is the number of unarced i i -removable boxes in 𝝀 ⁡ [ i ] \boldsymbol{\lambda}[i] , and φ i \varphi_{i} is implicitly defined by Definition  3.1 (1). The first few layers of the crystal ℳ ​ 𝒫 K 𝜿 \mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}} for 𝜿 = ( 0 , 1 , 0 , 1 , … ) \boldsymbol{\kappa}=(0,1,0,1,\ldots) are shown in Figure  6 .



 
 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 Figure 6. Initial layers of the ℳ ​ 𝒫 K 𝜿 \mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}} crystal for the multicharge 𝜿 = ( 0 , 1 , 0 , 1 , … ) \boldsymbol{\kappa}=(0,1,0,1,\ldots) . Empty components of multipartitions are not shown. The operators e 1 / f 1 e_{1}/f_{1} appear in red as upward/downward segments respectively. The operators e 0 / f 0 e_{0}/f_{0} appear in blue. 
 


 
 5.3. The highest weight crystal ℳ ​ 𝒫 K ( 𝜿 , ℓ ) \mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\ell)} 


 
 Fix ℓ ∈ ℤ > 0 \ell\in\mathbbm{Z}_{>0} . We define ℳ ​ 𝒫 K ( 𝜿 , ℓ ) \mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\ell)} to be the set of Kleshchev multipartitions in ℳ ​ 𝒫 K 𝜿 \mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}} of level ℓ \ell (i.e., those 𝝀 ∈ ℳ ​ 𝒫 K 𝜿 \boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}} with λ ( m ) = ∅ \lambda^{(m)}=\varnothing for m > ℓ m>\ell ), and we define the associated dominant integral weight Λ ⁡ ( 𝜿 , ℓ ) := ∑ t = 1 ℓ Λ κ t \Lambda(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\ell):=\sum_{t=1}^{\ell}\Lambda_{\kappa_{t}} . Then ( ℳ ​ 𝒫 K ( 𝜿 , ℓ ) , e i ( 𝜿 , ℓ ) , f i ( 𝜿 , ℓ ) , wt ( 𝜿 , ℓ ) , ε i ( 𝜿 , ℓ ) , φ i ( 𝜿 , ℓ ) ) (\mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\ell)},e_{i}^{(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\ell)},f_{i}^{(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\ell)},\textup{wt}^{(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\ell)},\varepsilon_{i}^{(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\ell)},\varphi_{i}^{(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\ell)}) 
is a highest weight combinatorial 𝙰 1 ( 1 ) {\tt A}_{1}^{(1)} -crystal, with operators e i ( 𝜿 , ℓ ) , f i ( 𝜿 , ℓ ) e_{i}^{(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\ell)},f_{i}^{(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\ell)} inherited from ℳ ​ 𝒫 K 𝜿 \mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}} by setting all 𝝀 ∉ ℳ ​ 𝒫 K ( 𝜿 , ℓ ) \boldsymbol{\lambda}\notin\mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\ell)} equal to 0. We have wt ( 𝜿 , ℓ ) = Λ ⁡ ( 𝜿 , ℓ ) + wt \textup{wt}^{(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\ell)}=\Lambda(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\ell)+\textup{wt} , ε i ( 𝜿 , ℓ ) = ε i \varepsilon_{i}^{(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\ell)}=\varepsilon_{i} , and φ i ( 𝜿 , ℓ ) ​ ( 𝝀 ) \varphi_{i}^{(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\ell)}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}) is the number of unarced i i -addable boxes in components 1 1 through ℓ \ell of 𝝀 ⁡ [ i ] \boldsymbol{\lambda}[i] .
The following is due to work of [ 35 , 36 , 40 , 46 ] in level one, and extended to higher levels in [ 4 ] . See also [ 14 , 58 ] .



 
 Theorem 5.2 . 

 
 Let Λ \Lambda be a dominant integral weight, and assume the multicharge 𝛋 \boldsymbol{\kappa} and ℓ ∈ ℤ > 0 \ell\in\mathbbm{Z}_{>0} are such that Λ = Λ ⁡ ( 𝛋 , ℓ ) \Lambda=\Lambda(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\ell) . Then the crystal ℳ ​ 𝒫 K ( 𝛋 , ℓ ) \mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\ell)} is isomorphic to the highest weight crystal ℬ ⁡ ( Λ ) \mathcal{B}(\Lambda) . 





 
 The following theorem, well-known to experts, can be seen as an ℓ → ∞ \ell\rightarrow\infty limit of Theorem  5.2 , or as a result of Theorems  4.5 , 9.6 and 9.12 .



 
 Theorem 5.3 . 

 
 Let 𝛋 \boldsymbol{\kappa} be a multicharge. Then the crystal ℳ ​ 𝒫 K 𝛋 \mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}} is isomorphic to ℬ ⁡ ( ∞ ) \mathcal{B}(\infty) . 







 
 5.4. A recognition theorem for Kleshchev multipartitions


 
 One of the main difficulties in working with the crystal ℳ ​ 𝒫 K 𝜿 \mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}} lies in the iterative definition of Kleshchevness. It is well-known that if 𝝀 \boldsymbol{\lambda} is of level one, then 𝝀 \boldsymbol{\lambda} is Kleshchev if and only if λ ( 1 ) \lambda^{(1)} is 2-restricted. Under a restrictive choice of multicharge and multipartition ( κ t ≠ κ t + 1 \kappa_{t}\neq\kappa_{t+1} for at most one t t less than the level of 𝝀 \boldsymbol{\lambda} ), a non-iterative condition for Kleshchevness was given in [ 43 , Proposition 4.9] (see also [ 3 , 20 ] for other settings and approaches).
But for general multicharge and multipartition, no similar characterizing condition is known, and a priori it requires a | 𝝀 | |\boldsymbol{\lambda}| -step process (either via repeated applications of the operators e 0 / e 1 e_{0}/e_{1} or by an approach as in [ 20 ] ) to determine whether 𝝀 \boldsymbol{\lambda} is Kleshchev or not. We rectify this with the following non-iterative recognition theorem, which appears as Theorem  9.14 .



 
 Theorem C . 

 
 Working in type 𝙰 1 ( 1 ) {\tt A}_{1}^{(1)} , let 𝛋 \boldsymbol{\kappa} be a multicharge, and let 𝛌 \boldsymbol{\lambda} be a multipartition of level ℓ \ell . Then 𝛌 \boldsymbol{\lambda} is Kleshchev if and only if it satisfies the following conditions: 

 
 (1) 
 
 The partition λ ( t ) \lambda^{(t)} is 2-restricted for all t ∈ [ 1 , ℓ ] t\in[1,\ell] . 




 (2) 
 
 For all t ∈ [ 1 , ℓ − 1 ] t\in[1,\ell-1] , we have λ 1 ( t ) − ( λ ( t + 1 ) ) 1 ′ ≥ δ κ t , κ t + 1 − 1 \lambda^{(t)}_{1}-(\lambda^{(t+1)})^{\prime}_{1}\geq\delta_{\kappa_{t},\kappa_{t+1}}-1 . 




 (3) 
 
 If s ∈ [ 1 , ℓ − 2 ] s\in[1,\ell-2] , u ∈ [ s + 2 , ℓ ] u\in[s+2,\ell] are such that λ ( u ) ≠ ∅ \lambda^{(u)}\neq\varnothing and κ s ≠ κ s + 1 = ⋯ = κ u − 1 ≠ κ u \kappa_{s}\neq\kappa_{s+1}=\cdots=\kappa_{u-1}\neq\kappa_{u} , then there exists t ∈ [ s , u − 1 ] t\in[s,u-1] such that λ 1 ( t ) − ( λ ( t + 1 ) ) 1 ′ ≥ δ κ t , κ t + 1 + 1 \lambda^{(t)}_{1}-(\lambda^{(t+1)})^{\prime}_{1}\geq\delta_{\kappa_{t},\kappa_{t+1}}+1 . 




 




 
 Remark 5.4 . 

 
 We learned after completing this work that Theorem  Theorem C was also discovered independently by Fayers, and will appear in his forthcoming book [ 15 ] .





 
 Remark 5.5 . 

 
 Conditions (1) and (2) appear in Mathas’s characterization in the restricted multicharge setting [ 43 ] . Condition (3) was identified with the aid of machine learning. We trained decision tree classifiers with an emphasis on features known to distinguish Kleshchevness in special cases, and interpreting the results helped identify the criterion for the general case. It would be interesting to explore whether machine learning could similarly assist in the study of Kleshchev multipartitions beyond the 𝙰 1 ( 1 ) {\tt A}_{1}^{(1)} case.









 
 6. Upper ledge diagrams


 
 6.1. Ledge diagrams


 
 We now define some new combinatorial objects.
A ledge diagram 𝖣 \mathsf{D} is a left-aligned arrangement of boxes with rows of length ( d 1 , … , d m ) ∈ ℤ > 0 m (d_{1},\ldots,d_{m})\in\mathbbm{Z}_{>0}^{m} from top to bottom, with each box ‘colored’ by i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in\mathbbm{Z}_{2} , such that:

 
 (1) 
 
 for some t ∈ [ 1 , m ] t\in[1,m] , ( d 1 , … , d t ) (d_{1},\ldots,d_{t}) is weakly increasing and ( d t , … , d m ) (d_{t},\ldots,d_{m}) is weakly decreasing, and;




 (2) 
 
 all boxes within a given row share the same color, and colors alternate down columns.




 
 We refer to any row of maximal length in a ledge diagram 𝖣 \mathsf{D} as a peak row of 𝖣 \mathsf{D} , and any row which is of maximal length among i i -colored rows as an i i -peak row. We say an i i -colored box in 𝖣 \mathsf{D} is i i -bottom (resp. i i -top) if there is no box directly below (resp. above) it. We define the content of 𝖣 \mathsf{D} to be

 

 
 
 cont ​ ( 𝖣 ) = # ⁡ { boxes of color  ​ 0 ​  in  ​ 𝖣 } ⋅ α 0 + # ⁡ { boxes of color  ​ 1 ​  in  ​ 𝖣 } ⋅ α 1 ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 ​ I . \displaystyle\textup{cont}(\mathsf{D})=\#\{\textup{boxes of color }0\textup{ in }\mathsf{D}\}\cdot\alpha_{0}+\#\{\textup{boxes of color }1\textup{ in }\mathsf{D}\}\cdot\alpha_{1}\in\mathbbm{Z}_{\geq 0}I. 
 
 
 
 An upper (resp. lower ) ledge diagram is one wherein we replace the ‘weakly’ in front of ‘increasing’ (resp. ‘decreasing’) with ‘strictly’ in (1) above.
We write 𝒰 ​ ℒ ​ ( θ ) \mathcal{UL}(\theta) (resp. ℒ ​ ℒ ​ ( θ ) \mathcal{LL}(\theta) ) for the upper (resp. lower) ledge diagrams of content θ ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 ​ I \theta\in\mathbbm{Z}_{\geq 0}I . We set 𝒰 ​ ℒ = ⨆ θ ∈ ℤ > 0 ​ I 𝒰 ​ ℒ ​ ( θ ) \mathcal{UL}=\bigsqcup_{\theta\in\mathbbm{Z}_{>0}I}\mathcal{UL}(\theta) and ℒ ​ ℒ = ⨆ θ ∈ ℤ > 0 ​ I ℒ ​ ℒ ​ ( θ ) \mathcal{LL}=\bigsqcup_{\theta\in\mathbbm{Z}_{>0}I}\mathcal{LL}(\theta) . Example upper/lower ledge diagrams appear in Figure  7 .



 
 Given an upper (resp. lower) ledge diagram 𝖣 \mathsf{D} , there is a unique way to rearrange 𝖣 \mathsf{D} into a lower (resp. upper) ledge diagram 𝖣 ′ \mathsf{D}^{\prime} by shifting columns of 𝖣 \mathsf{D} in increments of two so that box colors are preserved. We write sink : ℒ ​ ℒ ​ ( θ ) → 𝒰 ​ ℒ ​ ( θ ) \mathrm{sink}\colon\mathcal{LL}(\theta)\rightarrow\mathcal{UL}(\theta) and float : 𝒰 ​ ℒ ​ ( θ ) → ℒ ​ ℒ ​ ( θ ) \mathrm{float}\colon\mathcal{UL}(\theta)\rightarrow\mathcal{LL}(\theta) for these inverse bijections. See Figure  7 for an example. We will also write flip for the involution on ledge diagrams given by reflecting across a horizontal axis. For λ ∈ 𝒫 \lambda\in\mathcal{P} , we will write color i ​ ( λ ) ∈ 𝒰 ​ ℒ \textup{color}_{i}(\lambda)\in\mathcal{UL} for the upper ledge diagram with shape λ \lambda and with top row colored by i i . If a ledge diagram has weakly decreasing rows, then we write forget ​ ( 𝖣 ) ∈ 𝒫 \textup{forget}(\mathsf{D})\in\mathcal{P} for the partition given by forgetting the box colors of 𝖣 \mathsf{D} .



 
 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 Figure 7. Take θ = 14 ​ α 1 + 14 ​ α 0 \theta=14\alpha_{1}+14\alpha_{0} . An upper ledge diagram 𝖣 ∈ 𝒰 ​ ℒ ​ ( θ ) \mathsf{D}\in\mathcal{UL}(\theta) is shown at center, and lower ledge diagrams 𝖣 ′ = flip ​ ( 𝖣 ) \mathsf{D}^{\prime}=\textup{flip}(\mathsf{D}) and 𝖣 ′′ = sink ⁡ ( 𝖣 ) \mathsf{D}^{\prime\prime}=\mathrm{sink}(\mathsf{D}) are shown to either side. 
 


 
 6.2. The upper ledge diagram crystal


 
 Let i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in\mathbbm{Z}_{2} and 𝖣 ∈ 𝒰 ​ ℒ \mathsf{D}\in\mathcal{UL} . We define the i i -arc arrangement 𝖣 ⁡ [ i ] \mathsf{D}[i] for 𝖣 \mathsf{D} as follows. Reading right to left, whenever an i i -top (resp. i i -bottom) box is followed by an i ^ \widehat{i} -top (resp. i ^ \widehat{i} -bottom) box with no unarced top (resp. bottom) boxes between them, draw an arc connecting them. Repeat until no more such arcs are possible. An example is shown in Figure  8 .



 
 We give 𝒰 ​ ℒ \mathcal{UL} the structure of an 𝙰 1 ( 1 ) {\tt A}_{1}^{(1)} -bicrystal as follows. Let i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in\mathbbm{Z}_{2} and 𝖣 ∈ 𝒰 ​ ℒ \mathsf{D}\in\mathcal{UL} . We set wt ​ ( 𝖣 ) = − cont ​ ( 𝖣 ) \textup{wt}(\mathsf{D})=-\textup{cont}(\mathsf{D}) . The statistic ε i ​ ( 𝖣 ) \varepsilon_{i}(\mathsf{D}) is the number of unarced i i -bottom boxes in 𝖣 ⁡ [ i ] \mathsf{D}[i] , and ε i ∗ ​ ( 𝖣 ) \varepsilon_{i}^{*}(\mathsf{D}) is the number of unarced i i -top boxes in 𝖣 ⁡ [ i ] \mathsf{D}[i] . The statistics φ i , φ i ∗ \varphi_{i},\varphi_{i}^{*} are implicitly defined by Definition  3.1 (1).
Then

 
 (1) 
 
 e i ​ 𝖣 e_{i}\mathsf{D} is given by deleting the rightmost unarced i i -bottom box in 𝖣 ⁡ [ i ] \mathsf{D}[i] . If none exists, set e i ​ 𝖣 = 0 e_{i}\mathsf{D}=0 .




 (2) 
 
 f i ​ 𝖣 f_{i}\mathsf{D} is given by adding an i i -colored box directly below the leftmost unarced i ^ \widehat{i} -bottom box in 𝖣 ⁡ [ i ] \mathsf{D}[i] . If none exists, append an i i -colored box to the uppermost i i -peak row in 𝖣 \mathsf{D} .




 (3) 
 
 e i ∗ ​ 𝖣 e_{i}^{*}\mathsf{D} is given by deleting the rightmost unarced i i -top box in 𝖣 ⁡ [ i ] \mathsf{D}[i] . If none exists, set e i ∗ ​ 𝖣 = 0 e_{i}^{*}\mathsf{D}=0 .




 (4) 
 
 f i ∗ ​ 𝖣 f_{i}^{*}\mathsf{D} is given by adding an i i -colored box directly above the leftmost unarced i ^ \widehat{i} -top box in 𝖣 ⁡ [ i ] \mathsf{D}[i] . If none exists, append an i i -colored box to the uppermost i i -peak row in 𝖣 \mathsf{D} .




 
 An example application of these crystal operators is shown in Figure  8 .
As with affine MV polytopes, we will primarily treat 𝒰 ​ ℒ \mathcal{UL} as a crystal (rather than bicrystal) with unstarred operators. The first few layers of the crystal 𝒰 ​ ℒ \mathcal{UL} appear in Figure  9 .
The following theorem is a result of Theorems  4.5 and 9.6 .



 
 Theorem D . 

 
 The 𝙰 1 ( 1 ) {\tt A}_{1}^{(1)} -crystal 𝒰 ​ ℒ \mathcal{UL} is isomorphic to ℬ ⁡ ( ∞ ) \mathcal{B}(\infty) . 





 
 Remark 6.1 . 

 
 It is straightforward to check from Remark  4.6 and Theorem  4.5 that Kashiwara’s ∗ * -involution corresponds to the map float ∘ flip : 𝒰 ​ ℒ → 𝒰 ​ ℒ \textup{float}\circ\textup{flip}:\mathcal{UL}\rightarrow\mathcal{UL} under the isomorphism of Theorem  Theorem D .





 
 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 Figure 8. At left, an upper ledge diagram 𝖣 ∈ 𝒰 ​ ℒ \mathsf{D}\in\mathcal{UL} shown with 0 0 -arc arrangement 𝖣 ⁡ [ 0 ] \mathsf{D}[0] . Shown to the right are the upper ledge diagrams e 0 ​ 𝖣 e_{0}\mathsf{D} , f 0 ​ 𝖣 f_{0}\mathsf{D} , and f 0 ∗ ​ 𝖣 f_{0}^{*}\mathsf{D} (note e 0 ∗ ​ 𝖣 = 0 e_{0}^{*}\mathsf{D}=0 ). 
 
 
 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 Figure 9. Initial layers of the 𝒰 ​ ℒ \mathcal{UL} crystal. The operators e 1 / f 1 e_{1}/f_{1} appear in red as upward/downward segments respectively. The operators e 0 / f 0 e_{0}/f_{0} appear in blue. 
 


 
 6.3. The highest weight crystal 𝒰 ​ ℒ Λ \mathcal{UL}^{\Lambda} 


 
 Let t 1 , t 0 ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 t_{1},t_{0}\in\mathbbm{Z}_{\geq 0} , so Λ = t 1 ​ Λ 1 + t 0 ​ Λ 0 \Lambda=t_{1}\Lambda_{1}+t_{0}\Lambda_{0} is a dominant integral weight. Let 𝒰 ​ ℒ Λ ⊂ 𝒰 ​ ℒ \mathcal{UL}^{\Lambda}\subset\mathcal{UL} be the subset of upper ledge diagrams 𝖣 \mathsf{D} such that ε i ∗ ​ ( 𝖣 ) ≤ t i \varepsilon_{i}^{*}(\mathsf{D})\leq t_{i} for i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in\mathbbm{Z}_{2} . Then ( 𝒰 ​ ℒ Λ , e i Λ , f i Λ , wt Λ , ε i Λ , φ i Λ ) (\mathcal{UL}^{\Lambda},e_{i}^{\Lambda},f_{i}^{\Lambda},\textup{wt}^{\Lambda},\varepsilon_{i}^{\Lambda},\varphi_{i}^{\Lambda}) is a highest-weight combinatorial 𝙰 1 ( 1 ) {\tt A}_{1}^{(1)} -crystal, where e i Λ , f i Λ e_{i}^{\Lambda},f_{i}^{\Lambda} are the operators inherited from 𝒰 ​ ℒ \mathcal{UL} by setting all 𝖣 ∉ 𝒰 ​ ℒ Λ \mathsf{D}\notin\mathcal{UL}^{\Lambda} equal to 0 0 , and we have wt Λ = Λ + wt \textup{wt}^{\Lambda}=\Lambda+\textup{wt} , ε i Λ = ε i \varepsilon_{i}^{\Lambda}=\varepsilon_{i} , φ i Λ = φ i + t i \varphi_{i}^{\Lambda}=\varphi_{i}+t_{i} .
The following is immediate from Theorems  4.7 and 9.6 .



 
 Theorem 6.2 . 

 
 The 𝙰 1 ( 1 ) {\tt A}_{1}^{(1)} -crystal 𝒰 ​ ℒ Λ \mathcal{UL}^{\Lambda} is isomorphic to the highest weight crystal ℬ ⁡ ( Λ ) \mathcal{B}(\Lambda) . 









 
 7. Crystal isomorphisms


 
 In this section we describe the main results of the paper—a tool kit of explicit isomorphisms, couched in the language of partition combinatorics, between the 𝙰 1 ( 1 ) {\tt A}_{1}^{(1)} -crystal structures described in § 4 , 5 , and 6 .



 
 7.1. Crystal isomorphisms between affine MV polytopes and upper ledge diagrams


 
 In this subsection we provide an isomorphism between the 𝙰 1 ( 1 ) {\tt A}_{1}^{(1)} -bicrystals ℳ ​ 𝒱 \mathcal{MV} and 𝒰 ​ ℒ \mathcal{UL} .



 
 7.1.1. The i i -triple upper ledge diagrams


 
 We will first need to introduce a key combinatorial notion. We say ( 𝖣 i ↑ , 𝖣 i δ , 𝖣 i ↓ ) ∈ 𝒰 ​ ℒ 3 (\mathsf{D}^{\uparrow}_{i},\mathsf{D}^{\delta}_{i},\mathsf{D}^{\downarrow}_{i})\in\mathcal{UL}^{3} is an i i -triple upper ledge diagram provided:

 
 • 
 
 the row lengths of 𝖣 i ↑ \mathsf{D}^{\uparrow}_{i} strictly increase from top to bottom, and the bottom row has color i i ;




 • 
 
 the row lengths of 𝖣 i δ \mathsf{D}^{\delta}_{i} weakly decrease from top to bottom, all top boxes in 𝖣 i δ \mathsf{D}^{\delta}_{i} have color i ^ \widehat{i} , and all bottom boxes in 𝖣 i δ \mathsf{D}^{\delta}_{i} have color i i ;




 • 
 
 the row lengths of 𝖣 i ↓ \mathsf{D}^{\downarrow}_{i} strictly decrease from top to bottom, and the top row has color i ^ \widehat{i} .




 
 Note then that D i δ D_{i}^{\delta} will always have even-length columns. We write 𝒰 ​ ℒ i 3 \mathcal{UL}^{3}_{i} for the set of i i -triple upper ledge diagrams.



 
 To ( 𝖣 i ↑ , 𝖣 i δ , 𝖣 i ↓ ) ∈ 𝒰 ​ ℒ i 3 (\mathsf{D}^{\uparrow}_{i},\mathsf{D}^{\delta}_{i},\mathsf{D}^{\downarrow}_{i})\in\mathcal{UL}_{i}^{3} we may associate an upper ledge diagram stack i ​ ( 𝖣 i ↑ , 𝖣 i δ , 𝖣 i ↓ ) ∈ 𝒰 ​ ℒ \mathrm{stack}_{i}(\mathsf{D}^{\uparrow}_{i},\mathsf{D}^{\delta}_{i},\mathsf{D}^{\downarrow}_{i})\in\mathcal{UL} as follows. Place 𝖣 i ↑ \mathsf{D}^{\uparrow}_{i} directly above 𝖣 i δ \mathsf{D}^{\delta}_{i} and 𝖣 i ↓ \mathsf{D}^{\downarrow}_{i} directly below 𝖣 i δ \mathsf{D}^{\delta}_{i} with left columns aligned. Then shift the columns of 𝖣 i ↓ \mathsf{D}^{\downarrow}_{i} upward until they meet the bottom of 𝖣 i δ \mathsf{D}^{\delta}_{i} or else align with its top row. Then stack i ​ ( 𝖣 i ↑ , 𝖣 i δ , 𝖣 i ↓ ) \mathrm{stack}_{i}(\mathsf{D}^{\uparrow}_{i},\mathsf{D}^{\delta}_{i},\mathsf{D}^{\downarrow}_{i}) is the resulting diagram. See Figure  10 for an example.



 
 For 𝖣 ∈ 𝒰 ​ ℒ \mathsf{D}\in\mathcal{UL} , we define an associated i i -triple upper ledge diagram trip i ​ ( 𝖣 ) := ( 𝖣 i ↑ , 𝖣 i δ , 𝖣 i ↓ ) \mathrm{trip}_{i}(\mathsf{D}):=(\mathsf{D}^{\uparrow}_{i},\mathsf{D}^{\delta}_{i},\mathsf{D}^{\downarrow}_{i}) as follows.
Take 𝖣 i ↑ \mathsf{D}_{i}^{\uparrow} to be all boxes weakly northwest of the rightmost i i -top box in 𝖣 \mathsf{D} .
To define 𝖣 i ↓ \mathsf{D}_{i}^{\downarrow} , highlight the rightmost i ^ \widehat{i} -bottom box in 𝖣 \mathsf{D} , and then working leftward, highlight the same number of boxes at the bottom of each successive column in 𝖣 \mathsf{D} , increasing by one each time the bottom box color changes.
Shifting the highlighted columns down so they are top-aligned yields 𝖣 i ↓ \mathsf{D}_{i}^{\downarrow} .
(Equivalently, one could take 𝖣 1 ↓ \mathsf{D}_{1}^{\downarrow} to be all boxes weakly southwest of the rightmost i ^ \widehat{i} -bottom box in sink ⁡ ( 𝖣 ) \mathrm{sink}(\mathsf{D}) ).
We take the remaining diagram after removing the boxes in 𝖣 i ↑ \mathsf{D}^{\uparrow}_{i} and 𝖣 i ↓ \mathsf{D}^{\downarrow}_{i} from 𝖣 \mathsf{D} to be 𝖣 i δ \mathsf{D}^{\delta}_{i} . See Figure  10 for an example.
It is easy to check that trip i : 𝒰 ​ ℒ → 𝒰 ​ ℒ i 3 \mathrm{trip}_{i}:\mathcal{UL}\rightarrow\mathcal{UL}^{3}_{i} and stack i : 𝒰 ​ ℒ i 3 → 𝒰 ​ ℒ \mathrm{stack}_{i}:\mathcal{UL}^{3}_{i}\rightarrow\mathcal{UL} are mutually inverse bijections.



 
 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 Figure 10. At left, an upper ledge diagram 𝖣 ∈ 𝒰 ​ ℒ \mathsf{D}\in\mathcal{UL} , and at right its decomposition into the 1 1 -triple upper ledge diagram trip 1 ​ ( 𝖣 ) = ( 𝖣 1 ↑ , 𝖣 1 δ , 𝖣 1 ↓ ) ∈ 𝒰 ​ ℒ 1 3 \mathrm{trip}_{1}(\mathsf{D})=(\mathsf{D}_{1}^{\uparrow},\mathsf{D}^{\delta}_{1},\mathsf{D}^{\downarrow}_{1})\in\mathcal{UL}^{3}_{1} . 
 


 
 7.1.2. From root partitions to upper ledge diagrams


 
 Let θ ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 ​ I \theta\in\mathbbm{Z}_{\geq 0}I , and i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in\mathbbm{Z}_{2} . We define a map 𝒳 i : Π ⁡ ( θ ) → 𝒰 ​ ℒ ​ ( θ ) \mathcal{X}_{i}:\Pi(\theta)\rightarrow\mathcal{UL}(\theta) as follows. For π = { π 1 , π δ , π 0 } ∈ Π ⁡ ( θ ) \pi=\{\pi^{1},\pi^{\delta},\pi^{0}\}\in\Pi(\theta) , we set

 

 
 
 𝖣 i ↑ = flip ∘ color i ∘ ord ⁡ ( π i ) ; 𝖣 i δ = color i ^ ∘ double ​ ( π δ ) ; 𝖣 i ↓ = color i ^ ∘ ord ⁡ ( π i ^ ) , \displaystyle\mathsf{D}^{\uparrow}_{i}=\textup{flip}\circ\textup{color}_{i}\circ\mathrm{ord}(\pi^{i});\qquad\mathsf{D}^{\delta}_{i}=\textup{color}_{\,\widehat{i}}\circ\textup{double}(\pi^{\delta});\qquad\mathsf{D}^{\downarrow}_{i}=\textup{color}_{\,\widehat{i}}\circ\mathrm{ord}(\pi^{\widehat{i}}), 
 
 
 
 and define
 𝒳 i ​ ( π ) = stack i ​ ( 𝖣 i ↑ , 𝖣 i δ , 𝖣 i ↓ ) . \mathcal{X}_{i}(\pi)=\mathrm{stack}_{i}(\mathsf{D}^{\uparrow}_{i},\mathsf{D}^{\delta}_{i},\mathsf{D}^{\downarrow}_{i}). 
An example is shown in Figure  11 .



 
 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 Figure 11. Computing the upper ledge diagram 𝒳 1 ​ ( π ) = 𝖣 \mathcal{X}_{1}(\pi)=\mathsf{D} (at right) associated to the root partition π = { π 1 , π δ , π 0 } \pi=\{\pi^{1},\pi^{\delta},\pi^{0}\} (at left). 
 


 
 7.1.3. From upper ledge diagrams to root partitions


 
 Let θ ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 ​ I \theta\in\mathbbm{Z}_{\geq 0}I , and i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in\mathbbm{Z}_{2} .
We now define a map 𝒴 i : 𝒰 ​ ℒ ​ ( θ ) → Π ⁡ ( θ ) \mathcal{Y}_{i}:\mathcal{UL}(\theta)\rightarrow\Pi(\theta) as follows. Let 𝖣 ∈ 𝒰 ​ ℒ ​ ( θ ) \mathsf{D}\in\mathcal{UL}(\theta) , and set ( 𝖣 i ↑ , 𝖣 i δ , 𝖣 i ↓ ) = trip i ​ ( 𝖣 ) (\mathsf{D}^{\uparrow}_{i},\mathsf{D}^{\delta}_{i},\mathsf{D}^{\downarrow}_{i})=\mathrm{trip}_{i}(\mathsf{D}) . Then define

 

 
 
 π i = calc ∘ forget ∘ flip ​ ( 𝖣 i ↑ ) ; π δ = halve ∘ forget ​ ( 𝖣 i δ ) ; π i ^ = calc ∘ forget ​ ( 𝖣 i ↓ ) , \displaystyle\pi^{i}=\mathrm{calc}\circ\textup{forget}\circ\textup{flip}(\mathsf{D}^{\uparrow}_{i});\qquad\pi^{\delta}=\textup{halve}\circ\textup{forget}(\mathsf{D}^{\delta}_{i});\qquad\pi^{\widehat{i}}=\mathrm{calc}\circ\textup{forget}(\mathsf{D}^{\downarrow}_{i}), 
 
 
 
 and set 𝒴 i ​ ( 𝖣 ) = π = { π 1 , π δ , π 0 } \mathcal{Y}_{i}(\mathsf{D})=\pi=\{\pi^{1},\pi^{\delta},\pi^{0}\} .
An example is shown in Figure  12 .



 
 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 Figure 12. Computing the root partition 𝒴 1 ​ ( 𝖣 ) = π = { π 1 , π δ , π 0 } \mathcal{Y}_{1}(\mathsf{D})=\pi=\{\pi^{1},\pi^{\delta},\pi^{0}\} associated to an upper ledge diagram 𝖣 \mathsf{D} . 
 


 
 7.1.4. The crystal isomorphism ℳ ​ 𝒱 ↔ 𝒰 ​ ℒ \mathcal{MV}\leftrightarrow\mathcal{UL} 


 
 The following result appears as Theorem  9.6 .



 
 Theorem E . 

 
 For θ ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 ​ I \theta\in\mathbbm{Z}_{\geq 0}I and i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in\mathbbm{Z}_{2} , the maps 𝒳 i : Π ⁡ ( θ ) → 𝒰 ​ ℒ ​ ( θ ) \mathcal{X}_{i}:\Pi(\theta)\rightarrow\mathcal{UL}(\theta) and 𝒴 i : 𝒰 ​ ℒ ​ ( θ ) → Π ⁡ ( θ ) \mathcal{Y}_{i}:\mathcal{UL}(\theta)\rightarrow\Pi(\theta) are mutually inverse bijections. Moreover, we have mutually inverse 𝙰 1 ( 1 ) {\tt A}_{1}^{(1)} -bicrystal isomorphisms 

 

 
 
 
 𝒳 : ℳ ​ 𝒱 → ∼ 𝒰 ​ ℒ , ( π | ϕ ) ↦ 𝒳 1 ​ ( π ) = 𝒳 0 ​ ( ϕ ) \displaystyle\mathcal{X}:\mathcal{MV}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{UL},\quad(\pi|\phi)\mapsto\mathcal{X}_{1}(\pi)=\mathcal{X}_{0}(\phi) 
 
 
 
 
 
 𝒴 : 𝒰 ​ ℒ → ∼ ℳ ​ 𝒱 , 𝖣 ↦ ( 𝒴 1 ​ ( 𝖣 ) | 𝒴 0 ​ ( 𝖣 ) ) . \displaystyle\mathcal{Y}:\mathcal{UL}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{MV},\quad\mathsf{D}\mapsto(\mathcal{Y}_{1}(\mathsf{D})\,|\,\mathcal{Y}_{0}(\mathsf{D})). 
 
 
 




 
 Corollary F . 

 
 For any dominant integral weight Λ \Lambda , the maps 𝒳 , 𝒴 \mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y} restrict to mutually inverse 𝙰 1 ( 1 ) {\tt A}_{1}^{(1)} -crystal isomorphisms 

 

 
 
 𝒳 Λ : ℳ ​ 𝒱 Λ → ∼ 𝒰 ​ ℒ Λ and 𝒴 Λ : 𝒰 ​ ℒ Λ → ∼ ℳ ​ 𝒱 Λ . \displaystyle\mathcal{X}^{\Lambda}:\mathcal{MV}^{\Lambda}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{UL}^{\Lambda}\qquad\textup{and}\qquad\mathcal{Y}^{\Lambda}:\mathcal{UL}^{\Lambda}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{MV}^{\Lambda}. 
 
 
 




 
 The following would be implied by Conjecture  2.1 .



 
 Conjecture 7.1 . 

 
 Let i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in\mathbbm{Z}_{2} , π , π ~ ∈ Π \pi,\widetilde{\pi}\in\Pi , and assume π ~ \widetilde{\pi} is obtained by deleting the last row of π 1 \pi^{1} or π 0 \pi^{0} . Then 𝒳 i ​ ( π ~ ) ⊆ 𝒳 i ​ ( π ) \mathcal{X}_{i}(\widetilde{\pi})\subseteq\mathcal{X}_{i}(\pi) .









 
 7.2. Crystal isomorphisms between Kleshchev multipartitions and upper ledge diagrams


 
 In this subsection we provide an isomorphism between the 𝙰 1 ( 1 ) {\tt A}_{1}^{(1)} -bicrystals ℳ ​ 𝒫 K 𝜿 \mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}} and 𝒰 ​ ℒ \mathcal{UL} .



 
 7.2.1. From upper ledge diagrams to Kleshchev multipartitions


 
 Let i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in\mathbbm{Z}_{2} . We define now a map

 

 
 
 split i : 𝒰 ​ ℒ → 𝒰 ​ ℒ × 𝒰 ​ ℒ , 𝖣 ↦ ( split i ​ ( 𝖣 ) ↓ , split i ​ ( 𝖣 ) ↑ ) \displaystyle\mathrm{split}^{i}\colon\mathcal{UL}\rightarrow\mathcal{UL}\times\mathcal{UL},\quad\mathsf{D}\mapsto(\mathrm{split}^{i}(\mathsf{D})_{\downarrow},\mathrm{split}^{i}(\mathsf{D})_{\uparrow}) 
 
 
 
 as follows.
Let 𝖣 ∈ 𝒰 ​ ℒ \mathsf{D}\in\mathcal{UL} , and let u u be the uppermost box of color i i in the first column of 𝖣 \mathsf{D} . If there is no such u u , we set split i ​ ( 𝖣 ) = ( ∅ , 𝖣 ) \mathrm{split}^{i}(\mathsf{D})=(\varnothing,\mathsf{D}) . Otherwise, extending a ray r r directly southeast (i.e. at a − 45 ∘ -45^{\circ} angle) from the centroid of u u , let 𝖫 \mathsf{L} be those boxes in 𝖣 \mathsf{D} whose centroid lies weakly below r r , and let 𝖴 \mathsf{U} be those whose centroid lies strictly above r r . Now, 𝖫 \mathsf{L} is already an upper ledge diagram, and left-justifying the rows of 𝖴 \mathsf{U} gives a lower ledge diagram 𝖴 ′ \mathsf{U}^{\prime} . We set split i ​ ( 𝖣 ) ↓ = 𝖫 \mathrm{split}^{i}(\mathsf{D})_{\downarrow}=\mathsf{L} and split ↑ i ​ ( 𝖣 ) = float ⁡ ( 𝖴 ′ ) \mathrm{split}^{i}_{\uparrow}(\mathsf{D})=\mathrm{float}(\mathsf{U}^{\prime}) . An example is shown in Figure  13 .



 
 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 Figure 13. An application of split 1 \mathrm{split}^{1} to an upper ledge diagram 𝖣 \mathsf{D} is shown. The resulting upper ledge diagrams split 1 ​ ( 𝖣 ) ↓ \mathrm{split}^{1}(\mathsf{D})_{\downarrow} , split 1 ​ ( 𝖣 ) ↑ \mathrm{split}^{1}(\mathsf{D})_{\uparrow} are shown on the right. 
 
 
 More generally, if 𝜿 = ( κ 1 , κ 2 , … ) \boldsymbol{\kappa}=(\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2},\ldots) is a multicharge, we define an infinite sequence of upper ledge diagrams split 𝜿 ​ ( 𝖣 ) = ( 𝖣 1 , 𝖣 2 , … ) \mathrm{split}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\mathsf{D})=(\mathsf{D}_{1},\mathsf{D}_{2},\ldots) by iteratively splitting the upper component repeatedly; i.e., we set

 

 
 
 𝖣 1 = split κ 1 ​ ( 𝖣 ) ↓ , and ( 𝖣 2 , 𝖣 3 , … ) = split ( κ 2 , κ 3 , … ) ​ ( split κ 1 ​ ( 𝖣 ) ↑ ) , \displaystyle\mathsf{D}_{1}=\mathrm{split}^{\kappa_{1}}(\mathsf{D})_{\downarrow},\quad\textup{and}\quad(\mathsf{D}_{2},\mathsf{D}_{3},\ldots)=\mathrm{split}^{(\kappa_{2},\kappa_{3},\ldots)}(\mathrm{split}^{\kappa_{1}}(\mathsf{D})_{\uparrow}), 
 
 
 
 noting that 𝖣 k = ∅ \mathsf{D}_{k}=\varnothing for k ≫ 0 k\gg 0 by the definition of multicharges and the map split i \mathrm{split}^{i} .



 
 Now, we define a map 𝒲 𝜿 : 𝒰 ​ ℒ ​ ( θ ) → ℳ ​ 𝒫 K 𝜿 ​ ( θ ) \mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}:\mathcal{UL}(\theta)\rightarrow\mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\theta) as follows. For 𝖣 ∈ 𝒰 ​ ℒ ​ ( θ ) \mathsf{D}\in\mathcal{UL}(\theta) , assume split 𝜿 ​ ( 𝖣 ) = ( 𝖣 1 , 𝖣 2 , … ) \mathrm{split}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\mathsf{D})=(\mathsf{D}_{1},\mathsf{D}_{2},\ldots) . Then, for t ∈ ℤ > 0 t\in\mathbbm{Z}_{>0} , let λ ( t ) \lambda^{(t)} be the partition constructed by top-aligning every column in 𝖣 t \mathsf{D}_{t} , and set 𝒲 𝜿 ​ ( 𝖣 ) = ( λ ( 1 ) , λ ( 2 ) , … ) \mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\mathsf{D})=(\lambda^{(1)},\lambda^{(2)},\ldots) . An example is shown in Figure  14 .



 
 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 Figure 14. Let θ = 19 ​ α 1 + 19 ​ α 0 \theta=19\alpha_{1}+19\alpha_{0} , and 𝜿 \boldsymbol{\kappa} be a multicharge with κ 1 = 1 \kappa_{1}=1 , κ 2 = 0 \kappa_{2}=0 , κ 3 = 0 \kappa_{3}=0 .
At left, an upper ledge diagram 𝖣 ∈ 𝒰 ​ ℒ ​ ( θ ) \mathsf{D}\in\mathcal{UL}(\theta) . The computation of 𝒲 𝜿 ​ ( 𝖣 ) = 𝝀 = ( λ ( 1 ) , λ ( 2 ) , λ ( 3 ) , ∅ , … ) ∈ ℳ ​ 𝒫 𝜿 ​ ( θ ) \mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\mathsf{D})=\boldsymbol{\lambda}=(\lambda^{(1)},\lambda^{(2)},\lambda^{(3)},\varnothing,\ldots)\in\mathcal{MP}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\theta) is shown from left to right. The second-to-rightmost diagram is the sequence of upper ledge diagrams ( 𝖣 1 , 𝖣 2 , 𝖣 3 , ∅ , … ) = split 𝜿 ​ ( 𝖣 ) (\mathsf{D}_{1},\mathsf{D}_{2},\mathsf{D}_{3},\varnothing,\ldots)=\mathrm{split}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\mathsf{D}) . 
 


 
 7.2.2. From Kleshchev multipartitions to upper ledge diagrams


 
 Let 𝖣 1 , 𝖣 2 ∈ 𝒰 ​ ℒ \mathsf{D}_{1},\mathsf{D}_{2}\in\mathcal{UL} . We define an upper ledge diagram glue ⁡ ( 𝖣 1 , 𝖣 2 ) ∈ 𝒰 ​ ℒ \mathrm{glue}(\mathsf{D}_{1},\mathsf{D}_{2})\in\mathcal{UL} as follows. First set 𝖣 2 \mathsf{D}_{2} to the right of 𝖣 1 \mathsf{D}_{1} with top rows aligned if they have matching color, or so that the top row of 𝖣 2 \mathsf{D}_{2} is one notch higher otherwise. Then, starting from the leftmost column in 𝖣 2 \mathsf{D}_{2} whose bottom box is above the uppermost peak row of 𝖣 1 \mathsf{D}_{1} (if such exists), work to the right, shifting columns down as far as possible in steps of two so that the bottom box is still (a) not below the uppermost peak row of 𝖣 1 \mathsf{D}_{1} and (b) is not below the bottom box in the column directly to its left.
Then combine diagrams, left-justifying all rows to yield the diagram glue ⁡ ( 𝖣 1 , 𝖣 2 ) \mathrm{glue}(\mathsf{D}_{1},\mathsf{D}_{2}) . It is straightforward to check that this resulting diagram is upper ledge.
See Figure  15 for an example.



 
 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 Figure 15. The gluing process for upper ledge diagrams 𝖣 1 , 𝖣 2 \mathsf{D}_{1},\mathsf{D}_{2} . The alignment of diagrams is shown in the first pane, and the column shift is shown in the second pane, with the uppermost peak row of 𝖣 1 \mathsf{D}_{1} marked with a star. The last pane is the resulting upper ledge diagram glue ⁡ ( 𝖣 1 , 𝖣 2 ) \mathrm{glue}(\mathsf{D}_{1},\mathsf{D}_{2}) . 
 
 
 More generally, let 𝖣 ¯ = ( 𝖣 1 , 𝖣 2 , … ) \underline{\mathsf{D}}=(\mathsf{D}_{1},\mathsf{D}_{2},\ldots) be an infinite sequence of upper ledge diagrams such that 𝖣 k = ∅ \mathsf{D}_{k}=\varnothing for k ≫ 0 k\gg 0 . If all 𝖣 k \mathsf{D}_{k} are empty, set glue ⁡ ( 𝖣 ¯ ) = ∅ \mathrm{glue}(\underline{\mathsf{D}})=\varnothing . Otherwise, let M M be maximal such that 𝖣 M ≠ ∅ \mathsf{D}_{M}\neq\varnothing . Now, set 𝖣 M + 1 ′ = ∅ \mathsf{D}^{\prime}_{M+1}=\varnothing and proceeding downwards iteratively define 𝖣 t ′ = glue ⁡ ( 𝖣 t , 𝖣 t + 1 ′ ) \mathsf{D}_{t}^{\prime}=\mathrm{glue}(\mathsf{D}_{t},\mathsf{D}_{t+1}^{\prime}) for t = M , M − 1 , M − 2 , … , 1 t=M,M-1,M-2,\ldots,1 . Set glue ⁡ ( 𝖣 ¯ ) = 𝖣 1 ′ \mathrm{glue}(\underline{\mathsf{D}})=\mathsf{D}_{1}^{\prime} .



 
 Now we define a map 𝒵 𝜿 : ℳ ​ 𝒫 𝜿 ​ ( θ ) → 𝒰 ​ ℒ ​ ( θ ) \mathcal{Z}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}:\mathcal{MP}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\theta)\rightarrow\mathcal{UL}(\theta) as follows. Let 𝝀 = ( λ ( 1 ) , λ ( 2 ) , … ) ∈ ℳ ​ 𝒫 𝜿 ​ ( θ ) \boldsymbol{\lambda}=(\lambda^{(1)},\lambda^{(2)},\ldots)\in\mathcal{MP}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\theta) . We convert each λ ( t ) \lambda^{(t)} into an upper ledge diagram 𝖣 t \mathsf{D}_{t} by shifting (for all k k ) the k k th column of λ ( t ) \lambda^{(t)} down by k − 1 k-1 notches and then left aligning all rows—the color of each box in 𝖣 t \mathsf{D}_{t} being given by its pre-shift residue in λ ( t ) \lambda^{(t)} .
Taking 𝖣 ¯ = ( 𝖣 1 , 𝖣 2 , … ) \underline{\mathsf{D}}=(\mathsf{D}_{1},\mathsf{D}_{2},\ldots) , we set 𝒵 𝜿 ​ ( 𝝀 ) = glue ​ ( 𝖣 ¯ ) \mathcal{Z}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})=\mathrm{glue}(\underline{\mathsf{D}}) . An example is shown in Figure  16 .



 
 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 Figure 16. Let θ = 16 ​ α 1 + 21 ​ α 0 \theta=16\alpha_{1}+21\alpha_{0} and 𝜿 \boldsymbol{\kappa} be a multicharge with κ 1 = 1 \kappa_{1}=1 , κ 2 = 0 \kappa_{2}=0 , κ 3 = 1 \kappa_{3}=1 . At left, a Kleshchev multipartition 𝝀 = ( λ ( 1 ) , λ ( 2 ) , λ ( 3 ) , ∅ , … ) ∈ ℳ ​ 𝒫 K 𝜿 ​ ( θ ) \boldsymbol{\lambda}=(\lambda^{(1)},\lambda^{(2)},\lambda^{(3)},\varnothing,\ldots)\in\mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\theta) . The computation of 𝒵 𝜿 ​ ( 𝝀 ) = 𝖣 ∈ 𝒰 ​ ℒ ​ ( θ ) \mathcal{Z}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})=\mathsf{D}\in\mathcal{UL}(\theta) is shown from left to right. The second-to-leftmost diagram is the sequence of upper ledge diagrams 𝖣 ¯ = ( 𝖣 1 , 𝖣 2 , 𝖣 3 , ∅ , … ) \underline{\mathsf{D}}=(\mathsf{D}_{1},\mathsf{D}_{2},\mathsf{D}_{3},\varnothing,\ldots) with glue ⁡ ( 𝖣 ¯ ) = 𝖣 \mathrm{glue}(\underline{\mathsf{D}})=\mathsf{D} . 
 


 
 7.2.3. The crystal isomorphism ℳ ​ 𝒫 K 𝜿 ↔ 𝒰 ​ ℒ \mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}\leftrightarrow\mathcal{UL} 


 
 The following result appears as Theorem  9.12 .



 
 Theorem G . 

 
 For any multicharge 𝛋 \boldsymbol{\kappa} we have 𝒲 𝛋 ​ ( 𝒰 ​ ℒ ) = ℳ ​ 𝒫 K 𝛋 \mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\mathcal{UL})=\mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}} , and the maps 

 

 
 
 𝒲 𝜿 : 𝒰 ​ ℒ → ℳ ​ 𝒫 K 𝜿 and 𝒵 𝜿 : ℳ ​ 𝒫 K 𝜿 → 𝒰 ​ ℒ \displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}:\mathcal{UL}\rightarrow\mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}\qquad\textup{and}\qquad\mathcal{Z}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}:\mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}\rightarrow\mathcal{UL} 
 
 
 
 are mutually inverse crystal isomorphisms. 





 
 Corollary H . 

 
 Let Λ \Lambda be a dominant integral weight, and assume the multicharge 𝛋 \boldsymbol{\kappa} and ℓ ∈ ℤ > 0 \ell\in\mathbbm{Z}_{>0} are such that Λ = Λ ⁡ ( 𝛋 , ℓ ) \Lambda=\Lambda(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\ell) . Then the maps 𝒲 𝛋 , 𝒵 𝛋 \mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}},\mathcal{Z}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}} restrict to mutually inverse crystal isomorphisms 

 

 
 
 𝒲 ( 𝜿 , ℓ ) : 𝒰 ​ ℒ Λ → ℳ ​ 𝒫 K ( 𝜿 , ℓ ) and 𝒵 ( 𝜿 , ℓ ) : ℳ ​ 𝒫 K ( 𝜿 , ℓ ) → 𝒰 ​ ℒ Λ . \displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\ell)}:\mathcal{UL}^{\Lambda}\rightarrow\mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\ell)}\qquad\textup{and}\qquad\mathcal{Z}^{(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\ell)}:\mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\ell)}\rightarrow\mathcal{UL}^{\Lambda}. 
 
 
 








 
 7.3. A crystal isomorphism between affine MV polytopes and Kleshchev multipartitions


 
 Finally, the upper ledge diagrams act as an intermediary, yielding a straightforward combinatorial isomorphism between affine MV polytopes and Kleshchev multipartitions:



 
 Corollary I . 

 
 The maps
