 𝒲 𝜿 ∘ 𝒳 : ℳ ​ 𝒱 → ℳ ​ 𝒫 K 𝜿  and  𝒴 ∘ 𝒵 𝜿 : ℳ ​ 𝒫 K 𝜿 → ℳ ​ 𝒱 \displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}\circ\mathcal{X}:\mathcal{MV}\rightarrow\mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}\qquad\textup{ and }\qquad\mathcal{Y}\circ\mathcal{Z}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}:\mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}\rightarrow\mathcal{MV} 
 
 
 
 are mutually inverse crystal isomorphisms. 





 
 Corollary J . 

 
 Let Λ \Lambda be a dominant integral weight, and assume the multicharge 𝛋 \boldsymbol{\kappa} and ℓ ∈ ℤ > 0 \ell\in\mathbbm{Z}_{>0} are such that Λ = Λ ⁡ ( 𝛋 , ℓ ) \Lambda=\Lambda(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\ell) . Then the maps of Corollary  Corollary I restrict to mutually inverse crystal isomorphisms 

 

 
 
 𝒲 ( 𝜿 , ℓ ) ∘ 𝒳 Λ : ℳ ​ 𝒱 Λ → ℳ ​ 𝒫 K ( 𝜿 , ℓ )  and  𝒴 Λ ∘ 𝒵 ( 𝜿 , ℓ ) : ℳ ​ 𝒫 K ( 𝜿 , ℓ ) → ℳ ​ 𝒱 Λ . \displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\ell)}\circ\mathcal{X}^{\Lambda}:\mathcal{MV}^{\Lambda}\rightarrow\mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\ell)}\qquad\textup{ and }\qquad\mathcal{Y}^{\Lambda}\circ\mathcal{Z}^{(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\ell)}:\mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\ell)}\rightarrow\mathcal{MV}^{\Lambda}. 
 
 
 








 
 8. Bicompositions and binary sequences


 
 In this long and technical section we roll up our sleeves and prepare to prove the main results of § 2 – 7 . Most of these are downstream of the key combinatorial result (Theorem  8.24 ) proved in this section which establishes a hidden binary tree structure on bicompositions associated with the ‘real’ components of affine MV polytopes, see § 8.9 .



 
 8.1. Compositions


 
 A composition μ \mu is a finitely supported function μ : ℤ → ℤ ≥ 0 \mu:{\mathbbm{Z}}\rightarrow{\mathbbm{Z}}_{\geq 0} , which we write as μ = ( μ k ) k ∈ ℤ \mu=(\mu_{k})_{k\in{\mathbbm{Z}}} , where μ k = μ ⁡ ( k ) \mu_{k}=\mu(k) . We write | μ | = ∑ k ∈ ℤ μ k ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 |\mu|=\sum_{k\in{\mathbbm{Z}}}\mu_{k}\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{\geq 0} . In practice we will usually have μ k = 0 \mu_{k}=0 for k ≤ 0 k\leq 0 , but it is convenient for our purposes to allow μ \mu to be defined more generally. We write Ω \Omega for the set of all compositions. We also will occasionally add decorations to this set, writing

 

 
 
 Ω ≥ k \displaystyle\Omega_{\geq k} 
 = { μ ∈ Ω ∣ μ ℓ = 0  for all  ℓ < k } and Ω ( n ) = { μ ∈ Ω ∣ | μ | = n } , \displaystyle=\{\mu\in\Omega\mid\mu_{\ell}=0\textup{ for all }\ell<k\}\qquad\textup{and}\qquad\Omega(n)=\{\mu\in\Omega\mid|\mu|=n\}, 
 
 
 
 with multiple decorations like Ω ≥ k ​ ( n ) \Omega_{\geq k}(n) indicating the obvious intersections of the corresponding sets.



 
 For each μ ∈ Ω , k ∈ ℤ \mu\in\Omega,k\in{\mathbbm{Z}} , define a statistic ϑ k ​ ( μ ) ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 \vartheta_{k}(\mu)\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{\geq 0} to be the minimal nonnegative integer m m such that μ k − m = 0 \mu_{k-m}=0 , and define a statistic η k ​ ( μ ) ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 \eta_{k}(\mu)\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{\geq 0} to be the minimal nonnegative integer m m such that μ k + m = 0 \mu_{k+m}=0 . Note in particular that η k ​ ( μ ) = ϑ k ​ ( μ ) = 0 \eta_{k}(\mu)=\vartheta_{k}(\mu)=0 if and only if μ k = 0 \mu_{k}=0 .





 
 8.2. The functions 𝒢 \mathcal{G} and ℋ \mathcal{H} 


 
 We define a function 𝒢 : Ω → Ω \mathcal{G}:\Omega\rightarrow\Omega by setting 𝒢 ​ μ = ν \mathcal{G}\mu=\nu , where

 

 
 (8.1) 
 
 ν k = { μ k − 1 if  ϑ k ​ ( 𝝁 )  is odd ; μ k + 1 if  ϑ k − 1 ​ ( 𝝁 )  is odd ; 0 if  μ k = 0  and  ϑ k − 1 ​ ( 𝝁 )  is even. \displaystyle\nu_{k}=\begin{cases}\mu_{k}-1&\textup{if $\vartheta_{k}(\boldsymbol{\mu})$ is odd};\\
\mu_{k}+1&\textup{if $\vartheta_{k-1}(\boldsymbol{\mu})$ is odd};\\
0&\textup{if $\mu_{k}=0$ and $\vartheta_{k-1}(\boldsymbol{\mu})$ is even.}\\
\end{cases} 
 
 
 
 We also define a dual function ℋ : Ω → Ω \mathcal{H}:\Omega\rightarrow\Omega by setting ℋ ​ μ = ν \mathcal{H}\mu=\nu , where

 

 
 (8.2) 
 
 ν k = { μ k − 1 if  η k ​ ( 𝝁 )  is odd ; μ k + 1 if  η k + 1 ​ ( 𝝁 )  is odd ; 0 if  μ k = 0  and  η k + 1 ​ ( 𝝁 )  is even . \displaystyle\nu_{k}=\begin{cases}\mu_{k}-1&\textup{if $\eta_{k}(\boldsymbol{\mu})$ is odd};\\
\mu_{k}+1&\textup{if $\eta_{k+1}(\boldsymbol{\mu})$ is odd};\\
0&\textup{if $\mu_{k}=0$ and $\eta_{k+1}(\boldsymbol{\mu})$ is even}.\\
\end{cases} 
 
 
 
 We also
define maps zero 0 : Ω → Ω \textup{zero}_{0}:\Omega\rightarrow\Omega and one 0 : Ω → Ω \textup{one}_{0}:\Omega\rightarrow\Omega by setting

 

 
 
 ( zero 0 ​ ( μ ) ) k = { 0 if  ​ k = 0 ; μ k otherwise and ( one 0 ​ ( μ ) ) k = { 1 if  ​ k = 0 ; μ k otherwise \displaystyle(\textup{zero}_{0}(\mu))_{k}=\begin{cases}0&\textup{if }k=0;\\
\mu_{k}&\textup{otherwise}\end{cases}\qquad\textup{and}\qquad(\textup{one}_{0}(\mu))_{k}=\begin{cases}1&\textup{if }k=0;\\
\mu_{k}&\textup{otherwise}\end{cases} 
 
 
 
 In other words, zero 0 ​ ( μ ) \textup{zero}_{0}(\mu) is given by replacing μ 0 \mu_{0} with zero, and one 0 ​ ( μ ) \textup{one}_{0}(\mu) is given by replacing μ 0 \mu_{0} with one.
Then we define maps 𝒢 ¯ \bar{\mathcal{G}} , ℋ ¯ : Ω → Ω \bar{\mathcal{H}}:\Omega\rightarrow\Omega by setting

 

 
 
 𝒢 ¯ = 𝒢 ∘ one 0 and ℋ ¯ = zero 0 ∘ ℋ . \displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{G}}=\mathcal{G}\circ\textup{one}_{0}\qquad\textup{and}\qquad\bar{\mathcal{H}}=\textup{zero}_{0}\circ\mathcal{H}. 
 
 
 


 
 Let s : Ω → Ω s:\Omega\rightarrow\Omega be the ‘flip’ involution given by setting s ​ μ = ν s\mu=\nu , where ν k = μ − k \nu_{k}=\mu_{-k} for all k ∈ ℤ k\in{\mathbbm{Z}} . It follows then that ϑ k ​ ( s ​ μ ) = η − k ​ ( μ ) \vartheta_{k}(s\mu)=\eta_{-k}(\mu) and η k ​ ( s ​ μ ) = ϑ − k ​ ( μ ) \eta_{k}(s\mu)=\vartheta_{-k}(\mu) , and therefore that s ​ ℋ = 𝒢 ​ s s\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{G}s .
The following lemma provides an alternate description of the maps 𝒢 , ℋ : Ω → Ω \mathcal{G},\mathcal{H}:\Omega\rightarrow\Omega .



 
 Lemma 8.1 . 

 
 Let μ ∈ Ω \mu\in\Omega . Then we have that 

 
 (1) 
 
 ℋ ​ μ \mathcal{H}\mu is the unique element ν ∈ Ω \nu\in\Omega with the property that, for all k ∈ ℤ k\in{\mathbbm{Z}} : 

 
 (a) 
 
 ν k ∈ { μ k ± 1 } \nu_{k}\in\{\mu_{k}\pm 1\} if μ k > 0 \mu_{k}>0 ; 




 (b) 
 
 ν k ∈ { 0 , 1 } \nu_{k}\in\{0,1\} if μ k = 0 \mu_{k}=0 ; 




 (c) 
 
 ν k + 1 = μ k + 1 − 1 \nu_{k+1}=\mu_{k+1}-1 if and only if ν k = μ k + 1 \nu_{k}=\mu_{k}+1 . 




 
 Moreover if μ ∈ Ω ≥ 1 \mu\in\Omega_{\geq 1} , then ℋ ¯ ​ μ \bar{\mathcal{H}}\mu is the unique element ν ∈ Ω ≥ 1 \nu\in\Omega_{\geq 1} which satisfies (1a, 1b, 1c) for all k ≥ 1 k\geq 1 . 




 (2) 
 
 𝒢 ​ μ \mathcal{G}\mu is the unique element ν ∈ Ω \nu\in\Omega with the property that, for all k ∈ ℤ k\in{\mathbbm{Z}} : 

 
 (a) 
 
 ν k ∈ { μ k ± 1 } \nu_{k}\in\{\mu_{k}\pm 1\} if μ k > 0 \mu_{k}>0 ; 




 (b) 
 
 ν k ∈ { 0 , 1 } \nu_{k}\in\{0,1\} if μ k = 0 \mu_{k}=0 ; 




 (c) 
 
 ν k + 1 = μ k + 1 + 1 \nu_{k+1}=\mu_{k+1}+1 if and only if ν k = μ k − 1 \nu_{k}=\mu_{k}-1 . 




 
 Moreover if μ ∈ Ω ≥ 1 \mu\in\Omega_{\geq 1} , then 𝒢 ¯ ​ μ \bar{\mathcal{G}}\mu is the unique element ν ∈ Ω ≥ 1 \nu\in\Omega_{\geq 1} which satisfies (2a, 2b, 2c) for all k ≥ 1 k\geq 1 , and ν 1 = μ 1 + 1 \nu_{1}=\mu_{1}+1 . 




 




 
 Proof.

 
 Let ν \nu be the element defined as in (1a, 1b, 1c) above.
It is straightforward to see that ν \nu exists and is uniquely defined—if m m is maximal such that μ m > 0 \mu_{m}>0 , then we have ν m ′ = 0 \nu_{m^{\prime}}=0 for all m ′ > m m^{\prime}>m , ν m = μ m − 1 \nu_{m}=\mu_{m}-1 , and rules (1a, 1b, 1c) above iteratively force the choice of ν m ′′ \nu_{m^{\prime\prime}} for all m ′′ < m m^{\prime\prime}<m , proceeding in the downward direction. 



 
 We now claim that for all k ∈ ℤ k\in{\mathbbm{Z}} , we have 

 

 
 (8.3) 
 
 ν k = { μ k − 1 if  ​ η k ​ ( μ ) ​  is odd ; μ k + 1 if  ​ η k + 1 ​ ( μ ) ​  is odd ; 0 if  ​ μ k = 0 ​  and  ​ η k + 1 ​ ( μ ) ​  is even . \displaystyle\nu_{k}=\begin{cases}\mu_{k}-1&\textup{if }\eta_{k}(\mu)\textup{ is odd};\\
\mu_{k}+1&\textup{if }\eta_{k+1}(\mu)\textup{ is odd};\\
0&\textup{if }\mu_{k}=0\textup{ and }\eta_{k+1}(\mu)\textup{ is even}.\end{cases} 
 
 
 
 Clearly ( 8.3 ) holds for k ≫ 0 k\gg 0 , since in this case μ k = ν k = η k + 1 ​ ( μ ) = 0 \mu_{k}=\nu_{k}=\eta_{k+1}(\mu)=0 . Now, going by downward induction on k ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 k\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{\geq 0} , we assume that ( 8.3 ) holds for k ′ = k + 1 k^{\prime}=k+1 , and prove that ( 8.3 ) holds for k k .
 



 
 Case 1. Assume μ k = 0 ​  and  ​ η k + 1 ​ ( μ ) ​  is even . \mu_{k}=0\textup{ and }\eta_{k+1}(\mu)\textup{ is even}. Then by (1b) we have ν k ∈ { 0 , 1 } \nu_{k}\in\{0,1\} . Since η k ′ ​ ( μ ) \eta_{k^{\prime}}(\mu) is even, by induction assumption ν k ′ ≠ μ k ′ − 1 \nu_{k^{\prime}}\neq\mu_{k^{\prime}}-1 . Then by (1c) we have ν k ≠ ν k + 1 = 1 \nu_{k}\neq\nu_{k}+1=1 , so ν k = 0 \nu_{k}=0 , as desired.
 



 
 Case 2. Assume η k ​ ( μ ) \eta_{k}(\mu) is odd. Then μ k > 0 \mu_{k}>0 , so ν k ∈ { μ k ± 1 } \nu_{k}\in\{\mu_{k}\pm 1\} by (1a).
We have that η k ′ ​ ( μ ) \eta_{k^{\prime}}(\mu) is even, so by induction assumption ν k ′ ≠ μ k ′ − 1 \nu_{k^{\prime}}\neq\mu_{k^{\prime}}-1 . Then by (1c) we have ν k ≠ ν k + 1 \nu_{k}\neq\nu_{k}+1 , so ν k = μ k − 1 \nu_{k}=\mu_{k}-1 , as desired.
 



 
 Case 3. Assume η k + 1 ​ ( μ ) \eta_{k+1}(\mu) is odd. Then since η k ′ ​ ( μ ) \eta_{k^{\prime}}(\mu) is odd, we have ν k ′ = μ k ′ − 1 \nu_{k^{\prime}}=\mu_{k^{\prime}}-1 by induction assumption. Then by (1c) we have ν k = μ k + 1 \nu_{k}=\mu_{k}+1 , as desired.
 



 
 Since ( 8.3 ) holds for all k k , we have ν = ℋ ​ μ \nu=\mathcal{H}\mu . The ‘moreover’ clause in (1) is immediate then as well, completing the proof of (1). To prove (2), note that ν \nu satisfies (2a, 2b, 2c) for μ \mu if and only if ν ′ = s ⁡ ( ν ) \nu^{\prime}=s(\nu) satisfies (1a, 1b, 1c) for μ ′ = s ⁡ ( μ ) \mu^{\prime}=s(\mu) . Therefore using (1) we have 

 

 
 
 𝒢 ​ μ = 𝒢 ​ s ​ μ ′ = s ​ ℋ ​ μ ′ = s ​ ν ′ = ν , \displaystyle\mathcal{G}\mu=\mathcal{G}s\mu^{\prime}=s\mathcal{H}\mu^{\prime}=s\nu^{\prime}=\nu, 
 
 
 
 proving the initial statement in (2). The ‘moreover’ clause in (2) follows directly since ϑ ​ ( add 0 ​ ( μ ) ) = 1 \vartheta(\textup{add}_{0}(\mu))=1 in this setting.
∎ 





 
 Corollary 8.2 . 

 
 For all μ ∈ Ω \mu\in\Omega , we have | μ | = | ℋ ​ μ | = | 𝒢 ​ μ | |\mu|=|\mathcal{H}\mu|=|\mathcal{G}\mu| . 





 
 Proof.

 
 By construction, the maps 𝒢 \mathcal{G} and ℋ \mathcal{H} either preserve entries, or else raise or lower entries by one.
One sees from Lemma  8.1 (1c, 2c) that raised/lowered entries appear in neighboring pairs, so the overall sum of entries must be preserved.
∎ 





 
 Lemma 8.3 . 

 
 For n ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 n\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{\geq 0} , the maps 𝒢 , ℋ \mathcal{G},\mathcal{H} are mutually inverse on Ω ⁡ ( n ) \Omega(n) . 





 
 Proof.

 
 We have by Corollary  8.2 that
 𝒢 , ℋ \mathcal{G},\mathcal{H} restrict to maps on Ω ⁡ ( n ) \Omega(n) .
Let μ ∈ Ω \mu\in\Omega , and set ν = ℋ ​ μ \nu=\mathcal{H}\mu , ω = 𝒢 ​ ν \omega=\mathcal{G}\nu . Applications of 𝒢 \mathcal{G} and ℋ \mathcal{H} change entries of compositions by at most one, so we have ω m ∈ [ μ m − 2 , μ m + 2 ] \omega_{m}\in[\mu_{m}-2,\mu_{m}+2] for all m m . 
 



 
 Claim I. There exists no m ∈ ℤ m\in{\mathbbm{Z}} such that ω m = μ m + 2 \omega_{m}=\mu_{m}+2 . Indeed, assume by way of contradiction that there exists an occurrence of such m m . We assume moreover that m m is minimal with this property. Then in consideration of Lemma  8.1 , we must have ν m = μ m + 1 \nu_{m}=\mu_{m}+1 and ω m = ν m + 1 \omega_{m}=\nu_{m}+1 . Then by Lemma  8.1 (1c, 2c) we have ν m − 1 ≠ μ m − 1 + 1 \nu_{m-1}\neq\mu_{m-1}+1 and ω m − 1 = ν m − 1 − 1 \omega_{m-1}=\nu_{m-1}-1 . Hence ν m − 1 > 0 \nu_{m-1}>0 . If μ m − 1 = 0 \mu_{m-1}=0 , then Lemma  8.1 (1b) would force ν m − 1 = 0 \nu_{m-1}=0 or ν m − 1 = 1 = μ m − 1 + 1 \nu_{m-1}=1=\mu_{m-1}+1 , but as neither of these are the case, it must be that μ m − 1 > 0 \mu_{m-1}>0 , and hence ν m − 1 = μ m − 1 − 1 \nu_{m-1}=\mu_{m-1}-1 by Lemma  8.1 (1a). Then ν m − 2 = μ m − 2 + 1 \nu_{m-2}=\mu_{m-2}+1 by Lemma  8.1 (1c). Then ν m − 2 > 0 \nu_{m-2}>0 , so ω m − 2 ∈ { ν m − 2 ± 1 } \omega_{m-2}\in\{\nu_{m-2}\pm 1\} by Lemma  8.1 (2a). But since ω m − 1 = ν m − 1 − 1 \omega_{m-1}=\nu_{m-1}-1 , it follows from by Lemma  8.1 (2c) that ω m − 2 = ν m − 2 + 1 \omega_{m-2}=\nu_{m-2}+1 . Therefore

 

 
 
 ω m − 2 = ν m − 2 + 1 = μ m − 2 + 2 , \displaystyle\omega_{m-2}=\nu_{m-2}+1=\mu_{m-2}+2, 
 
 
 
 contradicting the minimality assumption on m m , and proving Claim I.
 



 
 Claim II. There exists no m ∈ ℤ m\in{\mathbbm{Z}} such that ω m = μ m + 1 \omega_{m}=\mu_{m}+1 . Indeed, assume by way of contradiction that there exists an occurrence of such m m . It follows then that either

 

 
 
 ν m = μ m + 1 , ω m = ν m , or ν m = μ m , ω m = ν m + 1 . \displaystyle\nu_{m}=\mu_{m}+1,\;\omega_{m}=\nu_{m},\qquad\textup{or}\qquad\nu_{m}=\mu_{m},\;\omega_{m}=\nu_{m}+1. 
 
 
 
 The former case is impossible however, since in this setting we have ν m > 0 \nu_{m}>0 , which would imply by Lemma  8.1 (2a) that ω m ∈ { ν m ± 1 } \omega_{m}\in\{\nu_{m}\pm 1\} . Thus we assume the latter; ν m = μ m , ω m = ν m + 1 \nu_{m}=\mu_{m},\;\omega_{m}=\nu_{m}+1 . Then μ m = ν m = 0 \mu_{m}=\nu_{m}=0 by Lemma  8.1 (1b). By Lemma  8.1 (2c) we have ω m − 1 = ν m − 1 − 1 \omega_{m-1}=\nu_{m-1}-1 , so ν m − 1 > 0 \nu_{m-1}>0 . Since ν m ≠ μ m − 1 \nu_{m}\neq\mu_{m}-1 , we cannot have that ν m − 1 = μ m − 1 + 1 \nu_{m-1}=\mu_{m-1}+1 by Lemma  8.1 (1c). Thus either ν m − 1 = μ m − 1 = 0 \nu_{m-1}=\mu_{m-1}=0 , or else ν m − 1 = μ m − 1 − 1 \nu_{m-1}=\mu_{m-1}-1 by Lemma  8.1 (1a,1b). But since ν m − 1 > 0 \nu_{m-1}>0 , it follows then that ν m − 1 = μ m − 1 − 1 \nu_{m-1}=\mu_{m-1}-1 , and so ν m − 2 = μ m − 2 + 1 \nu_{m-2}=\mu_{m-2}+1 by Lemma  8.1 (1c). Thus ν m − 2 > 0 \nu_{m-2}>0 , so ω m − 2 ∈ { ν m − 2 ± 1 } \omega_{m-2}\in\{\nu_{m-2}\pm 1\} . Since
 ω m − 1 = ν m − 1 − 1 \omega_{m-1}=\nu_{m-1}-1 , it cannot be that ω m − 2 = ν m − 2 − 1 \omega_{m-2}=\nu_{m-2}-1 by Lemma  8.1 (2c). Therefore ω m − 2 = ν m − 2 + 1 \omega_{m-2}=\nu_{m-2}+1 , and so we have

 

 
 
 ω m − 2 = ν m − 2 + 1 = μ m − 2 + 2 , \displaystyle\omega_{m-2}=\nu_{m-2}+1=\mu_{m-2}+2, 
 
 
 
 which contradicts Claim I, and thereby proves Claim II.



 
 Now, by Claim I and II, we have ω m ≥ μ m \omega_{m}\geq\mu_{m} for all m ∈ ℤ m\in{\mathbbm{Z}} . But then since | μ | = | ω | |\mu|=|\omega| by Corollary  8.2 , it must be that ω = μ \omega=\mu . Thus 𝒢 ​ ℋ ​ μ = μ \mathcal{G}\mathcal{H}\mu=\mu for all μ ∈ Ω \mu\in\Omega . Therefore 

 

 
 
 ℋ ​ 𝒢 ​ μ = ℋ ​ 𝒢 ​ s ​ s ​ μ = ℋ ​ s ​ ℋ ​ s ​ μ = s ​ 𝒢 ​ ℋ ​ s ​ μ = s ​ s ​ μ = μ \displaystyle\mathcal{H}\mathcal{G}\mu=\mathcal{H}\mathcal{G}ss\mu=\mathcal{H}s\mathcal{H}s\mu=s\mathcal{G}\mathcal{H}s\mu=ss\mu=\mu 
 
 
 
 for all μ ∈ Ω \mu\in\Omega as well, completing the proof.
∎ 





 
 Corollary 8.4 . 

 
 Restricted to Ω ≥ 1 ​ ( n ) \Omega_{\geq 1}(n) , the map ℋ ¯ \overline{\mathcal{H}} is a left inverse to both 𝒢 \mathcal{G} and 𝒢 ¯ \overline{\mathcal{G}} . 







 
 8.3. The ord \mathrm{ord} and calc \mathrm{calc} functions


 
 Recall the spar tableau, and the ord \mathrm{ord} and calc \mathrm{calc} functions defined in § 2 .
For μ ∈ 𝒫 2 ​ r ​ e ​ g \mu\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{2reg}} , it will be useful to define a (tableau for a) partition λ = calc alt ​ ( μ ) \lambda=\mathrm{calc}_{\textup{alt}}(\mu) as follows. Start by setting λ = ∅ \lambda=\varnothing , and then for each t = c ⁡ ( μ ) , c ⁡ ( μ ) − 1 , … , 1 t=c(\mu),c(\mu)-1,\ldots,1 , work from left to right and add a box labeled t t at addable positions in λ \lambda if there is not a box labeled t t in the column immediately to the left—starting from the first column if c t ​ ( μ ) c_{t}(\mu) is odd, and starting from the second column if c t ​ ( μ ) c_{t}(\mu) is even. The following lemma is straightforward to check, and we leave it as an exercise for the reader.



 
 Lemma 8.5 . 

 
 For μ ∈ 𝒫 2 ​ r ​ e ​ g \mu\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{2reg}} , the partitions (and associated tableau) calc ⁡ ( μ ) \mathrm{calc}(\mu) and calc alt ​ ( μ ) \mathrm{calc}_{\textup{alt}}(\mu) are identical. 





 
 Let mc : Ω ≥ 1 → 𝒫 \textup{mc}:\Omega_{\geq 1}\rightarrow\mathcal{P} be the bijection defined by setting mc ​ ( μ ) = λ \textup{mc}(\mu)=\lambda , where n k ​ ( λ ) = μ k n_{k}(\lambda)=\mu_{k} for all k ∈ ℤ > 0 k\in\mathbbm{Z}_{>0} .
Define operators 𝒢 𝒫 , 𝒢 ¯ 𝒫 , ℋ ¯ 𝒫 : 𝒫 → 𝒫 \mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{P}},\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathcal{P}},\overline{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{P}}:\mathcal{P}\rightarrow\mathcal{P} by setting

 

 
 
 𝒢 𝒫 = mc ∘ 𝒢 ∘ mc − 1 ; 𝒢 ¯ 𝒫 = mc ∘ 𝒢 ¯ ∘ mc − 1 ; ℋ ¯ 𝒫 = mc ∘ ℋ ¯ ∘ mc − 1 . \displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{P}}=\textup{mc}\circ\mathcal{G}\circ\textup{mc}^{-1};\qquad\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathcal{P}}=\textup{mc}\circ\overline{\mathcal{G}}\circ\textup{mc}^{-1};\qquad\overline{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{P}}=\textup{mc}\circ\overline{\mathcal{H}}\circ\textup{mc}^{-1}. 
 
 
 
 Note that if λ = mc ​ ( μ ) \lambda=\textup{mc}(\mu) , then adding an addable box to a row of length k k in λ \lambda is tantamount to subtracting one from μ k \mu_{k} and adding one to μ k + 1 \mu_{k+1} , and removing a removable box from a row of length k k in λ \lambda is tantamount to subtracting one from μ k \mu_{k} and (when k > 1 k>1 ) adding one to μ k − 1 \mu_{k-1} .
In view of Lemma  8.1 , we thus may straightforwardly describe the workings of these operators:

 
 (GP1) 
 
 𝒢 𝒫 \mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{P}} acts by adding a box at each addable position in λ \lambda if and only if there was not a box added in the column directly to the left, starting from the second column.




 (GP2) 
 
 𝒢 ¯ 𝒫 \overline{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathcal{P}} acts by adding a box at each addable position in λ \lambda if and only if there was not a box added in the column directly to the left, starting from the first column.




 (HP) 
 
 ℋ ¯ 𝒫 \overline{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{P}} acts by deleting the rightmost removable box, and then working to the left, deleting each removable box if and only if a box was not deleted in the column directly to the right.




 
 We may record one additional feature of these operators, thanks to Corollary  8.4 :

 
 (HG) 
 
 The operator ℋ ¯ \overline{\mathcal{H}} removes from 𝒢 ⁡ ( ω ) \mathcal{G}(\omega) (resp. 𝒢 ¯ ​ ( ω ) \overline{\mathcal{G}}(\omega) ) exactly those boxes which are added to ω \omega by 𝒢 \mathcal{G} (resp. 𝒢 ¯ \overline{\mathcal{G}} ).




 


 
 Theorem 8.6 . 

 
 The functions ord : 𝒫 → 𝒫 2 ​ r ​ e ​ g \mathrm{ord}\colon\mathcal{P}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{2reg}} and calc : 𝒫 2 ​ r ​ e ​ g → 𝒫 \mathrm{calc}\colon\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{2reg}}\rightarrow\mathcal{P} are mutually inverse bijections. Moreover, the tableau generated in the construction of calc ⁡ ( ν ) \mathrm{calc}(\nu) is exactly 𝚜𝚙𝚊𝚛 ⁡ ( calc ⁡ ( ν ) ) {\tt spar}(\mathrm{calc}(\nu)) . 





 
 Proof.

 
 Let ν ∈ 𝒫 2 ​ r ​ e ​ g \nu\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{2reg}} , and set λ = calc alt ​ ( ν ) \lambda=\mathrm{calc}_{\textup{alt}}(\nu) . Then from the definition of calc alt \mathrm{calc}_{\textup{alt}} and (GP1),(GP2), we have that λ \lambda is obtained by iterative applications of 𝒢 𝒫 \mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{P}} , 𝒢 ¯ 𝒫 \overline{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathcal{P}} to ∅ \varnothing ; applying 𝒢 ¯ 𝒫 \overline{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathcal{P}} in the t t th step if c t ​ ( ν ) c_{t}(\nu) is odd, applying 𝒢 𝒫 \mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{P}} instead if c t ​ ( ν ) c_{t}(\nu) is even. On the other hand,
we have that ℋ ¯ 𝒫 k ​ ( λ ) = ∅ \overline{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{P}}^{k}(\lambda)=\varnothing for k ≫ 0 k\gg 0 . It is clear from (HP) that a box is labeled t t in 𝚜𝚙𝚊𝚛 ⁡ ( λ ) {\tt spar}(\lambda) if it is ousted in the t t th application of ℋ ¯ 𝒫 \overline{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{P}} to λ \lambda . Hence by (HG), t t appears in the first column of 𝚜𝚙𝚊𝚛 ⁡ ( λ ) {\tt spar}(\lambda) if and only if c t ​ ( ν ) c_{t}(\nu) is odd. Thus the tableau generated in the construction of calc alt ​ ( ν ) \mathrm{calc}_{\textup{alt}}(\nu) is exactly 𝚜𝚙𝚊𝚛 ⁡ ( λ ) {\tt spar}(\lambda) , and so ν = ord ⁡ ( λ ) \nu=\mathrm{ord}(\lambda) . Then the result follows from Lemma  8.5 .
∎ 







 
 8.4. Bicompositions


 
 A bicomposition 𝝁 = ( μ 1 , μ 0 ) ∈ Ω 2 \boldsymbol{\mu}=(\mu^{1},\mu^{0})\in\Omega^{2} is an ordered pair of compositions. We write 𝛀 \boldsymbol{\Omega} for the set of all bicompositions.
Recall that we write the complementary element of i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{2} with a hat, i.e. 0 ^ = 1 \widehat{0}=1 , 1 ^ = 0 \widehat{1}=0 .



 
 Let i ∈ ℤ 2 , k ∈ ℤ , a , b , n ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 i\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{2},k\in{\mathbbm{Z}},a,b,n\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{\geq 0} . We define some important subsets of 𝛀 \boldsymbol{\Omega} as follows:

 

 
 
 𝛀 ≥ k \displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{\geq k} 
 = { 𝝁 ∈ 𝛀 ∣ μ 0 , μ 1 ∈ Ω ≥ k } \displaystyle=\{\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\boldsymbol{\Omega}\mid\mu^{0},\mu^{1}\in\Omega_{\geq k}\} 
 
 
 
 
 𝛀 ≥ 0 , i \displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{\geq 0,i} 
 = { 𝝁 ∈ 𝛀 ∣ μ 0 , μ 1 ∈ Ω ≥ 0 , μ 0 i = 1 , μ 0 i ^ = 0 } \displaystyle=\{\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\boldsymbol{\Omega}\mid\mu^{0},\mu^{1}\in\Omega_{\geq 0},\mu_{0}^{i}=1,\mu_{0}^{\widehat{i}}=0\} 
 
 
 
 
 𝛀 ≥ 0 , ∙ \displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{\geq 0,\bullet} 
 = 𝛀 ≥ 0 , 0 ⊔ 𝛀 ≥ 0 , 1 \displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{\geq 0,0}\sqcup\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{\geq 0,1} 
 
 
 
 
 𝛀 ⁡ ( a , b ) \displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Omega}(a,b) 
 = { 𝝁 ∈ 𝛀 ∣ | μ 0 | = a , | μ 1 | = b } \displaystyle=\{\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\boldsymbol{\Omega}\mid|\mu^{0}|=a,|\mu^{1}|=b\} 
 
 
 
 
 𝛀 ⁡ ( n ) \displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Omega}(n) 
 = { 𝝁 ∈ 𝛀 ​ ∣ | μ 0 | + | ​ μ 1 | = n } , \displaystyle=\{\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\boldsymbol{\Omega}\mid|\mu^{0}|+|\mu^{1}|=n\}, 
 
 
 
 and we will also mix decorations, writing 𝛀 ≥ 0 , ∙ ​ ( a , b ) \boldsymbol{\Omega}_{\geq 0,\bullet}(a,b) for instance, to be interpreted in the obvious way.



 
 8.4.1. Functions of bicompositions


 
 Abusing notation, we extend the functions 𝒢 , ℋ , ℋ ¯ \mathcal{G},\mathcal{H},\bar{\mathcal{H}} to bicompositions 𝛀 \boldsymbol{\Omega} by acting diagonally, so that, for instance 𝒢 ​ 𝝁 = 𝝂 \mathcal{G}\boldsymbol{\mu}=\boldsymbol{\nu} , where ν i = 𝒢 ​ μ i \nu^{i}=\mathcal{G}\mu^{i} . For i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{2} , we further define the function 𝒢 ¯ i : 𝛀 → 𝛀 \bar{\mathcal{G}}^{i}:\boldsymbol{\Omega}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\Omega} by setting 𝒢 ¯ i ​ 𝝁 = 𝝂 \bar{\mathcal{G}}^{i}\boldsymbol{\mu}=\boldsymbol{\nu} , where

 

 
 
 ν i = 𝒢 ¯ ​ μ i and ν i ^ = 𝒢 ​ μ i ^ . \displaystyle\nu^{i}=\bar{\mathcal{G}}\mu^{i}\qquad\textup{and}\qquad\nu^{\widehat{i}}=\mathcal{G}\mu^{\widehat{i}}. 
 
 
 






 
 8.5. Binary words


 
 We call a binary string 𝜺 = ε 1 ⋯ ε n ∈ ℤ 2 n \boldsymbol{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon_{1}\cdots\varepsilon_{n}\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{2}^{n} a word , and the entries of 𝜺 \boldsymbol{\varepsilon} letters . We write 𝜺 rev := ε n ⋯ ε 1 \boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{\textup{rev}}:=\varepsilon_{n}\cdots\varepsilon_{1} .
Let Θ ⁡ ( n ) \Theta(n) be the set of all words of length n n . A subword of 𝜺 \boldsymbol{\varepsilon} is a substring 𝜺 [ s , t ] := ε s ⋯ ε t \boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{[s,t]}:=\varepsilon_{s}\cdots\varepsilon_{t} for some 1 ≤ s ≤ t ≤ n 1\leq s\leq t\leq n . For i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{2} , we write # i ​ 𝜺 \#_{i}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon} for the number of entries in 𝜺 \boldsymbol{\varepsilon} equal to i i . We write

 

 
 
 Θ ( a , b ) = { 𝜺 ∈ Θ ∣ # 0 𝜺 = a , # 1 𝜺 = b } ⊆ Θ ( a + b ) . \displaystyle\Theta(a,b)=\{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\in\Theta\mid\#_{0}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}=a,\#_{1}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}=b\}\subseteq\Theta(a+b). 
 
 
 


 
 8.5.1. Peaks


 
 We say k ∈ [ 1 , n ] k\in[1,n] is a left i i -peak for 𝜺 ∈ Θ ⁡ ( n ) \boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\in\Theta(n) provided
 # i ​ 𝜺 [ j , k ] > # i ^ ​ 𝜺 [ j , k ] \#_{i}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{[j,k]}>\#_{\widehat{i}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{[j,k]} 
for all j ∈ [ 1 , k ] j\in[1,k] . We say k k is a right i i -peak for 𝜺 \boldsymbol{\varepsilon} provided
 # i ​ 𝜺 [ k , j ] > # i ^ ​ 𝜺 [ k , j ] \#_{i}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{[k,j]}>\#_{\widehat{i}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{[k,j]} 
for all j ∈ [ k , n ] j\in[k,n] . We write P i L ​ ( 𝜺 ) \textup{P}^{\textup{L}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}) and P i R ​ ( 𝜺 ) \textup{P}^{\textup{R}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}) for the set of left and right i i -peaks of 𝜺 \boldsymbol{\varepsilon} , respectively. If ε k = i \varepsilon_{k}=i but k k is not a left (resp. right) i i -peak for 𝜺 \boldsymbol{\varepsilon} , then we say k k is a left (resp. right) i i -nonpeak for 𝜺 \boldsymbol{\varepsilon} .
We write NP i L ​ ( 𝜺 ) \textup{NP}^{\textup{L}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}) and NP i R ​ ( 𝜺 ) \textup{NP}^{\textup{R}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}) for the set of left and right i i -nonpeaks of 𝜺 \boldsymbol{\varepsilon} , respectively.
It follows then that

 

 
 
 P i L ​ ( 𝜺 ) ⊔ P i ^ L ​ ( 𝜺 ) ⊔ NP i L ​ ( 𝜺 ) ⊔ NP i ^ L ​ ( 𝜺 ) = [ 1 , n ] , \displaystyle\textup{P}^{\textup{L}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})\sqcup\textup{P}^{\textup{L}}_{\widehat{i}}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})\sqcup\textup{NP}^{\textup{L}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})\sqcup\textup{NP}^{\textup{L}}_{\widehat{i}}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})=[1,n], 
 
 
 
 and similarly for the right.



 
 Lemma 8.7 . 

 
 Let 𝛆 ∈ Θ \boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\in\Theta , i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{2} .
There is a bijection 

 

 
 
 ζ : NP i ^ L ​ ( 𝜺 ) → NP i R ​ ( 𝜺 ) . \displaystyle\zeta:\textup{NP}^{\textup{L}}_{\widehat{i}}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})\rightarrow\textup{NP}^{\textup{R}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}). 
 
 
 
 given by sending k ∈ NP i ^ L ​ ( 𝛆 ) k\in\textup{NP}^{\textup{L}}_{\widehat{i}}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}) to the unique ζ ⁡ ( k ) ∈ [ 1 , k − 1 ] \zeta(k)\in[1,k-1] such that # i ​ 𝛆 [ ζ ⁡ ( k ) , k ] = # i ^ ​ 𝛆 [ ζ ⁡ ( k ) , k ] \#_{i}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{[\zeta(k),k]}=\#_{\widehat{i}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{[\zeta(k),k]} , # i ​ 𝛆 [ t , k ] < # i ^ ​ 𝛆 [ t , k ] \#_{i}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{[t,k]}<\#_{\widehat{i}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{[t,k]} for all ζ ⁡ ( k ) < t ≤ k \zeta(k)<t\leq k , and # i ​ 𝛆 [ ζ ⁡ ( k ) , u ] > # i ^ ​ 𝛆 [ ζ ⁡ ( k ) , u ] \#_{i}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{[\zeta(k),u]}>\#_{\widehat{i}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{[\zeta(k),u]} for all ζ ⁡ ( k ) ≤ u < k \zeta(k)\leq u<k . 





 
 Proof.

 
 Let k ∈ NP i ^ L ​ ( 𝜺 ) k\in\textup{NP}^{\textup{L}}_{\widehat{i}}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}) . Then by k k ’s nonpeakness there exists a maximal k ′ ∈ [ 1 , k − 1 ] k^{\prime}\in[1,k-1] such that # i ​ 𝜺 [ k ′ , k ] ≥ # i ^ ​ 𝜺 [ k ′ , k ] \#_{i}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{[k^{\prime},k]}\geq\#_{\widehat{i}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{[k^{\prime},k]} . Then by assumption # i ​ 𝜺 [ t , k ] < # i ^ ​ 𝜺 [ t , k ] \#_{i}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{[t,k]}<\#_{\widehat{i}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{[t,k]} for all k ′ < t ≤ k k^{\prime}<t\leq k . Note then that ε k ′ = i \varepsilon_{k^{\prime}}=i and # i ​ 𝜺 [ k ′ , k ] = # i ^ ​ 𝜺 [ k ′ , k ] \#_{i}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{[k^{\prime},k]}=\#_{\widehat{i}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{[k^{\prime},k]} . If # i ​ 𝜺 [ k ′ , u ] ≤ # i ^ ​ 𝜺 [ k ′ , u ] \#_{i}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{[k^{\prime},u]}\leq\#_{\widehat{i}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{[k^{\prime},u]} for some k ′ < u < k k^{\prime}<u<k , then 

 

 
 
 # i ​ 𝜺 [ k ′ , k ] = # i ​ 𝜺 [ k ′ , u ] + # i ​ 𝜺 [ u + 1 , k ] < # i ^ ​ 𝜺 [ k ′ , u ] + # i ^ ​ 𝜺 [ u + 1 , k ] = # i ^ ​ 𝜺 [ k ′ , k ] , \displaystyle\#_{i}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{[k^{\prime},k]}=\#_{i}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{[k^{\prime},u]}+\#_{i}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{[u+1,k]}<\#_{\widehat{i}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{[k^{\prime},u]}+\#_{\widehat{i}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{[u+1,k]}=\#_{\widehat{i}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{[k^{\prime},k]}, 
 
 
 
 a contradiction. Therefore # i ​ 𝜺 [ k ′ , u ] > # i ^ ​ 𝜺 [ k ′ , u ] \#_{i}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{[k^{\prime},u]}>\#_{\widehat{i}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{[k^{\prime},u]} for all k ′ ≤ u < k k^{\prime}\leq u<k . Thus we may set ζ ⁡ ( k ) = k ′ \zeta(k)=k^{\prime} , so the map ζ \zeta of the lemma statement is well-defined. There is plainly an inverse map constructed symmetrically, so ζ \zeta is a bijection.
∎ 









 
 8.6. Functions on binary words


 
 Fix n ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 n\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{\geq 0} . We define a map 𝒜 i : Θ ⁡ ( n ) → Θ ⁡ ( n + 1 ) \mathcal{A}^{i}:\Theta(n)\rightarrow\Theta(n+1) which sends 𝜺 ∈ Θ ⁡ ( n ) \boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\in\Theta(n) to 𝒜 i ​ 𝜺 ∈ Θ ⁡ ( n + 1 ) \mathcal{A}^{i}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\in\Theta(n+1) by appending an i i to the end of 𝜺 \boldsymbol{\varepsilon} . We define a map ℛ : Θ ⁡ ( n + 1 ) → Θ ⁡ ( n ) \mathcal{R}:\Theta(n+1)\rightarrow\Theta(n) which sends 𝜺 ∈ Θ ⁡ ( n + 1 ) \boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\in\Theta(n+1) to ℛ ​ 𝜺 ∈ Θ n \mathcal{R}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\in\Theta_{n} by deleting the last letter in 𝜺 \boldsymbol{\varepsilon} .



 
 Lemma 8.8 . 

 
 Let n ∈ ℤ ≥ 1 n\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{\geq 1} , i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{2} . Let 𝛆 ∈ Θ ⁡ ( n ) \boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\in\Theta(n) . Then 

 

 
 
 P i R ​ ( ℛ ​ 𝜺 ) = { P i R ​ ( 𝜺 ) \ { n } if  ​ ε n = i ; P i R ​ ( 𝜺 ) ⊔ { ζ ⁡ ( n ) } if  ​ n ∈ NP i ^ L ​ ( 𝜺 ) ; P i R ​ ( 𝜺 ) = ∅ if  ​ n ∈ P i ^ L ​ ( 𝜺 ) . \displaystyle\textup{P}^{\textup{R}}_{i}(\mathcal{R}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})=\begin{cases}\textup{P}^{\textup{R}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})\backslash\{n\}&\textup{if }\varepsilon_{n}=i;\\
\textup{P}^{\textup{R}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})\sqcup\{\zeta(n)\}&\textup{if }n\in\textup{NP}^{\textup{L}}_{\widehat{i}}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon});\\
\textup{P}^{\textup{R}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})=\varnothing&\textup{if }n\in\textup{P}^{\textup{L}}_{\widehat{i}}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}).\\
\end{cases} 
 
 
 




 
 Proof.

 
 This is a routine definition chase, occasionally invoking Lemma  8.7 , so we omit the proof here.
Full details can be found in the arXiv version of the paper as explained in § 1.3 .
∎ 





 
 8.6.1. The 𝒯 i \mathcal{T}_{i} map


 
 Now we define a function 𝒯 i : Θ ⁡ ( n ) → Θ ⁡ ( n ) \mathcal{T}_{i}:\Theta(n)\rightarrow\Theta(n) by setting
 𝒯 i ​ 𝜺 = 𝝆 \mathcal{T}_{i}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}=\boldsymbol{\rho} ,
where

 

 
 (8.4) 
 
 ρ r = { ε r if  ​ r ∈ P i ^ L ​ ( 𝜺 ) ; ε ^ r otherwise . \displaystyle\rho_{r}=\begin{cases}\varepsilon_{r}&\textup{if }r\in\textup{P}^{\textup{L}}_{\widehat{i}}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon});\\
\widehat{\varepsilon}_{r}&\textup{otherwise}.\end{cases} 
 
 
 


 
 Lemma 8.9 . 

 
 Let n ∈ ℤ ≥ 1 n\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{\geq 1} . We have ℛ ∘ 𝒯 i = 𝒯 i ∘ ℛ \mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{T}_{i}=\mathcal{T}_{i}\circ\mathcal{R} as functions on Θ ⁡ ( n ) \Theta(n) . 





 
 Proof.

 
 Let 𝜺 ∈ Θ ⁡ ( n ) \boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\in\Theta(n) . For j ∈ [ 1 , n − 1 ] j\in[1,n-1] , we have j ∈ P i ^ L ​ ( 𝜺 ) j\in\textup{P}^{\textup{L}}_{\widehat{i}}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}) if and only if j ∈ P i ^ L ​ ( ℛ ​ 𝜺 ) j\in\textup{P}^{\textup{L}}_{\widehat{i}}(\mathcal{R}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}) . From this it immediately follows that ℛ ​ 𝒯 i ​ 𝜺 = 𝒯 i ​ ℛ ​ 𝜺 \mathcal{R}\mathcal{T}_{i}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}=\mathcal{T}_{i}\mathcal{R}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon} .
∎ 





 
 Lemma 8.10 . 

 
 For 𝛆 ∈ Θ \boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\in\Theta , we have # i ​ 𝒯 i ​ 𝛆 = # i ​ 𝛆 − # ​ P i R ​ ( 𝛆 ) \#_{i}\mathcal{T}_{i}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}=\#_{i}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}-\#\textup{P}^{\textup{R}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}) . 





 
 Proof.

 
 By Lemma  8.7 and the definition of the map 𝒯 i \mathcal{T}_{i} , we have 

 

 
 
 # i ​ 𝜺 − # ​ P i R ​ ( 𝜺 ) = # ​ NP i R ​ ( 𝜺 ) = # ​ NP i ^ L ​ ( 𝜺 ) = # i ​ 𝒯 i ​ 𝜺 , \displaystyle\#_{i}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}-\#\textup{P}^{\textup{R}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})=\#\textup{NP}^{\textup{R}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})=\#\textup{NP}^{\textup{L}}_{\widehat{i}}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})=\#_{i}\mathcal{T}_{i}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}, 
 
 
 
 which yields the result.
∎ 





 
 Lemma 8.11 . 

 
 For 𝛆 ∈ Θ ⁡ ( n ) \boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\in\Theta(n) , we have 𝒯 i k ​ 𝛆 = i ^ n \mathcal{T}_{i}^{k}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}={\widehat{i}}^{n} for k ≥ n k\geq n . 





 
 Proof.

 
 Let 𝜺 ∈ Θ ⁡ ( n ) \boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\in\Theta(n) . If 𝜺 = i ^ n \boldsymbol{\varepsilon}=\widehat{i}^{n} , then by definition of the map 𝒯 i \mathcal{T}_{i} we have 𝒯 i k ​ 𝜺 = i ^ n \mathcal{T}_{i}^{k}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}=\widehat{i}^{n} for all k ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 k\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{\geq 0} , so the claim follows. Otherwise, let m m be minimal such that ε m = i \varepsilon_{m}=i . Setting 𝝆 = 𝒯 i ​ 𝜺 \boldsymbol{\rho}=\mathcal{T}_{i}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon} , we have [ 1 , m − 1 ] ∈ P i ^ L ​ ( 𝜺 ) [1,m-1]\in\textup{P}^{\textup{L}}_{\widehat{i}}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}) , so ρ 1 = ⋯ = ρ m = i ^ \rho_{1}=\cdots=\rho_{m}=\widehat{i} . Thus each application of 𝒯 i \mathcal{T}_{i} moves the first instance of i i to strictly later in the string, so by the n n th application of 𝒯 i \mathcal{T}_{i} there can be no more i i ’s. Therefore 𝒯 i n ​ 𝜺 = i ^ n \mathcal{T}_{i}^{n}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}={\widehat{i}}^{n} , and the claim follows.
∎ 





 
 Lemma 8.12 . 

 
 Let 𝛆 ∈ Θ \boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\in\Theta , i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{2} . Then P i ^ R ​ ( 𝒯 i ​ 𝛆 ) = P i ^ L ​ ( 𝛆 ) ⊔ P i R ​ ( 𝛆 ) \textup{P}^{\textup{R}}_{\widehat{i}}(\mathcal{T}_{i}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})=\textup{P}^{\textup{L}}_{\widehat{i}}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})\sqcup\textup{P}^{\textup{R}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}) , and j < k j<k for all j ∈ P i ^ L ​ ( 𝛆 ) j\in\textup{P}^{\textup{L}}_{\widehat{i}}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}) , k ∈ P i R ​ ( 𝛆 ) k\in\textup{P}^{\textup{R}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}) . 





 
 Proof.

 
 This is another routine definition chase making use of Lemma  8.7 , so we omit the proof here.
Full details can be found in the arXiv version of the paper as explained in § 1.3 .
∎ 







 
 8.6.2. The map 𝒰 i , m \mathcal{U}_{i,m} 


 
 Let 𝜺 ∈ Θ ⁡ ( n ) \boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\in\Theta(n) , m ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 m\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{\geq 0} . Set P i ^ R ​ ( 𝜺 ) − m \textup{P}^{\textup{R}}_{\widehat{i}}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})_{-m} to be the subset of P i ^ R ​ ( 𝜺 ) \textup{P}^{\textup{R}}_{\widehat{i}}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}) given by deleting the m m largest elements from P i ^ R ​ ( 𝜺 ) \textup{P}^{\textup{R}}_{\widehat{i}}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}) . We define a function 𝒰 i , m : Θ ⁡ ( n ) → Θ ⁡ ( n ) \mathcal{U}_{i,m}:\Theta(n)\rightarrow\Theta(n) by setting 𝒰 i , m ​ ( 𝜺 ) = 𝝆 \mathcal{U}_{i,m}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})=\boldsymbol{\rho} , where

 

 
 (8.5) 
 
 ρ r = { ε r if  ​ r ∈ P i ^ R ​ ( 𝜺 ) − m ε ^ r otherwise . \displaystyle\rho_{r}=\begin{cases}\varepsilon_{r}&\textup{if }r\in\textup{P}^{\textup{R}}_{\widehat{i}}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})_{-m}\\
\widehat{\varepsilon}_{r}&\textup{otherwise}.\end{cases} 
 
 
 
 We write 𝒰 i := 𝒰 i , 0 \mathcal{U}_{i}:=\mathcal{U}_{i,0} .



 
 Lemma 8.13 . 

 
 Let 𝛆 ∈ Θ \boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\in\Theta . Then 𝒰 i , # ​ P i R ​ ( 𝛆 ) ​ 𝒯 i ​ 𝛆 = 𝛆 \mathcal{U}_{i,\#\textup{P}^{\textup{R}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})}\mathcal{T}_{i}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}=\boldsymbol{\varepsilon} . 





 
 Proof.

 
 By Lemma  8.12 , we have P i ^ R ​ ( 𝒯 i ​ 𝜺 ) − # ​ P i R ​ ( 𝜺 ) = P i ^ L ​ ( 𝜺 ) \textup{P}^{\textup{R}}_{\widehat{i}}(\mathcal{T}_{i}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})_{-\#\textup{P}^{\textup{R}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})}=\textup{P}^{\textup{L}}_{\widehat{i}}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}) , and so it immediately follows from ( 8.4 , 8.5 ) that 𝒰 i , # ​ P i R ​ ( 𝜺 ) ​ 𝒯 i ​ 𝜺 = 𝜺 \mathcal{U}_{i,\#\textup{P}^{\textup{R}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})}\mathcal{T}_{i}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}=\boldsymbol{\varepsilon} .
∎ 









 
 8.7. From binary sequences to bicompositions


 
 We now define a function 𝒥 : Θ → 𝛀 ≥ 1 \mathcal{J}:\Theta\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{\geq 1} by setting 𝒥 ​ 𝜺 = 𝝁 \mathcal{J}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}=\boldsymbol{\mu} , where

 

 
 (8.6) 
 
 μ k i = # ​ P i R ​ ( 𝒯 i k − 1 ​ 𝜺 ) \displaystyle\mu^{i}_{k}=\#\textup{P}^{\textup{R}}_{i}(\mathcal{T}_{i}^{k-1}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}) 
 
 
 
 for i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{2} , k ∈ ℤ ≥ 1 k\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{\geq 1} . This map is well defined, since for k ≫ 0 k\gg 0 , we have μ k i = # ​ P i R ​ ( 𝒯 i k ​ 𝜺 ) = # ​ P i R ​ ( i ^ n ) = 0 \mu_{k}^{i}=\#\textup{P}^{\textup{R}}_{i}(\mathcal{T}_{i}^{k}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})=\#\textup{P}^{\textup{R}}_{i}(\widehat{i}^{n})=0 by Lemma  8.11 . In fact, it follows from Lemma  8.10 that | μ i | = # i ​ 𝜺 |\mu^{i}|=\#_{i}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon} , so 𝒥 \mathcal{J} restricts to a map

 

 
 
 𝒥 : Θ ⁡ ( a , b ) → 𝛀 ≥ 1 ​ ( a , b ) . \displaystyle\mathcal{J}:\Theta(a,b)\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{\geq 1}(a,b). 
 
 
 


 
 Lemma 8.14 . 

 
 Let n ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 , i ∈ ℤ 2 n\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{\geq 0},i\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{2} . We have equalities 

 

 
 (8.7) 
 
 ℋ ¯ ​ 𝒥 = 𝒥 ​ ℛ and 𝒢 ¯ i ​ 𝒥 = 𝒥 ​ 𝒜 i \displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{H}}\mathcal{J}=\mathcal{J}\mathcal{R}\qquad\textup{and}\qquad\bar{\mathcal{G}}^{i}\mathcal{J}=\mathcal{J}\mathcal{A}^{i} 
 
 
 
 of functions Θ ⁡ ( n + 1 ) → 𝛀 ≥ 1 ​ ( n ) \Theta(n+1)\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{\geq 1}(n) , and Θ ⁡ ( n ) → 𝛀 ≥ 1 ​ ( n + 1 ) \Theta(n)\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{\geq 1}(n+1) respectively. 





 
 Proof.

 
 We prove the left equality first.
Let 𝜺 ∈ Θ ⁡ ( n + 1 ) \boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\in\Theta(n+1) .
Let 𝝁 = 𝒥 ​ 𝜺 \boldsymbol{\mu}=\mathcal{J}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon} , and 𝝂 = 𝒥 ​ ℛ ​ 𝜺 \boldsymbol{\nu}=\mathcal{J}\mathcal{R}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon} . Let i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{2} , k ∈ ℤ ≥ 1 k\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{\geq 1} . Set 𝝆 = 𝒯 i k − 1 ​ 𝜺 \boldsymbol{\rho}=\mathcal{T}_{i}^{k-1}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon} and 𝝆 ¯ = 𝒯 i k ​ 𝜺 = 𝒯 i ​ 𝝆 \bar{\boldsymbol{\rho}}=\mathcal{T}_{i}^{k}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}=\mathcal{T}_{i}\boldsymbol{\rho} . Then μ k i = # ​ P i R ​ ( 𝝆 ) \mu_{k}^{i}=\#\textup{P}^{\textup{R}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\rho}) , μ k + 1 i = # ​ P i R ​ ( 𝝆 ¯ ) \mu_{k+1}^{i}=\#\textup{P}^{\textup{R}}_{i}(\bar{\boldsymbol{\rho}}) , ν k i = # ​ P i R ​ ( 𝒯 i k − 1 ​ ℛ ​ 𝜺 ) = # ​ P i R ​ ( ℛ ​ 𝝆 ) \nu_{k}^{i}=\#\textup{P}^{\textup{R}}_{i}(\mathcal{T}_{i}^{k-1}\mathcal{R}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})=\#\textup{P}^{\textup{R}}_{i}(\mathcal{R}\boldsymbol{\rho}) , and similarly ν k + 1 i = # ​ P i R ​ ( ℛ ​ 𝝆 ¯ ) \nu_{k+1}^{i}=\#\textup{P}^{\textup{R}}_{i}(\mathcal{R}\bar{\boldsymbol{\rho}}) . Then it follows from Lemma  8.8 that 

 

 
 (8.8) 
 
 ν k i = { μ k i − 1 if  ​ ρ n = i ; μ k i + 1 if  ​ n ∈ NP i ^ L ​ ( 𝝆 ) ; μ k i = 0 if  ​ n ∈ P i ^ L ​ ( 𝝆 ) , and ν k + 1 i = { μ k + 1 i − 1 if  ​ ρ ¯ n = i ; μ k + 1 i + 1 if  ​ n ∈ NP i ^ L ​ ( 𝝆 ¯ ) ; μ k + 1 i = 0 if  ​ n ∈ P i ^ L ​ ( 𝝆 ¯ ) . \displaystyle\nu_{k}^{i}=\begin{cases}\mu_{k}^{i}-1&\textup{if }\rho_{n}=i;\\
\mu_{k}^{i}+1&\textup{if }n\in\textup{NP}^{\textup{L}}_{\widehat{i}}(\boldsymbol{\rho});\\
\mu_{k}^{i}=0&\textup{if }n\in\textup{P}^{\textup{L}}_{\widehat{i}}(\boldsymbol{\rho}),\\
\end{cases}\qquad\textup{and}\qquad\nu_{k+1}^{i}=\begin{cases}\mu_{k+1}^{i}-1&\textup{if }\bar{\rho}_{n}=i;\\
\mu_{k+1}^{i}+1&\textup{if }n\in\textup{NP}^{\textup{L}}_{\widehat{i}}(\bar{\boldsymbol{\rho}});\\
\mu_{k+1}^{i}=0&\textup{if }n\in\textup{P}^{\textup{L}}_{\widehat{i}}(\bar{\boldsymbol{\rho}}).\\
\end{cases} 
 
 
 
 We have the following by the definition of the map 𝒯 i \mathcal{T}_{i} . 

 
 • 
 
 If ρ ¯ n = i \bar{\rho}_{n}=i , then n ∈ NP i ^ L ​ ( 𝝆 ) n\in\textup{NP}^{\textup{L}}_{\widehat{i}}(\boldsymbol{\rho}) .




 • 
 
 If n ∈ NP i ^ L ​ ( 𝝆 ¯ ) n\in\textup{NP}^{\textup{L}}_{\widehat{i}}(\bar{\boldsymbol{\rho}}) , then ρ n = i \rho_{n}=i or n ∈ P i ^ L ​ ( 𝝆 ¯ ) n\in\textup{P}^{\textup{L}}_{\widehat{i}}(\bar{\boldsymbol{\rho}}) .




 • 
 
 If n ∈ P i ^ L ​ ( 𝝆 ¯ ) n\in\textup{P}^{\textup{L}}_{\widehat{i}}(\bar{\boldsymbol{\rho}}) , then ρ n = i \rho_{n}=i or n ∈ P i ^ L ​ ( 𝝆 ¯ ) n\in\textup{P}^{\textup{L}}_{\widehat{i}}(\bar{\boldsymbol{\rho}}) .




 
 By ( 8.8 ) we must have ν k + 1 i = μ k + 1 i ± 1 \nu^{i}_{k+1}=\mu^{i}_{k+1}\pm 1 , or else ν k + 1 i = μ k + 1 i = 0 \nu^{i}_{k+1}=\mu^{i}_{k+1}=0 , and in view of ( 8.8 ) and the above bulleted facts it follows that: 

 
 • 
 
 If ν k + 1 i = μ k + 1 i − 1 \nu^{i}_{k+1}=\mu^{i}_{k+1}-1 , then ν k i = μ k + 1 \nu^{i}_{k}=\mu_{k}+1 .




 • 
 
 If ν k + 1 i = μ k + 1 i + 1 \nu^{i}_{k+1}=\mu^{i}_{k+1}+1 , then ν k i = 0 \nu^{i}_{k}=0 if μ k i = 0 \mu^{i}_{k}=0 , and otherwise ν k i = μ k i − 1 \nu^{i}_{k}=\mu^{i}_{k}-1 .




 • 
 
 If ν k + 1 i = μ k + 1 i = 0 \nu^{i}_{k+1}=\mu^{i}_{k+1}=0 , then ν k i = 0 \nu^{i}_{k}=0 if μ k i = 0 \mu^{i}_{k}=0 , and otherwise ν k i = μ k i − 1 \nu^{i}_{k}=\mu^{i}_{k}-1 .




 
 But then this situation is logically equivalent to (1a, 1b, 1c) of Lemma  8.1 holding for ν i \nu^{i} and μ i \mu^{i} and all k ∈ ℤ ≥ 1 k\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{\geq 1} , so it immediately follows from that Lemma  8.1 (1) that ν i = ℋ ¯ ​ μ i \nu^{i}=\bar{\mathcal{H}}\mu^{i} , and thus ℋ ¯ ​ 𝝁 = 𝝂 \bar{\mathcal{H}}\boldsymbol{\mu}=\boldsymbol{\nu} , verifying the left side of ( 8.7 ). 



 
 Now we work on the right side of ( 8.7 ).
Let 𝜸 ∈ Θ ⁡ ( n − 1 ) \boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\Theta(n-1) , and set 𝜺 = 𝒜 j ​ 𝜸 \boldsymbol{\varepsilon}=\mathcal{A}^{j}\boldsymbol{\gamma} . Then ε n = j \varepsilon_{n}=j and 𝜸 = ℛ ​ 𝜺 \boldsymbol{\gamma}=\mathcal{R}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon} . Set 𝝁 = 𝒥 ​ 𝜺 \boldsymbol{\mu}=\mathcal{J}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon} , and 𝝂 = 𝒥 ​ ℛ ​ 𝜺 = 𝒥 ​ 𝜸 \boldsymbol{\nu}=\mathcal{J}\mathcal{R}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}=\mathcal{J}\boldsymbol{\gamma} . Then by ( 8.8 ) we have, for i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{2} , that 

 

 
 
 ( ℋ ​ 𝝁 ) 1 i = ( ℋ ¯ ​ 𝝁 ) 1 i = ( ℋ ¯ ​ 𝒥 ​ 𝜺 ) 1 i = ( 𝒥 ​ ℛ ​ 𝜺 ) 1 i = ν 1 i = μ 1 i − 1 ​  if  ​ i = j and ( ℋ ​ 𝝁 ) 1 i = ν 1 i ∈ { μ 1 i + 1 , μ 1 i } ​  if  ​ i = j ^ . \displaystyle(\mathcal{H}\boldsymbol{\mu})^{i}_{1}=(\bar{\mathcal{H}}\boldsymbol{\mu})^{i}_{1}=(\bar{\mathcal{H}}\mathcal{J}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})^{i}_{1}=(\mathcal{JR\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})^{i}_{1}=\nu_{1}^{i}=\mu_{1}^{i}-1\textup{ if }i=j\qquad\textup{and}\qquad(\mathcal{H}\boldsymbol{\mu})^{i}_{1}=\nu_{1}^{i}\in\{\mu_{1}^{i}+1,\mu_{1}^{i}\}\textup{ if }i=\widehat{j}. 
 
 
 
 Therefore by ( 8.2 ) we have that
 η 1 i ​ ( 𝝁 ) \eta^{i}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mu}) is odd if i = j i=j and even if i = j ^ i=\widehat{j} , and so 

 

 
 
 ( ℋ ​ 𝝁 ) 0 j = μ 0 j + 1 = 1 and ( ℋ ​ 𝝁 ) 0 j ^ = μ 0 j ^ = 0 \displaystyle(\mathcal{H}\boldsymbol{\mu})^{j}_{0}=\mu_{0}^{j}+1=1\qquad\textup{and}\qquad(\mathcal{H}\boldsymbol{\mu})^{\widehat{j}}_{0}=\mu_{0}^{\widehat{j}}=0 
 
 
 
 by ( 8.2 ). It follows then that ℋ ​ 𝝁 = add 0 j ∘ del 0 ∘ ℋ ​ 𝝁 \mathcal{H}\boldsymbol{\mu}=\textup{add}^{j}_{0}\circ\textup{del}_{0}\circ\mathcal{H}\boldsymbol{\mu} , and so we have
 𝒢 ¯ j ​ ℋ ¯ ​ 𝝁 = 𝒢 ​ ℋ ​ 𝝁 . \bar{\mathcal{G}}^{j}\bar{\mathcal{H}}\boldsymbol{\mu}=\mathcal{G}\mathcal{H}\boldsymbol{\mu}. 
Therefore, applying the left side of ( 8.7 ) and Lemma  8.3 , we have 

 

 
 
 𝒢 ¯ j ​ 𝒥 ​ 𝜸 = 𝒢 ¯ j ​ 𝒥 ​ ℛ ​ 𝒜 j ​ 𝜸 = 𝒢 ¯ j ​ ℋ ¯ ​ 𝒥 ​ 𝒜 j ​ 𝜸 = 𝒢 ¯ j ​ ℋ ¯ ​ 𝝁 = 𝒢 ​ ℋ ​ 𝝁 = 𝝁 = 𝒥 ​ 𝒜 j ​ 𝜸 \displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{G}}^{j}\mathcal{J}\boldsymbol{\gamma}=\bar{\mathcal{G}}^{j}\mathcal{J}\mathcal{R}\mathcal{A}^{j}\boldsymbol{\gamma}=\bar{\mathcal{G}}^{j}\bar{\mathcal{H}}\mathcal{J}\mathcal{A}^{j}\boldsymbol{\gamma}=\bar{\mathcal{G}}^{j}\bar{\mathcal{H}}\boldsymbol{\mu}=\mathcal{G}\mathcal{H}\boldsymbol{\mu}=\boldsymbol{\mu}=\mathcal{J}\mathcal{A}^{j}\boldsymbol{\gamma} 
 
 
 
 as desired.
∎ 







 
 8.8. From bicompositions to binary sequences


 
 For i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{2} , define a map 𝒦 i : 𝛀 ≥ 1 ​ ( n ) → Θ ⁡ ( n ) \mathcal{K}^{i}:\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{\geq 1}(n)\rightarrow\Theta(n) as follows. If μ i = ∅ \mu^{i}=\varnothing , set 𝒦 i ​ ( 𝝁 ) = i ^ n \mathcal{K}^{i}(\boldsymbol{\mu})=\widehat{i}^{n} . Otherwise, let m m be maximal such that μ m i > 0 \mu^{i}_{m}>0 , and define

 

 
 (8.9) 
 
 𝒦 i ( 𝝁 ) = 𝒰 i , μ 1 i ⋯ 𝒰 i , μ m i ( i ^ n ) . \displaystyle\mathcal{K}^{i}(\boldsymbol{\mu})=\mathcal{U}_{i,\mu^{i}_{1}}\cdots\mathcal{U}_{i,\mu^{i}_{m}}(\widehat{i}^{n}). 
 
 
 




 
 8.9. MV bicompositions


 
 Let 𝝁 = ( μ 1 , μ 0 ) ∈ 𝛀 \boldsymbol{\mu}=(\mu^{1},\mu^{0})\in\boldsymbol{\Omega} , i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{2} and m ∈ ℤ ≥ 1 m\in\mathbbm{Z}_{\geq 1} .
We define points { β m i ( 𝝁 ) ∣ i ∈ ℤ 2 , m ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 } \{\beta^{i}_{m}(\boldsymbol{\mu})\mid i\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{2},m\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{\geq 0}\} in the lattice ℤ ≥ 0 ​ I {\mathbbm{Z}}_{\geq 0}I :

 

 
 
 β m i ​ ( 𝝁 ) = ∑ k = 1 m μ k ​ ( ( k − 1 ) ​ δ + α i ) = ( μ 1 + 2 ​ μ 2 + ⋯ + m ​ μ m ) ​ α i + ( μ 2 + 2 ​ μ 3 + ⋯ + ( m − 1 ) ​ μ m ) ​ α i ^ . \displaystyle\beta_{m}^{i}(\boldsymbol{\mu})=\sum_{k=1}^{m}\mu_{k}((k-1)\delta+\alpha_{i})=(\mu_{1}+2\mu_{2}+\cdots+m\mu_{m})\alpha_{i}+(\mu_{2}+2\mu_{3}+\cdots+(m-1)\mu_{m})\alpha_{\widehat{i}}. 
 
 
 
 We define P ⁡ ( 𝝁 ) P(\boldsymbol{\mu}) to be the convex hull of the points { β m i ( 𝝁 ) ∣ i ∈ ℤ 2 , m ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 } \{\beta^{i}_{m}(\boldsymbol{\mu})\mid i\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{2},m\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{\geq 0}\} .
(see Example  8.18 ).
We further define integers:

 

 
 (8.10) 
 
 c m i ​ ( 𝝁 ) := ∑ k = 1 m k ⁡ ( μ k + 1 i ^ − μ k i ) d m i ​ ( 𝝁 ) := m ​ μ m i ^ + ( m + 1 ) ​ μ m + 1 i ^ + ∑ k = 1 m − 1 k ⁡ ( μ k i ^ − μ k + 1 i ) \displaystyle c^{i}_{m}(\boldsymbol{\mu}):=\sum_{k=1}^{m}k(\mu_{k+1}^{\widehat{i}}-\mu^{i}_{k})\qquad d^{i}_{m}(\boldsymbol{\mu}):=m\mu_{m}^{\widehat{i}}+(m+1)\mu_{m+1}^{\widehat{i}}+\sum_{k=1}^{m-1}k(\mu_{k}^{\widehat{i}}-\mu_{k+1}^{i}) 
 
 
 
 so that

 

 
 
 β m + 1 i ^ ​ ( 𝝁 ) − β m i ​ ( 𝝁 ) := c m i ​ ( 𝝁 ) ​ α i + d m i ​ ( 𝝁 ) ​ α i ^ \displaystyle\beta^{\widehat{i}}_{m+1}(\boldsymbol{\mu})-\beta^{i}_{m}(\boldsymbol{\mu}):=c_{m}^{i}(\boldsymbol{\mu})\alpha_{i}+d_{m}^{i}(\boldsymbol{\mu})\alpha_{\widehat{i}} 
 
 
 
 for all i ∈ ℤ 2 , m ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 i\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{2},m\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{\geq 0} .
It will be convenient to record some equalities for future use:

 

 
 (8.11) 
 
 d m i ​ ( 𝝁 ) − c m i ​ ( 𝝁 ) \displaystyle d_{m}^{i}(\boldsymbol{\mu})-c_{m}^{i}(\boldsymbol{\mu}) 
 = ( μ 2 i + ⋯ + μ m i ) + ( μ 1 i ^ + ⋯ + μ m + 1 i ^ ) \displaystyle=(\mu_{2}^{i}+\cdots+\mu_{m}^{i})+(\mu_{1}^{\widehat{i}}+\cdots+\mu_{m+1}^{\widehat{i}}) 
 
 
 
 (8.12) 
 
 c m i ​ ( 𝝁 ) − c m − 1 i ​ ( 𝝁 ) \displaystyle c_{m}^{i}(\boldsymbol{\mu})-c_{m-1}^{i}(\boldsymbol{\mu}) 
 = m ⁡ ( μ m + 1 i ^ − μ m i ) \displaystyle=m(\mu_{m+1}^{\widehat{i}}-\mu_{m}^{i}) 
 
 
 
 (8.13) 
 
 d m i ​ ( 𝝁 ) − d m − 1 i ​ ( 𝝁 ) \displaystyle d_{m}^{i}(\boldsymbol{\mu})-d_{m-1}^{i}(\boldsymbol{\mu}) 
 = ( m − 1 ) ​ μ m i + ( m + 1 ) ​ μ m + 1 i ^ \displaystyle=(m-1)\mu^{i}_{m}+(m+1)\mu^{\widehat{i}}_{m+1} 
 
 
 
 (8.14) 
 
 c m i ​ ( 𝝁 ) + d m i ^ ​ ( 𝝁 ) \displaystyle c_{m}^{i}(\boldsymbol{\mu})+d_{m}^{\widehat{i}}(\boldsymbol{\mu}) 
 = m ​ μ m + 1 i + ( m + 1 ) ​ μ m + 1 i ^ \displaystyle=m\mu_{m+1}^{i}+(m+1)\mu_{m+1}^{\widehat{i}} 
 
 
 
 (8.15) 
 
 c m i ​ ( 𝝁 ) + d m − 1 i ^ ​ ( 𝝁 ) \displaystyle c_{m}^{i}(\boldsymbol{\mu})+d_{m-1}^{\widehat{i}}(\boldsymbol{\mu}) 
 = ( m − 1 ) ​ μ m i ^ + m ​ μ m + 1 i ^ \displaystyle=(m-1)\mu_{m}^{\widehat{i}}+m\mu_{m+1}^{\widehat{i}} 
 
 
 
 (8.16) 
 
 c m − 1 i ^ ​ ( 𝝁 ) + d m i ​ ( 𝝁 ) \displaystyle c^{\widehat{i}}_{m-1}(\boldsymbol{\mu})+d_{m}^{i}(\boldsymbol{\mu}) 
 = m ​ μ m i ^ + ( m + 1 ) ​ μ m + 1 i ^ \displaystyle=m\mu^{\widehat{i}}_{m}+(m+1)\mu^{\widehat{i}}_{m+1} 
 
 
 


 
 Finally, we define η m i ​ ( 𝝁 ) \eta^{i}_{m}(\boldsymbol{\mu}) to be smallest non-negative integer t t such that μ m + t i = 0 \mu^{i}_{m+t}=0 , and we set ϑ m i ​ ( 𝝁 ) \vartheta^{i}_{m}(\boldsymbol{\mu}) to be the smallest non-negative integer t t such that μ m − t i = 0 \mu^{i}_{m-t}=0 .
We will occasionally write β m i , c m i , d m i , η m i , ϑ m i \beta^{i}_{m},c^{i}_{m},d^{i}_{m},\eta^{i}_{m},\vartheta^{i}_{m} when 𝝁 \boldsymbol{\mu} is clear from context.



 
 Definition 8.15 . 

 
 Let 𝝁 = ( μ 1 , μ 0 ) ∈ 𝛀 \boldsymbol{\mu}=(\mu^{1},\mu^{0})\in\boldsymbol{\Omega} . Then we say 𝝁 \boldsymbol{\mu} is MV provided that for all m ∈ ℤ > 0 m\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{>0} we have

 

 
 (8.17) 
 
 0 ∈ { c m 1 ​ ( 𝝁 ) , c m 0 ​ ( 𝝁 ) } ⊆ ℤ ≤ 0 . \displaystyle 0\in\{c_{m}^{1}(\boldsymbol{\mu}),c_{m}^{0}(\boldsymbol{\mu})\}\subseteq{\mathbbm{Z}}_{\leq 0}. 
 
 
 
 We write 𝛀 MV \boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\textup{MV}} for the set of all MV bicompositions. We say that 𝝁 ∈ 𝛀 MV \boldsymbol{\mu}\in\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\textup{MV}} is nontrivial if there exists some k > 0 k>0 , i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{2} with μ k i > 0 \mu_{k}^{i}>0 . For j ∈ ℤ 2 j\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{2} we define subsets

 

 
 
 𝛀 ≥ 1 , + MV \displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{\geq 1,+}^{\textup{MV}} 
 = { 𝝁 ∈ 𝛀 MV ∣ 𝝁  is nontrivial and  μ m i = 0  for all  i ∈ ℤ 2 , m ≤ 0 } \displaystyle=\{\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\textup{MV}}\mid\boldsymbol{\mu}\textup{ is nontrivial and }\mu^{i}_{m}=0\textup{ for all }i\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{2},m\leq 0\} 
 
 
 
 
 𝛀 ≥ 0 , j MV \displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{\geq 0,j}^{\textup{MV}} 
 = { 𝝁 ∈ 𝛀 MV ∣ μ 0 j = 1 , μ 0 j ^ = 0 , μ m i = 0  for all  i ∈ ℤ 2 , m < 0 } \displaystyle=\{\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\textup{MV}}\mid\mu^{j}_{0}=1,\mu^{\widehat{j}}_{0}=0,\mu^{i}_{m}=0\textup{ for all }i\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{2},m<0\} 
 
 
 
 
 𝛀 ≥ 0 , ∙ MV \displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{\geq 0,\bullet}^{\textup{MV}} 
 = { 𝝁 ∈ 𝛀 MV ∣ μ 0 0 + μ 0 1 = 1 , μ m i = 0  for all  i ∈ ℤ 2 , m < 0 } = 𝛀 ≥ 0 , 0 MV ⊔ 𝛀 ≥ 0 , 1 MV . \displaystyle=\{\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\textup{MV}}\mid\mu^{0}_{0}+\mu^{1}_{0}=1,\mu^{i}_{m}=0\textup{ for all }i\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{2},m<0\}=\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{\geq 0,0}^{\textup{MV}}\sqcup\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{\geq 0,1}^{\textup{MV}}. 
 
 
 




 
 Lemma 8.16 . 

 
 Let 𝛍 ∈ 𝛀 MV \boldsymbol{\mu}\in\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\textup{MV}} be nontrivial. Then | η 1 1 ​ ( 𝛍 ) − η 1 0 ​ ( 𝛍 ) | = 1 |\eta^{1}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mu})-\eta^{0}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mu})|=1 . 





 
 Proof.

 
 Let i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{2} be such that s = η 1 i ​ ( 𝝁 ) ≤ η 1 i ^ ​ ( 𝝁 ) = t s=\eta^{i}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mu})\leq\eta^{\widehat{i}}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mu})=t . Since 𝝁 \boldsymbol{\mu} is nontrivial, there exists some j ∈ ℤ 2 , r ≥ 0 j\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{2},r\geq 0 such that 

 

 
 
 μ 1 i = ⋯ = μ r i = 0 , μ 1 i ^ = ⋯ = μ r i ^ = 0 , μ r + 1 j > 0 . \displaystyle\mu^{i}_{1}=\cdots=\mu^{i}_{r}=0,\qquad\mu^{\widehat{i}}_{1}=\cdots=\mu^{\widehat{i}}_{r}=0,\qquad\mu^{j}_{r+1}>0. 
 
 
 
 If r > 0 r>0 , then by ( 8.10 ) we would have c r j ^ = r ​ μ r + 1 j > 0 c^{\widehat{j}}_{r}=r\mu^{j}_{r+1}>0 , a contradiction. Therefore r = 0 r=0 , and therefore t ≥ 1 t\geq 1 . 



 
 First assume s = 0 s=0 . Then μ 1 i = 0 \mu^{i}_{1}=0 , and we have 

 

 
 
 0 ≥ c 1 i = μ 2 i ^ − μ 1 i = μ 2 i ^ , \displaystyle 0\geq c^{i}_{1}=\mu^{\widehat{i}}_{2}-\mu^{i}_{1}=\mu^{\widehat{i}}_{2}, 
 
 
 
 and thus μ 2 i ^ = 0 \mu^{\widehat{i}}_{2}=0 , so t ≤ 1 t\leq 1 , which implies that t = 1 t=1 , and we are done. 



 
 Next we assume s > 0 s>0 .
We have μ s i ^ > 0 \mu^{\widehat{i}}_{s}>0 and μ s + 1 i = 0 \mu^{i}_{s+1}=0 . Then by ( 8.12 ) we have 

 

 
 
 c s i ^ = c s − 1 i ^ + s ⁡ ( μ s + 1 i − μ s i ^ ) = c s − 1 i − s ​ μ s i ^ < c s − 1 i ^ ≤ 0 . \displaystyle c^{\widehat{i}}_{s}=c^{\widehat{i}}_{s-1}+s(\mu^{i}_{s+1}-\mu^{\widehat{i}}_{s})=c^{i}_{s-1}-s\mu^{\widehat{i}}_{s}<c^{\widehat{i}}_{s-1}\leq 0. 
 
 
 
 Thus c s i ^ < 0 c^{\widehat{i}}_{s}<0 , so c s i = 0 c^{i}_{s}=0 . Thus, noting that μ s i > 0 \mu^{i}_{s}>0 and c s − 1 i ≤ 0 c^{i}_{s-1}\leq 0 , we have by ( 8.12 ) that 

 

 
 
 0 = c s i = c s − 1 i + s ⁡ ( μ s + 1 i ^ − μ s i ) = c s − 1 i + s ​ μ s + 1 i ^ − s ​ μ s i < c s − 1 i + s ​ μ s + 1 i ^ ≤ s ​ μ s + 1 i ^ , \displaystyle 0=c^{i}_{s}=c^{i}_{s-1}+s(\mu^{\widehat{i}}_{s+1}-\mu^{i}_{s})=c^{i}_{s-1}+s\mu^{\widehat{i}}_{s+1}-s\mu^{i}_{s}<c^{i}_{s-1}+s\mu^{\widehat{i}}_{s+1}\leq s\mu^{\widehat{i}}_{s+1}, 
 
 
 
 which implies that μ s + 1 i ^ > 0 \mu^{\widehat{i}}_{s+1}>0 , so t ≥ s + 1 t\geq s+1 .
Next we have by ( 8.12 ) that 

 

 
 
 0 ≥ c s + 1 i = c s i + ( s + 1 ) ​ ( μ s + 2 i ^ − μ s + 1 i ) = ( s + 1 ) ​ μ s + 2 i ^ , \displaystyle 0\geq c^{i}_{s+1}=c^{i}_{s}+(s+1)(\mu^{\widehat{i}}_{s+2}-\mu^{i}_{s+1})=(s+1)\mu^{\widehat{i}}_{s+2}, 
 
 
 
 and so μ s + 2 i ^ = 0 \mu^{\widehat{i}}_{s+2}=0 . Thus t = s + 1 t=s+1 , completing the proof.
∎ 





 
 Lemma 8.17 . 

 
 Let 𝛍 ∈ 𝛀 MV \boldsymbol{\mu}\in\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\textup{MV}} be nontrivial. Then there exists i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{2} , M ∈ ℤ > 0 M\in\mathbbm{Z}_{>0} such that μ M i > 0 \mu^{i}_{M}>0 and μ m i ^ = 0 \mu^{\widehat{i}}_{m}=0 for all m ≥ M m\geq M . 





 
 Proof.

 
 By way of contradiction assume that there exists N ∈ ℤ > 0 N\in\mathbbm{Z}_{>0} such that μ N 1 , μ N 0 > 0 \mu^{1}_{N},\mu^{0}_{N}>0 and μ j = 0 \mu^{j}=0 for all m > N m>N . Since 𝝁 ∈ 𝛀 MV \boldsymbol{\mu}\in\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\textup{MV}} , we have c N − 1 1 ​ ( 𝝁 ) , c N − 1 0 ​ ( 𝝁 ) ≤ 0 c^{1}_{N-1}(\boldsymbol{\mu}),c^{0}_{N-1}(\boldsymbol{\mu})\leq 0 . Therefore: 

 

 
 
 c N 1 ​ ( 𝝁 ) \displaystyle c^{1}_{N}(\boldsymbol{\mu}) 
 = c N − 1 1 ​ ( 𝝁 ) + N ⁡ ( μ N + 1 0 − μ N 1 ) = c N − 1 1 ​ ( 𝝁 ) − N ​ μ N 1 < 0 ; \displaystyle=c^{1}_{N-1}(\boldsymbol{\mu})+N(\mu^{0}_{N+1}-\mu^{1}_{N})=c^{1}_{N-1}(\boldsymbol{\mu})-N\mu^{1}_{N}<0; 
 
 
 
 
 c N 0 ​ ( 𝝁 ) \displaystyle c^{0}_{N}(\boldsymbol{\mu}) 
 = c N − 1 0 ​ ( 𝝁 ) + N ⁡ ( μ N + 1 1 − μ N 0 ) = c N − 1 0 ​ ( 𝝁 ) − N ​ μ N 0 < 0 , \displaystyle=c^{0}_{N-1}(\boldsymbol{\mu})+N(\mu^{1}_{N+1}-\mu^{0}_{N})=c^{0}_{N-1}(\boldsymbol{\mu})-N\mu^{0}_{N}<0, 
 
 
 
 which contradicts the fact that 0 ∈ { c N 1 ​ ( 𝝁 ) , c N 0 ​ ( 𝝁 ) } 0\in\{c^{1}_{N}(\boldsymbol{\mu}),c^{0}_{N}(\boldsymbol{\mu})\} .
∎ 







 
 8.10. Diagonalization


 
 Let 𝝁 ∈ 𝛀 MV \boldsymbol{\mu}\in\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\textup{MV}} . For i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{2} , m ∈ ℤ ≥ 1 m\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{\geq 1} ,
if c m i ​ ( 𝝁 ) = 0 c^{i}_{m}(\boldsymbol{\mu})=0 , then β m + 1 i ^ − β m i = d m i ​ ( 𝝁 ) ​ α i ^ \beta^{\widehat{i}}_{m+1}-\beta^{i}_{m}=d^{i}_{m}(\boldsymbol{\mu})\alpha_{\widehat{i}} , and we refer to the line segment connecting the points β m i , β m + 1 i ^ \beta^{i}_{m},\beta^{\widehat{i}}_{m+1} as a diagonal .
A diagonalization of 𝝁 ∈ 𝛀 MV \boldsymbol{\mu}\in\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\textup{MV}} is a function
 ξ : ℤ ≥ 0 → ℤ 2 \xi:{\mathbbm{Z}}_{\geq 0}\rightarrow{\mathbbm{Z}}_{2} 
such that c m ξ ⁡ ( m ) ​ ( 𝝁 ) = 0 c^{\xi(m)}_{m}(\boldsymbol{\mu})=0 for all m ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 m\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{\geq 0} ; i.e. ξ \xi gives a specific choice of diagonal for each m ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 m\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{\geq 0} . By the definition of MV bicompositions, diagonalizations always exist.



 
 Given a diagonalization ξ \xi of 𝝁 \boldsymbol{\mu} , we refer to the portion of P ⁡ ( 𝝁 ) P(\boldsymbol{\mu}) between the ( m − 1 ) (m-1) th and m m th diagonals in ξ \xi as the m m th segment of ξ \xi . In other words, the m m th segment of ξ \xi is the convex hull of the points

 

 
 
 β m − 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) , β m ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ , β m ξ ⁡ ( m ) , β m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ , β m ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) . \displaystyle\beta^{\xi(m-1)}_{m-1},\beta^{\widehat{\xi(m-1)}}_{m},\beta^{\xi(m)}_{m},\beta^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}_{m+1},\beta^{\xi(m-1)}_{m}. 
 
 
 




 
 8.11. Downward diagonalization


 
 We inductively construct a specific ‘downward’ diagonalization ξ 𝝁 \xi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}} of 𝝁 ∈ 𝛀 MV \boldsymbol{\mu}\in\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\textup{MV}} as follows.
First, let M ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 M\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{\geq 0} be minimal such that μ k 1 = μ k 0 = 0 \mu^{1}_{k}=\mu^{0}_{k}=0 for all k ≥ M k\geq M . Note that for i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{2} and m ≥ M − 1 m\geq M-1 , we have

 

 
 (8.18) 
 
 c m 0 = c M − 2 0 − ( M − 1 ) ​ μ M − 1 0 and c m 1 = c M − 2 1 − ( M − 1 ) ​ μ M − 1 1 . \displaystyle c^{0}_{m}=c^{0}_{M-2}-(M-1)\mu^{0}_{M-1}\qquad\textup{and}\qquad c^{1}_{m}=c^{1}_{M-2}-(M-1)\mu^{1}_{M-1}. 
 
 
 
 Since at least one of c m 0 c^{0}_{m} , c m 1 c^{1}_{m} is equal to zero and c M − 2 i ≤ 0 c^{i}_{M-2}\leq 0 , it follows from the minimality of M M that μ M − 1 i = 0 \mu^{i}_{M-1}=0 for exactly one i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{2} . Letting i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{2} be such that μ M − 1 i = 0 \mu^{i}_{M-1}=0 and μ M − 1 i ^ > 0 \mu^{\widehat{i}}_{M-1}>0 , it follows from ( 8.18 ) that c m i ^ < 0 c^{\widehat{i}}_{m}<0 for all m ≥ M − 1 m\geq M-1 . Hence by the properties of MV bicompositions, we have that c m i = 0 c^{i}_{m}=0 for all m ≥ M − 1 m\geq M-1 . We therefore may construct a well-defined diagonalization ξ 𝝁 \xi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}} of 𝝁 \boldsymbol{\mu} by downward induction, setting:

 

 
 (8.19) 
 
 ξ 𝝁 ​ ( m ) = { i if  ​ m ≥ M − 1 ; ξ 𝝁 ​ ( m + 1 ) if  ​ m < M − 1 ​  and  ​ c m ξ 𝝁 ​ ( m + 1 ) = 0 ; ξ 𝝁 ​ ( m + 1 ) ^ if  ​ m < M − 1 ​  and  ​ c m ξ 𝝁 ​ ( m + 1 ) < 0 ; \displaystyle\xi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m)=\begin{cases}\;i&\textup{if }m\geq M-1;\\
\;\xi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m+1)&\textup{if }m<M-1\textup{ and }c^{\xi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m+1)}_{m}=0;\\
\;\widehat{\xi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m+1)}&\textup{if }m<M-1\textup{ and }c^{\xi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m+1)}_{m}<0;\\
\end{cases} 
 
 
 
 for all m ∈ ℤ ≥ 1 m\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{\geq 1} . This choice of diagonalization gives us some control over the structure of its segments, as noted in the next subsection.



 
 8.11.1. Typifying segments


 
 Let ξ \xi be a diagonalization of 𝝁 ∈ 𝛀 MV \boldsymbol{\mu}\in\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\textup{MV}} . We define a function

 

 
 
 Type ξ , 𝝁 : ℤ ≥ 1 → { A ∅ , A + , A − , B + , B − , Z } \displaystyle{\rm Type}_{\xi,\boldsymbol{\mu}}:{\mathbbm{Z}}_{\geq 1}\rightarrow\{\textup{A}_{\varnothing},\textup{A}_{+},\textup{A}_{-},\textup{B}_{+},\textup{B}_{-},\textup{Z}\} 
 
 
 
 given by setting

 

 
 
 Type ξ , 𝝁 ( m ) = { A ∅ if  ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) = ξ ⁡ ( m ) ,  μ m ξ ⁡ ( m ) = μ m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ = 0 , and  η m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m ) ​ ( 𝝁 ) , η m + 2 ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ ​ ( 𝝁 )  are even. A + if  ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) = ξ ⁡ ( m )  and  η m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m ) ​ ( 𝝁 ) , η m + 2 ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ ​ ( 𝝁 )  are odd. A − if  ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) = ξ ⁡ ( m )  and  η m ξ ⁡ ( m ) ​ ( 𝝁 ) , η m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ ​ ( 𝝁 )  are odd. B + if  ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) = ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ ,  c m − 1 ξ ⁡ ( m ) ​ ( 𝝁 ) ≠ 0 ,  η m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ ​ ( 𝝁 )  is odd and  η m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m ) ​ ( 𝝁 )  is even. B − if  ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) = ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ ,  c m − 1 ξ ⁡ ( m ) ​ ( 𝝁 ) ≠ 0 ,  μ m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ > 0 , and  η m ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ ​ ( 𝝁 ) ,  η m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m ) ​ ( 𝝁 )  are odd. Z otherwise , \displaystyle{\rm Type}_{\xi,\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m)=\begin{cases}\textup{A}_{\varnothing}&\textup{if $\xi(m-1)=\xi(m)$,
$\mu^{\xi(m)}_{m}=\mu^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}_{m+1}=0$, and $\eta^{\xi(m)}_{m+1}(\boldsymbol{\mu}),\eta^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}_{m+2}(\boldsymbol{\mu})$ are even.}\\
\textup{A}_{+}&\textup{if $\xi(m-1)=\xi(m)$ and $\eta^{\xi(m)}_{m+1}(\boldsymbol{\mu}),\eta^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}_{m+2}(\boldsymbol{\mu})$ are odd.}\\
\textup{A}_{-}&\textup{if $\xi(m-1)=\xi(m)$ and $\eta^{\xi(m)}_{m}(\boldsymbol{\mu}),\eta^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}_{m+1}(\boldsymbol{\mu})$ are odd.}\\
\textup{B}_{+}&\textup{if $\xi(m-1)=\widehat{\xi(m)}$, $c^{\xi(m)}_{m-1}(\boldsymbol{\mu})\neq 0$, $\eta^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}_{m+1}(\boldsymbol{\mu})$ is odd and $\eta^{\xi(m)}_{m+1}(\boldsymbol{\mu})$ is even.}\\
\textup{B}_{-}&\textup{if $\xi(m-1)=\widehat{\xi(m)}$, $c^{\xi(m)}_{m-1}(\boldsymbol{\mu})\neq 0$, $\mu^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}_{m+1}>0$, and $\eta^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}_{m}(\boldsymbol{\mu})$, $\eta^{\xi(m)}_{m+1}(\boldsymbol{\mu})$ are odd.}\\
\textup{Z}&\textup{otherwise},\end{cases} 
 
 
 
 and we refer to Type ξ , 𝝁 ​ ( m ) {\rm Type}_{\xi,\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m) as the (downward) type of the m m th segment of ξ \xi in 𝛍 \boldsymbol{\mu} .



 
 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 Figure 17. The polytope P ⁡ ( 𝝁 ) P(\boldsymbol{\mu}) associated to the MV bicomposition 𝝁 \boldsymbol{\mu} as considered in Example  8.18 . 
 
 
 Example 8.18 . 

 
 Consider the case of 𝝁 ∈ 𝛀 MV \boldsymbol{\mu}\in\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\textup{MV}} , with μ \mu given by:

 

 
 
 μ 1 1 = 1 ; μ 3 1 = 1 ; μ 4 1 = 1 ; μ 5 1 = 1 ; μ 1 0 = 3 ; μ 2 0 = 1 ; μ 4 0 = 1 , \displaystyle\mu^{1}_{1}=1;\quad\mu^{1}_{3}=1;\quad\mu^{1}_{4}=1;\quad\mu^{1}_{5}=1;\qquad\mu^{0}_{1}=3;\quad\mu^{0}_{2}=1;\quad\mu^{0}_{4}=1, 
 
 
 
 and μ m i = 0 \mu^{i}_{m}=0 otherwise. Then the polytope P ⁡ ( 𝝁 ) P(\boldsymbol{\mu}) which is the convex hull of the points β m i ​ ( 𝝁 ) \beta^{i}_{m}(\boldsymbol{\mu}) is shown in Figure  17 . Following § 8.11 , the downward diagonalization ξ 𝝁 \xi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}} gives us ξ 𝝁 ​ ( 0 ) = ξ 𝝁 ​ ( 1 ) = ξ 𝝁 ​ ( 2 ) = 1 \xi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(0)=\xi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(1)=\xi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(2)=1 and ξ 𝝁 ​ ( m ) = 0 \xi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m)=0 otherwise. The associated m m th diagonals—line segments connecting β m ξ 𝝁 ​ ( m ) \beta_{m}^{\xi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m)} and β m + 1 ξ 𝝁 ​ ( m ) ^ \beta_{m+1}^{\widehat{\xi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m)}} —are shown in red in the figure.
The (downward) types of the segments in the diagonalization ξ 𝝁 \xi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}} are given by

 

 
 
 Type ξ 𝝁 , 𝝁 ​ ( 1 ) = A − ; Type ξ 𝝁 , 𝝁 ​ ( 2 ) = A + ; Type ξ 𝝁 , 𝝁 ​ ( 3 ) = B − ; Type ξ 𝝁 , 𝝁 ​ ( 4 ) = A − ; Type ξ 𝝁 , 𝝁 ​ ( m ) = A ∅ , \displaystyle\textup{Type}_{\xi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}},\boldsymbol{\mu}}(1)=\textup{A}_{-};\,\,\,\textup{Type}_{\xi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}},\boldsymbol{\mu}}(2)=\textup{A}_{+};\,\,\,\textup{Type}_{\xi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}},\boldsymbol{\mu}}(3)=\textup{B}_{-};\,\,\,\textup{Type}_{\xi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}},\boldsymbol{\mu}}(4)=\textup{A}_{-};\,\,\,\textup{Type}_{\xi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}},\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m)=\textup{A}_{\varnothing}, 
 
 
 
 for m ≥ 5 m\geq 5 . The reader should compare these segment types against ( 8.20 ).





 
 Lemma 8.19 . 

 
 Let 𝛍 ∈ 𝛀 MV \boldsymbol{\mu}\in\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\textup{MV}} .
For all m ∈ ℤ ≥ 1 m\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{\geq 1} , we have Type ξ 𝛍 , 𝛍 ​ ( m ) ∈ { A ∅ , A + , A − , B + , B − } {\rm Type}_{\xi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}},\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m)\in\{\textup{A}_{\varnothing},\textup{A}_{+},\textup{A}_{-},\textup{B}_{+},\textup{B}_{-}\} . Moreover,
for all m ∈ ℤ ≥ 2 m\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{\geq 2} , we have that 

 

 
 
 ( Type ξ 𝝁 , 𝝁 ​ ( m − 1 ) , Type ξ 𝝁 , 𝝁 ​ ( m ) ) \displaystyle({\rm Type}_{\xi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}},\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m-1),{\rm Type}_{\xi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}},\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m)) 
 
 
 
 is equal to one of 

 

 
 
 
 { ( A ∅ , A ∅ ) , ( A − , A ∅ ) , ( B + , A ∅ ) , ( A ∅ , A + ) , ( A − , A + ) , ( B + , A + ) , ( A ∅ , B + ) , \displaystyle\{(\textup{A}_{\varnothing},\textup{A}_{\varnothing}),(\textup{A}_{-},\textup{A}_{\varnothing}),(\textup{B}_{+},\textup{A}_{\varnothing}),(\textup{A}_{\varnothing},\textup{A}_{+}),(\textup{A}_{-},\textup{A}_{+}),(\textup{B}_{+},\textup{A}_{+}),(\textup{A}_{\varnothing},\textup{B}_{+}), 
 
 
 
 (8.20) 
 
 
 ( A − , B + ) , ( B + , B + ) , ( A + , A − ) , ( B − , A − ) , ( A + , B − ) , ( B − , B − ) } . \displaystyle\qquad\;\;\;(\textup{A}_{-},\textup{B}_{+}),(\textup{B}_{+},\textup{B}_{+}),(\textup{A}_{+},\textup{A}_{-}),(\textup{B}_{-},\textup{A}_{-}),(\textup{A}_{+},\textup{B}_{-}),(\textup{B}_{-},\textup{B}_{-})\}. 
 
 
 




 
 Proof.

 
 Write ξ = ξ 𝝁 \xi=\xi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}} .
By construction of the diagonalization ξ \xi , it is immediate that Type ξ , 𝝁 ​ ( m ) = A ∅ {\rm Type}_{\xi,\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m)=\textup{A}_{\varnothing} 
for all m ≥ M − 1 m\geq M-1 . Now assume that m ∈ ℤ ≥ 2 m\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{\geq 2} and Type ξ , 𝝁 ​ ( m ) ∈ { A ∅ , A + , A − , B + , B − } {\rm Type}_{\xi,\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m)\in\{\textup{A}_{\varnothing},\textup{A}_{+},\textup{A}_{-},\textup{B}_{+},\textup{B}_{-}\} . We prove accordingly that Type ξ , 𝝁 ​ ( m − 1 ) ∈ { A ∅ , A + , A − , B + , B − } {\rm Type}_{\xi,\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m-1)\in\{\textup{A}_{\varnothing},\textup{A}_{+},\textup{A}_{-},\textup{B}_{+},\textup{B}_{-}\} , and satisfies ( 8.20 ). We consider the possibilities 

 

 
 
 μ m − 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) = μ m ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ = 0 , μ m − 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) = μ m ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ > 0 , μ m − 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ≠ μ m ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ \displaystyle\mu^{\xi(m-1)}_{m-1}=\mu^{\widehat{\xi(m-1)}}_{m}=0,\qquad\mu^{\xi(m-1)}_{m-1}=\mu^{\widehat{\xi(m-1)}}_{m}>0,\qquad\mu^{\xi(m-1)}_{m-1}\neq\mu^{\widehat{\xi(m-1)}}_{m} 
 
 
 
 as three individual cases. 



 
 Case 1. Assume that μ m − 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) = μ m ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ = 0 \mu^{\xi(m-1)}_{m-1}=\mu^{\widehat{\xi(m-1)}}_{m}=0 . Then by ( 8.12 ) we have c m − 2 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) = c m − 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) = 0 c^{\xi(m-1)}_{m-2}=c^{\xi(m-1)}_{m-1}=0 , and thus by ( 8.19 ) we have ξ ⁡ ( m − 2 ) = ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) \xi(m-2)={\xi(m-1)} . Now, note that

 

 
 
 η m ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) = { 1 + η m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) if  ​ μ m ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) > 0 0 if  ​ μ m ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) = 0 and η m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ = { 1 + η m + 2 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ if  ​ μ m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ > 0 0 if  ​ μ m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ = 0 . \displaystyle\eta^{\xi(m-1)}_{m}=\begin{cases}1+\eta^{\xi(m-1)}_{m+1}&\textup{if }\mu^{\xi(m-1)}_{m}>0\\
0&\textup{if }\mu^{\xi(m-1)}_{m}=0\end{cases}\qquad\textup{and}\qquad\eta^{\widehat{\xi(m-1)}}_{m+1}=\begin{cases}1+\eta^{\widehat{\xi(m-1)}}_{m+2}&\textup{if }\mu^{\widehat{\xi(m-1)}}_{m+1}>0\\
0&\textup{if }\mu^{\widehat{\xi(m-1)}}_{m+1}=0.\end{cases} 
 
 
 
 We consider some subcases:

 
 (1a) 
 
 If Type ξ , 𝝁 ​ ( m ) = A ∅ {\rm Type}_{\xi,\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m)=\textup{A}_{\varnothing} , then we have that μ m ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) = μ m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ = 0 \mu^{\xi(m-1)}_{m}=\mu^{\widehat{\xi(m-1)}}_{m+1}=0 , so η m ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) = η m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ = 0 \eta^{\xi(m-1)}_{m}=\eta^{\widehat{\xi(m-1)}}_{m+1}=0 , and thus Type ξ , 𝝁 ​ ( m − 1 ) = A ∅ {\rm Type}_{\xi,\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m-1)=\textup{A}_{\varnothing} .




 (1b) 
 
 If Type ξ , 𝝁 ​ ( m ) = A + {\rm Type}_{\xi,\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m)=\textup{A}_{+} , then we have that η m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) \eta^{\xi(m-1)}_{m+1} , η m + 2 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ \eta^{\widehat{\xi(m-1)}}_{m+2} are odd, and therefore in any case η m ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) , η m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ \eta^{\xi(m-1)}_{m},\eta^{\widehat{\xi(m-1)}}_{m+1} are even, so Type ξ , 𝝁 ​ ( m − 1 ) = A ∅ {\rm Type}_{\xi,\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m-1)=\textup{A}_{\varnothing} .




 (1c) 
 
 If the Type ξ , 𝝁 ​ ( m ) = B + {\rm Type}_{\xi,\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m)=\textup{B}_{+} , then we have that η m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) \eta^{\xi(m-1)}_{m+1} is odd and η m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ \eta^{\widehat{\xi(m-1)}}_{m+1} is even. Thus η m ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) \eta^{\xi(m-1)}_{m} is even in any case, and so Type ξ , 𝝁 ​ ( m − 1 ) = A ∅ {\rm Type}_{\xi,\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m-1)=\textup{A}_{\varnothing} .




 (1d) 
 
 If Type ξ , 𝝁 ​ ( m ) ∈ { A − , B − } {\rm Type}_{\xi,\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m)\in\{\textup{A}_{-},\textup{B}_{-}\} , then we have that η m ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) , η m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ \eta^{\xi(m-1)}_{m},\eta^{\widehat{\xi(m-1)}}_{m+1} are odd, which implies that
 Type ξ , 𝝁 ​ ( m − 1 ) = A + {\rm Type}_{\xi,\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m-1)=\textup{A}_{+} .




 


 
 Case 2. Assume that μ m − 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) = μ m ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ > 0 \mu^{\xi(m-1)}_{m-1}=\mu^{\widehat{\xi(m-1)}}_{m}>0 . Then by ( 8.12 ) we have c m − 2 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) = c m − 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) = 0 c^{\xi(m-1)}_{m-2}=c^{\xi(m-1)}_{m-1}=0 , and thus by ( 8.19 ) we have ξ ⁡ ( m − 2 ) = ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) \xi(m-2)={\xi(m-1)} . Now, note that

 

 
 
 η m − 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) = { 2 + η m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) if  ​ μ m ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) > 0 1 if  ​ μ m ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) = 0 and η m ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ = { 2 + η m + 2 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ if  ​ μ m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ > 0 1 if  ​ μ m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ = 0 \displaystyle\eta^{\xi(m-1)}_{m-1}=\begin{cases}2+\eta^{\xi(m-1)}_{m+1}&\textup{if }\mu^{\xi(m-1)}_{m}>0\\
1&\textup{if }\mu^{\xi(m-1)}_{m}=0\end{cases}\qquad\textup{and}\qquad\eta^{\widehat{\xi(m-1)}}_{m}=\begin{cases}2+\eta^{\widehat{\xi(m-1)}}_{m+2}&\textup{if }\mu^{\widehat{\xi(m-1)}}_{m+1}>0\\
1&\textup{if }\mu^{\widehat{\xi(m-1)}}_{m+1}=0\end{cases} 
 
 
 
 We consider some subcases:

 
 (2a) 
 
 If Type ξ , 𝝁 ​ ( m ) = A ∅ {\rm Type}_{\xi,\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m)=\textup{A}_{\varnothing} , then we have that μ m ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) = μ m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ = 0 \mu^{\xi(m-1)}_{m}=\mu^{\widehat{\xi(m-1)}}_{m+1}=0 , so η m − 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) = η m ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ = 1 \eta^{\xi(m-1)}_{m-1}=\eta^{\widehat{\xi(m-1)}}_{m}=1 , and thus
 Type ξ , 𝝁 ​ ( m − 1 ) = A − {\rm Type}_{\xi,\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m-1)=\textup{A}_{-} .




 (2b) 
 
 If Type ξ , 𝝁 ​ ( m ) = A + {\rm Type}_{\xi,\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m)=\textup{A}_{+} , then we have that η m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) \eta^{\xi(m-1)}_{m+1} , η m + 2 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ \eta^{\widehat{\xi(m-1)}}_{m+2} are odd, and therefore in any case η m − 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) , η m ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ \eta^{\xi(m-1)}_{m-1},\eta^{\widehat{\xi(m-1)}}_{m} are odd, so
 Type ξ , 𝝁 ​ ( m − 1 ) = A − {\rm Type}_{\xi,\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m-1)=\textup{A}_{-} .




 (2c) 
 
 If
 Type ξ , 𝝁 ​ ( m ) = B + {\rm Type}_{\xi,\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m)=\textup{B}_{+} , then we have that η m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) \eta^{\xi(m-1)}_{m+1} is odd and η m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ \eta^{\widehat{\xi(m-1)}}_{m+1} is even. Thus either μ m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ = 0 \mu^{\widehat{\xi(m-1)}}_{m+1}=0 or η m + 2 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ \eta^{\widehat{\xi(m-1)}}_{m+2} is odd. Thus η m − 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) , η m ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ \eta^{\xi(m-1)}_{m-1},\eta^{\widehat{\xi(m-1)}}_{m} are odd in any case, and so
 Type ξ , 𝝁 ​ ( m − 1 ) = A − {\rm Type}_{\xi,\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m-1)=\textup{A}_{-} .




 (2d) 
 
 Type ξ , 𝝁 ​ ( m ) ∈ { A − , B − } {\rm Type}_{\xi,\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m)\in\{\textup{A}_{-},\textup{B}_{-}\} , then we have that η m ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) , η m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ \eta^{\xi(m-1)}_{m},\eta^{\widehat{\xi(m-1)}}_{m+1} are odd, which implies that Type ξ , 𝝁 ​ ( m − 1 ) = A + {\rm Type}_{\xi,\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m-1)=\textup{A}_{+} 




 


 
 Case 3. Assume that μ m − 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ≠ μ m ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ \mu^{\xi(m-1)}_{m-1}\neq\mu^{\widehat{\xi(m-1)}}_{m} . Then by ( 8.12 ) we have c m − 2 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ≠ 0 c^{\xi(m-1)}_{m-2}\neq 0 . Thus, since 𝝁 ∈ 𝛀 MV \boldsymbol{\mu}\in\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\textup{MV}} we have that c m − 2 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) < 0 c^{\xi(m-1)}_{m-2}<0 , so c m − 2 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ = 0 c^{\widehat{\xi(m-1)}}_{m-2}=0 and μ m ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ > μ m − 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ≥ 0 \mu^{\widehat{\xi(m-1)}}_{m}>\mu^{\xi(m-1)}_{m-1}\geq 0 . By ( 8.19 ) we have ξ ⁡ ( m − 2 ) = ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ \xi(m-2)=\widehat{\xi(m-1)} . Note that

 

 
 
 η m ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ = { 2 + η m + 2 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ if  ​ μ m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ > 0 1 if  ​ μ m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ = 0 and η m ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) = { 1 + η m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) if  ​ μ m ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) > 0 0 if  ​ μ m ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) = 0 . \displaystyle\eta^{\widehat{\xi(m-1)}}_{m}=\begin{cases}2+\eta_{m+2}^{\widehat{\xi(m-1)}}&\textup{if }\mu_{m+1}^{\widehat{\xi(m-1)}}>0\\
1&\textup{if }\mu_{m+1}^{\widehat{\xi(m-1)}}=0\end{cases}\qquad\textup{and}\qquad\eta^{\xi(m-1)}_{m}=\begin{cases}1+\eta_{m+1}^{\xi(m-1)}&\textup{if }\mu_{m}^{\xi(m-1)}>0\\
0&\textup{if }\mu_{m}^{\xi(m-1)}=0.\end{cases} 
 
 
 
 We again consider some subcases:

 
 (3a) 
 
 If Type ξ , 𝝁 ​ ( m ) = A ∅ {\rm Type}_{\xi,\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m)=\textup{A}_{\varnothing} , then
 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) = ξ ⁡ ( m ) \xi(m-1)=\xi(m) and
 μ m ξ ⁡ ( m ) = μ m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ = 0 \mu^{\xi(m)}_{m}=\mu^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}_{m+1}=0 . We have that η m ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ = 1 \eta_{m}^{\widehat{\xi(m-1)}}=1 and η m ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) = 0 \eta^{\xi(m-1)}_{m}=0 , so
 Type ξ , 𝝁 ​ ( m − 1 ) = B + {\rm Type}_{\xi,\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m-1)=\textup{B}_{+} .




 (3b) 
 
 If
 Type ξ , 𝝁 ​ ( m ) = A + {\rm Type}_{\xi,\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m)=\textup{A}_{+} , then
 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) = ξ ⁡ ( m ) \xi(m-1)=\xi(m) and η m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m ) , η m + 2 ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ \eta^{\xi(m)}_{m+1},\eta^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}_{m+2} are odd. Then in any case we have that η m ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ \eta^{\widehat{\xi(m-1)}}_{m} is odd, and η m ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) \eta^{\xi(m-1)}_{m} is even, so
 Type ξ , 𝝁 ​ ( m − 1 ) = B + {\rm Type}_{\xi,\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m-1)=\textup{B}_{+} .




 (3c) 
 
 If
 Type ξ , 𝝁 ​ ( m ) = A − {\rm Type}_{\xi,\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m)=\textup{A}_{-} , then
 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) = ξ ⁡ ( m ) \xi(m-1)=\xi(m) and η m ξ ⁡ ( m ) , η m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ \eta^{\xi(m)}_{m},\eta^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}_{m+1} are odd.
Since η m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ \eta_{m+1}^{\widehat{\xi(m-1)}} is odd, it follows that μ m ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) , μ m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ > 0 \mu_{m}^{\xi(m-1)},\mu_{m+1}^{\widehat{\xi(m-1)}}>0 and η m + 2 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ \eta_{m+2}^{\widehat{\xi(m-1)}} is even. Moreover, we have

 

 
 
 0 ≥ c m − 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ = c m − 2 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ + ( m − 1 ) ​ ( μ m ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) − μ m − 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ ) = ( m − 1 ) ​ ( μ m ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) − μ m − 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ ) , \displaystyle 0\geq c^{\widehat{\xi(m-1)}}_{m-1}=c^{\widehat{\xi(m-1)}}_{m-2}+(m-1)(\mu_{m}^{\xi(m-1)}-\mu_{m-1}^{\widehat{\xi(m-1)}})=(m-1)(\mu_{m}^{\xi(m-1)}-\mu_{m-1}^{\widehat{\xi(m-1)}}), 
 
 
 
 which implies that μ m − 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ ≥ μ m ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) > 0 \mu_{m-1}^{\widehat{\xi(m-1)}}\geq\mu_{m}^{\xi(m-1)}>0 . Thus η m − 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ = 1 + η m ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ \eta_{m-1}^{\widehat{\xi(m-1)}}=1+\eta_{m}^{\widehat{\xi(m-1)}} is odd.
Therefore
 Type ξ , 𝝁 ​ ( m − 1 ) = B − {\rm Type}_{\xi,\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m-1)=\textup{B}_{-} .




 (3d) 
 
 If
 Type ξ , 𝝁 ​ ( m ) = B + {\rm Type}_{\xi,\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m)=\textup{B}_{+} , we have that ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) = ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ \xi(m-1)=\widehat{\xi(m)} , c m − 1 ξ ⁡ ( m ) ≠ 0 c^{\xi(m)}_{m-1}\neq 0 , η m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ \eta^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}_{m+1} is odd and η m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m ) \eta^{\xi(m)}_{m+1} is even. Since η m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ \eta^{\widehat{\xi(m-1)}}_{m+1} is even, either μ m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ = 0 \mu_{m+1}^{\widehat{\xi(m-1)}}=0 or η m + 2 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ \eta^{\widehat{\xi(m-1)}}_{m+2} is odd. In any case then it follows that η m ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ \eta^{\widehat{\xi(m-1)}}_{m} is odd and η m ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) \eta_{m}^{\xi(m-1)} is even. Therefore
 Type ξ , 𝝁 ​ ( m − 1 ) = B + {\rm Type}_{\xi,\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m-1)=\textup{B}_{+} 




 (3e) 
 
 If
 Type ξ , 𝝁 ​ ( m ) = B − {\rm Type}_{\xi,\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m)=\textup{B}_{-} , we have that ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) = ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ \xi(m-1)=\widehat{\xi(m)} , c m − 1 ξ ⁡ ( m ) ≠ 0 c^{\xi(m)}_{m-1}\neq 0 , μ m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ > 0 \mu^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}_{m+1}>0 , and η m ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ \eta^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}_{m} , η m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m ) \eta^{\xi(m)}_{m+1} are odd.
Since η m ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ \eta_{m}^{\widehat{\xi(m)}} is odd, we have that μ m ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ = μ m ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) > 0 \mu_{m}^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}=\mu_{m}^{\xi(m-1)}>0 .
Moreover, we have

 

 
 
 0 ≥ c m − 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ = c m − 2 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ + ( m − 1 ) ​ ( μ m ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) − μ m − 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ ) = ( m − 1 ) ​ ( μ m ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) − μ m − 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ ) , \displaystyle 0\geq c^{\widehat{\xi(m-1)}}_{m-1}=c^{\widehat{\xi(m-1)}}_{m-2}+(m-1)(\mu_{m}^{\xi(m-1)}-\mu_{m-1}^{\widehat{\xi(m-1)}})=(m-1)(\mu_{m}^{\xi(m-1)}-\mu_{m-1}^{\widehat{\xi(m-1)}}), 
 
 
 
 which implies that μ m − 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ ≥ μ m ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) > 0 \mu_{m-1}^{\widehat{\xi(m-1)}}\geq\mu_{m}^{\xi(m-1)}>0 . Moreover, since μ m ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ > 0 \mu_{m}^{\widehat{\xi(m-1)}}>0 , we have that η m − 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ = 2 + η m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ^ = 2 + η m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m ) \eta^{\widehat{\xi(m-1)}}_{m-1}=2+\eta^{\widehat{\xi(m-1)}}_{m+1}=2+\eta^{\xi(m)}_{m+1} is odd. Therefore
 Type ξ , 𝝁 ​ ( m − 1 ) = B − {\rm Type}_{\xi,\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m-1)=\textup{B}_{-} .




 


 
 Thus in every case we have that ( Type ξ , 𝝁 ​ ( m − 1 ) , Type ξ , 𝝁 ​ ( m ) ) ({\rm Type}_{\xi,\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m-1),{\rm Type}_{\xi,\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m)) is in the set ( 8.20 ), completing the proof.
∎ 









 
 8.12. The ℋ \mathcal{H} map and MV bicompositions


 
 The downward diagonalization (and associated control over segment types), allows us to prove the following key result, working on a ‘segment-by-segment’ basis.



 
 Lemma 8.20 . 

 
 The map ℋ \mathcal{H} restricts to a map of MV bicompositions ℋ : 𝛀 ≥ 1 , + MV → 𝛀 ≥ 0 , ∙ MV \mathcal{H}:\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\textup{MV}}_{\geq 1,+}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\textup{MV}}_{\geq 0,\bullet} . Moreover, if 𝛍 ∈ 𝛀 ≥ 1 , + MV \boldsymbol{\mu}\in\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\textup{MV}}_{\geq 1,+} , then ξ 𝛍 \xi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}} is a diagonalization of 𝛎 = ℋ ⁡ ( 𝛍 ) \boldsymbol{\nu}=\mathcal{H}(\boldsymbol{\mu}) , and: 

 

 
 (8.21) 
 
 d m − 1 ξ 𝝁 ​ ( m − 1 ) ​ ( 𝝂 ) \displaystyle d^{\xi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m-1)}_{m-1}(\boldsymbol{\nu}) 
 = { d m − 1 ξ 𝝁 ​ ( m − 1 ) ​ ( 𝝁 ) − 1 if  ​ Type ξ 𝝁 , 𝝁 ​ ( m ) ∈ { A ∅ , A + , B + } d m − 1 ξ 𝝁 ​ ( m − 1 ) ​ ( 𝝁 ) + 1 if  ​ Type ξ 𝝁 , 𝝁 ​ ( m ) ∈ { A − , B − } \displaystyle=\begin{cases}d^{\xi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m-1)}_{m-1}(\boldsymbol{\mu})-1&\textup{if }{\rm Type}_{\xi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}},\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m)\in\{\textup{A}_{\varnothing},\textup{A}_{+},\textup{B}_{+}\}\\
d^{\xi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m-1)}_{m-1}(\boldsymbol{\mu})+1&\textup{if }{\rm Type}_{\xi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}},\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m)\in\{\textup{A}_{-},\textup{B}_{-}\}\\
\end{cases} 
 
 
 
 (8.22) 
 
 d m ξ 𝝁 ​ ( m ) ​ ( 𝝂 ) \displaystyle d^{\xi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m)}_{m}(\boldsymbol{\nu}) 
 = { d m ξ 𝝁 ​ ( m ) ​ ( 𝝁 ) − 1 if  ​ Type ξ 𝝁 , 𝝁 ​ ( m ) ∈ { A ∅ , A − , B + } d m ξ 𝝁 ​ ( m ) ​ ( 𝝁 ) + 1 if  ​ Type ξ 𝝁 , 𝝁 ​ ( m ) ∈ { A + , B − } . \displaystyle=\begin{cases}d^{\xi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m)}_{m}(\boldsymbol{\mu})-1&\textup{if }{\rm Type}_{\xi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}},\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m)\in\{\textup{A}_{\varnothing},\textup{A}_{-},\textup{B}_{+}\}\\
d^{\xi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m)}_{m}(\boldsymbol{\mu})+1&\textup{if }{\rm Type}_{\xi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}},\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m)\in\{\textup{A}_{+},\textup{B}_{-}\}.\\
\end{cases} 
 
 
 




 
 Proof.

 
 Let 𝝁 ∈ 𝛀 ≥ 1 , + MV \boldsymbol{\mu}\in\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\textup{MV}}_{\geq 1,+} .
We will write ξ := ξ 𝝁 \xi:=\xi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}} . For i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{2} , m ∈ ℤ ≥ 1 m\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{\geq 1} , the value ν m i \nu^{i}_{m} is directly related to the type of the segment in the diagonalization ξ \xi of 𝝁 \boldsymbol{\mu} which the edge corresponding to μ m i \mu^{i}_{m} belongs to. Unpacking the definitions in § 8.11.1 alongside ( 8.2 ), we have the following facts, which we will use repeatedly: 

 
 (i) 
 
 If Type ξ , 𝝁 ​ ( m ) = A ∅ {\rm Type}_{\xi,\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m)=\textup{A}_{\varnothing} then ν m ξ ⁡ ( m ) = ν m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ = 0 \nu^{\xi(m)}_{m}=\nu^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}_{m+1}=0 ;




 (ii) 
 
 If Type ξ , 𝝁 ​ ( m ) = A + {\rm Type}_{\xi,\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m)=\textup{A}_{+} then ν m ξ ⁡ ( m ) = μ m ξ ⁡ ( m ) + 1 \nu^{\xi(m)}_{m}=\mu^{\xi(m)}_{m}+1 and ν m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ = μ m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ + 1 \nu^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}_{m+1}=\mu^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}_{m+1}+1 ;




 (iii) 
 
 If Type ξ , 𝝁 ​ ( m ) = A − {\rm Type}_{\xi,\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m)=\textup{A}_{-} then ν m ξ ⁡ ( m ) = μ m ξ ⁡ ( m ) − 1 \nu^{\xi(m)}_{m}=\mu^{\xi(m)}_{m}-1 and ν m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ = μ m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ − 1 \nu^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}_{m+1}=\mu^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}_{m+1}-1 ;




 (iv) 
 
 If Type ξ , 𝝁 ​ ( m ) = B + {\rm Type}_{\xi,\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m)=\textup{B}_{+} then ν m ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ = μ m ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ + 1 \nu^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}_{m}=\mu^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}_{m}+1 and ν m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ = μ m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ − 1 \nu^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}_{m+1}=\mu^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}_{m+1}-1 ;




 (v) 
 
 If Type ξ , 𝝁 ​ ( m ) = B − {\rm Type}_{\xi,\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m)=\textup{B}_{-} then ν m ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ = μ m ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ − 1 \nu^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}_{m}=\mu^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}_{m}-1 and ν m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ = μ m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ + 1 \nu^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}_{m+1}=\mu^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}_{m+1}+1 .




 
 We proceed via a number of claims.
 



 
 Claim I: Let m ∈ ℤ ≥ 1 m\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{\geq 1} , and set m ′ = m + 1 m^{\prime}=m+1 . If ( 8.22 ) holds for m m , then ( 8.21 ) holds for m ′ m^{\prime} . 
We have from Lemma  8.19 that Type ξ , 𝝁 ​ ( m ′ ) ∈ { A ∅ , A + , B + } {\rm Type}_{\xi,\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m^{\prime})\in\{\textup{A}_{\varnothing},\textup{A}_{+},\textup{B}_{+}\} if and only if Type ξ , 𝝁 ​ ( m ) ∈ { A ∅ , A − , B + } {\rm Type}_{\xi,\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m)\in\{\textup{A}_{\varnothing},\textup{A}_{-},\textup{B}_{+}\} , and Type ξ , 𝝁 ​ ( m ′ ) ∈ { A − , B − } {\rm Type}_{\xi,\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m^{\prime})\in\{\textup{A}_{-},\textup{B}_{-}\} if and only if Type ξ , 𝝁 ​ ( m ) ∈ { A + , B − } {\rm Type}_{\xi,\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m)\in\{\textup{A}_{+},\textup{B}_{-}\} , which immediately implies the claim.
