 Claim II: The equalities c m − 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ​ ( 𝛎 ) = 0 c_{m-1}^{\xi(m-1)}(\boldsymbol{\nu})=0 , and ( 8.21 ) hold when m = 1 m=1 . 
The first equality is obvious, since c 0 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ​ ( 𝝂 ) c_{0}^{\xi(m-1)}(\boldsymbol{\nu}) is definitionally zero.
Since c 0 i ​ ( 𝝁 ) = 0 c^{i}_{0}(\boldsymbol{\mu})=0 for both i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{2} , it follows from ( 8.19 ) and Lemma  8.19 that ξ ⁡ ( 0 ) = ξ ⁡ ( 1 ) \xi(0)=\xi(1) and Type ξ , 𝝁 ​ ( 1 ) ∈ { A ∅ , A + , A − } {\rm Type}_{\xi,\boldsymbol{\mu}}(1)\in\{\textup{A}_{\varnothing},\textup{A}_{+},\textup{A}_{-}\} . If the type is A ∅ \textup{A}_{\varnothing} or A + \textup{A}_{+} then η 1 ξ ⁡ ( 1 ) \eta_{1}^{\xi(1)} is even, so by Lemma  8.16 , η 1 ξ ⁡ ( 1 ) ^ \eta_{1}^{\widehat{\xi(1)}} is odd. Thus

 

 
 
 d 0 ξ ⁡ ( 0 ) ​ ( 𝝂 ) \displaystyle d_{0}^{\xi(0)}(\boldsymbol{\nu}) 
 = ν 1 ξ ⁡ ( 1 ) ^ = μ 1 ξ ⁡ ( 1 ) ^ − 1 = d 0 ξ ⁡ ( 0 ) ​ ( 𝝁 ) − 1 . \displaystyle=\nu^{\widehat{\xi(1)}}_{1}=\mu^{\widehat{\xi(1)}}_{1}-1=d_{0}^{\xi(0)}(\boldsymbol{\mu})-1. 
 
 
 
 On the other hand, if Type ξ , 𝝁 ​ ( 1 ) = A − {\rm Type}_{\xi,\boldsymbol{\mu}}(1)=\textup{A}_{-} , then η 1 ξ ⁡ ( 1 ) \eta_{1}^{\xi(1)} is odd, so by Lemma  8.16 , η 1 ξ ⁡ ( 1 ) ^ \eta_{1}^{\widehat{\xi(1)}} is even. Thus

 

 
 
 d 0 ξ ⁡ ( 0 ) ​ ( 𝝂 ) \displaystyle d_{0}^{\xi(0)}(\boldsymbol{\nu}) 
 = ν 1 ξ ⁡ ( 1 ) ^ = μ 1 ξ ⁡ ( 1 ) ^ + 1 = d 0 ξ ⁡ ( 0 ) ​ ( 𝝁 ) + 1 . \displaystyle=\nu^{\widehat{\xi(1)}}_{1}=\mu^{\widehat{\xi(1)}}_{1}+1=d_{0}^{\xi(0)}(\boldsymbol{\mu})+1. 
 
 
 
 Thus we have verified Claim II.
 



 
 Claim III: If c m − 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ​ ( 𝛎 ) = 0 c^{\xi(m-1)}_{m-1}(\boldsymbol{\nu})=0 and ( 8.21 ) hold for a given m ∈ ℤ ≥ 1 m\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{\geq 1} , then c m ξ ⁡ ( m ) ​ ( 𝛎 ) = 0 c^{\xi(m)}_{m}(\boldsymbol{\nu})=0 and ( 8.22 ) both hold for m m . 
Assume that c m − 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ​ ( 𝝂 ) = 0 c^{\xi(m-1)}_{m-1}(\boldsymbol{\nu})=0 and ( 8.21 ) hold. We have by Lemma  8.19 that Type ξ 𝝁 , 𝝁 ​ ( m ) ∈ { A ∅ , A + , A − , B + , B − } {\rm Type}_{\xi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}},\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m)\in\{\textup{A}_{\varnothing},\textup{A}_{+},\textup{A}_{-},\textup{B}_{+},\textup{B}_{-}\} . We consider these cases separately.



 
 Case 1. Assume
 Type ξ , 𝛍 ​ ( m ) = A ∅ {\rm Type}_{\xi,\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m)=\textup{A}_{\varnothing} . Then ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) = ξ ⁡ ( m ) \xi(m-1)=\xi(m) , so in view of ( 8.21 ) and (i) above we have

 

 
 
 ν m ξ ⁡ ( m ) = 0 ν m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ = 0 d m − 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ​ ( 𝝂 ) = d m − 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ​ ( 𝝁 ) − 1 \displaystyle\nu_{m}^{\xi(m)}=0\qquad\nu_{m+1}^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}=0\qquad d_{m-1}^{\xi(m-1)}(\boldsymbol{\nu})=d_{m-1}^{\xi(m-1)}(\boldsymbol{\mu})-1 
 
 
 
 so in consideration of ( 8.12 , 8.13 ) we have

 

 
 
 c m ξ ⁡ ( m ) ​ ( 𝝂 ) \displaystyle c_{m}^{\xi(m)}(\boldsymbol{\nu}) 
 = c m − 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ​ ( 𝝂 ) + m ⁡ ( ν m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ − ν m ξ ⁡ ( m ) ) = 0 \displaystyle=c_{m-1}^{\xi(m-1)}(\boldsymbol{\nu})+m(\nu_{m+1}^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}-\nu_{m}^{\xi(m)})=0 
 
 
 
 
 d m ξ ⁡ ( m ) ​ ( 𝝂 ) \displaystyle d_{m}^{\xi(m)}(\boldsymbol{\nu}) 
 = d m − 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ​ ( 𝝂 ) − ( m − 1 ) ​ ν m ξ ⁡ ( m ) + ( m + 1 ) ​ ν m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ \displaystyle=d_{m-1}^{\xi(m-1)}(\boldsymbol{\nu})-(m-1)\nu^{\xi(m)}_{m}+(m+1)\nu^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}_{m+1} 
 
 
 
 
 
 = d m − 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ​ ( 𝝁 ) − 1 + ( m − 1 ) ​ μ m ξ ⁡ ( m ) − ( m + 1 ) ​ μ m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ = d m ξ ⁡ ( m ) ​ ( 𝝁 ) − 1 . \displaystyle=d_{m-1}^{\xi(m-1)}(\boldsymbol{\mu})-1+(m-1)\mu^{\xi(m)}_{m}-(m+1)\mu^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}_{m+1}=d_{m}^{\xi(m)}(\boldsymbol{\mu})-1. 
 
 
 


 
 Case 2. Assume
 Type ξ , 𝛍 ​ ( m ) = A + {\rm Type}_{\xi,\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m)=\textup{A}_{+} . Then ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) = ξ ⁡ ( m ) \xi(m-1)=\xi(m) , so in view of ( 8.21 ) and (ii) above we have

 

 
 
 ν m ξ ⁡ ( m ) = μ m ξ ⁡ ( m ) + 1 ν m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ = μ m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ + 1 d m − 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ​ ( 𝝂 ) = d m − 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ​ ( 𝝁 ) − 1 \displaystyle\nu_{m}^{\xi(m)}=\mu_{m}^{\xi(m)}+1\qquad\nu_{m+1}^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}=\mu_{m+1}^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}+1\qquad d_{m-1}^{\xi(m-1)}(\boldsymbol{\nu})=d_{m-1}^{\xi(m-1)}(\boldsymbol{\mu})-1 
 
 
 
 so in consideration of ( 8.12 , 8.13 ) we have

 

 
 
 c m ξ ⁡ ( m ) ​ ( 𝝂 ) \displaystyle c_{m}^{\xi(m)}(\boldsymbol{\nu}) 
 = c m − 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ​ ( 𝝂 ) + m ⁡ ( ν m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ − ν m ξ ⁡ ( m ) ) = c m − 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ​ ( 𝝁 ) + m ⁡ ( μ m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ − μ m ξ ⁡ ( m ) ) = c m ξ ⁡ ( m ) ​ ( 𝝁 ) = 0 \displaystyle=c_{m-1}^{\xi(m-1)}(\boldsymbol{\nu})+m(\nu_{m+1}^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}-\nu_{m}^{\xi(m)})=c_{m-1}^{\xi(m-1)}(\boldsymbol{\mu})+m(\mu_{m+1}^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}-\mu_{m}^{\xi(m)})=c_{m}^{\xi(m)}(\boldsymbol{\mu})=0 
 
 
 
 
 d m ξ ⁡ ( m ) ​ ( 𝝂 ) \displaystyle d_{m}^{\xi(m)}(\boldsymbol{\nu}) 
 = d m − 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ​ ( 𝝂 ) − ( m − 1 ) ​ ν m ξ ⁡ ( m ) + ( m + 1 ) ​ ν m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ \displaystyle=d_{m-1}^{\xi(m-1)}(\boldsymbol{\nu})-(m-1)\nu^{\xi(m)}_{m}+(m+1)\nu^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}_{m+1} 
 
 
 
 
 
 = d m − 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ​ ( 𝝁 ) − ( m − 1 ) ​ μ m ξ ⁡ ( m ) + ( m + 1 ) ​ μ m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ + 1 = d m ξ ⁡ ( m ) ​ ( 𝝁 ) + 1 . \displaystyle=d_{m-1}^{\xi(m-1)}(\boldsymbol{\mu})-(m-1)\mu^{\xi(m)}_{m}+(m+1)\mu^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}_{m+1}+1=d_{m}^{\xi(m)}(\boldsymbol{\mu})+1. 
 
 
 


 
 Case 3. Assume
 Type ξ , 𝛍 ​ ( m ) = A − {\rm Type}_{\xi,\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m)=\textup{A}_{-} . Then ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) = ξ ⁡ ( m ) \xi(m-1)=\xi(m) so in view of ( 8.21 ) and (iii) above we have

 

 
 
 ν m ξ ⁡ ( m ) = μ m ξ ⁡ ( m ) − 1 ν m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ = μ m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ − 1 d m − 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ​ ( 𝝂 ) = d m − 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ​ ( 𝝁 ) + 1 , \displaystyle\nu_{m}^{\xi(m)}=\mu_{m}^{\xi(m)}-1\qquad\nu_{m+1}^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}=\mu_{m+1}^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}-1\qquad d_{m-1}^{\xi(m-1)}(\boldsymbol{\nu})=d_{m-1}^{\xi(m-1)}(\boldsymbol{\mu})+1, 
 
 
 
 so in consideration of ( 8.12 , 8.13 ) we have

 

 
 
 c m ξ ⁡ ( m ) ​ ( 𝝂 ) \displaystyle c_{m}^{\xi(m)}(\boldsymbol{\nu}) 
 = c m − 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ​ ( 𝝂 ) + m ⁡ ( ν m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ − ν m ξ ⁡ ( m ) ) = c m − 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ​ ( 𝝁 ) + m ⁡ ( μ m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ − μ m ξ ⁡ ( m ) ) = c m ξ ⁡ ( m ) ​ ( 𝝁 ) = 0 \displaystyle=c_{m-1}^{\xi(m-1)}(\boldsymbol{\nu})+m(\nu_{m+1}^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}-\nu_{m}^{\xi(m)})=c_{m-1}^{\xi(m-1)}(\boldsymbol{\mu})+m(\mu_{m+1}^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}-\mu_{m}^{\xi(m)})=c_{m}^{\xi(m)}(\boldsymbol{\mu})=0 
 
 
 
 
 d m ξ ⁡ ( m ) ​ ( 𝝂 ) \displaystyle d_{m}^{\xi(m)}(\boldsymbol{\nu}) 
 = d m − 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ​ ( 𝝂 ) − ( m − 1 ) ​ ν m ξ ⁡ ( m ) + ( m + 1 ) ​ ν m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ \displaystyle=d_{m-1}^{\xi(m-1)}(\boldsymbol{\nu})-(m-1)\nu^{\xi(m)}_{m}+(m+1)\nu^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}_{m+1} 
 
 
 
 
 
 = d m − 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ​ ( 𝝁 ) − ( m − 1 ) ​ μ m ξ ⁡ ( m ) + ( m + 1 ) ​ μ m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ − 1 = d m ξ ⁡ ( m ) ​ ( 𝝁 ) − 1 . \displaystyle=d_{m-1}^{\xi(m-1)}(\boldsymbol{\mu})-(m-1)\mu^{\xi(m)}_{m}+(m+1)\mu^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}_{m+1}-1=d_{m}^{\xi(m)}(\boldsymbol{\mu})-1. 
 
 
 


 
 Case 4. Assume
 Type ξ , 𝛍 ​ ( m ) = B + {\rm Type}_{\xi,\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m)=\textup{B}_{+} . Then ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) = ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ \xi(m-1)=\widehat{\xi(m)} , so in view of ( 8.21 ) and (iv) above we have

 

 
 
 ν m ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ = μ m ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ + 1 ν m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ = μ m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ − 1 d m − 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ​ ( 𝝂 ) = d m − 1 ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ​ ( 𝝁 ) − 1 , \displaystyle\nu_{m}^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}=\mu_{m}^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}+1\qquad\nu_{m+1}^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}=\mu_{m+1}^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}-1\qquad d_{m-1}^{\xi(m-1)}(\boldsymbol{\nu})=d_{m-1}^{\xi(m-1)}(\boldsymbol{\mu})-1, 
 
 
 
 so in consideration of ( 8.15 , 8.16 ) we have

 

 
 
 c m ξ ⁡ ( m ) ​ ( 𝝂 ) \displaystyle c_{m}^{\xi(m)}(\boldsymbol{\nu}) 
 = ( m − 1 ) ​ ν m ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ + m ​ ν m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ − d m − 1 ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ ​ ( 𝝂 ) \displaystyle=(m-1)\nu_{m}^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}+m\nu_{m+1}^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}-d_{m-1}^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}(\boldsymbol{\nu})

 = m ​ μ m ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ + ( m + 1 ) ​ μ m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ − c m − 1 ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ ​ ( 𝝁 ) − 1 = d m ξ ⁡ ( m ) ​ ( 𝝁 ) − 1 . \displaystyle=m\mu^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}_{m}+(m+1)\mu^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}_{m+1}-c_{m-1}^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}(\boldsymbol{\mu})-1=d_{m}^{\xi(m)}(\boldsymbol{\mu})-1. 
 
 
 


 
 Case 5. Assume

 This completes the proof of Claim III.
 



 
 Combining Claims I, II, III, we have that c m ξ ⁡ ( m ) ​ ( 𝝂 ) = 0 c^{\xi(m)}_{m}(\boldsymbol{\nu})=0 , and ( 8.21 , 8.22 ) hold for all m ∈ ℤ ≥ 1 m\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{\geq 1} .
Moreover, by Lemma  8.16 , we have for some i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{2} that η 1 i ​ ( 𝝁 ) \eta^{i}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mu}) is odd and η 1 i ^ ​ ( 𝝁 ) \eta^{\widehat{i}}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mu}) is even. Thus by ( 8.2 ) we have that ν 0 i = μ 0 i + 1 = 1 \nu^{i}_{0}=\mu^{i}_{0}+1=1 , and ν 0 i ^ = 0 \nu^{\widehat{i}}_{0}=0 . It remains to finish the check that 𝝂 \boldsymbol{\nu} is an MV bicomposition; i.e., we must check that c m ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ ​ ( 𝝂 ) ≤ 0 c^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}_{m}(\boldsymbol{\nu})\leq 0 for all m ∈ ℤ ≥ 1 m\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{\geq 1} .
Fix m ∈ ℤ ≥ 1 m\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{\geq 1} , and set 

 

 
 
 ( X , Y ) = ( Type ξ 𝝁 , 𝝁 ​ ( m ) , Type ξ 𝝁 , 𝝁 ​ ( m + 1 ) ) . \displaystyle(\textup{X},\textup{Y})=({\rm Type}_{\xi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}},\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m),{\rm Type}_{\xi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}},\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m+1)).

 for some ε 1 , ε 2 , ε 3 ∈ { − 1 , 0 , 1 } \varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2},\varepsilon_{3}\in\{-1,0,1\} . Therefore applying ( 8.14 ) we have

This yields: 

 

 
 (8.23) 
 
 c m ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ ​ ( 𝝂 ) = { c m ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ ​ ( 𝝁 ) + 1 if  ​ ( X , Y ) = ( A ∅ , A ∅ ) ; c m ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ ​ ( 𝝁 ) + m + 1 if  ​ ( X , Y ) ∈ { ( A ∅ , A + ) , ( A ∅ , B + ) } ; c m ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ ​ ( 𝝁 ) − m if  ​ ( X , Y ) ∈ { ( A − , A ∅ ) , ( B + , A ∅ ) } ; c m ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ ​ ( 𝝁 ) otherwise . \displaystyle c^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}_{m}(\boldsymbol{\nu})=\begin{cases}c^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}_{m}(\boldsymbol{\mu})+1&\textup{if }(\textup{X},\textup{Y})=(\textup{A}_{\varnothing},\textup{A}_{\varnothing});\\

 We have c m ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ ​ ( 𝝁 ) ≤ 0 c^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}_{m}(\boldsymbol{\mu})\leq 0 since 𝝁 ∈ 𝛀 MV \boldsymbol{\mu}\in\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{\textup{MV}} , so c m ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ ​ ( 𝝂 ) ≤ 0 c^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}_{m}(\boldsymbol{\nu})\leq 0 in all cases except perhaps for

∎ 







 
 8.13. Upward diagonalization


 
 Now we repeat the approach of § 8.11 , 8.12 , but with diagonalization and segment type defintions adjusted to play well with the 𝒢 \mathcal{G} map instead.
Let j ∈ ℤ 2 j\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{2} , and 𝝁 ∈ 𝛀 ≥ 0 , j MV \boldsymbol{\mu}\in\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{\geq 0,j}^{\textup{MV}} .
We inductively construct a specific ‘upward’ diagonalization ξ j 𝝁 \xi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{j} of 𝝁 \boldsymbol{\mu} as follows, by setting

 

 
 (8.24) 
 
 ξ j 𝝁 ​ ( m ) = { j ^ if  ​ m = 0 ; ξ j 𝝁 ​ ( m − 1 ) if  ​ m > 0 ​  and  ​ c m ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) = 0 ; ξ j 𝝁 ​ ( m − 1 ) ^ if  ​ m > 0 ​  and  ​ c m ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) < 0 . \displaystyle\xi_{j}^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m)=\begin{cases}\widehat{j}&\textup{if }m=0;\\
\xi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{j}(m-1)&\textup{if }m>0\textup{ and }c_{m}^{\xi(m-1)}=0;\\
\widehat{\xi_{j}^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m-1)}&\textup{if }m>0\textup{ and }c_{m}^{\xi(m-1)}<0.\end{cases} 
 
 
 
 This choice of diagonalization gives us some control over the structure of its segments, as noted in the next subsection.



 
 8.13.1. Typifying segments, part deuce


 
 Let ξ \xi be a diagonalization of 𝝁 ∈ 𝛀 MV \boldsymbol{\mu}\in\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\textup{MV}} . We define a function

 

 
 
 Type ξ , 𝝁 : ℤ ≥ 1 → { A ∅ , A + , A − , B + , B − , Z } \displaystyle{\rm Type}^{\xi,\boldsymbol{\mu}}:{\mathbbm{Z}}_{\geq 1}\rightarrow\{\textup{A}^{\varnothing},\textup{A}^{+},\textup{A}^{-},\textup{B}^{+},\textup{B}^{-},\textup{Z}\} 
 
 
 
 given by setting

 

 
 
 Type ξ , 𝝁 ( m ) = { A ∅ if  ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) = ξ ⁡ ( m ) ,  μ m ξ ⁡ ( m ) = μ m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ = 0 , and  ϑ m − 1 ξ ⁡ ( m ) ​ ( 𝝁 ) , ϑ m ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ ​ ( 𝝁 )  are even. A + if  ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) = ξ ⁡ ( m )  and  ϑ m − 1 ξ ⁡ ( m ) ​ ( 𝝁 ) , ϑ m ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ ​ ( 𝝁 )  are odd. A − if  ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) = ξ ⁡ ( m )  and  ϑ m ξ ⁡ ( m ) ​ ( 𝝁 ) , ϑ m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ ​ ( 𝝁 )  are odd. B − if  ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) = ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ ,  c m ξ ⁡ ( m − 1 ) ​ ( 𝝁 ) ≠ 0 ,  μ m ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ > 0 , and  ϑ m + 1 ξ ⁡ ( m ) ^ ​ ( 𝝁 ) ,  ϑ m ξ ⁡ ( m ) ​ ( 𝝁 )  are odd. Z otherwise , \displaystyle{\rm Type}^{\xi,\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m)=\begin{cases}\textup{A}^{\varnothing}&\textup{if $\xi(m-1)=\xi(m)$,
$\mu^{\xi(m)}_{m}=\mu^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}_{m+1}=0$, and $\vartheta^{\xi(m)}_{m-1}(\boldsymbol{\mu}),\vartheta^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}_{m}(\boldsymbol{\mu})$ are even.}\\
\textup{A}^{+}&\textup{if $\xi(m-1)=\xi(m)$ and $\vartheta^{\xi(m)}_{m-1}(\boldsymbol{\mu}),\vartheta^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}_{m}(\boldsymbol{\mu})$ are odd.}\\
\textup{A}^{-}&\textup{if $\xi(m-1)=\xi(m)$ and $\vartheta^{\xi(m)}_{m}(\boldsymbol{\mu}),\vartheta^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}_{m+1}(\boldsymbol{\mu})$ are odd.}\\

\textup{B}^{-}&\textup{if $\xi(m-1)=\widehat{\xi(m)}$, $c^{\xi(m-1)}_{m}(\boldsymbol{\mu})\neq 0$, $\mu^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}_{m}>0$, and $\vartheta^{\widehat{\xi(m)}}_{m+1}(\boldsymbol{\mu})$, $\vartheta^{\xi(m)}_{m}(\boldsymbol{\mu})$ are odd.}\\
\textup{Z}&\textup{otherwise},\end{cases} 
 
 
 
 and we refer to Type ξ , 𝝁 ​ ( m ) {\rm Type}^{\xi,\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m) as the (upward) type of the m m th segment of ξ \xi in 𝛍 \boldsymbol{\mu} .



 
 Lemma 8.21 . 

 
 Let j ∈ ℤ 2 j\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{2} , 𝛍 ∈ 𝛀 ≥ 0 , j MV \boldsymbol{\mu}\in\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{\geq 0,j}^{\textup{MV}} .
For all m ∈ ℤ ≥ 1 m\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{\geq 1} , we have Type ξ j 𝛍 , 𝛍 ​ ( m ) ∈ { A ∅ , A + , A − , B + , B − } {\rm Type}^{\xi_{j}^{\boldsymbol{\mu}},\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m)\in\{\textup{A}^{\varnothing},\textup{A}^{+},\textup{A}^{-},\textup{B}^{+},\textup{B}^{-}\} . Moreover,
for all m ∈ ℤ ≥ 1 m\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{\geq 1} , we have that 

 

 
 
 ( Type ξ j 𝝁 , 𝝁 ​ ( m ) , Type ξ j 𝝁 , 𝝁 ​ ( m + 1 ) ) \displaystyle({\rm Type}^{\xi_{j}^{\boldsymbol{\mu}},\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m),{\rm Type}^{\xi_{j}^{\boldsymbol{\mu}},\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m+1)) 
 
 
 
 is equal to one of 

 

 
 
 
 { ( A ∅ , A ∅ ) , ( A ∅ , A − ) , ( A ∅ , B + ) , ( A + , A ∅ ) , ( A + , A − ) , ( A + , B + ) , ( A − , A + ) , \displaystyle\{(\textup{A}^{\varnothing},\textup{A}^{\varnothing}),(\textup{A}^{\varnothing},\textup{A}^{-}),(\textup{A}^{\varnothing},\textup{B}^{+}),(\textup{A}^{+},\textup{A}^{\varnothing}),(\textup{A}^{+},\textup{A}^{-}),(\textup{A}^{+},\textup{B}^{+}),(\textup{A}^{-},\textup{A}^{+}), 
 
 
 
 (8.25) 
 
 
 ( A − , B − ) , ( B + , A ∅ ) , ( B + , A − ) , ( B + , B + ) , ( B − , A + ) , ( B − , B − ) } . \displaystyle\qquad\;\;\;(\textup{A}^{-},\textup{B}^{-}),(\textup{B}^{+},\textup{A}^{\varnothing}),(\textup{B}^{+},\textup{A}^{-}),(\textup{B}^{+},\textup{B}^{+}),(\textup{B}^{-},\textup{A}^{+}),(\textup{B}^{-},\textup{B}^{-})\}. 
 
 
 




 
 Proof.

 
 We omit the proof as it is very similar in spirit to the proof of Lemma  8.19 , with minor alteration—one proceeds by upward induction rather than downward, and so the base case of induction is somewhat different. Full details can be found in the arXiv version of the paper as explained in § 1.3 .
∎ 









 
 8.14. The 𝒢 \mathcal{G} map and bicompositions


 
 Lemma 8.22 . 

 
 The map 𝒢 \mathcal{G} restricts to a map of MV bicompositions 𝒢 : 𝛀 ≥ 0 , ∙ MV → 𝛀 ≥ 1 , + MV \mathcal{G}:\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{\geq 0,\bullet}^{\textup{MV}}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\textup{MV}}_{\geq 1,+} .  (8.26) 
 
 d m − 1 ξ j 𝝁 ​ ( m − 1 ) ​ ( 𝝂 ) \displaystyle d^{\xi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{j}(m-1)}_{m-1}(\boldsymbol{\nu})

 d m ξ 𝝁 ​ ( m ) ​ ( 𝝂 ) \displaystyle d^{\xi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(m)}_{m}(\boldsymbol{\nu})

 Corollary 8.23 . 

 
 The functions 

 

 
 
 𝒢 : 𝛀 ≥ 0 , ∙ MV → 𝛀 ≥ 1 , + MV and ℋ : 𝛀 ≥ 1 , + MV → 𝛀 ≥ 0 , ∙ MV \displaystyle\mathcal{G}:\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{\geq 0,\bullet}^{\textup{MV}}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{\geq 1,+}^{\textup{MV}}\qquad\textup{and}\qquad\mathcal{H}:\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{\geq 1,+}^{\textup{MV}}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{\geq 0,\bullet}^{\textup{MV}} 
 
 
 
 are mutual inverses. 





 
 Proof.

 
 Follows immediately by Lemmas  8.3 , 8.20 , 8.22 .
∎ 







 
 8.15. Directed binary graphs


 
 Let DG ​ ( 𝛀 ≥ 1 MV ) \textup{DG}(\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\textup{MV}}_{\geq 1}) be the directed graph whose vertices are the MV bicompositions 𝛀 ≥ 1 MV \boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\textup{MV}}_{\geq 1} , with an oriented edge 𝝁 → 𝒢 ¯ i ​ 𝝁 \boldsymbol{\mu}\rightarrow\bar{\mathcal{G}}^{i}\boldsymbol{\mu} for each 𝝁 ∈ 𝛀 ≥ 1 MV \boldsymbol{\mu}\in\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\textup{MV}}_{\geq 1} , i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{2} . Note that if 𝝁 ∈ 𝛀 ≥ 1 MV ​ ( n ) \boldsymbol{\mu}\in\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\textup{MV}}_{\geq 1}(n) , then 𝒢 ¯ i ​ 𝝁 ∈ 𝛀 ≥ 1 MV ​ ( n + 1 ) \bar{\mathcal{G}}^{i}\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\textup{MV}}_{\geq 1}(n+1) , so 𝝁 ≠ 𝒢 ¯ i ​ 𝝁 \boldsymbol{\mu}\neq\bar{\mathcal{G}}^{i}\boldsymbol{\mu} . By definition of the map 𝒢 ¯ i \bar{\mathcal{G}}^{i} we also have

 

 
 (8.28) 
 
 ( 𝒢 ¯ 0 ​ 𝝁 ) 1 0 = μ 1 0 + 1 ; ( 𝒢 ¯ 0 ​ 𝝁 ) 1 1 ≠ μ 1 1 + 1 ; ( 𝒢 ¯ 1 ​ 𝝁 ) 1 0 ≠ μ 1 0 + 1 ; ( 𝒢 ¯ 1 ​ 𝝁 ) 1 1 = μ 1 1 + 1 , \displaystyle(\bar{\mathcal{G}}^{0}\boldsymbol{\mu})^{0}_{1}=\mu^{0}_{1}+1;\qquad(\bar{\mathcal{G}}^{0}\boldsymbol{\mu})^{1}_{1}\neq\mu^{1}_{1}+1;\qquad(\bar{\mathcal{G}}^{1}\boldsymbol{\mu})^{0}_{1}\neq\mu^{0}_{1}+1;\qquad(\bar{\mathcal{G}}^{1}\boldsymbol{\mu})^{1}_{1}=\mu^{1}_{1}+1, 
 
 
 
 and therefore 𝒢 ¯ 0 ​ 𝝁 ≠ 𝒢 ¯ 1 ​ 𝝁 \bar{\mathcal{G}}^{0}\boldsymbol{\mu}\neq\bar{\mathcal{G}}^{1}\boldsymbol{\mu} for all 𝝁 ∈ 𝛀 ≥ 1 MV \boldsymbol{\mu}\in\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\textup{MV}}_{\geq 1} . Thus DG ​ ( 𝛀 ≥ 1 MV ) \textup{DG}(\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\textup{MV}}_{\geq 1}) is a simple directed graph.



 
 Let DG ​ ( Θ ) \textup{DG}(\Theta) be the simple directed graph whose vertices are the binary words Θ \Theta , with an oriented edge 𝜺 → 𝒜 i ​ 𝜺 \boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}^{i}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon} for each 𝜺 ∈ Θ \boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\in\Theta , i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{2} .



 
 Theorem 8.24 . 

 
 Let i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{2} . We have inverse isomorphisms of directed graphs 

 

 
 
 𝒥 : DG ​ ( Θ ) → DG ​ ( 𝛀 ≥ 1 MV ) and 𝒦 i : DG ​ ( 𝛀 ≥ 1 MV ) → DG ​ ( Θ ) . \displaystyle\mathcal{J}:\textup{DG}(\Theta)\rightarrow\textup{DG}(\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\textup{MV}}_{\geq 1})\qquad\textup{and}\qquad\mathcal{K}^{i}:\textup{DG}(\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\textup{MV}}_{\geq 1})\rightarrow\textup{DG}(\Theta). 
 
 
 




 
 Proof.

 
 Let n ∈ ℤ ≥ 1 n\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{\geq 1} , and 𝝁 ∈ 𝛀 ≥ 1 MV ​ ( n ) \boldsymbol{\mu}\in\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\textup{MV}}_{\geq 1}(n) . We proceed via a number of claims.
 



 
 Claim I.
We have 𝒥 ⁡ ( Θ ⁡ ( n ) ) ⊇ 𝛀 ≥ 1 MV ​ ( n ) \mathcal{J}(\Theta(n))\supseteq\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\textup{MV}}_{\geq 1}(n) . We go by induction on n n . The base case n = 0 n=0 is immediate, since

 

 
 (8.29) 
 
 𝛀 ≥ 1 MV ​ ( 0 ) = 𝛀 ≥ 1 ​ ( 0 ) = { ( ∅ , ∅ ) } = { 𝒥 ​ ∅ } . \displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\textup{MV}}_{\geq 1}(0)=\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{\geq 1}(0)=\{(\varnothing,\varnothing)\}=\{\mathcal{J}\varnothing\}. 
 
 
 
 Now assume the claim holds for n ≥ 0 n\geq 0 . Let 𝝁 ∈ 𝛀 ≥ 1 MV ​ ( n + 1 ) \boldsymbol{\mu}\in\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\textup{MV}}_{\geq 1}(n+1) . By Lemma  8.3 we have ℋ ​ 𝝁 ∈ 𝛀 ≥ 0 , j MV \mathcal{H}\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\textup{MV}}_{\geq 0,j} for some j ∈ ℤ 2 j\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{2} , and so one 0 j ∘ zero 0 ∘ ℋ ​ 𝝁 = ℋ ​ 𝝁 \textup{one}_{0}^{j}\circ\textup{zero}_{0}\circ\mathcal{H}\boldsymbol{\mu}=\mathcal{H}\boldsymbol{\mu} . Thus

 

 
 (8.30) 
 
 𝒢 ¯ j ​ ℋ ¯ ​ 𝝁 = 𝒢 ∘ one 0 j ∘ zero 0 ∘ ℋ ​ 𝝁 = 𝒢 ​ ℋ ​ 𝝁 = 𝝁 , \displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{G}}^{j}\bar{\mathcal{H}}\boldsymbol{\mu}=\mathcal{G}\circ\textup{one}_{0}^{j}\circ\textup{zero}_{0}\circ\mathcal{H}\boldsymbol{\mu}=\mathcal{G}\mathcal{H}\boldsymbol{\mu}=\boldsymbol{\mu}, 
 
 
 
 by Lemma  8.3 .
Since ℋ ¯ ​ 𝝁 ∈ 𝛀 ≥ 1 MV ​ ( n ) \bar{\mathcal{H}}\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\textup{MV}}_{\geq 1}(n) , by induction assumption there exists 𝜺 ∈ Θ ⁡ ( n ) \boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\in\Theta(n) such that 𝒥 ​ 𝜺 = ℋ ¯ ​ 𝝁 \mathcal{J}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}=\bar{\mathcal{H}}\boldsymbol{\mu} . But then 𝒜 j ​ 𝜺 ∈ Θ ⁡ ( n + 1 ) \mathcal{A}^{j}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\in\Theta(n+1) , so we have by Lemma  8.14 and ( 8.30 ) that

 

 
 
 𝒥 ​ 𝒜 j ​ 𝜺 = 𝒢 ¯ j ​ 𝒥 ​ 𝜺 = 𝒢 ¯ j ​ ℋ ¯ ​ 𝝁 = 𝝁 , \displaystyle\mathcal{J}\mathcal{A}^{j}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}=\bar{\mathcal{G}}^{j}\mathcal{J}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}=\bar{\mathcal{G}}^{j}\bar{\mathcal{H}}\boldsymbol{\mu}=\boldsymbol{\mu}, 
 
 
 
 and so 𝒥 ⁡ ( Θ ⁡ ( n + 1 ) ) ⊇ 𝛀 ≥ 1 MV ​ ( n + 1 ) \mathcal{J}(\Theta(n+1))\supseteq\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\textup{MV}}_{\geq 1}(n+1) , proving the claim.
 



 
 Claim II. We have 𝒥 ⁡ ( Θ ⁡ ( n ) ) ⊆ 𝛀 ≥ 1 MV ​ ( n ) \mathcal{J}(\Theta(n))\subseteq\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\textup{MV}}_{\geq 1}(n) . We again go by induction on n n , with the base case n = 0 n=0 following from ( 8.29 ). Assume the claim holds for n ≥ 0 n\geq 0 . Let 𝜺 ∈ Θ ⁡ ( n + 1 ) \boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\in\Theta(n+1) . Then ℛ ​ 𝜺 ∈ Θ ⁡ ( n ) \mathcal{R}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\in\Theta(n) , so by induction assumption 𝒥 ​ ℛ ​ 𝜺 ∈ 𝛀 ≥ 1 MV ​ ( n ) \mathcal{J}\mathcal{R}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\in\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\textup{MV}}_{\geq 1}(n) . Then one 0 ε n + 1 ∘ 𝒥 ​ ℛ ​ 𝜺 ∈ 𝛀 ≥ 0 , ε n + 1 MV ​ ( n + 1 ) \textup{one}_{0}^{\varepsilon_{n+1}}\circ\mathcal{J}\mathcal{R}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\in\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\textup{MV}}_{\geq 0,\varepsilon_{n+1}}(n+1) , so by Lemma  8.22 we have that 𝒢 ∘ one 0 ε n + 1 ∘ 𝒥 ​ ℛ ​ 𝜺 ∈ 𝛀 ≥ 1 MV ​ ( n + 1 ) \mathcal{G}\circ\textup{one}_{0}^{\varepsilon_{n+1}}\circ\mathcal{J}\mathcal{R}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\in\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\textup{MV}}_{\geq 1}(n+1) .
But then Lemma  8.14 implies that

 

 
 
 𝒥 ​ 𝜺 = 𝒥 ​ 𝒜 ε n + 1 ​ ℛ ​ 𝜺 = 𝒢 ¯ ε n + 1 ​ 𝒥 ​ ℛ ​ 𝜺 = 𝒢 ∘ one 0 ε n + 1 ∘ 𝒥 ​ ℛ ​ 𝜺 ∈ 𝛀 ≥ 1 MV ​ ( n + 1 ) , \displaystyle\mathcal{J}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}=\mathcal{J}\mathcal{A}^{\varepsilon_{n+1}}\mathcal{R}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}=\bar{\mathcal{G}}^{\varepsilon_{n+1}}\mathcal{J}\mathcal{R}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}=\mathcal{G}\circ\textup{one}_{0}^{\varepsilon_{n+1}}\circ\mathcal{J}\mathcal{R}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\in\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\textup{MV}}_{\geq 1}(n+1), 
 
 
 
 and so 𝒥 ⁡ ( Θ ⁡ ( n + 1 ) ) ⊆ 𝛀 ≥ 1 MV ​ ( n + 1 ) \mathcal{J}(\Theta(n+1))\subseteq\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\textup{MV}}_{\geq 1}(n+1) , proving the claim.
 



 
 Claim III. The map 𝒥 : Θ ⁡ ( n ) → 𝛀 ≥ 1 MV ​ ( n ) \mathcal{J}:\Theta(n)\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{\geq 1}^{\textup{MV}}(n) is injective. 
We again go by induction on n n , with the base case n = 0 n=0 following from ( 8.29 ). Assume the claim holds for n ≥ 0 n\geq 0 , and let 𝜺 , 𝝆 ∈ Θ ⁡ ( n + 1 ) \boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{\rho}\in\Theta(n+1) , and assume that 𝒥 ​ 𝜺 = 𝒥 ​ 𝝆 \mathcal{J}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}=\mathcal{J}\boldsymbol{\rho} . Then by Lemma  8.14 we have

 

 
 
 𝒥 ​ ℛ ​ 𝜺 = ℋ ¯ ​ 𝒥 ​ 𝜺 = ℋ ¯ ​ 𝒥 ​ 𝝆 = 𝒥 ​ ℛ ​ 𝝆 . \displaystyle\mathcal{J}\mathcal{R}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}=\bar{\mathcal{H}}\mathcal{J}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}=\bar{\mathcal{H}}\mathcal{J}\boldsymbol{\rho}=\mathcal{J}\mathcal{R}\boldsymbol{\rho}. 
 
 
 
 Since ℛ ​ 𝜺 , ℛ ​ 𝝆 ∈ Θ ⁡ ( n ) \mathcal{R}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\mathcal{R}\boldsymbol{\rho}\in\Theta(n) , the induction assumption implies that ℛ ​ 𝜺 = ℛ ​ 𝝆 \mathcal{R}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}=\mathcal{R}\boldsymbol{\rho} . Therefore, writing 𝜸 = ℛ ​ 𝜺 = ℛ ​ 𝝆 \boldsymbol{\gamma}=\mathcal{R}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}=\mathcal{R}\boldsymbol{\rho} , we have 𝒜 i ​ 𝜸 = 𝜺 \mathcal{A}^{i}\boldsymbol{\gamma}=\boldsymbol{\varepsilon} and 𝒜 j ​ 𝜸 = 𝝆 \mathcal{A}^{j}\boldsymbol{\gamma}=\boldsymbol{\rho} for some i , j ∈ ℤ 2 i,j\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{2} . Then we have

 

 
 (8.31) 
 
 𝒢 ¯ i ​ 𝒥 ​ 𝜸 = 𝒥 ​ 𝒜 i ​ 𝜸 = 𝒥 ​ 𝜺 = 𝒥 ​ 𝝆 = 𝒥 ​ 𝒜 j ​ 𝜸 = 𝒢 ¯ j ​ 𝒥 ​ 𝜸 . \displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{G}}^{i}\mathcal{J}\boldsymbol{\gamma}=\mathcal{J}\mathcal{A}^{i}\boldsymbol{\gamma}=\mathcal{J}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}=\mathcal{J}\boldsymbol{\rho}=\mathcal{J}\mathcal{A}^{j}\boldsymbol{\gamma}=\bar{\mathcal{G}}^{j}\mathcal{J}\boldsymbol{\gamma}. 
 
 
 
 But then one may see as in ( 8.28 ) that 𝒢 ¯ i ​ 𝒥 ​ 𝜸 = 𝒢 ¯ j ​ 𝒥 ​ 𝜸 \bar{\mathcal{G}}^{i}\mathcal{J}\boldsymbol{\gamma}=\bar{\mathcal{G}}^{j}\mathcal{J}\boldsymbol{\gamma} only if i = j i=j , and so 𝜺 = 𝝆 \boldsymbol{\varepsilon}=\boldsymbol{\rho} , proving the claim.
 



 
 Claim IV. The map 𝒥 : DG ​ ( Θ ) → DG ​ ( 𝛀 ≥ 1 MV ) \mathcal{J}:\textup{DG}(\Theta)\rightarrow\textup{DG}(\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\textup{MV}}_{\geq 1}) is an isomorphism of directed graphs. We have by Claims I–III that 𝒥 \mathcal{J} is a bijection of vertices of the graphs. Thus it remains to show that 𝜺 → 𝝆 \boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\rho} is an arrow in DG ​ ( Θ ) \textup{DG}(\Theta) if and only if 𝒥 ​ 𝜺 → 𝒥 ​ 𝝆 \mathcal{J}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\rightarrow\mathcal{J}\boldsymbol{\rho} is an arrow in DG ​ ( 𝛀 ≥ 1 MV ) \textup{DG}(\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\textup{MV}}_{\geq 1}) .
First assume 𝜺 → 𝝆 \boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\rho} is an arrow in DG ​ ( Θ ) \textup{DG}(\Theta) . Then 𝝆 = 𝒜 i ​ 𝜺 \boldsymbol{\rho}=\mathcal{A}^{i}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon} for some i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{2} . Then by Lemma  8.14 we have

 

 
 
 𝒥 ​ 𝝆 = 𝒥 ​ 𝒜 i ​ 𝜺 = 𝒢 ¯ i ​ 𝒥 ​ 𝜺 , \displaystyle\mathcal{J}\boldsymbol{\rho}=\mathcal{J}\mathcal{A}^{i}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}=\bar{\mathcal{G}}^{i}\mathcal{J}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}, 
 
 
 
 so we have an arrow 𝒥 ​ 𝜺 → 𝒥 ​ 𝝆 \mathcal{J}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\rightarrow\mathcal{J}\boldsymbol{\rho} in DG ​ ( 𝛀 ≥ 1 MV ) \textup{DG}(\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\textup{MV}}_{\geq 1}) . On the other hand, let 𝒥 ​ 𝜺 → 𝒥 ​ 𝝆 \mathcal{J}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\rightarrow\mathcal{J}\boldsymbol{\rho} be an arrow in DG ​ ( 𝛀 ≥ 1 MV ) \textup{DG}(\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\textup{MV}}_{\geq 1}) . Then 𝒥 ​ 𝝆 = 𝒢 ¯ j ​ 𝒥 ​ 𝜺 \mathcal{J}\boldsymbol{\rho}=\bar{\mathcal{G}}^{j}\mathcal{J}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon} for some j ∈ ℤ 2 j\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{2} , so by Lemma  8.14 we have

 

 
 
 𝒥 ​ 𝒜 j ​ 𝜺 = 𝒢 ¯ j ​ 𝒥 ​ 𝜺 = 𝒥 ​ 𝝆 . \displaystyle\mathcal{J}\mathcal{A}^{j}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}=\bar{\mathcal{G}}^{j}\mathcal{J}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}=\mathcal{J}\boldsymbol{\rho}. 
 
 
 
 By injectivity of 𝒥 \mathcal{J} , we have that 𝒜 j ​ 𝜺 = 𝝆 \mathcal{A}^{j}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}=\boldsymbol{\rho} , and so 𝜺 → 𝝆 \boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\rho} is an arrow in DG ​ ( Θ ) \textup{DG}(\Theta) , proving the claim.
 



 
 Claim V.
We have 𝒦 i = 𝒥 − 1 \mathcal{K}^{i}=\mathcal{J}^{-1} .
 
Let 𝜺 ∈ Θ ⁡ ( n ) \boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\in\Theta(n) . If n = 0 n=0 , then 𝜺 = ∅ \boldsymbol{\varepsilon}=\varnothing and we have 𝒦 i ​ 𝒥 ​ 𝜺 = 𝒦 i ​ ( ∅ , ∅ ) = ∅ = 𝜺 \mathcal{K}^{i}\mathcal{J}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}=\mathcal{K}^{i}(\varnothing,\varnothing)=\varnothing=\boldsymbol{\varepsilon} . Assume n > 0 n>0 .
Then set 𝝁 = 𝒥 ​ 𝜺 ∈ 𝛀 ≥ 1 MV ​ ( n ) \boldsymbol{\mu}=\mathcal{J}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\in\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\textup{MV}}_{\geq 1}(n) . We have μ k i = # ​ P i R ​ ( 𝒯 i k − 1 ​ 𝜺 ) \mu^{i}_{k}=\#\textup{P}^{\textup{R}}_{i}(\mathcal{T}_{i}^{k-1}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}) for all i ∈ ℤ 2 , k ∈ ℤ ≥ 1 i\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{2},k\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{\geq 1} . Let m m be maximal such that μ m i > 0 \mu^{i}_{m}>0 . Then 𝒯 i m + 1 ​ 𝜺 = i ^ n \mathcal{T}_{i}^{m+1}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}=\widehat{i}^{n} . Then by ( 8.9 ) we have

 

 
 
 𝒦 i 𝝁 = 𝒰 i , μ 1 ⋯ 𝒰 i , μ m ( i ^ n ) = 𝒰 i , # ​ P i R ​ ( 𝒯 i 0 ​ 𝜺 ) ⋯ 𝒰 i , # ​ P i R ​ ( 𝒯 i m ​ 𝜺 ) ( 𝒯 i m + 1 𝜺 ) = 𝜺 \displaystyle\mathcal{K}^{i}\boldsymbol{\mu}=\mathcal{U}_{i,\mu_{1}}\cdots\mathcal{U}_{i,\mu_{m}}(\widehat{i}^{n})=\mathcal{U}_{i,\#\textup{P}^{\textup{R}}_{i}(\mathcal{T}_{i}^{0}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})}\cdots\mathcal{U}_{i,\#\textup{P}^{\textup{R}}_{i}(\mathcal{T}_{i}^{m}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})}(\mathcal{T}_{i}^{m+1}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})=\boldsymbol{\varepsilon} 
 
 
 
 by repeated applications of Lemma  8.13 . Therefore 𝒦 i \mathcal{K}^{i} is a left inverse for 𝒥 \mathcal{J} , and so 𝒦 i = 𝒥 − 1 \mathcal{K}^{i}=\mathcal{J}^{-1} since 𝒥 \mathcal{J} is an isomorphism.
∎





 
 Lemma 8.25 . 

 
 Let i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{2} , n ∈ ℤ > 0 n\in\mathbbm{Z}_{>0} , 𝛍 ∈ 𝛀 ≥ 1 MV ​ ( n ) \boldsymbol{\mu}\in\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{\geq 1}^{\textup{MV}}(n) , and set 𝛆 = 𝒦 i ​ 𝛍 ∈ 𝚯 ⁡ ( n ) \boldsymbol{\varepsilon}=\mathcal{K}^{i}\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}(n) . Then there exists M ∈ ℤ > 0 M\in\mathbbm{Z}_{>0} such that μ M ε 1 > 0 \mu_{M}^{\varepsilon_{1}}>0 and μ m ε ^ 1 = 0 \mu_{m}^{\widehat{\varepsilon}_{1}}=0 for all m ≥ M m\geq M . 





 
 Proof.

 
 When n = 1 n=1 , we have 𝜺 = j \boldsymbol{\varepsilon}=j if and only if μ 1 j = 1 \mu^{j}_{1}=1 , μ k j = 0 \mu^{j}_{k}=0 for k > 1 k>1 , and μ j ^ = ∅ \mu_{\widehat{j}}=\varnothing , so the claim holds. Now assume n > 1 n>1 . Set 𝜺 ′ = ℛ ​ 𝜺 \boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{\prime}=\mathcal{R}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon} and 𝝂 = ℋ ¯ ​ 𝝁 \boldsymbol{\nu}=\overline{\mathcal{H}}\boldsymbol{\mu} , so that 

 

 
 
 𝒦 i ​ 𝝂 = 𝒦 i ​ ℋ ¯ ​ 𝝁 = 𝒦 i ​ ℋ ¯ ​ 𝒥 ​ 𝜺 = 𝒦 i ​ ℋ ¯ ​ 𝒥 ​ 𝒜 ε n ​ 𝜺 ′ = 𝒦 i ​ ℋ ¯ ​ 𝒢 ¯ ε n ​ 𝒥 ​ 𝜺 ′ = 𝒦 i ​ 𝒥 ​ 𝜺 ′ = 𝜺 ′ , \displaystyle\mathcal{K}^{i}\boldsymbol{\nu}=\mathcal{K}^{i}\overline{\mathcal{H}}\boldsymbol{\mu}=\mathcal{K}^{i}\overline{\mathcal{H}}\mathcal{J}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}=\mathcal{K}^{i}\overline{\mathcal{H}}\mathcal{J}\mathcal{A}^{\varepsilon_{n}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{\prime}=\mathcal{K}^{i}\overline{\mathcal{H}}\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{\varepsilon_{n}}\mathcal{J}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{\prime}=\mathcal{K}^{i}\mathcal{J}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{\prime}, 
 
 
 
 where the second, fourth and sixth equalities are by Theorem  8.24 , and the fifth equality is by Corollary  8.23 . Then ε 1 = ε 1 ′ \varepsilon_{1}=\varepsilon_{1}^{\prime} , and by induction assumption there exists M ′ ∈ ℤ > 0 M^{\prime}\in\mathbbm{Z}_{>0} such that ν M ′ ε 1 > 0 \nu^{\varepsilon_{1}}_{M^{\prime}}>0 and ν m ε ^ 1 = 0 \nu^{\widehat{\varepsilon}_{1}}_{m}=0 for all m ≥ M ′ m\geq M^{\prime} .
By Lemma  8.1 , since ν M ′ ε 1 > 0 \nu^{\varepsilon_{1}}_{M^{\prime}}>0 , it must be that μ M ′ ε 1 > 0 \mu^{\varepsilon_{1}}_{M^{\prime}}>0 or μ M ′ + 1 ε 1 > 0 \mu^{\varepsilon_{1}}_{M^{\prime}+1}>0 , and since ν m ε ^ 1 = 0 \nu^{\widehat{\varepsilon}_{1}}_{m}=0 for m ≥ M ′ m\geq M^{\prime} , it must be that μ m ε ^ 1 = 0 \mu^{\widehat{\varepsilon}_{1}}_{m}=0 for m > M ′ m>M^{\prime} . But then by Lemma  8.17 this occurs if and only if there exists M ∈ { M ′ , M ′ + 1 } M\in\{M^{\prime},M^{\prime}+1\} such that μ M ε 1 > 0 \mu^{\varepsilon_{1}}_{M}>0 and μ m ε ^ 1 = 0 \mu^{\widehat{\varepsilon}_{1}}_{m}=0 for all m ≥ M m\geq M .
∎ 









 
 9. Proofs of the main theorems


 
 9.1. Characterizing affine MV polytopes


 
 Recall now the definitions of affine MV polytopes in § 4.2 .



 
 Lemma 9.1 . 

 
 Let ( π | ϕ ) ∈ ℳ ​ 𝒱 ​ ( θ ) (\pi|\phi)\in\mathcal{MV}(\theta) . Let w = p ⁡ ( π 1 ) + p ⁡ ( ϕ 0 ) w=p(\pi^{1})+p(\phi^{0}) . Then 

 
 (1) 
 
 π 1 1 ≠ ϕ 1 0 \pi^{1}_{1}\neq\phi^{0}_{1} , and π 1 1 < ϕ 1 0 \pi^{1}_{1}<\phi^{0}_{1} implies
 ∑ k = 1 ∞ ( n k ( ϕ 0 ) α 0 : k − n k ( π 1 ) α 1 : k ) = w α 0 ; \sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left(n_{k}(\phi^{0})\alpha_{0:k}-n_{k}(\pi^{1})\alpha_{1:k}\right)=w\alpha_{0}; 




 (2) 
 
 π 1 0 ≠ ϕ 1 1 \pi^{0}_{1}\neq\phi^{1}_{1} , and π 1 0 < ϕ 1 1 \pi^{0}_{1}<\phi^{1}_{1} implies
 ∑ k = 1 ∞ ( n k ( ϕ 1 ) α 1 : k − n k ( π 0 ) α 0 : k ) = w α 1 ; \sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left(n_{k}(\phi^{1})\alpha_{1:k}-n_{k}(\pi^{0})\alpha_{0:k}\right)=w\alpha_{1}; 




 (3) 
 
 π 1 1 < ϕ 1 0 \pi^{1}_{1}<\phi^{0}_{1} and π 1 0 < ϕ 1 1 \pi^{0}_{1}<\phi^{1}_{1} implies π δ = ( w , ϕ 1 δ , … , ϕ p ⁡ ( ϕ δ ) δ ) \pi^{\delta}=(w,\phi^{\delta}_{1},\ldots,\phi^{\delta}_{p(\phi^{\delta})}) . 




 (4) 
 
 π 1 1 > ϕ 1 0 \pi^{1}_{1}>\phi^{0}_{1} and π 1 0 > ϕ 1 1 \pi^{0}_{1}>\phi^{1}_{1} implies ϕ δ = ( w , π 1 δ , … , π p ⁡ ( ϕ δ ) δ ) \phi^{\delta}=(w,\pi^{\delta}_{1},\ldots,\pi^{\delta}_{p(\phi^{\delta})}) . 




 (5) 
 
 π 1 1 > ϕ 1 0 \pi^{1}_{1}>\phi^{0}_{1} and π 1 0 < ϕ 1 1 \pi^{0}_{1}<\phi^{1}_{1} , or π 1 1 < ϕ 1 0 \pi^{1}_{1}<\phi^{0}_{1} and π 1 0 > ϕ 1 1 \pi^{0}_{1}>\phi^{1}_{1} implies π δ = ϕ δ \pi^{\delta}=\phi^{\delta} . 




 




 
 Proof.

 
 Assume π 1 1 ≤ ϕ 1 0 = M \pi_{1}^{1}\leq\phi^{0}_{1}=M . Then n M + 1 ​ ( π 1 ) = n M + 1 ​ ( ϕ 0 ) = 0 n_{M+1}(\pi^{1})=n_{M+1}(\phi^{0})=0 and n M ​ ( ϕ 0 ) > 0 n_{M}(\phi^{0})>0 . We also have that c M − 1 → ​ ( π | ϕ ) , c M − 1 ↑ ​ ( π | ϕ ) ≤ 0 c_{M-1}^{\rightarrow}(\pi|\phi),c_{M-1}^{\uparrow}(\pi|\phi)\leq 0 since ( π | ϕ ) ∈ ℳ ​ 𝒱 (\pi|\phi)\in\mathcal{MV} . Therefore 

 

 
 
 c M → ​ ( π | ϕ ) = − M ​ n M ​ ( ϕ 0 ) + c M − 1 → ​ ( π | ϕ ) < 0 and c M ↑ ​ ( π | ϕ ) = − M ​ n M ​ ( π 1 ) + c M − 1 ↑ ​ ( π | ϕ ) , \displaystyle c_{M}^{\rightarrow}(\pi|\phi)=-Mn_{M}(\phi^{0})+c_{M-1}^{\rightarrow}(\pi|\phi)<0\qquad\textup{and}\qquad c_{M}^{\uparrow}(\pi|\phi)=-Mn_{M}(\pi^{1})+c_{M-1}^{\uparrow}(\pi|\phi), 
 
 
 
 which implies that c M ↑ ​ ( π | ϕ ) = 0 c_{M}^{\uparrow}(\pi|\phi)=0 since 0 ∈ { c M → ​ ( π | ϕ ) , c M ↑ ​ ( π | ϕ ) } 0\in\{c_{M}^{\rightarrow}(\pi|\phi),c_{M}^{\uparrow}(\pi|\phi)\} . But then the above implies that c M − 1 ↑ ​ ( π | ϕ ) = 0 c_{M-1}^{\uparrow}(\pi|\phi)=0 and n M ​ ( π 1 ) = 0 n_{M}(\pi^{1})=0 . Therefore π 1 1 < ϕ 1 0 \pi_{1}^{1}<\phi^{0}_{1} , and so we have: 

 

 
 
 ∑ k = 1 ∞ ( n k ( ϕ 0 ) α 0 : k − n k ( π 1 ) α 1 : k ) \displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left(n_{k}(\phi^{0})\alpha_{0:k}-n_{k}(\pi^{1})\alpha_{1:k}\right) 
 = ∑ k = 1 M ( n k ( ϕ 0 ) α 0 : k − n k ( π 1 ) α 1 : k ) \displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{M}\left(n_{k}(\phi^{0})\alpha_{0:k}-n_{k}(\pi^{1})\alpha_{1:k}\right) 
 
 
 
 
 
 = ∑ k = 1 M ( n k ​ ( ϕ 0 ) ​ ( k ​ α 0 + ( k − 1 ) ​ α 1 ) − n k ​ ( π 1 ) ​ ( k ​ α 1 + ( k − 1 ) ​ α 0 ) ) \displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{M}\left(n_{k}(\phi^{0})(k\alpha_{0}+(k-1)\alpha_{1})-n_{k}(\pi^{1})(k\alpha_{1}+(k-1)\alpha_{0})\right) 
 
 
 
 
 
 = c M − 1 ↑ ​ ( π | ϕ ) ​ ( α 1 + α 0 ) + ∑ k = 1 M ( n k ​ ( π 1 ) + n k ​ ( ϕ 0 ) ) ​ α 0 = w ​ α 0 . \displaystyle=c_{M-1}^{\uparrow}(\pi|\phi)(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{0})+\sum_{k=1}^{M}(n_{k}(\pi^{1})+n_{k}(\phi^{0}))\alpha_{0}=w\alpha_{0}. 
 
 
 
 This completes the proof of (1). The proof of (2) is similar. For (3) note that 

 

 
 
 ∑ k = 1 ∞ ( n k ( π 1 ) α 1 : k + n k ( π 0 ) α 0 : k ) + | π δ | δ = θ = ∑ k = 1 ∞ ( n k ( ϕ 1 ) α 1 : k + n k ( ϕ 0 ) α 0 : k ) + | ϕ δ | δ \displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(n_{k}(\pi^{1})\alpha_{1:k}+n_{k}(\pi^{0})\alpha_{0:k})+|\pi^{\delta}|\delta=\theta=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(n_{k}(\phi^{1})\alpha_{1:k}+n_{k}(\phi^{0})\alpha_{0:k})+|\phi^{\delta}|\delta 
 
 
 
 since π , ϕ \pi,\phi are root partitions of θ \theta . But on the other hand by (1) and (2) we have that 

 

 
 
 ∑ k = 1 ∞ ( n k ( ϕ 1 ) α 1 : k + n k ( ϕ 0 ) α 0 : k ) − ∑ k = 1 ∞ ( n k ( π 1 ) α 1 : k + n k ( π 0 ) α 0 : k ) = w δ \displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(n_{k}(\phi^{1})\alpha_{1:k}+n_{k}(\phi^{0})\alpha_{0:k})-\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(n_{k}(\pi^{1})\alpha_{1:k}+n_{k}(\pi^{0})\alpha_{0:k})=w\delta 
 
 
 
 which implies that | π δ | = | ϕ δ | + w |\pi^{\delta}|=|\phi^{\delta}|+w . Then the result follows by Definition  4.2 (2). Part (4) follows by symmetry with (3). 



 
 For (5), we note that by (1) and (2), we have in either case that 

 

 
 
 ∑ k = 1 ∞ ( n k ( ϕ 1 ) α 1 : k + n k ( ϕ 0 ) α 0 : k ) − ∑ k = 1 ∞ ( n k ( π 1 ) α 1 : k + n k ( π 0 ) α 0 : k ) = 0 , \displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(n_{k}(\phi^{1})\alpha_{1:k}+n_{k}(\phi^{0})\alpha_{0:k})-\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(n_{k}(\pi^{1})\alpha_{1:k}+n_{k}(\pi^{0})\alpha_{0:k})=0, 
 
 
 
 which implies that | π δ | = | ϕ δ | |\pi^{\delta}|=|\phi^{\delta}| . Then the result follows by Definition  4.2 (2).
∎ 





 
 Now
recall the bijection mc : Ω ≥ 1 → 𝒫 \textup{mc}:\Omega_{\geq 1}\rightarrow\mathcal{P} from § 8.3 , given by
setting mc ​ ( μ ) = λ \textup{mc}(\mu)=\lambda , where n k ​ ( λ ) = μ k n_{k}(\lambda)=\mu_{k} for all k ∈ ℤ > 0 k\in\mathbbm{Z}_{>0} . We abuse notation to extend mc to a bijection:

 

 
 
 mc : 𝛀 ≥ 1 → 𝒫 2 , ( μ 1 , μ 0 ) ↦ ( λ 1 := mc ​ ( μ 1 ) , λ 0 := mc ​ ( μ 0 ) ) , \displaystyle\textup{mc}:\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{\geq 1}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}^{2},\qquad(\mu^{1},\mu^{0})\mapsto(\lambda^{1}:=\textup{mc}(\mu^{1}),\lambda^{0}:=\textup{mc}(\mu^{0})), 
 
 
 
 and write 𝒫 MV := mc ​ ( 𝛀 ≥ 1 MV ) \mathcal{P}^{\textup{MV}}:=\textup{mc}(\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{\geq 1}^{\textup{MV}}) for the image of the MV-bicompositions under this bijection.
In view of § 8.3 , the actions of 𝒢 ¯ i \overline{\mathcal{G}}^{i} , ℋ ¯ \overline{\mathcal{H}} on 𝛀 ≥ 1 MV \boldsymbol{\Omega}_{\geq 1}^{\textup{MV}} induce actions of operators 𝒢 ¯ 𝒫 2 i \overline{\mathcal{G}}^{i}_{\mathcal{P}^{2}} , ℋ ¯ 𝒫 2 \overline{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{P}^{2}} on 𝒫 MV \mathcal{P}^{\textup{MV}} , where

 

 
 
 𝒢 ¯ 𝒫 2 1 : ( λ 1 , λ 0 ) ↦ ( 𝒢 ¯ 𝒫 ​ λ 1 , 𝒢 𝒫 ​ λ 0 ) ; 𝒢 ¯ 𝒫 2 0 : ( λ 1 , λ 0 ) ↦ ( 𝒢 𝒫 ​ λ 1 , 𝒢 ¯ 𝒫 ​ λ 0 ) ; ℋ ¯ 𝒫 2 : ( λ 1 , λ 0 ) ↦ ( ℋ ¯ 𝒫 ​ λ 1 , ℋ ¯ 𝒫 ​ λ 0 ) . \displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{1}_{\mathcal{P}^{2}}:(\lambda^{1},\lambda^{0})\mapsto(\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathcal{P}}\lambda^{1},\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{P}}\lambda^{0});\qquad\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{0}_{\mathcal{P}^{2}}:(\lambda^{1},\lambda^{0})\mapsto(\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{P}}\lambda^{1},\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathcal{P}}\lambda^{0});\qquad\overline{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{P}^{2}}:(\lambda^{1},\lambda^{0})\mapsto(\overline{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{P}}\lambda^{1},\overline{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{P}}\lambda^{0}). 
 
 
 


 
 Lemma 9.2 . 

 
 Let ( λ 1 , λ 0 ) ∈ 𝒫 2 (\lambda^{1},\lambda^{0})\in\mathcal{P}^{2} , and set 𝚆 = p ⁡ ( λ 1 ) + p ⁡ ( λ 0 ) {\tt W}=p(\lambda^{1})+p(\lambda^{0}) . Then ( λ 1 , λ 0 ) ∈ 𝒫 MV (\lambda^{1},\lambda^{0})\in\mathcal{P}^{\textup{MV}} if and only if λ 0 = orth 𝚆 ​ ( λ 1 ) \lambda^{0}=\mathrm{orth}^{\tt W}(\lambda^{1}) . Moreover, if ( λ 1 , λ 0 ) ∈ 𝒫 MV (\lambda^{1},\lambda^{0})\in\mathcal{P}^{\textup{MV}} then we have 

 

 
 
 ( λ 1 , λ 0 ) = mc ​ ( 𝒥 ​ 𝝆 rev ) , \displaystyle(\lambda^{1},\lambda^{0})=\textup{mc}(\mathcal{J}\boldsymbol{\rho}^{\textup{rev}}), 
 
 
 
 where 𝛒 ∈ 𝚯 ⁡ ( 𝚆 ) \boldsymbol{\rho}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}({\tt W}) and ρ k = j \rho_{k}=j exactly when k k appears in the first column of 𝚜𝚙𝚊𝚛 ⁡ ( λ j ) {\tt spar}(\lambda^{j}) . 





 
 Proof.

 
 ( ⟹ ) (\implies) 
Assume ( λ 1 , λ 0 ) ∈ 𝒫 MV (\lambda^{1},\lambda^{0})\in\mathcal{P}^{\textup{MV}} . Then ( λ 1 , λ 0 ) = mc ​ ( 𝝁 ) (\lambda^{1},\lambda^{0})=\textup{mc}(\boldsymbol{\mu}) for some 𝝁 ∈ 𝛀 ≥ 1 MV ​ ( 𝚆 ) \boldsymbol{\mu}\in\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\textup{MV}}_{\geq 1}({\tt W}) .
Set 𝜺 = 𝒦 1 ​ 𝝁 \boldsymbol{\varepsilon}=\mathcal{K}^{1}\boldsymbol{\mu} . Then by Theorem  8.24 , we have that 

 

 
 
 ( λ 1 , λ 0 ) \displaystyle(\lambda^{1},\lambda^{0}) 
 = mc ( 𝝁 ) = mc ( 𝒥 𝜺 ) = mc ( 𝒥 𝒜 ε 𝚆 ⋯ 𝒜 ε 1 ∅ ) = mc ( 𝒢 ¯ ε 𝚆 ⋯ 𝒢 ¯ ε 1 𝒥 ∅ ) \displaystyle=\textup{mc}(\boldsymbol{\mu})=\textup{mc}(\mathcal{J}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})=\textup{mc}(\mathcal{J}\mathcal{A}^{\varepsilon_{\tt W}}\cdots\mathcal{A}^{\varepsilon_{1}}\varnothing)=\textup{mc}(\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{\varepsilon_{\tt W}}\cdots\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{\varepsilon_{1}}\mathcal{J}\varnothing) 
 
 
 
 
 
 = mc ( 𝒢 ¯ ε 𝚆 ⋯ 𝒢 ¯ ε 1 ( ∅ , ∅ ) ) = 𝒢 ¯ 𝒫 2 ε 𝚆 ⋯ 𝒢 ¯ 𝒫 2 ε 1 ( ∅ , ∅ ) = 𝒢 ¯ 𝒫 2 ρ 1 ⋯ 𝒢 ¯ 𝒫 2 ρ 𝚆 ( ∅ , ∅ ) , \displaystyle=\textup{mc}(\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{\varepsilon_{\tt W}}\cdots\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{\varepsilon_{1}}(\varnothing,\varnothing))=\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathcal{P}^{2}}^{\varepsilon_{\tt W}}\cdots\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathcal{P}^{2}}^{\varepsilon_{1}}(\varnothing,\varnothing)=\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathcal{P}^{2}}^{\rho_{1}}\cdots\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathcal{P}^{2}}^{\rho_{\tt W}}(\varnothing,\varnothing), 
 
 
 
 where 𝝆 := 𝜺 rev \boldsymbol{\rho}:=\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{\textup{rev}} . Then ( λ 1 , λ 0 ) = mc ​ ( 𝒥 ​ 𝝆 rev ) (\lambda^{1},\lambda^{0})=\textup{mc}(\mathcal{J}\boldsymbol{\rho}^{\textup{rev}}) is constructed by iteratively adding boxes to the empty bipartition. If one labels the boxes that are added in the application of 𝒢 ¯ 𝒫 2 ρ k \overline{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathcal{P}^{2}}^{\rho_{k}} with k k , it follows from § 8.3 (GP1), (GP2) and Proposition  8.6 that this yields exactly the spar tableau for each λ j \lambda^{j} , with k k in the first column of λ j \lambda^{j} exactly when ρ k = j \rho_{k}=j . Then the entries in the first columns of 𝚜𝚙𝚊𝚛 ⁡ ( λ 1 ) , 𝚜𝚙𝚊𝚛 ⁡ ( λ 0 ) {\tt spar}(\lambda^{1}),{\tt spar}(\lambda^{0}) are disjoint and their union is [ 1 , 𝚆 ] [1,{\tt W}] , so λ 0 = orth 𝚆 ​ ( λ 1 ) \lambda^{0}=\mathrm{orth}^{\tt W}(\lambda^{1}) , as desired. 



 
 ( ⟸ ) (\impliedby) In the other direction, assume λ 0 = orth 𝚆 ​ ( λ 1 ) \lambda^{0}=\mathrm{orth}^{\tt W}(\lambda^{1}) . Then we may define 𝝆 ∈ 𝚯 ⁡ ( 𝚆 ) \boldsymbol{\rho}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}({\tt W}) by setting ρ k = j \rho_{k}=j exactly when k k appears in the first column of 𝚜𝚙𝚊𝚛 ⁡ ( λ j ) {\tt spar}(\lambda^{j}) . It follows then as in the remarks above that ( λ 1 , λ 0 ) = mc ​ ( 𝒥 ​ 𝝆 rev ) (\lambda^{1},\lambda^{0})=\textup{mc}(\mathcal{J}\boldsymbol{\rho}^{\textup{rev}}) , which implies that ( λ 1 , λ 0 ) ∈ 𝒫 MV (\lambda^{1},\lambda^{0})\in\mathcal{P}^{\textup{MV}} since 𝒥 ​ 𝝆 rev ∈ 𝛀 ≥ 1 MV \mathcal{J}\boldsymbol{\rho}^{\textup{rev}}\in\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{\geq 1}^{\textup{MV}} by Theorem  8.24 .
∎ 





 
 Now we are in position to provide a tableau-theoretic description of affine MV-polytopes, which gives a combinatorial description of the lozenge map.



 
 Theorem 9.3 . 

 
 Let π , ϕ \pi,\phi be root partitions. Write 𝚆 = p ⁡ ( π 1 ) + p ⁡ ( ϕ 0 ) {\tt W}=p(\pi^{1})+p(\phi^{0}) and 𝚆 ′ = p ⁡ ( π 0 ) + p ⁡ ( ϕ 1 ) {\tt W}^{\prime}=p(\pi^{0})+p(\phi^{1}) . Then ( π | ϕ ) ∈ ℳ ​ 𝒱 (\pi|\phi)\in\mathcal{MV} if and only if: 

 
 (1) 
 
 𝚆 = 𝚆 ′ {\tt W}={\tt W}^{\prime} ; 




 (2) 
 
 orth 𝚆 ​ ( π 1 ) = ϕ 0 \mathrm{orth}^{\tt W}(\pi^{1})=\phi^{0} and orth 𝚆 ​ ( π 0 ) = ϕ 1 \mathrm{orth}^{\tt W}(\pi^{0})=\phi^{1} ; 




 (3) 
 
 We have 

 

 
 
 ϕ δ = { ( 𝚆 , π 1 δ , … , π p ⁡ ( π δ ) δ ) if  ​ 𝚆 = 𝚠 ⁡ ( π 0 ) = 𝚠 ⁡ ( π 1 ) ; ( π 2 δ , … , π p ⁡ ( π δ ) δ ) if  ​ 𝚆 > 𝚠 ⁡ ( π 0 ) , 𝚠 ⁡ ( π 1 ) ; π δ otherwise . \displaystyle\phi^{\delta}=\begin{cases}({\tt W},\pi_{1}^{\delta},\ldots,\pi_{p(\pi^{\delta})}^{\delta})&\textup{if }{\tt W}={\tt w}(\pi^{0})={\tt w}(\pi^{1});\\
(\pi^{\delta}_{2},\ldots,\pi^{\delta}_{p(\pi^{\delta})})&\textup{if }{\tt W}>{\tt w}(\pi^{0}),{\tt w}(\pi^{1});\\
\pi^{\delta}&\textup{otherwise}.\end{cases} 
 
 
 



 




 
 Proof.

 
 It will suffice to demonstrate the ‘only if’ direction, thanks to Theorem  4.4 . Assume ( π | ϕ ) ∈ ℳ ​ 𝒱 ​ ( θ ) (\pi|\phi)\in\mathcal{MV}(\theta) for some θ ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 ​ I \theta\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{\geq 0}I . Then since π , ϕ ∈ Π ⁡ ( θ ) \pi,\phi\in\Pi(\theta) we have 

 

 
 
 Λ 1 ​ ( θ ) = p ⁡ ( π 1 ) − p ⁡ ( π 0 ) = p ⁡ ( ϕ 1 ) − p ⁡ ( ϕ 0 ) , \displaystyle\Lambda_{1}(\theta)=p(\pi^{1})-p(\pi^{0})=p(\phi^{1})-p(\phi^{0}), 
 
 
 
 which implies (1). Now, define 𝝁 , 𝝂 ∈ 𝛀 ≥ 1 ​ ( 𝚆 ) \boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{\geq 1}({\tt W}) by setting 

 

 
 
 μ 1 = mc − 1 ​ ( π 1 ) ; μ 0 = mc − 1 ​ ( ϕ 0 ) ; ν 1 = mc − 1 ​ ( ϕ 1 ) ; ν 0 = mc − 1 ​ ( π 0 ) . \displaystyle\mu^{1}=\textup{mc}^{-1}(\pi^{1});\qquad\mu^{0}=\textup{mc}^{-1}(\phi^{0});\qquad\nu^{1}=\textup{mc}^{-1}(\phi^{1});\qquad\nu^{0}=\textup{mc}^{-1}(\pi^{0}). 
 
 
 
 By Definition  4.2 (1) we have that 

 

 
 
 0 ∈ { c k ↑ ​ ( π | ϕ ) , c k → ​ ( π | ϕ ) } ⊆ ℤ ≤ 0 ; and 0 ∈ { c k ↓ ​ ( π | ϕ ) , c k ← ​ ( π | ϕ ) } ⊆ ℤ ≤ 0 , \displaystyle 0\in\{c^{\uparrow}_{k}(\pi|\phi),c^{\rightarrow}_{k}(\pi|\phi)\}\subseteq{\mathbbm{Z}}_{\leq 0};\qquad\textup{and}\qquad 0\in\{c^{\downarrow}_{k}(\pi|\phi),c^{\leftarrow}_{k}(\pi|\phi)\}\subseteq{\mathbbm{Z}}_{\leq 0}, 
 
 
 
 for all k ∈ ℤ > 0 k\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{>0} , which, translating through the bijection mc and considering Definition  8.15 gives us that 𝝁 , 𝝂 ∈ 𝛀 ≥ 1 MV \boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\textup{MV}}_{\geq 1} . Thus (2) follows from Lemma  9.2 . 



 
 Finally, we confirm (3), in three cases.
 



 
 Case 1. Assume first that 𝚠 ⁡ ( π 1 ) , 𝚠 ⁡ ( π 0 ) < 𝚆 {\tt w}(\pi^{1}),{\tt w}(\pi^{0})<{\tt W} . Note that by Lemma  9.2 we have ( π 1 , ϕ 0 ) = mc ​ ( 𝒥 ​ 𝝆 rev ) (\pi^{1},\phi^{0})=\textup{mc}(\mathcal{J}\boldsymbol{\rho}^{\textup{rev}}) for some 𝝆 ∈ 𝚯 ⁡ ( 𝚆 ) \boldsymbol{\rho}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}({\tt W}) , where
 ρ 𝚆 = 0 \rho_{\tt W}=0 since 𝚠 ⁡ ( π 1 ) < 𝚆 {\tt w}(\pi^{1})<{\tt W} . Thus ρ 1 rev = 0 \rho^{\textup{rev}}_{1}=0 , and so it follows from Lemma  8.25 that π 1 1 < ϕ 1 0 \pi^{1}_{1}<\phi^{0}_{1} . In similar fashion we see that π 1 0 < ϕ 1 1 \pi^{0}_{1}<\phi^{1}_{1} . But then by Lemma  9.1 we have that

 

 
 
 π δ = ( 𝚆 , ϕ 1 δ , … , ϕ p ⁡ ( ϕ δ ) δ ) and so ϕ δ = ( π 2 δ , … , π p ⁡ ( π δ ) δ ) . \displaystyle\pi^{\delta}=({\tt W},\phi_{1}^{\delta},\ldots,\phi^{\delta}_{p(\phi^{\delta})})\qquad\textup{and so}\qquad\phi^{\delta}=(\pi^{\delta}_{2},\ldots,\pi^{\delta}_{p(\pi^{\delta})}). 
 
 
 


 
 Case 2. Assume that 𝚠 ⁡ ( π 1 ) = 𝚠 ⁡ ( π 0 ) = 𝚆 {\tt w}(\pi^{1})={\tt w}(\pi^{0})={\tt W} . Then we have 𝚠 ⁡ ( ϕ 1 ) , 𝚠 ⁡ ( ϕ 0 ) < 𝚆 {\tt w}(\phi^{1}),{\tt w}(\phi^{0})<{\tt W} . Then appealing to symmetry and swapping π , ϕ \pi,\phi in the Case 1 argument above, we have

 

 
 
 ϕ δ = ( 𝚆 , π 1 δ , … , π p ⁡ ( π δ ) δ ) and π δ = ( ϕ 2 δ , … , ϕ p ⁡ ( ϕ δ ) δ ) . \displaystyle\phi^{\delta}=({\tt W},\pi_{1}^{\delta},\ldots,\pi^{\delta}_{p(\pi^{\delta})})\qquad\textup{and}\qquad\pi^{\delta}=(\phi^{\delta}_{2},\ldots,\phi^{\delta}_{p(\phi^{\delta})}). 
 
 
 


 
 Case 3. Assume 𝚠 ⁡ ( π 1 ) < 𝚆 {\tt w}(\pi^{1})<{\tt W} and 𝚠 ⁡ ( π 0 ) = 𝚆 {\tt w}(\pi^{0})={\tt W} , or 𝚠 ⁡ ( π 1 ) = 𝚆 {\tt w}(\pi^{1})={\tt W} and 𝚠 ⁡ ( π 0 ) < 𝚆 {\tt w}(\pi^{0})<{\tt W} . 
Then again as in the cases above, we see that either π 1 1 < ϕ 1 0 \pi^{1}_{1}<\phi^{0}_{1} and ϕ 1 1 < π 1 0 \phi^{1}_{1}<\pi^{0}_{1} , or π 1 1 > ϕ 1 0 \pi^{1}_{1}>\phi^{0}_{1} and ϕ 1 1 > π 1 0 \phi^{1}_{1}>\pi^{0}_{1} . Then we have by Lemma  9.1 that ϕ δ = π δ \phi^{\delta}=\pi^{\delta} , as desired.
∎







 
 9.2. Establishing the crystal isomorphisms in § 7.1 


 
 Recall now the definition of upper ledge diagrams in § 6 and the functions 𝒳 , 𝒴 \mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y} and associated combinatorial data introduced in § 7.1 . For an upper ledge diagram 𝖣 ∈ 𝒰 ​ ℒ \mathsf{D}\in\mathcal{UL} , we will write 𝜺 ↑ ​ ( 𝖣 ) \boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{\uparrow}(\mathsf{D}) (resp. 𝜺 ↓ ​ ( 𝖣 ) \boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{\downarrow}(\mathsf{D}) ) for the colors of top (resp. bottom) boxes in 𝖣 \mathsf{D} , read from left to right.



 
 Lemma 9.4 . 

 
 Let 𝖣 ∈ 𝒰 ​ ℒ \mathsf{D}\in\mathcal{UL} . Then ( 𝒳 1 ​ ( 𝖣 ) ∣ 𝒳 0 ​ ( 𝖣 ) ) ∈ ℳ ​ 𝒱 (\mathcal{X}_{1}(\mathsf{D})\mid\mathcal{X}_{0}(\mathsf{D}))\in\mathcal{MV} , and so the map 

 

 
 
 𝒳 : 𝒰 ​ ℒ → ℳ ​ 𝒱 , 𝖣 ↦ ( 𝒳 1 ​ ( 𝖣 ) ∣ 𝒳 0 ​ ( 𝖣 ) ) \displaystyle\mathcal{X}:\mathcal{UL}\rightarrow\mathcal{MV},\qquad\mathsf{D}\mapsto(\mathcal{X}_{1}(\mathsf{D})\mid\mathcal{X}_{0}(\mathsf{D})) 
 
 
 
 is well-defined. 





 
 Proof.

 
 For a given upper ledge diagram 𝖣 \mathsf{D} , it is straightforward to translate between the 1-triple upper ledge diagram decomposition trip 1 ​ ( 𝖣 ) = ( 𝖣 1 ↑ , 𝖣 1 δ , 𝖣 1 ↓ ) \mathrm{trip}_{1}(\mathsf{D})=(\mathsf{D}^{\uparrow}_{1},\mathsf{D}^{\delta}_{1},\mathsf{D}^{\downarrow}_{1}) and the 0-triple upper ledge diagram decomposition trip 0 ​ ( 𝖣 ) = ( 𝖣 0 ↑ , 𝖣 0 δ , 𝖣 0 ↓ ) \mathrm{trip}_{0}(\mathsf{D})=(\mathsf{D}^{\uparrow}_{0},\mathsf{D}^{\delta}_{0},\mathsf{D}^{\downarrow}_{0}) for 𝖣 \mathsf{D} .
Writing 𝚆 {\tt W} for the length of a peak row of 𝖣 \mathsf{D} , we have the following: 

 
 (a) 
 
 If 𝜺 ↑ ​ ( 𝖣 ) 𝚆 = 𝜺 ↓ ​ ( 𝖣 ) 𝚆 = i \boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{\uparrow}(\mathsf{D})_{\tt W}=\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{\downarrow}(\mathsf{D})_{\tt W}=i ,
then

 
 • 
 
 𝖣 i ↑ \mathsf{D}_{i}^{\uparrow} is achieved by adding a 𝚆 {\tt W} -length row of color i i to the bottom of 𝖣 i ^ ↑ \mathsf{D}_{\widehat{i}}^{\uparrow} ;




 • 
 
 𝖣 i ↓ \mathsf{D}_{i}^{\downarrow} is achieved by deleting a 𝚆 {\tt W} -length row of color i i from the top of 𝖣 i ^ ↓ \mathsf{D}_{\widehat{i}}^{\downarrow} ;




 • 
 
 𝖣 i δ \mathsf{D}_{i}^{\delta} and 𝖣 i ^ δ \mathsf{D}_{\widehat{i}}^{\delta} have identical shape, with opposite colors.




 



 (b) 
 
 If 𝜺 ↑ ​ ( 𝖣 ) 𝚆 = i \boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{\uparrow}(\mathsf{D})_{\tt W}=i and 𝜺 ↓ ​ ( 𝖣 ) 𝚆 = i ^ \boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{\downarrow}(\mathsf{D})_{\tt W}=\widehat{i} ,
then

 
 • 
 
 𝖣 i ↑ \mathsf{D}_{i}^{\uparrow} is achieved by adding a 𝚆 {\tt W} -length row of color i i to the bottom of 𝖣 i ^ ↑ \mathsf{D}_{\widehat{i}}^{\uparrow} ;




 • 
 
 𝖣 i ↓ \mathsf{D}_{i}^{\downarrow} is achieved by adding a 𝚆 {\tt W} -length row of color i ^ \widehat{i} to the top of 𝖣 i ^ ↓ \mathsf{D}_{\widehat{i}}^{\downarrow} ;




 • 
 
 𝖣 i δ \mathsf{D}_{i}^{\delta} is achieved by deleting the top two ( 𝚆 {\tt W} -length) rows of 𝖣 i ^ δ \mathsf{D}_{\widehat{i}}^{\delta} .




 



 


 
 Write π = 𝒳 1 ​ ( 𝖣 ) \pi=\mathcal{X}_{1}(\mathsf{D}) and ϕ = 𝒳 0 ​ ( 𝖣 ) \phi=\mathcal{X}_{0}(\mathsf{D}) . In any case, it follows from (a) and (b) above that the k k th column of 𝖣 i ↑ \mathsf{D}^{\uparrow}_{i} (resp. 𝖣 i ↓ \mathsf{D}^{\downarrow}_{i} ) will have odd length if and only if the k k th column of 𝖣 i ^ ↑ \mathsf{D}^{\uparrow}_{\widehat{i}} (resp. 𝖣 i ^ ↓ \mathsf{D}^{\downarrow}_{\widehat{i}} ) has even length. Therefore we have 

 

 
 (9.1) 
 
 ϕ 0 = calc ∘ forget ​ ( 𝖣 0 ↑ ) = orth 𝚆 ​ ( calc ∘ forget ​ ( 𝖣 1 ↑ ) ) = orth 𝚆 ​ ( π 1 ) \displaystyle\phi^{0}=\mathrm{calc}\circ\textup{forget}(\mathsf{D}^{\uparrow}_{0})=\mathrm{orth}^{\tt W}(\mathrm{calc}\circ\textup{forget}(\mathsf{D}^{\uparrow}_{1}))=\mathrm{orth}^{\tt W}(\pi^{1}) 
 
 
 
 and 

 

 
 (9.2) 
 
 ϕ 1 = calc ∘ forget ​ ( 𝖣 0 ↓ ) = orth 𝚆 ​ ( calc ∘ forget ​ ( 𝖣 1 ↓ ) ) = orth 𝚆 ​ ( π 0 ) . \displaystyle\phi^{1}=\mathrm{calc}\circ\textup{forget}(\mathsf{D}^{\downarrow}_{0})=\mathrm{orth}^{\tt W}(\mathrm{calc}\circ\textup{forget}(\mathsf{D}^{\downarrow}_{1}))=\mathrm{orth}^{\tt W}(\pi^{0}). 
 
 
 


 
 We now consider four possible cases.
 



 
 Case 1. Assume first that 𝛆 ↑ ​ ( 𝖣 ) 𝚆 = 𝛆 ↓ ​ ( 𝖣 ) 𝚆 = 1 {\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{\uparrow}(\mathsf{D})_{\tt W}=\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{\downarrow}(\mathsf{D})_{\tt W}=1 . It follows then that the 𝚆 {\tt W} th (last) column of 𝖣 1 ↑ \mathsf{D}^{\uparrow}_{1} has length one, so 𝚆 {\tt W} appears in the first column of the spar tableau for π 1 = calc ∘ forget ​ ( 𝖣 1 ↑ ) \pi^{1}=\mathrm{calc}\circ\textup{forget}(\mathsf{D}^{\uparrow}_{1}) , and so 𝚠 ⁡ ( π 1 ) = 𝚆 {\tt w}(\pi^{1})={\tt W} .
We also have that the 𝚆 {\tt W} th (last) column of 𝖣 0 ↓ \mathsf{D}^{\downarrow}_{0} has length one, so 𝚆 {\tt W} appears in the first column of the spar tableau for ϕ 1 = calc ∘ forget ​ ( 𝖣 0 ↓ ) \phi^{1}=\mathrm{calc}\circ\textup{forget}(\mathsf{D}^{\downarrow}_{0}) , and so 𝚠 ⁡ ( ϕ 1 ) = 𝚆 {\tt w}(\phi^{1})={\tt W} . Since π 0 = orth 𝚆 ​ ( ϕ 1 ) \pi^{0}=\mathrm{orth}^{\tt W}(\phi^{1}) , this implies that 𝚠 ⁡ ( π 0 ) < 𝚆 {\tt w}(\pi^{0})<{\tt W} . We also have that

 

 
 (9.3) 
 
 π δ = halve ∘ forget ​ ( 𝖣 1 δ ) = halve ∘ forget ​ ( 𝖣 0 δ ) = ϕ δ \displaystyle\pi^{\delta}=\textup{halve}\circ\textup{forget}(\mathsf{D}^{\delta}_{1})=\textup{halve}\circ\textup{forget}(\mathsf{D}^{\delta}_{0})=\phi^{\delta} 
 
 
 
 by (a) above. Since 𝚠 ⁡ ( π 1 ) = 𝚆 > 𝚠 ⁡ ( π 0 ) {\tt w}(\pi^{1})={\tt W}>{\tt w}(\pi^{0}) , it follows by Theorem  9.3 and ( 9.1 ), ( 9.2 ), ( 9.3 ) that ( π | ϕ ) ∈ ℳ ​ 𝒱 (\pi|\phi)\in\mathcal{MV} .
 



 
 Case 2. Assume that 𝛆 ↑ ​ ( 𝖣 ) 𝚆 = 𝛆 ↓ ​ ( 𝖣 ) 𝚆 = 0 {\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{\uparrow}(\mathsf{D})_{\tt W}=\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{\downarrow}(\mathsf{D})_{\tt W}=0 . This case plays out similar to Case 1, where now 𝚠 ⁡ ( π 0 ) = 𝚆 < 𝚠 ⁡ ( π 1 ) {\tt w}(\pi^{0})={\tt W}<{\tt w}(\pi^{1}) and ( 9.3 ) still holds, so ( π | ϕ ) ∈ ℳ ​ 𝒱 (\pi|\phi)\in\mathcal{MV} .
 



 
 Case 3. Assume that 𝛆 ↑ ​ ( 𝖣 ) 𝚆 = 1 , 𝛆 ↓ ​ ( 𝖣 ) 𝚆 = 0 {\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{\uparrow}(\mathsf{D})_{\tt W}=1,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{\downarrow}(\mathsf{D})_{\tt W}=0 . It follows as in Case 1 that 𝚠 ⁡ ( π 1 ) = 𝚆 {\tt w}(\pi^{1})={\tt W} and 𝚠 ⁡ ( π 0 ) = 𝚆 {\tt w}(\pi^{0})={\tt W} . We also have by (b) above that π δ = halve ∘ forget ​ ( 𝖣 1 δ ) \pi^{\delta}=\textup{halve}\circ\textup{forget}(\mathsf{D}^{\delta}_{1}) is achieved by deleting the top ( 𝚆 {\tt W} -length) row of halve ∘ forget ​ ( 𝖣 0 δ ) = ϕ δ \textup{halve}\circ\textup{forget}(\mathsf{D}^{\delta}_{0})=\phi^{\delta} . Then again it follows from Theorem  9.3 that ( π | ϕ ) ∈ ℳ ​ 𝒱 (\pi|\phi)\in\mathcal{MV} .
 



 
 Case 4. Assume that 𝛆 ↑ ​ ( 𝖣 ) 𝚆 = 0 , 𝛆 ↓ ​ ( 𝖣 ) 𝚆 = 1 {\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{\uparrow}(\mathsf{D})_{\tt W}=0,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{\downarrow}(\mathsf{D})_{\tt W}=1 . It follows as in Case 1 that 𝚠 ⁡ ( ϕ 1 ) = 𝚆 {\tt w}(\phi^{1})={\tt W} and 𝚠 ⁡ ( ϕ 0 ) = 𝚆 {\tt w}(\phi^{0})={\tt W} , and therefore 𝚠 ⁡ ( π 1 ) , 𝚠 ⁡ ( π 0 ) < 𝚆 {\tt w}(\pi^{1}),{\tt w}(\pi^{0})<{\tt W} by ( 9.1 ), ( 9.2 ). We also have by (b) above that ϕ δ = halve ∘ forget ​ ( 𝖣 1 δ ) \phi^{\delta}=\textup{halve}\circ\textup{forget}(\mathsf{D}^{\delta}_{1}) is achieved by deleting the top ( 𝚆 {\tt W} -length) row of halve ∘ forget ​ ( 𝖣 0 δ ) = π δ \textup{halve}\circ\textup{forget}(\mathsf{D}^{\delta}_{0})=\pi^{\delta} . Then again it follows from Theorem  9.3 that ( π | ϕ ) ∈ ℳ ​ 𝒱 (\pi|\phi)\in\mathcal{MV} .
∎





 
 Lemma 9.5 . 

 
 For ( π | ϕ ) ∈ ℳ ​ 𝒱 (\pi|\phi)\in\mathcal{MV} , we have 𝒴 1 ​ ( π ) = 𝒴 0 ​ ( ϕ ) \mathcal{Y}_{1}(\pi)=\mathcal{Y}_{0}(\phi) , and therefore the map 

 

 
 
 𝒴 : ℳ ​ 𝒱 → 𝒰 ​ ℒ , 𝒴 ⁡ ( π | ϕ ) = 𝒴 1 ​ ( π ) = 𝒴 0 ​ ( ϕ ) \displaystyle\mathcal{Y}:\mathcal{MV}\rightarrow\mathcal{UL},\qquad\mathcal{Y}(\pi|\phi)=\mathcal{Y}_{1}(\pi)=\mathcal{Y}_{0}(\phi) 
 
 
 
 is well-defined. 





 
 Proof.

 
 For a given i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in\mathbbm{Z}_{2} , it is clear that 𝒴 i : Π → 𝒰 ​ ℒ \mathcal{Y}_{i}:\Pi\rightarrow\mathcal{UL} and 𝒳 i : 𝒰 ​ ℒ → Π \mathcal{X}_{i}:\mathcal{UL}\rightarrow\Pi are mutual inverses since ord \mathrm{ord} / calc \mathrm{calc} and stack i \mathrm{stack}_{i} / trip i \mathrm{trip}_{i} are mutual inverses. Thus we have that ( π | ϕ ) = ( 𝒳 1 ​ 𝒴 1 ​ π | 𝒳 0 ​ 𝒴 0 ​ ϕ ) . (\pi|\phi)=(\mathcal{X}_{1}\mathcal{Y}_{1}\pi\,|\,\mathcal{X}_{0}\mathcal{Y}_{0}\phi). 
On the other hand by Lemma  9.4 we have that
 ( 𝒳 1 ​ 𝒴 1 ​ π | 𝒳 0 ​ 𝒴 1 ​ π ) ∈ ℳ ​ 𝒱 (\mathcal{X}_{1}\mathcal{Y}_{1}\pi\,|\,\mathcal{X}_{0}\mathcal{Y}_{1}\pi)\in\mathcal{MV} .
Therefore
 𝒳 0 ​ 𝒴 0 ​ ϕ = ( 𝒳 1 ​ 𝒴 1 ​ π ) ◊ = 𝒳 0 ​ 𝒴 1 ​ π \mathcal{X}_{0}\mathcal{Y}_{0}\phi=(\mathcal{X}_{1}\mathcal{Y}_{1}\pi)_{\lozenge}=\mathcal{X}_{0}\mathcal{Y}_{1}\pi 
by Theorem  4.4 ,
which implies that 𝒴 0 ​ ϕ = 𝒴 1 ​ π \mathcal{Y}_{0}\phi=\mathcal{Y}_{1}\pi since 𝒳 0 \mathcal{X}_{0} is injective.
∎ 





 
 Theorem 9.6 . 

 
 The maps 𝒳 : 𝒰 ​ ℒ → ℳ ​ 𝒱 \mathcal{X}:\mathcal{UL}\rightarrow\mathcal{MV} and 𝒴 : ℳ ​ 𝒱 → 𝒰 ​ ℒ \mathcal{Y}:\mathcal{MV}\rightarrow\mathcal{UL} are mutually inverse 𝙰 1 ( 1 ) {\tt A}_{1}^{(1)} -bicrystal isomorphisms. 





 
 Proof.

 
 That the maps are inverse functions is clear thanks to Lemmas  9.4 and 9.5 , since 𝒳 i \mathcal{X}_{i} and 𝒴 i \mathcal{Y}_{i} are inverse functions, as noted in the proof of Lemma  9.5 . It thus remains to check that 𝒳 \mathcal{X} is a bicrystal map.
We will show that f 0 ​ 𝒳 ​ ( 𝖣 ) = 𝒳 ⁡ ( f 0 ​ 𝖣 ) f_{0}\mathcal{X}(\mathsf{D})=\mathcal{X}(f_{0}\mathsf{D}) ; the proofs that f 1 , f 1 ∗ , f 0 ∗ f_{1},f_{1}^{*},f_{0}^{*} commute with 𝒳 \mathcal{X} follow in a similar fashion.
Write 𝖣 ~ := f 0 ​ 𝖣 \widetilde{\mathsf{D}}:=f_{0}\mathsf{D} , π = 𝒳 1 ​ ( 𝖣 ) \pi=\mathcal{X}_{1}(\mathsf{D}) , and ϖ = 𝒳 1 ​ ( 𝖣 ~ ) \varpi=\mathcal{X}_{1}(\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}) . In view of § 4.3 , it suffices to check that ϖ = π + 0 : 1 \varpi=\pi_{+0:1} . 



 
 Recall that 𝖣 ~ \widetilde{\mathsf{D}} is achieved by adding a box colored 0 0 to the bottom of the leftmost unarced 1 1 -bottom box in 𝖣 ⁡ [ 0 ] \mathsf{D}[0] , if it exists, and otherwise by appending a box to the right end of the uppermost 0 0 -peak row in 𝖣 \mathsf{D} . In either case, the new box is an unarced 0 0 -bottom box in 𝖣 ~ ​ [ 0 ] \widetilde{\mathsf{D}}[0] , and all other bottom boxes are arced in 𝖣 ~ ​ [ 0 ] \widetilde{\mathsf{D}}[0] if and only if they were arced in 𝖣 ⁡ [ 0 ] \mathsf{D}[0] .
It immediately follows that trip 1 ​ ( 𝖣 ~ ) = ( 𝖣 1 ↑ , 𝖣 1 δ , f 0 ​ 𝖣 1 ↓ ) \mathrm{trip}_{1}(\widetilde{\mathsf{D}})=(\mathsf{D}^{\uparrow}_{1},\mathsf{D}^{\delta}_{1},f_{0}\mathsf{D}^{\downarrow}_{1}) , and so ϖ 1 = π 1 \varpi^{1}=\pi^{1} and ϖ δ = π δ \varpi^{\delta}=\pi^{\delta} . 



 
 Write 𝝆 = 𝜺 ↓ ​ ( 𝖣 ) \boldsymbol{\rho}=\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{\downarrow}(\mathsf{D}) , 𝝆 ~ = 𝜺 ↓ ​ ( 𝖣 ~ ) \widetilde{\boldsymbol{\rho}}=\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{\downarrow}(\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}) .
It follows from definitions that the k k th bottom box from the left in 𝖣 \mathsf{D} is 

 
 (i) 
 
 1 1 -colored and unarced in 𝖣 ⁡ [ 0 ] \mathsf{D}[0] if and only if k ∈ P 1 L ​ ( 𝝆 rev ) k\in\textup{P}^{\textup{L}}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\rho}^{\textup{rev}}) ;




 (ii) 
 
 0 0 -colored and unarced in 𝖣 ⁡ [ 0 ] \mathsf{D}[0] if and only if k ∈ P 0 R ​ ( 𝝆 rev ) k\in\textup{P}^{\textup{R}}_{0}(\boldsymbol{\rho}^{\textup{rev}}) ,




 
 and similarly for 𝖣 ~ \widetilde{\mathsf{D}} and 𝝆 ~ \widetilde{\boldsymbol{\rho}} .
Writing 𝝂 = 𝒥 ​ 𝝆 rev \boldsymbol{\nu}=\mathcal{J}\boldsymbol{\rho}^{\textup{rev}} and 𝝂 ~ = 𝒥 ​ 𝝆 ~ rev \widetilde{\boldsymbol{\nu}}=\mathcal{J}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{\textup{rev}} , we have by (i), (ii) and ( 8.6 ) that 

 

 
 
 ν ~ 1 0 = # ​ P 0 R ​ ( 𝝆 ~ rev ) = # ​ P 0 R ​ ( 𝝆 rev ) + 1 = ν 1 0 + 1 . \displaystyle\widetilde{\nu}_{1}^{0}=\#\textup{P}^{\textup{R}}_{0}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{\textup{rev}})=\#\textup{P}^{\textup{R}}_{0}(\boldsymbol{\rho}^{\textup{rev}})+1=\nu_{1}^{0}+1. 
 
 
 
 We moreover have by the above discussion and ( 8.4 ) that 𝒯 0 ​ 𝝆 ~ rev = 1 x ​ 𝒯 0 ​ 𝝆 rev \mathcal{T}_{0}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{\textup{rev}}=1^{x}\mathcal{T}_{0}\boldsymbol{\rho}^{\textup{rev}} , where x = 0 x=0 if the new box in 𝖣 ~ \widetilde{\mathsf{D}} was added below a 1 1 -bottom box in 𝖣 \mathsf{D} , and x = 1 x=1 if it was added to the uppermost 0 0 -peak row in 𝖣 \mathsf{D} (hence increasing the width of the diagram by one).
So it follows from ( 8.6 ) that ν ~ k 0 = ν k 0 \widetilde{\nu}_{k}^{0}=\nu_{k}^{0} for all k > 1 k>1 . Writing ( λ 1 , λ 0 ) = mc ​ ( 𝝂 ) (\lambda^{1},\lambda^{0})=\textup{mc}(\boldsymbol{\nu}) and ( λ ~ 1 , λ ~ 0 ) = mc ​ ( 𝝂 ~ ) (\widetilde{\lambda}^{1},\widetilde{\lambda}^{0})=\textup{mc}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\nu}}) , we have then that n 1 ​ ( λ ~ 0 ) = n 1 ​ ( λ 0 ) + 1 n_{1}(\widetilde{\lambda}^{0})=n_{1}(\lambda^{0})+1 , and n k ​ ( λ ~ 0 ) = n k ​ ( λ 0 ) n_{k}(\widetilde{\lambda}^{0})=n_{k}(\lambda^{0}) for k > 1 k>1 . By Lemma  9.2 , note that ρ k = j \rho_{k}=j (resp. ρ ~ k = j \widetilde{\rho}_{k}=j ) if and only if k k appears in the first column of 𝚜𝚙𝚊𝚛 ⁡ ( λ j ) {\tt spar}(\lambda^{j}) (resp. 𝚜𝚙𝚊𝚛 ⁡ ( λ ~ j ) {\tt spar}(\widetilde{\lambda}^{j}) ). 



 
 Now, we have π 0 = calc ⁡ ( 𝖣 1 ↓ ) \pi^{0}=\mathrm{calc}(\mathsf{D}^{\downarrow}_{1}) by definition. Then k k appears in the first column of 𝚜𝚙𝚊𝚛 ⁡ ( π 0 ) {\tt spar}(\pi^{0}) if and only if the k k th column of 𝖣 1 ↓ \mathsf{D}^{\downarrow}_{1} has odd length, which happens if and only if the k k th bottom box from the left in 𝖣 1 ↓ \mathsf{D}^{\downarrow}_{1} is colored 0, which happens if and only if 𝜺 ↓ ​ ( 𝖣 1 ↓ ) k = 0 \boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{\downarrow}(\mathsf{D}^{\downarrow}_{1})_{k}=0 . We further have 𝝆 = 𝜺 ↓ ​ ( 𝖣 ) = 𝜺 ↓ ​ ( 𝖣 1 ↓ ) ​ 1 y \boldsymbol{\rho}=\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{\downarrow}(\mathsf{D})=\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{\downarrow}(\mathsf{D}^{\downarrow}_{1})1^{y} for some y ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 y\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{\geq 0} . Thus ρ k = 0 \rho_{k}=0 if and only if k k appears in the first column of 𝚜𝚙𝚊𝚛 ⁡ ( π 0 ) {\tt spar}(\pi^{0}) . But then we have ord ⁡ ( π 0 ) = ord ⁡ ( λ 0 ) \mathrm{ord}(\pi^{0})=\mathrm{ord}(\lambda^{0}) , which implies π 0 = λ 0 \pi^{0}=\lambda^{0} since ord \mathrm{ord} is injective by Theorem  Theorem A . In similar fashion we see that ϖ 0 = λ ~ 0 \varpi^{0}=\widetilde{\lambda}^{0} , so it follows that
 n 1 ​ ( ϖ 0 ) = n 1 ​ ( π 0 ) + 1 n_{1}(\varpi^{0})=n_{1}(\pi^{0})+1 and n k ​ ( ϖ 0 ) = n k ​ ( π 0 ) n_{k}(\varpi^{0})=n_{k}(\pi^{0}) for k > 1 k>1 , so ϖ = π + 0 : 1 \varpi=\pi_{+0:1} . 



 
 Finally, the fact that the statistics wt , ε i \varepsilon_{i} , ε i ∗ \varepsilon^{*}_{i} , φ i \varphi_{i} , φ i ∗ \varphi^{*}_{i} commute with 𝒳 \mathcal{X} is easily checked by induction, taking advantage of the fact that 𝒰 ​ ℒ \mathcal{UL} , ℳ ​ 𝒱 \mathcal{MV} are highest weight.
∎ 







 
 9.3. Establishing the crystal isomorphisms in § 7.2 


 
 Recall now the maps 𝒵 𝜿 , 𝒲 𝜿 \mathcal{Z}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}},\mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}} , and associated gluing and splitting operations from § 7.2 .



 
 9.3.1. Freezing boxes in upper ledge diagrams


 
 Let 𝖣 ¯ = ( 𝖣 1 , … , 𝖣 m + 1 ) ∈ 𝒰 ​ ℒ m + 1 \underline{\mathsf{D}}=(\mathsf{D}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{D}_{m+1})\in\mathcal{UL}^{m+1} be a sequence of upper ledge diagrams. We will consider the diagrams arranged horizontally so 𝖣 t + 1 \mathsf{D}_{t+1} is to the right of 𝖣 t \mathsf{D}_{t} . Then the frozen boxes of 𝖣 ¯ \underline{\mathsf{D}} are defined as follows. For t ∈ [ 1 , m ] t\in[1,m] , let v t v_{t} be the rightmost box in the uppermost peak row in 𝖣 t \mathsf{D}_{t} . Then for each t = m , m − 1 , … , 1 t=m,m-1,\ldots,1 , freeze the nearest unfrozen bottom box to the right of v t v_{t} whose color is opposite v t v_{t} , and then freeze the nearest unfrozen bottom box to the right whose color is opposite that, and so on, greedily freezing the nearest unfrozen opposite-color bottom box to the right at each step until exhaustion. We say boxes frozen in the t t th step are t t -frozen when it is useful to be specific.



 
 Lemma 9.7 . 

 
 Let 𝖣 ∈ 𝒰 ​ ℒ \mathsf{D}\in\mathcal{UL} , i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in\mathbbm{Z}_{2} , and set 𝖣 ¯ = ( 𝖣 1 , 𝖣 2 ) = split i ​ ( 𝖣 ) \underline{\mathsf{D}}=(\mathsf{D}_{1},\mathsf{D}_{2})=\mathrm{split}^{i}(\mathsf{D}) . Then the unfrozen bottom boxes in ( 𝖣 1 , 𝖣 2 ) (\mathsf{D}_{1},\mathsf{D}_{2}) , read from left to right, are exactly the images under the split i \mathrm{split}^{i} map of the bottom boxes in 𝖣 \mathsf{D} , read from left to right. 





 
 Proof.

 
 The statement is trivial if 𝖣 1 \mathsf{D}_{1} or 𝖣 2 \mathsf{D}_{2} are empty, so let us assume otherwise.
We utilize the terminology from § 7.2.1 .
Let u u be the uppermost box of color i i in the first column of 𝖣 \mathsf{D} . Let r r be the ray extending directly southeast from the centroid of u u . Let 𝖫 \mathsf{L} be the set of boxes in 𝖣 \mathsf{D} which are weakly southwest of r r , and let 𝖴 \mathsf{U} be the set of boxes in 𝖣 \mathsf{D} which are strictly northeast of r r . Let 𝖴 ′ \mathsf{U}^{\prime} be the ledge diagram obtained by left-justifying all boxes in 𝖴 \mathsf{U} . Let 𝖴 ′′ = float ⁡ ( 𝖴 ′ ) \mathsf{U}^{\prime\prime}=\mathrm{float}(\mathsf{U}^{\prime}) . Then we have 𝖣 1 = 𝖫 \mathsf{D}_{1}=\mathsf{L} and 𝖣 2 = 𝖴 ′′ \mathsf{D}_{2}=\mathsf{U}^{\prime\prime} .
Let x x be the leftmost among the lowest row of boxes in 𝖴 \mathsf{U} , and let y y be the rightmost box in the uppermost peak row of 𝖣 \mathsf{D} . See Figure  18 for an example.
 



 
 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 Figure 18. At left, the upper ledge diagram 𝖣 \mathsf{D} . The diagram 𝖫 \mathsf{L} is shaded in blue, with the boxes lying on r r shaded in darker blue. The diagram 𝖴 \mathsf{U} is shaded red, B B shaded darker red, and the bottom-box members of B B shaded darkest red. At center is 𝖴 ′ \mathsf{U}^{\prime} , and at right is 𝖴 ′′ \mathsf{U}^{\prime\prime} —note that the darkest red boxes become the unfrozen bottom boxes in ( 𝖫 , 𝖴 ′′ ) (\mathsf{L},\mathsf{U}^{\prime\prime}) . 
 
 
 Claim 1. The box y y is weakly northeast of x x . Clearly y y is weakly east of x x . Assume by way of contradiction that x x is strictly north of y y , i.e., in a row above the uppermost peak row of 𝖣 \mathsf{D} . Then by the definition of upper ledge diagrams, the row below x x is necessarily longer than the row containing x x , so the box z z directly southeast of x x must be in 𝖣 \mathsf{D} . Since x x is strictly northeast of r r , this implies z z is strictly northeast of r r as well, so z ∈ 𝖴 z\in\mathsf{U} , in contradiction of the definition of x x .
 



 
 By Claim 1, there must be a path B B in 𝖣 \mathsf{D} from x x to y y given by stepping east whenever possible, and north otherwise. In fact, B B is contained in 𝖴 \mathsf{U} since every box traversed in B B is weakly northeast of x x , which is strictly northeast of r r . Write x = b 1 , b 2 , … , b m = y x=b_{1},b_{2},\ldots,b_{m}=y for the boxes in B B traversed in northeasterly order. If b 1 b_{1} is bottom, we define b 0 b_{0} to be the box to its left, and if b 1 b_{1} is non-bottom, we define b 0 b_{0} to be the box below it.
See again Figure  18 for an example.
 



 
 Claim 2. If b ∈ B b\in B , then there is no box directly southeast of b b in 𝖣 \mathsf{D} . 
We prove the claim for b t b_{t} by induction on t t . First note that if there were a box z z directly southeast of b 1 = x b_{1}=x , then since x x is strictly northeast of r r , z z would be as well, so z ∈ 𝖴 z\in\mathsf{U} , in contradiction of the definition of x x . Thus the claim holds for b 1 b_{1} .
Now assume the claim holds for b t b_{t} , for some 1 ≤ t < m 1\leq t<m . If b t + 1 b_{t+1} is east of b t b_{t} , then there is no box directly below b t + 1 b_{t+1} , and therefore there cannot be a box directly southeast of b t + 1 b_{t+1} by the definition of ledge diagrams. If b t + 1 b_{t+1} is north of b t b_{t} , then by the definition of the path B B , there is no box to the east of b t b_{t} , and thus again no box directly southeast of b t + 1 b_{t+1} .
 



 
 Claim 3. If z ∈ 𝖴 \ B z\in\mathsf{U}\backslash B , then there is a box directly southeast of z z in 𝖣 \mathsf{D} . Assume by way of contradiction that z ∈ 𝖴 \ B z\in\mathsf{U}\backslash B and there is not a box directly southeast of z z in 𝖣 \mathsf{D} . Let R R be the row containing z z . Note then that the row below R R is therefore weakly shorter than R R , so it must be that R R is weakly below the uppermost peak row of 𝖣 \mathsf{D} , and weakly above the row containing x x . Thus R R must contain some box b ∈ B b\in B . By the definition of the path B B , every box in R R to the east of b b is in B B , so it must be that z z is west of b b . Since there is no box directly to the southeast of z z in 𝖣 \mathsf{D} , it follows that no box in R R east of z z has a box below it. Since z ∉ B z\notin B , this implies that all steps in B B were eastward steps prior to b b , so b b must be in the same row as x x . By construction, the path B B contains all boxes in R R weakly east of x x , so it must be that z z is strictly west of x x , a contradiction of the definition of x x .
 



 
 Claim 4. The box b 0 b_{0} is the lowest box in 𝖣 \mathsf{D} which lies along r r . First note that b 0 b_{0} must be in 𝖫 \mathsf{L} by the definition of x x . Since x x is strictly northeast of r r , it must be then that b 0 b_{0} lies along r r . If x x is bottom, then b 0 b_{0} is to the left of x x and there is no box directly southeast of b 0 b_{0} , so b 0 b_{0} is lowest box in 𝖣 \mathsf{D} which lies along r r . Thus we may assume that b 0 b_{0} is non-bottom. Then b 0 b_{0} is directly below x x . There is no box directly southeast of b 1 b_{1} by Claim 2, so there is no box directly to the right of b 0 b_{0} . Then since the row containing b 0 b_{0} is weakly lower than the uppermost peak row of 𝖣 \mathsf{D} , it must be that there is no box directly to the southeast of b 0 b_{0} . Thus b 0 b_{0} is the lowest box in 𝖣 \mathsf{D} which lies along r r .
 



 
 Claim 5. Reading from left to right, the bottom boxes in 𝖴 ′ \mathsf{U}^{\prime} (and thus in 𝖴 ′′ \mathsf{U}^{\prime\prime} ) are exactly b 1 , … , b m b_{1},\ldots,b_{m} . 
Let z , w z,w be boxes in 𝖴 \mathsf{U} . Then w w is directly southeast of z z in 𝖴 \mathsf{U} if and only if w w is directly under z z in 𝖴 ′ \mathsf{U}^{\prime} , since the left justification process shifts every row in 𝖴 \mathsf{U} one notch to the right relative to the row under it. Then by Claims 1 and 2, a box z ∈ 𝖴 z\in\mathsf{U} will be bottom in 𝖴 ′ \mathsf{U}^{\prime} if and only if z ∈ B z\in B . Again, since 𝖴 ′ \mathsf{U}^{\prime} is obtained by shifting every row in 𝖴 \mathsf{U} one notch to the right relative to the row under it, any two boxes arranged such that one is weakly northeast of the other in 𝖴 \mathsf{U} will retain that relationship in 𝖴 ′ \mathsf{U}^{\prime} . Thus since b 1 , … , b m b_{1},\ldots,b_{m} are in weakly increasing order of northeasternness, they retain that relationship in 𝖴 ′ \mathsf{U}^{\prime} , completing the proof.
 



 
 Claim 6. For t ∈ [ 1 , m ] t\in[1,m] , the box b t b_{t} is bottom in 𝖣 \mathsf{D} if and only if b t − 1 b_{t-1} is of the same color. By construction, each b t b_{t} is either a north or east step from b t − 1 b_{t-1} , and thus b t b_{t} will have the same color as b t − 1 b_{t-1} if and only if it is an east step from b t − 1 b_{t-1} . Thus we must show that b t b_{t} is bottom if and only if b t b_{t} is an east step from b t − 1 b_{t-1} .
If b t b_{t} is a north step from b t − 1 b_{t-1} , then b t b_{t} is immediately not bottom. If t > 1 t>1 and b t b_{t} is an east step from b t − 1 b_{t-1} , then there is no box directly southeast of b t − 1 b_{t-1} by Claim 2, so b t b_{t} is bottom. Finally, assume b 1 b_{1} is an east step from b 0 b_{0} . Then by Claim 4 there is no box directly southeast of b 0 b_{0} , so again b 1 b_{1} is bottom. 
 



 
 Claim 7. The set of bottom boxes in 𝖣 \mathsf{D} which belong to 𝖴 \mathsf{U} , read from left to right in 𝖣 \mathsf{D} , is the subsequence of b 1 , … , b m b_{1},\ldots,b_{m} consisting of those b t b_{t} which match the color of b t − 1 b_{t-1} . Let z ∈ 𝖴 z\in\mathsf{U} be bottom in 𝖣 \mathsf{D} . Then since there is no box directly below z z , there can be no box directly to the southeast of z z , so z = b t z=b_{t} for some t ∈ [ 1 , m ] t\in[1,m] thanks to Claim 3. Thus the bottom boxes of 𝖣 \mathsf{D} which belong to 𝖴 \mathsf{U} are exactly those b t b_{t} which match the color of b t − 1 b_{t-1} by Claim 6, and thus the claim follows since the sequence b 1 , … , b m b_{1},\ldots,b_{m} proceeds in a northeasterly direction.

 



 
 Claim 8. The unfrozen bottom boxes in ( 𝖫 , 𝖴 ′′ ) (\mathsf{L},\mathsf{U}^{\prime\prime}) read from left to right are exactly the bottom boxes in 𝖣 \mathsf{D} read from left to right. Since no boxes are frozen in 𝖫 \mathsf{L} , it is enough to show that the bottom boxes in 𝖴 \mathsf{U} read from left to right are exactly the bottom boxes in 𝖴 ′′ \mathsf{U}^{\prime\prime} read from left to right. Note that since b 0 b_{0} is the lowest element of 𝖣 \mathsf{D} that lies along r r , we have that b 0 b_{0} is in the uppermost peak row of 𝖫 \mathsf{L} , and by Claim 5, the bottom boxes in 𝖴 ′′ \mathsf{U}^{\prime\prime} read from left to right are b 1 , … , b m b_{1},\ldots,b_{m} . Then by definition of the freezing process, the box b t b_{t} will be frozen in 𝖴 ′′ \mathsf{U}^{\prime\prime} if and only if b t b_{t} is of opposite color to b t − 1 b_{t-1} . Thus the unfrozen bottom boxes in 𝖴 ′′ \mathsf{U}^{\prime\prime} read from left to right is the subsequence of b 1 , … , b m b_{1},\ldots,b_{m} consisting of those b t b_{t} which match the color of b t − 1 b_{t-1} , and thus Claim 7 gives the result.
∎





 
 Let 𝜿 \boldsymbol{\kappa} be a multicharge, and let 𝖣 ¯ = ( 𝖣 1 , … , 𝖣 m ) ∈ 𝒰 ​ ℒ m \underline{\mathsf{D}}=(\mathsf{D}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{D}_{m})\in\mathcal{UL}^{m} . We define operators on such sequences:

 

 
 
 s 𝜿 ​ ( 𝖣 ¯ ) \displaystyle s^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\underline{\mathsf{D}}) 
 = ( 𝖣 1 , … , 𝖣 m − 1 , split κ m ​ ( 𝖣 m ) ↓ , split κ m ​ ( 𝖣 m ) ↑ ) ∈ 𝒰 ​ ℒ m + 1 ; \displaystyle=(\mathsf{D}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{D}_{m-1},\mathrm{split}^{\kappa_{m}}(\mathsf{D}_{m})_{\downarrow},\mathrm{split}^{\kappa_{m}}(\mathsf{D}_{m})_{\uparrow})\in\mathcal{UL}^{m+1}; 
 
 
 
 
 g 𝜿 ​ ( 𝖣 ¯ ) \displaystyle g^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\underline{\mathsf{D}}) 
 = ( 𝖣 1 , … , 𝖣 m − 2 , glue ( 𝖣 m − 1 , 𝖣 m ) ) ∈ 𝒰 ℒ m − 1 ( m > 1 ) . \displaystyle=(\mathsf{D}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{D}_{m-2},\mathrm{glue}(\mathsf{D}_{m-1},\mathsf{D}_{m}))\in\mathcal{UL}^{m-1}\qquad(m>1). 
 
 
 


 
 Lemma 9.8 . 

 
 Let 𝛋 \boldsymbol{\kappa} be a multicharge, and 𝖣 ∈ 𝒰 ​ ℒ \mathsf{D}\in\mathcal{UL} . Let 𝖣 ¯ = ( 𝖣 1 , … , 𝖣 m + 1 ) = ( s 𝛋 ) ∘ m ​ ( 𝖣 ) \underline{\mathsf{D}}=(\mathsf{D}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{D}_{m+1})=(s^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}})^{\circ m}(\mathsf{D}) . Then the unfrozen bottom boxes in 𝖣 ¯ \underline{\mathsf{D}} , read from left to right, are exactly the images under ( s 𝛋 ) ∘ m (s^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}})^{\circ m} of the bottom boxes in 𝖣 \mathsf{D} , read from left to right. 





 
 Proof.

 
 We go by induction on m m . The base case m = 0 m=0 is trivial, so assume m > 0 m>0 and the claim holds for m − 1 m-1 . Write 𝖢 ¯ = ( s 𝜿 ) ∘ ( m − 1 ) ​ ( 𝖣 ) \underline{\mathsf{C}}=(s^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}})^{\circ(m-1)}(\mathsf{D}) .
By Lemma  9.7 , the bottom boxes in 𝖣 ¯ \underline{\mathsf{D}} which are not m m -frozen, read from left to right, are exactly the images under s 𝜿 s^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}} of the bottom boxes in 𝖢 ¯ \underline{\mathsf{C}} , read from left to right.
Thus, by definition of the freezing process, the unfrozen bottom boxes in 𝖣 ¯ \underline{\mathsf{D}} , read from left to right, are the images under s 𝜿 s^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}} of the unfrozen bottom boxes in 𝖢 ¯ \underline{\mathsf{C}} , read from left to right.
By induction assumption, the unfrozen bottom boxes in 𝖢 ¯ \underline{\mathsf{C}} , read from left to right, are exactly the images under ( s 𝜿 ) ∘ ( m − 1 ) (s^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}})^{\circ(m-1)} of the bottom boxes in 𝖣 \mathsf{D} , read from left to right, which yields the result.
∎ 







 
 9.3.2. Frozen arc diagrams for multipartitions


 
 Let 𝜿 \boldsymbol{\kappa} be a multicharge, and 𝝀 ∈ ℳ ​ 𝒫 𝜿 \boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{MP}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}} , of level ℓ \ell . The frozen boxes of 𝝀 \boldsymbol{\lambda} are defined as follows. For t ∈ ℤ > 0 t\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{>0} , let a t a_{t} be the uppermost addable position in λ ( t ) \lambda^{(t)} , and let r t = res ​ ( a t ) r_{t}=\textup{res}(a_{t}) . We now iteratively freeze removable boxes in 𝝀 \boldsymbol{\lambda} : for each t = ℓ − 1 , ℓ − 2 , … , 1 t=\ell-1,\ell-2,\ldots,1 , freeze the nearest unfrozen removable box of residue r t r_{t} above a t a_{t} , and then freeze the nearest unfrozen removable box with residue r ^ t \widehat{r}_{t} above that, and so on, alternating residue at each step, until exhaustion. We say boxes frozen in the t t th step are t t - frozen when it is useful to be specific.



 
 Let i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in\mathbbm{Z}_{2} . We define the frozen i i -arc diagram 𝝀 ​ [ i ]                                                                                                                       \boldsymbol{\lambda}[i]^{\,\,\makebox[0.0pt]{\hbox to5.12pt{\vbox to5.12pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\>\lower-2.56064pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}{{}}{}
{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

 Proof.

 
 Say that 𝝀 \boldsymbol{\lambda} is of level ℓ \ell . Then we have that 𝝀 = 𝒲 𝜿 ​ ( 𝖣 ) \boldsymbol{\lambda}=\mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\mathsf{D}) is obtained by computing 𝖣 ¯ = ( s 𝜿 ) ∘ ℓ ​ ( 𝖣 ) \underline{\mathsf{D}}=(s^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}})^{\circ\ell}(\mathsf{D}) , and then shifting the k k th column in each component up by k − 1 k-1 notches. It is clear then that a box is bottom in 𝖣 ¯ \underline{\mathsf{D}} if and only if its image is removable in 𝝀 \boldsymbol{\lambda} , and a bottom box y y is right of a bottom box x x in 𝖣 ¯ \underline{\mathsf{D}} if and only if the image of y y is above the image of x x in 𝝀 \boldsymbol{\lambda} . Then, in consideration of the respective freezing processes and Lemma  9.8 , claims (1) and (2) immediately follow. Then (1), (2) immediately imply (3) and (4), since, reading left to right, the bottom boxes in 𝖣 \mathsf{D} appear in the same sequence as their images (the unfrozen removable boxes) appear in 𝝀 \boldsymbol{\lambda} , read bottom to top.
∎ 





 
 Lemma 9.10 . 

 
 Let 𝛋 \boldsymbol{\kappa} be a multicharge and 𝖣 ∈ 𝒰 ​ ℒ \mathsf{D}\in\mathcal{UL} . Let x x be a bottom box in 𝖣 \mathsf{D} and let x ~ \widetilde{x} be its image in 𝒲 𝛋 ​ ( 𝖣 ) \mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\mathsf{D}) . Assume 𝖣 ′ ∈ 𝒰 ​ ℒ \mathsf{D}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{UL} is obtained by removing x x from 𝖣 \mathsf{D} . Then 𝒲 𝛋 ​ ( 𝖣 ′ ) \mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\mathsf{D}^{\prime}) is obtained by removing x ~ \widetilde{x} from 𝒲 𝛋 ​ ( 𝖣 ) \mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\mathsf{D}) . 





 
 Proof.

 
 Let y y be a box in 𝖣 \mathsf{D} , and let ( 𝖣 1 , 𝖣 2 ) = split i ​ ( 𝖣 ) (\mathsf{D}_{1},\mathsf{D}_{2})=\mathrm{split}^{i}(\mathsf{D}) . It is straightforward to see from the definition of the split i \mathrm{split}^{i} map that whether y y lands in 𝖣 1 \mathsf{D}_{1} or 𝖣 2 \mathsf{D}_{2} , and what its position is relative to the northwesternmost box in the component in which it lands, is dependent only on the set of boxes in 𝖣 \mathsf{D} which are weakly northwest of y y . Since x x is bottom and 𝖣 ′ ∈ 𝒰 ​ ℒ \mathsf{D}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{UL} , it follows that x x is not weakly northwest of any other box in 𝖣 \mathsf{D} , so no other box’s position depends on x x . Thus we have that split i ​ ( 𝖣 ′ ) \mathrm{split}^{i}(\mathsf{D}^{\prime}) is obtained by deleting the image of x x from split i ​ ( 𝖣 ) \mathrm{split}^{i}(\mathsf{D}) . Moreover, we have by Lemma  9.7 that the image of x x is bottom in split i ​ ( 𝖣 ) \mathrm{split}^{i}(\mathsf{D}) . Iterating on these facts then, we have that ( s 𝜿 ) ∘ m ​ ( 𝖣 ′ ) (s^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}})^{\circ m}(\mathsf{D}^{\prime}) is obtained by deleting the image of x x in ( s 𝜿 ) ∘ m ​ ( 𝖣 ′ ) (s^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}})^{\circ m}(\mathsf{D}^{\prime}) for any m ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 m\in\mathbbm{Z}_{\geq 0} . Finally, if 𝝀 \boldsymbol{\lambda} is of level ℓ \ell , then 𝒲 𝜿 ​ ( 𝖣 ) \mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\mathsf{D}) (resp. 𝒲 𝜿 ​ ( 𝖣 ′ ) \mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\mathsf{D}^{\prime}) ) is obtained by shifting the k k th column in each component of ( s 𝜿 ) ∘ ℓ ​ ( 𝖣 ) (s^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}})^{\circ\ell}(\mathsf{D}) (resp. ( s 𝜿 ) ∘ ℓ ​ ( 𝖣 ′ ) (s^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}})^{\circ\ell}(\mathsf{D}^{\prime}) ) up by k − 1 k-1 notches, so the result follows.
∎
