Let X X be the set of i i -addable boxes below u u , and Y Y be the set of i i -removable boxes below u u . We define now a function q : X → Y q:X\rightarrow Y , considering separately the three possible cases. Let x ∈ X x\in X .
 



 
 Case 1. Say x x is in the top row of λ ( t ) \lambda^{(t)} . Then set q ⁡ ( x ) q(x) to be the lowest t t -frozen box—which must be in Y Y since u u is unfrozen, above x x , and the freezing process greedily t t -freezes the lowest unfrozen i i -removable box above x x .
 



 
 Case 2. Say x x is directly beneath an i ^ \widehat{i} -removable t t -frozen box x ′ x^{\prime} . Then set q ⁡ ( x ) q(x) to be the lowest t t -frozen i i -removable box above x ′ x^{\prime} —which again must be in Y Y since u u is unfrozen, above x ′ x^{\prime} , and the freezing process greedily t t -freezes the lowest unfrozen i i -removable box above x ′ x^{\prime} .

 It is clear from construction that q q is injective and q ⁡ ( x ) q(x) is above x x for all x ∈ X x\in X , so we have that 

 

 
 (9.4) 
 
 # ⁡ { x ′ ∈ X ∣ x ′ ​  is above  ​ x } < # ⁡ { y ∈ Y ∣ y ​  is above  ​ x } \displaystyle\#\{x^{\prime}\in X\mid x^{\prime}\textup{ is above }x\}<\#\{y\in Y\mid y\textup{ is above }x\} 
 
 
 
 for all x ∈ X x\in X . Now, say by way of contradiction that u u is arced in 𝝀 ⁡ [ i ] \boldsymbol{\lambda}[i] to some x ∈ X x\in X . Then every i i -removable and i i -addable box strictly between u u and x x must be arced in 𝝀 ⁡ [ i ] \boldsymbol{\lambda}[i] , so 

 

 
 
 # ⁡ { x ′ ∈ X ∣ x ′ ​  is above  ​ x } = # ⁡ { y ∈ Y ∣ y ​  is above  ​ x } \displaystyle\#\{x^{\prime}\in X\mid x^{\prime}\textup{ is above }x\}=\#\{y\in Y\mid y\textup{ is above }x\} 
 
 
 
 in contradiction of ( 9.4 ). Thus u u is unarced in 𝝀 ⁡ [ i ] \boldsymbol{\lambda}[i] , completing the proof of (1).

 We define now a function p : Z → W p:Z\rightarrow W , considering the two possible cases. Let z ∈ Z z\in Z .
 



 
 Case 1. Say z z is the lowest t t -frozen box. Then set p ⁡ ( z ) p(z) to be the i i -addable box in the top row of λ ( t ) \lambda^{(t)} —such a box must exist and be below z z by definition of the freezing process.
 



 
 Case 2. Say z z is not the lowest t t -frozen box. Let w ′ w^{\prime} be the nearest t t -frozen box below z z , and set p ⁡ ( z ) p(z) to be the addable box directly beneath w ′ w^{\prime} —note that by definition of the freezing process that p ⁡ ( z ) p(z) will have residue i i and z ′ z^{\prime} is not arced in 𝝀 ​ [ i ]                                                                                                                       \boldsymbol{\lambda}[i]^{\,\,\makebox[0.0pt]{\hbox to5.12pt{\vbox to5.12pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\>\lower-2.56064pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}{{}}{}

Let M M be the set of i i -addable boxes between u u and v v , and let N N be the set of i i -removable boxes between u u and v v . We define a map s : M → N s:M\rightarrow N as follows.
Letting m ∈ M m\in M , we consider the three possible cases:
 



 
 Case 1. Say that m m is in the top row of λ ( t ) \lambda^{(t)} . Set s ⁡ ( m ) s(m) to be the lowest t t -frozen box—since m m has residue i i , and u u is unfrozen, it must be that s ⁡ ( m ) s(m) has residue i i and lies below u u , so s ⁡ ( m ) s(m) is in N N .
 



 
 Case 2. Say there is a removable box m ′ m^{\prime} directly above m m which is t t -frozen. Set s ⁡ ( m ) s(m) to be the nearest t t -frozen box above m ′ m^{\prime} —since m ′ m^{\prime} has residue i ^ \widehat{i} and u u is unfrozen, it must be that s ⁡ ( m ) s(m) lies below u u , so s ⁡ ( m ) s(m) is in N N .
 



 
 Case 3. Say there is a removable box m ′ m^{\prime} directly above m m which is unfrozen. Then since m ′ m^{\prime} is unfrozen and falls within the arc connecting u u and v ′ v^{\prime} in 𝝀 ​ [ i ]                                                                                                                       \boldsymbol{\lambda}[i]^{\,\,\makebox[0.0pt]{\hbox to5.12pt{\vbox to5.12pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\>\lower-2.56064pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}{{}}{}

 for all m ∈ M m\in M .  Theorem 9.12 . 

 
 We have 𝒲 𝛋 ​ ( 𝒰 ​ ℒ ) ⊆ ℳ ​ 𝒫 K 𝛋 \mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\mathcal{UL})\subseteq\mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}} , and 𝒲 𝛋 : 𝒰 ​ ℒ → ℳ ​ 𝒫 K 𝛋 \mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}:\mathcal{UL}\rightarrow\mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}} and 𝒵 𝛋 : ℳ ​ 𝒫 K 𝛋 → 𝒰 ​ ℒ \mathcal{Z}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}:\mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}\rightarrow\mathcal{UL} are mutually inverse crystal isomorphisms.

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\,} , and so x x is an unarced i i -bottom box in 𝖣 \mathsf{D} by Lemma  9.9 (3). 



 
 Thus by Lemma  9.9 (2) x x is the rightmost unarced i i -bottom box in 𝖣 ⁡ [ i ] \mathsf{D}[i] if and only if x ~ \widetilde{x} is the uppermost unarced i i -removable box in 𝝀 ⁡ [ i ] \boldsymbol{\lambda}[i] . Therefore e i ​ 𝖣 = 0 e_{i}\mathsf{D}=0 if and only if e i ​ 𝝀 = 0 e_{i}\boldsymbol{\lambda}=0 . On the other hand if e i ​ 𝖣 , e i ​ 𝝀 ≠ 0 e_{i}\mathsf{D},e_{i}\boldsymbol{\lambda}\neq 0 , then by Lemma  9.10 we have that 𝒲 𝜿 ​ ( e i ​ 𝖣 ) = e i ​ 𝝀 = e i ​ 𝒲 𝜿 ​ ( 𝖣 ) \mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(e_{i}\mathsf{D})=e_{i}\boldsymbol{\lambda}=e_{i}\mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\mathsf{D}) . 



 
 Assume that 𝒲 𝜿 ( f i k ⋯ f i 1 ∅ ) = f i k ⋯ f i 1 ∅ \mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(f_{i_{k}}\cdots f_{i_{1}}\varnothing)=f_{i_{k}}\cdots f_{i_{1}}\varnothing for some i 1 , … , i k , i k + 1 ∈ ℤ 2 i_{1},\ldots,i_{k},i_{k+1}\in\mathbbm{Z}_{2} .
Then we have 

 

 
 
 e i k + 1 f i k + 1 ⋯ f i 1 ∅ \displaystyle e_{i_{k+1}}f_{i_{k+1}}\cdots f_{i_{1}}\varnothing 
 = f i k ⋯ f i 1 ∅ = 𝒲 𝜿 ( f i k ⋯ f i 1 ∅ ) = 𝒲 𝜿 ( e i k + 1 f i k + 1 ⋯ f i 1 ∅ ) = e i k + 1 𝒲 𝜿 ( f i k + 1 ⋯ f i 1 ∅ ) , \displaystyle=f_{i_{k}}\cdots f_{i_{1}}\varnothing=\mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(f_{i_{k}}\cdots f_{i_{1}}\varnothing)=\mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(e_{i_{k+1}}f_{i_{k+1}}\cdots f_{i_{1}}\varnothing)=e_{i_{k+1}}\mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(f_{i_{k+1}}\cdots f_{i_{1}}\varnothing), 
 
 
 
 and therefore 

 

 
 
 f i k + 1 ⋯ f i 1 = f i k + 1 e i k + 1 f i k + 1 ⋯ f i 1 = f i k + 1 e i k + 1 𝒲 𝜿 ( f i k + 1 ⋯ f i 1 ∅ ) = 𝒲 𝜿 ( f i k + 1 ⋯ f i 1 ∅ ) . \displaystyle f_{i_{k+1}}\cdots f_{i_{1}}=f_{i_{k+1}}e_{i_{k+1}}f_{i_{k+1}}\cdots f_{i_{1}}=f_{i_{k+1}}e_{i_{k+1}}\mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(f_{i_{k+1}}\cdots f_{i_{1}}\varnothing)=\mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(f_{i_{k+1}}\cdots f_{i_{1}}\varnothing). 
 
 
 
 Thus, since 𝒲 𝜿 ​ ( ∅ ) = ∅ \mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\varnothing)=\varnothing by definition, it follows from induction that 𝒲 𝜿 ( f i m ⋯ f i 1 ∅ ) = f i m ⋯ f i 1 ∅ \mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(f_{i_{m}}\cdots f_{i_{1}}\varnothing)=f_{i_{m}}\cdots f_{i_{1}}\varnothing for all m ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 m\in\mathbbm{Z}_{\geq 0} and all i 1 , … , i m ∈ ℤ 2 i_{1},\ldots,i_{m}\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{2} . Then since 𝒰 ​ ℒ \mathcal{UL} and ℳ ​ 𝒫 K 𝜿 \mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}} are highest weight 𝙰 1 ( 1 ) {\tt A}^{(1)}_{1} -crystals generated by ∅ \varnothing , it follows that 𝒲 𝜿 ​ ( 𝒰 ​ ℒ ) ⊆ ℳ ​ 𝒫 K 𝜿 \mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\mathcal{UL})\subseteq\mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}} and 𝒲 𝜿 \mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}} commutes with f i f_{i} for i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in\mathbbm{Z}_{2} . 



 
 To see injectivity of 𝒲 𝜿 \mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}} , assume by way of contradiction that 𝖣 , 𝖣 ′ ∈ 𝒰 ​ ℒ \mathsf{D},\mathsf{D}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{UL} are of minimal rank such that 𝖣 ≠ 𝖣 ′ \mathsf{D}\neq\mathsf{D}^{\prime} and 𝒲 𝜿 ​ ( 𝖣 ) = 𝒲 𝜿 ​ ( 𝖣 ′ ) \mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\mathsf{D})=\mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\mathsf{D}^{\prime}) . There exists i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in\mathbbm{Z}_{2} such that e i ​ 𝒲 𝜿 ​ ( 𝖣 ) ≠ 0 e_{i}\mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\mathsf{D})\neq 0 , and by above we have 

 

 
 
 𝒲 𝜿 ​ ( e i ​ 𝖣 ) = e i ​ 𝒲 𝜿 ​ ( 𝖣 ) = e i ​ 𝒲 𝜿 ​ ( 𝖣 ′ ) = 𝒲 𝜿 ​ ( e i ​ 𝖣 ′ ) , \displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(e_{i}\mathsf{D})=e_{i}\mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\mathsf{D})=e_{i}\mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\mathsf{D}^{\prime})=\mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(e_{i}\mathsf{D}^{\prime}), 
 
 
 
 which implies by minimality that e i ​ 𝖣 = e i ​ 𝖣 ′ e_{i}\mathsf{D}=e_{i}\mathsf{D}^{\prime} , and so 𝖣 = f i ​ e i ​ 𝖣 = f i ​ e i ​ 𝖣 ′ = 𝖣 ′ \mathsf{D}=f_{i}e_{i}\mathsf{D}=f_{i}e_{i}\mathsf{D}^{\prime}=\mathsf{D}^{\prime} , giving the desired contradiction. 



 
 That 𝒲 𝜿 \mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}} commutes with wt , φ i \varphi_{i} , ε i \varepsilon_{i} follows from definitions, so 𝒲 𝜿 : 𝒰 ​ ℒ → ℳ ​ 𝒫 K 𝜿 \mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}:\mathcal{UL}\rightarrow\mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}} is a crystal isomorphism. By construction, the map glue \mathrm{glue} is a left inverse to split i \mathrm{split}^{i} , so 𝒵 𝜿 \mathcal{Z}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}} is a left inverse to 𝒲 𝜿 \mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}} , which completes the proof.
∎ 









 
 9.4. Recognizing Kleshchev multipartitions


 
 For 𝖣 ∈ 𝒰 ​ ℒ \mathsf{D}\in\mathcal{UL} , we will write ht ​ ( 𝖣 ) \textup{ht}(\mathsf{D}) for the height (number of nonzero rows) of 𝖣 \mathsf{D} . We will write wid ​ ( 𝖣 ) \textup{wid}(\mathsf{D}) for the width (length of a peak row) of 𝖣 \mathsf{D} .
For a multicharge 𝜿 \boldsymbol{\kappa} , we will say 𝖣 ¯ ∈ 𝒰 ​ ℒ m \underline{\mathsf{D}}\in\mathcal{UL}^{m} is 𝜿 \boldsymbol{\kappa} -good provided it satisfies the following criteria. For t ∈ [ 1 , m ] t\in[1,m] , set κ ~ t ​ ( 𝖣 ¯ ) = κ t \widetilde{\kappa}_{t}(\underline{\mathsf{D}})=\kappa_{t} if 𝖣 t = ∅ \mathsf{D}_{t}=\varnothing , and otherwise set κ ~ t ​ ( 𝖣 ¯ ) \widetilde{\kappa}_{t}(\underline{\mathsf{D}}) to be the color of the top row of 𝖣 t \mathsf{D}_{t} .

 
 (1) 
 
 For all t ∈ [ 1 , m − 1 ] t\in[1,m-1] , κ ~ t ​ ( 𝖣 ¯ ) = κ t \widetilde{\kappa}_{t}(\underline{\mathsf{D}})=\kappa_{t} .




 (2) 
 
 For all t ∈ [ 1 , m − 1 ] t\in[1,m-1] , 𝖣 t \mathsf{D}_{t} is staircase ; the top boxes of 𝖣 t \mathsf{D}_{t} lie along a − 45 ∘ -45^{\circ} diagonal.




 (3) 
 
 For all t ∈ [ 1 , m − 1 ] t\in[1,m-1] , wid ​ ( 𝖣 t ) − ht ​ ( 𝖣 t + 1 ) ≥ δ κ ~ t ​ ( 𝖣 ¯ ) , κ ~ t + 1 ​ ( 𝖣 ¯ ) − 1 \textup{wid}(\mathsf{D}_{t})-\textup{ht}(\mathsf{D}_{t+1})\geq\delta_{\widetilde{\kappa}_{t}(\underline{\mathsf{D}}),\widetilde{\kappa}_{t+1}(\underline{\mathsf{D}})}-1 .




 (4) 
 
 For all s ∈ [ 1 , m − 2 ] s\in[1,m-2] , u ∈ [ s + 2 , m ] u\in[s+2,m] such that 𝖣 u ≠ ∅ \mathsf{D}_{u}\neq\varnothing and κ ~ s ​ ( 𝖣 ¯ ) ≠ κ ~ s + 1 ​ ( 𝖣 ¯ ) = ⋯ = κ ~ u − 1 ​ ( 𝖣 ¯ ) ≠ κ ~ u ​ ( 𝖣 ¯ ) \widetilde{\kappa}_{s}(\underline{\mathsf{D}})\neq\widetilde{\kappa}_{s+1}(\underline{\mathsf{D}})=\cdots=\widetilde{\kappa}_{u-1}(\underline{\mathsf{D}})\neq\widetilde{\kappa}_{u}(\underline{\mathsf{D}}) , there exists some t ∈ [ s , u − 1 ] t\in[s,u-1] such that wid ​ ( 𝖣 t ) − ht ​ ( 𝖣 t + 1 ) ≥ δ κ ~ t ​ ( 𝖣 ¯ ) , κ ~ t + 1 ​ ( 𝖣 ¯ ) + 1 . \textup{wid}(\mathsf{D}_{t})-\textup{ht}(\mathsf{D}_{t+1})\geq\delta_{\widetilde{\kappa}_{t}(\underline{\mathsf{D}}),\widetilde{\kappa}_{t+1}(\underline{\mathsf{D}})}+1. 




 


 
 Lemma 9.13 . 

 
 Let 𝖣 ¯ ∈ 𝒰 ​ ℒ m \underline{\mathsf{D}}\in\mathcal{UL}^{m} be 𝛋 \boldsymbol{\kappa} -good. Then we have: 

 
 (a) 
 
 s 𝜿 ​ ( 𝖣 ¯ ) s^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\underline{\mathsf{D}}) is 𝜿 \boldsymbol{\kappa} -good and g 𝜿 ∘ s 𝜿 ​ ( 𝖣 ¯ ) = 𝖣 ¯ g^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}\circ s^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\underline{\mathsf{D}})=\underline{\mathsf{D}} . 




 (b) 
 
 If m > 1 m>1 then g 𝜿 ​ ( 𝖣 ¯ ) g^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\underline{\mathsf{D}}) is 𝜿 \boldsymbol{\kappa} -good and s 𝜿 ∘ g 𝜿 ​ ( 𝖣 ¯ ) = 𝖣 ¯ s^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}\circ g^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\underline{\mathsf{D}})=\underline{\mathsf{D}} . 




 




 
 Proof.

 
 We first record some easy observations about the split function. For 𝖣 ∈ 𝒰 ​ ℒ \mathsf{D}\in\mathcal{UL} and i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in\mathbbm{Z}_{2} we have: 

 
 (i) 
 
 If the top row of 𝖣 \mathsf{D} is colored i i , then ht ​ ( split i ​ ( 𝖣 ) ↓ ) = ht ​ ( 𝖣 ) \textup{ht}(\mathrm{split}^{i}(\mathsf{D})_{\downarrow})=\textup{ht}(\mathsf{D}) and ht ​ ( split i ​ ( 𝖣 ) ↑ ) ≤ wid ​ ( split i ​ ( 𝖣 ) ↓ ) \textup{ht}(\mathrm{split}^{i}(\mathsf{D})_{\uparrow})\leq\textup{wid}(\mathrm{split}^{i}(\mathsf{D})_{\downarrow}) , with equality only if the top row of split i ​ ( 𝖣 ) ↑ \mathrm{split}^{i}(\mathsf{D})_{\uparrow} is colored i i .




 (ii) 
 
 If the top row of 𝖣 \mathsf{D} is colored i ^ \widehat{i} , then ht ​ ( split i ​ ( 𝖣 ) ↓ ) = ht ​ ( 𝖣 ) − 1 \textup{ht}(\mathrm{split}^{i}(\mathsf{D})_{\downarrow})=\textup{ht}(\mathsf{D})-1 and we have ht ​ ( split i ​ ( 𝖣 ) ↑ ) ∈ { wid ​ ( split i ​ ( 𝖣 ) ↓ ) , wid ​ ( split i ​ ( 𝖣 ) ↓ ) + 1 } \textup{ht}(\mathrm{split}^{i}(\mathsf{D})_{\uparrow})\in\{\textup{wid}(\mathrm{split}^{i}(\mathsf{D})_{\downarrow}),\textup{wid}(\mathrm{split}^{i}(\mathsf{D})_{\downarrow})+1\} and the top row of split i ​ ( 𝖣 ) ↑ \mathrm{split}^{i}(\mathsf{D})_{\uparrow} is colored i ^ \widehat{i} .




 


 
 Now we consider (a). It is straightforward to check that glue \mathrm{glue} is a left inverse of split i \mathrm{split}^{i} for all upper ledge diagrams, so the latter claim is immediate. Assume 𝖢 ¯ ∈ 𝒰 ​ ℒ m − 1 \underline{\mathsf{C}}\in\mathcal{UL}^{m-1} is 𝜿 \boldsymbol{\kappa} -good, and set 𝖣 ¯ = s 𝜿 ​ ( 𝖣 ) ∈ 𝒰 ​ ℒ m \underline{\mathsf{D}}=s^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\mathsf{D})\in\mathcal{UL}^{m} . We check that properties (1)–(4) hold for 𝖣 ¯ \underline{\mathsf{D}} . Note first that since 𝖢 ¯ \underline{\mathsf{C}} is 𝜿 \boldsymbol{\kappa} -good and 𝖣 t = 𝖢 t \mathsf{D}_{t}=\mathsf{C}_{t} for t ∈ [ 1 , m − 2 ] t\in[1,m-2] , we have that (1),(2) hold when t ∈ [ 1 , m − 2 ] t\in[1,m-2] , (3) holds when t ∈ [ 1 , m − 3 ] t\in[1,m-3] , and (4) holds when u ∈ [ 1 , m − 3 ] u\in[1,m-3] . Moreover, since 𝖣 m − 1 = split κ m − 1 ​ ( 𝖢 m − 1 ) ↓ \mathsf{D}_{m-1}=\mathrm{split}^{\kappa_{m-1}}(\mathsf{C}_{m-1})_{\downarrow} , we have that (1),(2) hold for t = m − 1 t=m-1 by definition of the map split κ m − 1 \mathrm{split}^{\kappa_{m-1}} . Thus it remains to check (3) when t ∈ { m − 2 , m − 1 } t\in\{m-2,m-1\} and (4) when u ∈ { m − 2 , m − 1 } u\in\{m-2,m-1\} .
We consider this in cases.
 



 
 Case 1. Assume κ ~ m − 1 ​ ( 𝖢 ¯ ) = κ m − 1 \widetilde{\kappa}_{m-1}(\underline{\mathsf{C}})=\kappa_{m-1} . In this case we have by (i) that

 

 
 
 ht ​ ( 𝖣 m − 1 ) = ht ​ ( 𝖢 m − 1 ) ≤ wid ​ ( 𝖢 m − 2 ) + 1 − δ κ ~ m − 2 ​ ( 𝖢 ¯ ) , κ ~ m − 1 ​ ( 𝖢 ¯ ) = wid ​ ( 𝖣 m − 2 ) + 1 − δ κ ~ m − 2 ​ ( 𝖣 ¯ ) , κ ~ m − 1 ​ ( 𝖣 ¯ ) , \displaystyle\textup{ht}(\mathsf{D}_{m-1})=\textup{ht}(\mathsf{C}_{m-1})\leq\textup{wid}(\mathsf{C}_{m-2})+1-\delta_{\widetilde{\kappa}_{m-2}(\underline{\mathsf{C}}),\widetilde{\kappa}_{m-1}(\underline{\mathsf{C}})}=\textup{wid}(\mathsf{D}_{m-2})+1-\delta_{\widetilde{\kappa}_{m-2}(\underline{\mathsf{D}}),\widetilde{\kappa}_{m-1}(\underline{\mathsf{D}})}, 
 
 
 
 where the inequality follows from the fact that 𝖢 ¯ \underline{\mathsf{C}} is 𝜿 \boldsymbol{\kappa} -good. Thus (3) holds when t = m − 1 t=m-1 . We also have by (i) that

 

 
 (9.8) 
 
 wid ​ ( 𝖣 m − 1 ) ≥ ht ​ ( 𝖣 m ) + 1 − δ κ ~ m ​ ( 𝖣 ¯ ) , κ m − 1 = ht ​ ( 𝖣 m ) + 1 − δ κ ~ m ​ ( 𝖣 ¯ ) , κ ~ m − 1 ​ ( 𝖣 ¯ ) \displaystyle\textup{wid}(\mathsf{D}_{m-1})\geq\textup{ht}(\mathsf{D}_{m})+1-\delta_{\widetilde{\kappa}_{m}(\underline{\mathsf{D}}),\kappa_{m-1}}=\textup{ht}(\mathsf{D}_{m})+1-\delta_{\widetilde{\kappa}_{m}(\underline{\mathsf{D}}),\widetilde{\kappa}_{m-1}(\underline{\mathsf{D}})} 
 
 
 
 which implies that (3) holds when t = m t=m .



 
 Now assume that the assumptions of (4) are met in 𝖣 ¯ \underline{\mathsf{D}} , with u = m − 1 u=m-1 . Then the same assumptions are met in 𝖢 ¯ \underline{\mathsf{C}} since κ ~ m − 1 ​ ( 𝖣 ¯ ) = κ m − 1 = κ ~ m − 1 ​ ( 𝖢 ¯ ) \widetilde{\kappa}_{m-1}(\underline{\mathsf{D}})=\kappa_{m-1}=\widetilde{\kappa}_{m-1}(\underline{\mathsf{C}}) . But then (4) holds for u = m − 1 u=m-1 in 𝖣 ¯ \underline{\mathsf{D}} because it holds in 𝖢 ¯ \underline{\mathsf{C}} and ht ​ ( 𝖣 m − 1 ) = ht ​ ( 𝖢 m − 1 ) \textup{ht}(\mathsf{D}_{m-1})=\textup{ht}(\mathsf{C}_{m-1}) . Now assume the conditions of (4) are met in 𝖣 ¯ \underline{\mathsf{D}} , with u = m u=m . Then κ ~ m ​ ( 𝖣 ) ≠ κ ~ m − 1 ​ ( 𝖣 ) \widetilde{\kappa}_{m}(\mathsf{D})\neq\widetilde{\kappa}_{m-1}(\mathsf{D}) , so by ( 9.8 ) we have that wid ​ ( 𝖣 m − 1 ) − ht ​ ( 𝖣 m ) ≥ 1 \textup{wid}(\mathsf{D}_{m-1})-\textup{ht}(\mathsf{D}_{m})\geq 1 , and thus (4) holds for 𝖣 ¯ \underline{\mathsf{D}} when u = m u=m .
 



 
 Case 2. Assume κ ~ m − 1 ​ ( 𝖢 ¯ ) ≠ κ m − 1 \widetilde{\kappa}_{m-1}(\underline{\mathsf{C}})\neq\kappa_{m-1} . In this case we have that by (ii) that

 

 
 
 ht ​ ( 𝖣 m − 1 ) \displaystyle\textup{ht}(\mathsf{D}_{m-1}) 
 = ht ​ ( 𝖢 m − 1 ) − 1 ≤ wid ​ ( 𝖢 m − 2 ) − δ κ ~ m − 2 ​ ( 𝖢 ¯ ) , κ ~ m − 1 ​ ( 𝖢 ¯ ) = wid ​ ( 𝖣 m − 2 ) − 1 + δ κ ~ m − 2 ​ ( 𝖣 ¯ ) , κ ~ m − 1 ​ ( 𝖣 ¯ ) \displaystyle=\textup{ht}(\mathsf{C}_{m-1})-1\leq\textup{wid}(\mathsf{C}_{m-2})-\delta_{\widetilde{\kappa}_{m-2}(\underline{\mathsf{C}}),\widetilde{\kappa}_{m-1}(\underline{\mathsf{C}})}=\textup{wid}(\mathsf{D}_{m-2})-1+\delta_{\widetilde{\kappa}_{m-2}(\underline{\mathsf{D}}),\widetilde{\kappa}_{m-1}(\underline{\mathsf{D}})} 
 
 
 
 where the inequality follows from the fact that 𝖢 ¯ \underline{\mathsf{C}} is 𝜿 \boldsymbol{\kappa} -good, and the last equality follows from the fact that κ ~ m ​ ( 𝖣 ¯ ) = κ ~ m − 1 ​ ( 𝖢 ¯ ) ≠ κ m − 1 = κ ~ m − 1 ​ ( 𝖣 ¯ ) \widetilde{\kappa}_{m}(\underline{\mathsf{D}})=\widetilde{\kappa}_{m-1}(\underline{\mathsf{C}})\neq\kappa_{m-1}=\widetilde{\kappa}_{m-1}(\underline{\mathsf{D}}) . Thus (3) holds when t = m − 1 t=m-1 . We also have by (ii) that

 

 
 
 wid ​ ( 𝖣 m − 1 ) ≥ ht ​ ( 𝖣 m ) − 1 , \displaystyle\textup{wid}(\mathsf{D}_{m-1})\geq\textup{ht}(\mathsf{D}_{m})-1, 
 
 
 
 which implies that (3) holds when t = m t=m .



 
 Now we assume the assumptions of (4) are met in 𝖣 ¯ \underline{\mathsf{D}} , with u = m − 1 u=m-1 . Then κ ~ m − 2 ​ ( 𝖢 ¯ ) = κ ~ m − 2 ​ ( 𝖣 ¯ ) ≠ κ ~ m − 1 ​ ( 𝖣 ¯ ) ≠ κ ~ m − 1 ​ ( 𝖢 ¯ ) \widetilde{\kappa}_{m-2}(\underline{\mathsf{C}})=\widetilde{\kappa}_{m-2}(\underline{\mathsf{D}})\neq\widetilde{\kappa}_{m-1}(\underline{\mathsf{D}})\neq\widetilde{\kappa}_{m-1}(\underline{\mathsf{C}}) , and since 𝖢 ¯ \underline{\mathsf{C}} is 𝜿 \boldsymbol{\kappa} -good, we have that 

 

 
 (9.9) 
 
 wid ​ ( 𝖣 m − 2 ) = wid ​ ( 𝖢 m − 2 ) ≥ ht ​ ( 𝖢 m − 1 ) = ht ​ ( 𝖣 m − 1 ) + 1 . \displaystyle\textup{wid}(\mathsf{D}_{m-2})=\textup{wid}(\mathsf{C}_{m-2})\geq\textup{ht}(\mathsf{C}_{m-1})=\textup{ht}(\mathsf{D}_{m-1})+1. 
 
 
 
 thus (4) holds for u = m − 1 u=m-1 in 𝖣 ¯ \underline{\mathsf{D}} . 



 
 Now assume the conditions of (4) are met in 𝖣 ¯ \underline{\mathsf{D}} , with u = m u=m . If s = m − 2 s=m-2 , then κ ~ m − 2 ​ ( 𝖢 ¯ ) = κ ~ m − 2 ​ ( 𝖣 ¯ ) ≠ κ ~ m − 1 ​ ( 𝖣 ¯ ) ≠ κ ~ m − 1 ​ ( 𝖢 ¯ ) \widetilde{\kappa}_{m-2}(\underline{\mathsf{C}})=\widetilde{\kappa}_{m-2}(\underline{\mathsf{D}})\neq\widetilde{\kappa}_{m-1}(\underline{\mathsf{D}})\neq\widetilde{\kappa}_{m-1}(\underline{\mathsf{C}}) , and thus (4) holds again as in ( 9.9 ).
Thus we may assume s ≤ m − 3 s\leq m-3 . Then we have that 

 

 
 
 κ ~ ​ ( 𝖢 ¯ ) s ≠ κ ~ ​ ( 𝖢 ¯ ) s + 1 = ⋯ = κ ~ ​ ( 𝖢 ¯ ) m − 2 ≠ κ ~ ​ ( 𝖢 ¯ ) m − 1 , \displaystyle\widetilde{\kappa}(\underline{\mathsf{C}})_{s}\neq\widetilde{\kappa}(\underline{\mathsf{C}})_{s+1}=\cdots=\widetilde{\kappa}(\underline{\mathsf{C}})_{m-2}\neq\widetilde{\kappa}(\underline{\mathsf{C}})_{m-1}, 
 
 
 
 and since 𝖢 ¯ \underline{\mathsf{C}} is 𝜿 \boldsymbol{\kappa} -good, there exists some t ∈ [ s , m − 2 ] t\in[s,m-2] such that wid ​ ( 𝖢 t ) − ht ​ ( 𝖢 t + 1 ) ≥ δ κ ~ t ​ ( 𝖢 ¯ ) , κ ~ t + 1 ​ ( 𝖢 ¯ ) + 1 \textup{wid}(\mathsf{C}_{t})-\textup{ht}(\mathsf{C}_{t+1})\geq\delta_{\widetilde{\kappa}_{t}(\underline{\mathsf{C}}),\widetilde{\kappa}_{t+1}(\underline{\mathsf{C}})}+1 . If t ≤ m − 3 t\leq m-3 , then this immediately implies that wid ​ ( 𝖣 t ) − ht ​ ( 𝖣 t + 1 ) ≥ δ κ ~ t ​ ( 𝖣 ¯ ) , κ ~ t + 1 ​ ( 𝖣 ¯ ) + 1 \textup{wid}(\mathsf{D}_{t})-\textup{ht}(\mathsf{D}_{t+1})\geq\delta_{\widetilde{\kappa}_{t}(\underline{\mathsf{D}}),\widetilde{\kappa}_{t+1}(\underline{\mathsf{D}})}+1 , as desired. If t = m − 2 t=m-2 , then 

 

 
 
 wid ​ ( 𝖣 m − 2 ) = wid ​ ( 𝖢 m − 2 ) ≥ ht ​ ( 𝖢 m − 1 ) + 1 = ht ​ ( 𝖣 m − 1 ) + 2 , \displaystyle\textup{wid}(\mathsf{D}_{m-2})=\textup{wid}(\mathsf{C}_{m-2})\geq\textup{ht}(\mathsf{C}_{m-1})+1=\textup{ht}(\mathsf{D}_{m-1})+2, 
 
 
 
 as desired. Thus (4) holds for u = m u=m in 𝖣 ¯ \underline{\mathsf{D}} , which completes the proof of (a). 



 
 Now we consider (b). Assume 𝖢 ¯ ∈ 𝒰 ​ ℒ m + 1 \underline{\mathsf{C}}\in\mathcal{UL}^{m+1} is 𝜿 \boldsymbol{\kappa} -good, and set 𝖣 ¯ = g 𝜿 ​ ( 𝖢 ¯ ) \underline{\mathsf{D}}=g^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\underline{\mathsf{C}}) .
Since 𝖢 ¯ \underline{\mathsf{C}} is 𝜿 \boldsymbol{\kappa} -good, we have by (3) that wid ​ ( 𝖢 m ) ≥ ht ​ ( 𝖢 m + 1 ) + δ κ ~ m ​ ( 𝖢 ¯ ) , κ ~ m + 1 ​ ( 𝖢 ¯ ) − 1 \textup{wid}(\mathsf{C}_{m})\geq\textup{ht}(\mathsf{C}_{m+1})+\delta_{\widetilde{\kappa}_{m}(\underline{\mathsf{C}}),\widetilde{\kappa}_{m+1}(\underline{\mathsf{C}})}-1 . This, together with the fact that 𝖢 m \mathsf{C}_{m} is staircase, ensures that, when lined up as in Figure  15 to compute glue ⁡ ( 𝖢 m , 𝖢 m + 1 ) \mathrm{glue}(\mathsf{C}_{m},\mathsf{C}_{m+1}) , the bottom row of 𝖢 m + 1 \mathsf{C}_{m+1} does not extend below the uppermost peak row of 𝖢 m \mathsf{C}_{m} . From this fact, it is straightforward to see that split κ m ∘ glue ⁡ ( 𝖢 m , 𝖢 m + 1 ) = ( 𝖢 m , 𝖢 m + 1 ) \mathrm{split}^{\kappa_{m}}\circ\mathrm{glue}(\mathsf{C}_{m},\mathsf{C}_{m+1})=(\mathsf{C}_{m},\mathsf{C}_{m+1}) , and so s 𝜿 ∘ g 𝜿 ​ ( 𝖢 ¯ ) = 𝖢 ¯ s^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}\circ g^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\underline{\mathsf{C}})=\underline{\mathsf{C}} , proving the latter part of (b). 



 
 We now check that properties (1)–(4) hold for 𝖣 ¯ \underline{\mathsf{D}} . Note first that since 𝖢 ¯ \underline{\mathsf{C}} is 𝜿 \boldsymbol{\kappa} -good and 𝖣 t = 𝖢 t \mathsf{D}_{t}=\mathsf{C}_{t} for t ∈ [ 1 , m − 1 ] t\in[1,m-1] , we have immediately that (1), (2) hold, (3) holds when t ∈ [ 1 , m − 2 ] t\in[1,m-2] , and (4) holds when u ∈ [ 1 , m − 1 ] u\in[1,m-1] . Thus it remains to check (3) when t = m − 1 t=m-1 and (4) when u = m u=m . We do so now, in three separate cases. 
 



 
 Case 1. Assume κ ~ m ​ ( 𝖢 ¯ ) = κ ~ m + 1 ​ ( 𝖢 ¯ ) \widetilde{\kappa}_{m}(\underline{\mathsf{C}})=\widetilde{\kappa}_{m+1}(\underline{\mathsf{C}}) or ht ​ ( 𝖢 m + 1 ) < wid ​ ( 𝖢 m ) \textup{ht}(\mathsf{C}_{m+1})<\textup{wid}(\mathsf{C}_{m}) . 
By definition of the glue \mathrm{glue} map and the fact that 𝖢 m \mathsf{C}_{m} is staircase, we have κ ~ m ​ ( 𝖣 ¯ ) = κ ~ m ​ ( 𝖢 ¯ ) \widetilde{\kappa}_{m}(\underline{\mathsf{D}})=\widetilde{\kappa}_{m}(\underline{\mathsf{C}}) , and

 

 
 
 ht ​ ( 𝖣 m ) = ht ​ ( 𝖢 m ) \displaystyle\textup{ht}(\mathsf{D}_{m})=\textup{ht}(\mathsf{C}_{m}) 
 ≤ wid ​ ( 𝖢 m − 1 ) − δ κ ~ m − 1 ​ ( 𝖢 ¯ ) , κ ~ m ​ ( 𝖢 ¯ ) + 1 = wid ​ ( 𝖣 m − 1 ) − δ κ ~ m − 1 ​ ( 𝖣 ¯ ) , κ ~ m ​ ( 𝖣 ¯ ) + 1 , \displaystyle\leq\textup{wid}(\mathsf{C}_{m-1})-\delta_{\widetilde{\kappa}_{m-1}(\underline{\mathsf{C}}),\widetilde{\kappa}_{m}(\underline{\mathsf{C}})}+1=\textup{wid}(\mathsf{D}_{m-1})-\delta_{\widetilde{\kappa}_{m-1}(\underline{\mathsf{D}}),\widetilde{\kappa}_{m}(\underline{\mathsf{D}})}+1, 
 
 
 
 where the inequality follows from the fact that 𝖢 ¯ \underline{\mathsf{C}} is 𝜿 \boldsymbol{\kappa} -good. Therefore (3) holds when t = m − 1 t=m-1 . Note moreover that since ht ​ ( 𝖣 m ) = ht ​ ( 𝖢 m ) \textup{ht}(\mathsf{D}_{m})=\textup{ht}(\mathsf{C}_{m}) and 𝖣 t = 𝖢 t \mathsf{D}_{t}=\mathsf{C}_{t} for t ∈ [ 1 , m − 1 ] t\in[1,m-1] , if the conditions for (4) are met in 𝖣 ¯ \underline{\mathsf{D}} with s = m s=m , then they are similarly met in 𝖢 ¯ \underline{\mathsf{C}} , and so (4) holds for 𝖣 ¯ \underline{\mathsf{D}} since 𝖢 ¯ \underline{\mathsf{C}} is 𝜿 \boldsymbol{\kappa} -good.

 



 
 Case 2. Assume κ ~ m ​ ( 𝖢 ¯ ) ≠ κ ~ m − 1 ​ ( 𝖢 ¯ ) = κ ~ m + 1 ​ ( 𝖢 ¯ ) \widetilde{\kappa}_{m}(\underline{\mathsf{C}})\neq\widetilde{\kappa}_{m-1}(\underline{\mathsf{C}})=\widetilde{\kappa}_{m+1}(\underline{\mathsf{C}}) and ht ​ ( 𝖢 m + 1 ) ≥ wid ​ ( 𝖢 m ) \textup{ht}(\mathsf{C}_{m+1})\geq\textup{wid}(\mathsf{C}_{m}) . Since 𝖢 ¯ \underline{\mathsf{C}} is 𝜿 \boldsymbol{\kappa} -good, we have that ht ​ ( 𝖢 m + 1 ) ∈ { wid ​ ( 𝖢 m ) , wid ​ ( 𝖢 m ) + 1 } \textup{ht}(\mathsf{C}_{m+1})\in\{\textup{wid}(\mathsf{C}_{m}),\textup{wid}(\mathsf{C}_{m})+1\} . As 𝖢 m \mathsf{C}_{m} is staircase, the definition of the glue \mathrm{glue} map gives us that κ ~ m ​ ( 𝖣 ¯ ) = κ ~ m + 1 ​ ( 𝖢 ¯ ) = κ ~ m − 1 ​ ( 𝖢 ) = κ ~ m − 1 ​ ( 𝖣 ) \widetilde{\kappa}_{m}(\underline{\mathsf{D}})=\widetilde{\kappa}_{m+1}(\underline{\mathsf{C}})=\widetilde{\kappa}_{m-1}(\mathsf{C})=\widetilde{\kappa}_{m-1}(\mathsf{D}) (so condition (4), u = m u=m cannot be met in 𝖣 ¯ \underline{\mathsf{D}} ), and ht ​ ( 𝖣 m ) = ht ​ ( 𝖢 m ) + 1 \textup{ht}(\mathsf{D}_{m})=\textup{ht}(\mathsf{C}_{m})+1 . Moreover, since 𝖢 ¯ \underline{\mathsf{C}} is 𝜿 \boldsymbol{\kappa} -good, we have by (4) then that wid ​ ( 𝖢 m − 1 ) − ht ​ ( 𝖢 m ) ≥ 1 \textup{wid}(\mathsf{C}_{m-1})-\textup{ht}(\mathsf{C}_{m})\geq 1 , and thus

 

 
 
 wid ​ ( 𝖣 m − 1 ) = wid ​ ( 𝖢 m − 1 ) ≥ ht ​ ( 𝖢 m ) + 1 = ht ​ ( 𝖣 m ) , \displaystyle\textup{wid}(\mathsf{D}_{m-1})=\textup{wid}(\mathsf{C}_{m-1})\geq\textup{ht}(\mathsf{C}_{m})+1=\textup{ht}(\mathsf{D}_{m}), 
 
 
 
 so (3) holds for 𝖣 ¯ \underline{\mathsf{D}} when t = m − 1 t=m-1 . 
 



 
 Case 3. Assume κ ~ m − 1 ​ ( 𝖢 ¯ ) = κ ~ m ​ ( 𝖢 ¯ ) ≠ κ ~ m + 1 ​ ( 𝖢 ¯ ) \widetilde{\kappa}_{m-1}(\underline{\mathsf{C}})=\widetilde{\kappa}_{m}(\underline{\mathsf{C}})\neq\widetilde{\kappa}_{m+1}(\underline{\mathsf{C}}) and ht ​ ( 𝖢 m + 1 ) ≥ wid ​ ( 𝖢 m ) \textup{ht}(\mathsf{C}_{m+1})\geq\textup{wid}(\mathsf{C}_{m}) . As above, we have that ht ​ ( 𝖢 m + 1 ) ∈ { wid ​ ( 𝖢 m ) , wid ​ ( 𝖢 m ) + 1 } \textup{ht}(\mathsf{C}_{m+1})\in\{\textup{wid}(\mathsf{C}_{m}),\textup{wid}(\mathsf{C}_{m})+1\} . As 𝖢 m \mathsf{C}_{m} is staircase, the definition of the glue \mathrm{glue} map gives us that κ ~ m ​ ( 𝖣 ¯ ) = κ ~ m + 1 ​ ( 𝖢 ¯ ) ≠ κ ~ m − 1 ​ ( 𝖢 ) = κ ~ m − 1 ​ ( 𝖣 ) \widetilde{\kappa}_{m}(\underline{\mathsf{D}})=\widetilde{\kappa}_{m+1}(\underline{\mathsf{C}})\neq\widetilde{\kappa}_{m-1}(\mathsf{C})=\widetilde{\kappa}_{m-1}(\mathsf{D}) , and ht ​ ( 𝖣 m ) = ht ​ ( 𝖢 m ) + 1 \textup{ht}(\mathsf{D}_{m})=\textup{ht}(\mathsf{C}_{m})+1 . Therefore since 𝖢 ¯ \underline{\mathsf{C}} is 𝜿 \boldsymbol{\kappa} -good, we have by (3) that

 

 
 
 wid ​ ( 𝖣 m − 1 ) = wid ​ ( 𝖢 m − 1 ) ≥ ht ​ ( C m ) = ht ​ ( 𝖣 m ) − 1 , \displaystyle\textup{wid}(\mathsf{D}_{m-1})=\textup{wid}(\mathsf{C}_{m-1})\geq\textup{ht}(C_{m})=\textup{ht}(\mathsf{D}_{m})-1, 
 
 
 
 so (3) holds for 𝖣 ¯ \underline{\mathsf{D}} when t = m − 1 t=m-1 .



 
 Now assume the conditions of (4) are met in 𝖣 ¯ \underline{\mathsf{D}} , with u = m u=m . Then we must have s ≤ m − 2 s\leq m-2 , and 

 

 
 
 κ ~ s ​ ( 𝖢 ¯ ) ≠ κ ~ s + 1 ​ ( 𝖢 ¯ ) = ⋯ = κ ~ m ​ ( 𝖢 ¯ ) ≠ κ ~ m + 1 ​ ( 𝖢 ¯ ) \displaystyle\widetilde{\kappa}_{s}(\underline{\mathsf{C}})\neq\widetilde{\kappa}_{s+1}(\underline{\mathsf{C}})=\cdots=\widetilde{\kappa}_{m}(\underline{\mathsf{C}})\neq\widetilde{\kappa}_{m+1}(\underline{\mathsf{C}}) 
 
 
 
 and so since 𝖢 ¯ \underline{\mathsf{C}} is 𝜿 \boldsymbol{\kappa} -good, we have by (4) that wid ​ ( 𝖢 t ) − ht ​ ( 𝖢 t + 1 ) ≥ δ κ ~ t ​ ( 𝖢 ¯ ) , κ ~ t + 1 ​ ( 𝖢 ¯ ) + 1 \textup{wid}(\mathsf{C}_{t})-\textup{ht}(\mathsf{C}_{t+1})\geq\delta_{\widetilde{\kappa}_{t}(\underline{\mathsf{C}}),\widetilde{\kappa}_{t+1}(\underline{\mathsf{C}})}+1 for some t ∈ [ s , m − 1 ] t\in[s,m-1] (the case t = m t=m being already ruled out above). If t ≤ m − 2 t\leq m-2 , then we immediately have that (4) holds for 𝖣 ¯ \underline{\mathsf{D}} , so assume finally that t = m − 1 t=m-1 . Then 

 

 
 
 wid ​ ( 𝖣 m − 1 ) = wid ​ ( 𝖢 m − 1 ) ≥ ht ​ ( 𝖢 m ) + 2 = ht ​ ( 𝖣 m ) + 1 , \displaystyle\textup{wid}(\mathsf{D}_{m-1})=\textup{wid}(\mathsf{C}_{m-1})\geq\textup{ht}(\mathsf{C}_{m})+2=\textup{ht}(\mathsf{D}_{m})+1, 
 
 
 
 and so (4) holds for 𝖣 ¯ \underline{\mathsf{D}} , completing the proof.
∎ 





 
 Theorem 9.14 . 

 
 Let 𝛋 \boldsymbol{\kappa} be a multicharge, and let 𝛌 ∈ ℳ ​ 𝒫 𝛋 \boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{MP}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}} be a multipartition of level ℓ \ell . Then 𝛌 ∈ ℳ ​ 𝒫 K 𝛋 \boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}} if and only if it satisfies the following conditions: 

 
 (1) 
 
 The partition λ ( t ) \lambda^{(t)} is 2-restricted for all t ∈ [ 1 , ℓ ] t\in[1,\ell] . 




 (2) 
 
 For all t ∈ [ 1 , ℓ − 1 ] t\in[1,\ell-1] , we have λ 1 ( t ) − ( λ ( t + 1 ) ) 1 ′ ≥ δ κ t , κ t + 1 − 1 \lambda^{(t)}_{1}-(\lambda^{(t+1)})^{\prime}_{1}\geq\delta_{\kappa_{t},\kappa_{t+1}}-1 . 




 (3) 
 
 If s ∈ [ 1 , ℓ − 2 ] s\in[1,\ell-2] , u ∈ [ s + 2 , ℓ ] u\in[s+2,\ell] are such that λ ( u ) ≠ ∅ \lambda^{(u)}\neq\varnothing and κ s ≠ κ s + 1 = ⋯ = κ u − 1 ≠ κ u \kappa_{s}\neq\kappa_{s+1}=\cdots=\kappa_{u-1}\neq\kappa_{u} , then there exists t ∈ [ s , u − 1 ] t\in[s,u-1] such that λ 1 ( t ) − ( λ ( t + 1 ) ) 1 ′ ≥ δ κ t , κ t + 1 + 1 \lambda^{(t)}_{1}-(\lambda^{(t+1)})^{\prime}_{1}\geq\delta_{\kappa_{t},\kappa_{t+1}}+1 . 




 




 
 Proof.

 
 Assume 𝝀 ∈ ℳ ​ 𝒫 K 𝜿 \boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}} . Then by Theorem  9.12 , we have 𝝀 = 𝒲 𝜿 ​ ( 𝖣 ) \boldsymbol{\lambda}=\mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\mathsf{D}) for some 𝖣 ∈ 𝒰 ​ ℒ \mathsf{D}\in\mathcal{UL} . In view of the definition of 𝒲 𝜿 \mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}} , we have that 𝝀 \boldsymbol{\lambda} is achieved by computing
 𝖣 ¯ = ( 𝖣 1 , … , 𝖣 ℓ , ∅ ) := s κ ℓ ∘ ⋯ ∘ s κ 1 ( 𝖣 ) , \underline{\mathsf{D}}=(\mathsf{D}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{D}_{\ell},\varnothing):=s^{\kappa_{\ell}}\circ\cdots\circ s^{\kappa_{1}}(\mathsf{D}), 
and then shifting the k k th column in each component up by k − 1 k-1 notches. Now, the single upper ledge diagram 𝖣 \mathsf{D} is vacuously a 𝜿 \boldsymbol{\kappa} -good sequence, and so it follows from Lemma  9.13 that 𝖣 ¯ \underline{\mathsf{D}} is 𝜿 \boldsymbol{\kappa} -good. It is immediate that column-shifting a staircase upper ledge diagram in the above fashion yields a 2-restricted partition, so (1) is satisfied for 𝝀 \boldsymbol{\lambda} . Moreover, we have wid ​ ( 𝖣 t ) = λ 1 ( t ) \textup{wid}(\mathsf{D}_{t})=\lambda^{(t)}_{1} and ht ​ ( 𝖣 t ) = ( λ ( t ) ) 1 ′ \textup{ht}(\mathsf{D}_{t})=(\lambda^{(t)})^{\prime}_{1} for all t ∈ [ 1 , ℓ ] t\in[1,\ell] , so (2), (3) are satisfied for 𝝀 \boldsymbol{\lambda} as well since 𝖣 ¯ \underline{\mathsf{D}} is 𝜿 \boldsymbol{\kappa} -good. 



 
 Now assume 𝝀 \boldsymbol{\lambda} satisfies conditions (1)–(3) above. Let 𝖣 ¯ = ( 𝖣 1 , … , 𝖣 ℓ , ∅ ) \underline{\mathsf{D}}=(\mathsf{D}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{D}_{\ell},\varnothing) be the sequence of diagrams given by shifting the k k th column in each component down by k − 1 k-1 notches. By (1), each component is staircase upper ledge, and (2), (3) ensure that 𝖣 ¯ \underline{\mathsf{D}} is 𝜿 \boldsymbol{\kappa} -good. Therefore, setting 𝖣 := g κ 1 ∘ ⋯ ∘ g κ ℓ ( 𝖣 ¯ ) ∈ 𝒰 ℒ \mathsf{D}:=g^{\kappa_{1}}\circ\cdots\circ g^{\kappa_{\ell}}(\underline{\mathsf{D}})\in\mathcal{UL} , we have by Lemma  9.13 that 𝖣 ¯ = s κ ℓ ∘ ⋯ ∘ s κ 1 ( 𝖣 ) \underline{\mathsf{D}}=s^{\kappa_{\ell}}\circ\cdots\circ s^{\kappa_{1}}(\mathsf{D}) , and so 𝝀 = 𝒲 𝜿 ​ ( 𝖣 ) \boldsymbol{\lambda}=\mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\mathsf{D}) . Thus by Theorem  9.12 we have that 𝝀 ∈ ℳ ​ 𝒫 K 𝜿 \boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}} .
∎ 









 
 10. Representations of KLR algebras


 
 10.1. The KLR algebra of type 𝙰 1 ( 1 ) {\tt A}_{1}^{(1)} .


 
 Fix a field 𝕜 \mathbbm{k} of characteristic p ≥ 0 p\geq 0 . Let θ ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 ​ I \theta\in{\mathbbm{Z}}_{\geq 0}I , and set n = ht ⁡ ( θ ) n={\operatorname{ht}}(\theta) . We write I θ I^{\theta} for the set of binary words 𝒊 = i 1 ⋯ i n ∈ ℤ 2 n \boldsymbol{i}=i_{1}\cdots i_{n}\in\mathbbm{Z}_{2}^{n} such that ∑ k = 1 n α i k = θ \sum_{k=1}^{n}\alpha_{i_{k}}=\theta .
As in [ 28 , 54 ] , the KLR (or quiver Hecke ) algebra of type 𝙰 1 ( 1 ) {\tt A}_{1}^{(1)} is the ℤ \mathbbm{Z} -graded 𝕜 \mathbbm{k} -algebra R θ R_{\theta} generated by elements

 

 
 
 { 1 𝒊 ∣ 𝒊 ∈ I θ } ∪ { y 1 , … , y n } ∪ { ψ 1 , … , ψ n − 1 } , \displaystyle\{1_{\boldsymbol{i}}\mid\boldsymbol{i}\in I^{\theta}\}\cup\{y_{1},\dots,y_{n}\}\cup\{\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{n-1}\}, 
 
 
 
 subject to the relations:

 

 
 
 1 𝒊 ​ 1 𝒋 \displaystyle 1_{\boldsymbol{i}}1_{\boldsymbol{j}} 
 = δ 𝒊 , 𝒋 ​ 1 𝒊 ; ∑ 𝒊 ∈ I θ 1 𝒊 = 1 ; y r ​ 1 𝒊 = 1 𝒊 ​ y r ; y r ​ y s = y s ​ y r ; ψ r ​ 1 𝒊 = 1 s r ​ 𝒊 ​ ψ r ; \displaystyle=\delta_{\boldsymbol{i},\boldsymbol{j}}1_{\boldsymbol{i}};\qquad\sum_{\boldsymbol{i}\in I^{\theta}}1_{\boldsymbol{i}}=1;\qquad y_{r}1_{\boldsymbol{i}}=1_{\boldsymbol{i}}y_{r};\qquad y_{r}y_{s}=y_{s}y_{r};\qquad\psi_{r}1_{\boldsymbol{i}}=1_{s_{r}\boldsymbol{i}}\psi_{r}; 
 
 
 
 

 
 
 ( y r ​ ψ r − ψ r ​ y r + 1 ) ​ 1 𝒊 = δ i r , i r + 1 ​ 1 𝒊 = ( y r + 1 ​ ψ r − ψ r ​ y r ) ; ψ r 2 ​ 1 𝒊 = − δ i r , i ^ r + 1 ​ ( y r 2 − 2 ​ y r ​ y r + 1 + y r + 1 2 ) ​ 1 𝒊 ; \displaystyle(y_{r}\psi_{r}-\psi_{r}y_{r+1})1_{\boldsymbol{i}}=\delta_{i_{r},i_{r+1}}1_{\boldsymbol{i}}=(y_{r+1}\psi_{r}-\psi_{r}y_{r});\qquad\psi_{r}^{2}1_{\boldsymbol{i}}=-\delta_{i_{r},\widehat{i}_{r+1}}(y_{r}^{2}-2y_{r}y_{r+1}+y_{r+1}^{2})1_{\boldsymbol{i}}; 
 
 
 
 

 
 
 ( ψ r + 1 ​ ψ r ​ ψ r + 1 − ψ r ​ ψ r + 1 ​ ψ r ) ​ 1 𝒊 = − δ i r , i r + 2 ​ δ i r , i ^ r + 1 ​ ( y r − 2 ​ y r + 1 + y r + 2 ) ​ 1 𝒊 , \displaystyle(\psi_{r+1}\psi_{r}\psi_{r+1}-\psi_{r}\psi_{r+1}\psi_{r})1_{\boldsymbol{i}}=-\delta_{i_{r},i_{r+2}}\delta_{i_{r},\widehat{i}_{r+1}}(y_{r}-2y_{r+1}+y_{r+2})1_{\boldsymbol{i}}, 
 
 
 
 for all admissible 𝒊 , 𝒋 ∈ I θ \boldsymbol{i},\boldsymbol{j}\in I^{\theta} and r , s ∈ [ 1 , n ] r,s\in[1,n] .
The ℤ \mathbbm{Z} -grading is given by setting deg ⁡ ( 1 𝒊 ) = 0 \deg(1_{\boldsymbol{i}})=0 , deg ⁡ ( y r ) = 2 \deg(y_{r})=2 , deg ⁡ ( ψ r ​ 1 𝒊 ) = 2 − 4 ​ δ i r , i r + 1 \deg(\psi_{r}1_{\boldsymbol{i}})=2-4\delta_{i_{r},i_{r+1}} . To each permutation σ ∈ 𝔖 n \sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n} , we associate a choice of distinguished reduced expression σ = s j 1 ​ … ​ s j k \sigma=s_{j_{1}}\dots s_{j_{k}} and define an associated element ψ σ := ψ j 1 ​ … ​ ψ j k ∈ R θ \psi_{\sigma}:=\psi_{j_{1}}\dots\psi_{j_{k}}\in R_{\theta} .





 
 10.2. Representation theory of R θ R_{\theta} 


 
 We consider the category R θ R_{\theta} -mod of finitely generated ℤ {\mathbbm{Z}} -graded R θ R_{\theta} -modules. We will use the ≅ \cong symbol to indicate a (degree-preserving) isomorphism of R θ R_{\theta} -modules, and ≈ \approx to indicate an isomorphism of R θ R_{\theta} -modules up to some grading shift.
For θ 1 , … , θ k ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 ​ I \theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{k}\in\mathbbm{Z}_{\geq 0}I , there is a natural inclusion

 

 
 
 R θ 1 , … , θ k := R θ 1 ⊗ ⋯ ⊗ R θ k ↪ R θ 1 + ⋯ + θ k , \displaystyle R_{\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{k}}:=R_{\theta_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes R_{\theta_{k}}\hookrightarrow R_{\theta_{1}+\cdots+\theta_{k}}, 
 
 
 
 and we write 1 θ 1 , … , θ k 1_{\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{k}} for the image of the identity under this inclusion.
This yields accompanying exact induction and restriction functors

 

 
 
 
 Ind θ 1 , … , θ k θ 1 + ⋯ + θ k := R θ 1 + ⋯ + θ k ​ 1 θ 1 , … , θ k ⊗ R θ 1 , … , θ k ? : R θ 1 , … , θ k ​ -mod → R θ 1 + ⋯ + θ k ​ -mod , \displaystyle{\mathrm{Ind}}_{\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{k}}^{\theta_{1}+\cdots+\theta_{k}}:=R_{\theta_{1}+\cdots+\theta_{k}}1_{\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{k}}\otimes_{R_{\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{k}}}?\colon\;R_{\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{k}}\textup{-mod}\rightarrow R_{\theta_{1}+\cdots+\theta_{k}}\textup{-mod}, 
 
 
 
 
 
 Res θ 1 , … , θ k θ 1 + ⋯ + θ k := 1 θ 1 , … , θ k ​ R θ 1 + ⋯ + θ k ⊗ R θ 1 + ⋯ + θ k ? : R θ 1 + ⋯ + θ k ​ -mod → R θ 1 , … , θ k ​ -mod . \displaystyle{\mathrm{Res}}_{\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{k}}^{\theta_{1}+\cdots+\theta_{k}}:=1_{\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{k}}R_{\theta_{1}+\cdots+\theta_{k}}\otimes_{R_{\theta_{1}+\cdots+\theta_{k}}}?\colon\;R_{\theta_{1}+\cdots+\theta_{k}}\textup{-mod}\rightarrow R_{\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{k}}\textup{-mod}. 
 
 
 
 For M i ∈ R θ i M_{i}\in R_{\theta_{i}} we will sometimes use the shorthand M 1 ∘ ⋯ ∘ M k := Ind θ 1 , … , θ k θ 1 + ⋯ + θ k ( M 1 ⊠ ⋯ ⊠ M k ) M_{1}\circ\cdots\circ M_{k}:={\mathrm{Ind}}_{\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{k}}^{\theta_{1}+\cdots+\theta_{k}}(M_{1}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes M_{k}) .



 
 10.2.1. Chevalley functors


 
 The KLR algebras R 0 , R α 1 , R α 0 R_{0},R_{\alpha_{1}},R_{\alpha_{0}} each have a single one-dimensional simple module concentrated in degree zero, which we denote as 𝕜 0 , 𝕜 α 1 , 𝕜 α 0 \mathbbm{k}_{0},\mathbbm{k}_{\alpha_{1}},\mathbbm{k}_{\alpha_{0}} , respectively. For i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in\mathbbm{Z}_{2} we denote the functors

 

 
 
 
 E i : R θ + α i ​ -mod → R θ ​ -mod , M ↦ Res θ , α i θ + α i ​ M \displaystyle E_{i}:R_{\theta+\alpha_{i}}\textup{-mod}\rightarrow R_{\theta}\textup{-mod},\quad M\mapsto{\mathrm{Res}}_{\theta,\alpha_{i}}^{\theta+\alpha_{i}}M 
 
 
 
 
 
 F i : R θ ​ -mod → R θ + α i ​ -mod , M ↦ Ind θ , α i θ + α i ​ ( M ⊠ 𝕜 α i ) \displaystyle F_{i}:R_{\theta}\textup{-mod}\rightarrow R_{\theta+\alpha_{i}}\textup{-mod},\quad M\mapsto{\mathrm{Ind}}_{\theta,\alpha_{i}}^{\theta+\alpha_{i}}(M\boxtimes\mathbbm{k}_{\alpha_{i}}) 
 
 
 
 
 
 E i ∗ : R θ + α i ​ -mod → R θ ​ -mod , M ↦ Res α i , θ θ + α i ​ M \displaystyle E_{i}^{*}:R_{\theta+\alpha_{i}}\textup{-mod}\rightarrow R_{\theta}\textup{-mod},\quad M\mapsto{\mathrm{Res}}_{\alpha_{i},\theta}^{\theta+\alpha_{i}}M 
 
 
 
 
 
 F i ∗ : R θ ​ -mod → R θ + α i ​ -mod , M ↦ Ind α i , θ θ + α i ​ ( 𝕜 α i ⊠ M ) , \displaystyle F_{i}^{*}:R_{\theta}\textup{-mod}\rightarrow R_{\theta+\alpha_{i}}\textup{-mod},\quad M\mapsto{\mathrm{Ind}}_{\alpha_{i},\theta}^{\theta+\alpha_{i}}(\mathbbm{k}_{\alpha_{i}}\boxtimes M), 
 
 
 
 where we interpret E i ​ M E_{i}M (resp. E i ∗ ​ M E_{i}^{*}M ) as an R θ R_{\theta} -module via the restriction induced by the inclusion R θ ↪ R θ , α i R_{\theta}\hookrightarrow R_{\theta,\alpha_{i}} (resp. R θ ↪ R α i , θ R_{\theta}\hookrightarrow R_{\alpha_{i},\theta} ).



 
 Remark 10.1 . 

 
 The KLR algebras R θ R_{\theta} categorify the lower half of the quantum group associated to 𝔰 ​ 𝔩 ^ 2 ​ ( ℂ ) \widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}({\mathbb{C}}) , in the sense that Grothendieck rings of the categories of finitely generated projective and finite dimensional graded KLR modules recover (integral forms of) U q − ​ ( 𝔰 ​ 𝔩 ^ 2 ) U_{q}^{-}(\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}) and its dual, respectively [ 28 , 54 ] . The functors E i , F i , E i ∗ , F i ∗ E_{i},F_{i},E_{i}^{*},F_{i}^{*} satisfy categorified Serre relations, and the set of simple KLR modules taken together with the action of these functors underwrites an 𝙰 1 ( 1 ) {\tt A}^{(1)}_{1} -crystal structure isomorphic to ℬ ⁡ ( ∞ ) \mathcal{B}(\infty) , see [ 41 ] and § 10.8 .







 
 10.2.2. Graded characters


 
 Let θ ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 ​ I \theta\in\mathbbm{Z}_{\geq 0}I and M ∈ R θ ​ -mod M\in R_{\theta}\textup{-mod} . For d ∈ ℤ d\in\mathbbm{Z} we denote the d d th degree subspace in M M by M d M_{d} , and use q c ​ M q^{c}M to denote grading shift by c c , so ( q c ​ M ) m = M m − c (q^{c}M)_{m}=M_{m-c} . The graded character of M M is

 

 
 
 ch ​ M := ∑ 𝒊 ∈ I θ ∑ d ∈ ℤ dim ( 1 𝒊 ​ M d ) ​ q d ​ 𝒊 ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 ​ [ q ± 1 ] ​ I θ . \displaystyle\textup{ch}\,M:=\sum_{\boldsymbol{i}\in I^{\theta}}\sum_{d\in\mathbbm{Z}}\dim(1_{\boldsymbol{i}}M_{d})q^{d}\boldsymbol{i}\in\mathbbm{Z}_{\geq 0}[q^{\pm 1}]I^{\theta}. 
 
 
 
 We say that 𝒊 ∈ I θ \boldsymbol{i}\in I^{\theta} is a word in M M provided that 1 𝒊 ​ M ≠ 0 1_{\boldsymbol{i}}M\neq 0 , i.e., 𝒊 \boldsymbol{i} is in the support of ch ​ M \textup{ch}\,M .







 
 10.3. Specht modules


 
 Much of KLR representation theory can be couched in the language of (skew) Specht modules, which have a nicely combinatorial structure and graded character.



 
 10.3.1. Skew diagrams


 
 A skew diagram μ \mu is a collection of boxes (with inherent residues) that can be realized as the set difference of Young diagrams μ = λ \ ν \mu=\lambda\backslash\nu for some charge κ ∈ ℤ 2 \kappa\in\mathbbm{Z}_{2} and some λ ⊇ ν ∈ 𝒫 κ \lambda\supseteq\nu\in\mathcal{P}^{\kappa} . We say μ \mu is a ribbon if it is connected and contains no 2 × 2 2\times 2 squares. We extend the notion of content to skew diagrams in the obvious way.
See Figure  20 for an example.





 
 10.3.2. Standard tableaux and residue sequences


 
 Given a skew diagram μ \mu , a μ \mu -tableau is a bijective labeling 𝚝 : [ 1 , | μ | ] → μ {\tt t}:[1,|\mu|]\rightarrow\mu for the boxes of μ \mu . We say 𝚝 {\tt t} is standard if labels increase left to right along rows and top to bottom down columns. We write Std ​ ( μ ) \textup{Std}(\mu) for the set of standard μ \mu -tableaux. The leading tableau 𝚝 μ {\tt t}^{\mu} is the standard tableau which labels boxes row-by-row, left to right, beginning with the top row (see Figure  20 ). If μ \mu has content θ ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 ​ I \theta\in\mathbbm{Z}_{\geq 0}I , then the residue sequence of a μ \mu -tableau 𝚝 {\tt t} is
 𝒊 𝚝 = res ( 𝚝 ( 1 ) ) ⋯ res ( 𝚝 ( | μ | ) ) ∈ I θ \boldsymbol{i}^{\tt t}=\textup{res}({\tt t}(1))\cdots\textup{res}({\tt t}(|\mu|))\in I^{\theta} .
We write 𝒊 μ := 𝒊 𝚝 μ \boldsymbol{i}^{\mu}:=\boldsymbol{i}^{{\tt t}^{\mu}} for the residue sequence of the leading tableau. The symmetric group 𝔖 | μ | \mathfrak{S}_{|\mu|} has a left action on μ \mu -tableaux given by ( ω ⋅ 𝚝 ) ​ ( x ) = 𝚝 ⁡ ( ω − 1 ⋅ x ) (\omega\cdot{\tt t})(x)={\tt t}(\omega^{-1}\cdot x) ; we write ω 𝚝 \omega_{\tt t} for the permutation such that ω 𝚝 ⋅ 𝚝 μ = 𝚝 \omega_{\tt t}\cdot{\tt t}^{\mu}={\tt t} , and set ψ 𝚝 := ψ ω 𝚝 \psi_{\tt t}:=\psi_{\omega_{{\tt t}}} .





 
 10.3.3. Garnir elements


 
 For d ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 d\in\mathbbm{Z}_{\geq 0} and r ∈ [ 1 , d − 1 ] r\in[1,d-1] , we define

 

 
 
 τ r := 1 + ψ 2 ​ r ​ ψ 2 ​ r − 1 ​ ψ 2 ​ r + 1 ​ ψ 2 ​ r ∈ R d ​ δ . \displaystyle\tau_{r}:=1+\psi_{2r}\psi_{2r-1}\psi_{2r+1}\psi_{2r}\in R_{d\delta}. 
 
 
 
 Recalling that for each ω ∈ 𝔖 d \omega\in\mathfrak{S}_{d} we have a chosen reduced expression ω = s r 1 ⋯ s r m \omega=s_{r_{1}}\cdots s_{r_{m}} , we associate ω \omega with the element τ ω := τ r 1 ⋯ τ r m ∈ R d ​ δ \tau_{\omega}:=\tau_{r_{1}}\cdots\tau_{r_{m}}\in R_{d\delta} . For r + s = d r+s=d , we write 𝒟 d r , s \mathscr{D}^{r,s}_{d} for the set of minimal coset representatives in 𝔖 d / ( 𝔖 r × 𝔖 s ) \mathfrak{S}_{d}/(\mathfrak{S}_{r}\times\mathfrak{S}_{s}) , and set

 

 
 
 g r , s := ∑ ω ∈ 𝒟 d r , s τ ω ∈ R d ​ δ . \displaystyle g_{r,s}:=\sum_{\omega\in\mathscr{D}_{d}^{r,s}}\tau_{\omega}\in R_{d\delta}. 
 
 
 


 
 Let μ \mu be a skew diagram of content θ \theta . Assume u u is a box in μ \mu with residue i i and there is a box v v directly below u u . We call such u u a Garnir box for μ \mu . The Garnir belt of u u is the set of boxes to the right of u u and to the left of v v (inclusive). Assume [ x , y ] [x,y] is the interval of labels in the Garnir belt in the leading tableau 𝚝 μ {\tt t}^{\mu} .
The Garnir tableau 𝚐 u μ {\tt g}^{\mu}_{u} is the tableau defined as follows. Outside of the Garnir belt, all labels match those in 𝚝 μ {\tt t}^{\mu} . If the lower row of the Garnir belt is an odd number of boxes, label the first box in this row with x x . If the upper row of the Garnir belt is an odd number of boxes, label the last box in this row with y y . The remaining interval of labels [ x ′ , y ′ ] [x^{\prime},y^{\prime}] then are used to fill in the 2 ​ r 2r boxes in the top row of the belt first, followed by the 2 ​ s 2s boxes in the bottom row to complete 𝚐 u μ {\tt g}^{\mu}_{u} . Then the Garnir element for u u is

 

 
 
 Gar u μ ​ 1 𝒊 μ = ( 1 𝒋 ⊗ g r , s ​ 1 ( i ​ i ^ ) r + s ⊗ 1 𝒌 ) ​ ψ 𝚐 u μ ​ 1 𝒊 μ ∈ R θ . \displaystyle\textup{Gar}^{\mu}_{u}1_{\boldsymbol{i}^{\mu}}=(1_{\boldsymbol{j}}\otimes g_{r,s}1_{(i\widehat{i})^{r+s}}\otimes 1_{\boldsymbol{k}})\psi_{{\tt g}^{\mu}_{u}}1_{\boldsymbol{i}^{\mu}}\in R_{\theta}. 
 
 
 
 where 𝒋 , 𝒌 \boldsymbol{j},\boldsymbol{k} are the residue sequences associated with the labels [ 1 , x ′ − 1 ] [1,x^{\prime}-1] and [ y ′ + 1 , | μ | ] [y^{\prime}+1,|\mu|] in 𝚝 μ {\tt t}^{\mu} , respectively. See Figure  20 .



 
 

 
 
       
 
 
 
 Figure 20. From left to right, a skew diagram μ \mu of content 20 ​ α 0 + 21 ​ α 1 20\alpha_{0}+21\alpha_{1} ; the leading tableau 𝚝 μ {\tt t}^{\mu} , and the Garnir tableau 𝚐 u μ {\tt g}^{\mu}_{u} for a box u u with Garnir belt highlighted in red. 
 


 
 10.3.4. Specht modules


 
 We now define Specht modules for the KLR algebra associated to skew diagrams, following [ 31 , 49 ] .



 
 Definition 10.2 . 

 
 Let μ \mu be a skew diagram of content θ ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 ​ I \theta\in\mathbbm{Z}_{\geq 0}I . The (skew) Specht module 𝗦 ⁡ ( μ ) ∈ R θ \boldsymbol{\mathsf{S}}(\mu)\in R_{\theta} -mod is the left ideal R θ ​ 1 𝒊 μ R_{\theta}1_{\boldsymbol{i}^{\mu}} modulo the left submodule generated by the elements

 
 (1) 
 
 y r ​ 1 𝒊 μ y_{r}1_{\boldsymbol{i}^{\mu}} , for every r ∈ [ 1 , | μ | ] r\in[1,|\mu|] ;




 (2) 
 
 ψ r ​ 1 𝒊 μ \psi_{r}1_{\boldsymbol{i}^{\mu}} , for every pair of labels r , r + 1 r,r+1 in the same row of 𝚝 μ {\tt t}^{\mu} ;




 (3) 
 
 Gar u μ ​ 1 𝒊 μ \textup{Gar}^{\mu}_{u}1_{\boldsymbol{i}^{\mu}} , for every Garnir box u u in μ \mu .




 




 
 We will henceforth drop the ‘skew’ and refer to 𝗦 ⁡ ( μ ) \boldsymbol{\mathsf{S}}(\mu) merely as a Specht module, heedless of whether μ \mu is a skew diagram or a proper Young diagram. Specht modules have a combinatorial basis and graded character indexed by the standard tableaux:



 
 Theorem 10.3 . 

 
 [ 49 , Theorem 5.1] 

Let μ \mu be a skew diagram.
The Specht module 𝗦 ⁡ ( μ ) \boldsymbol{\mathsf{S}}(\mu) has homogeneous 𝕜 \mathbbm{k} -basis given by 

 

 
 
 { 1 𝒊 𝚝 ​ ψ 𝚝 ​ 1 𝒊 μ ∣ 𝚝 ∈ Std ​ ( μ ) } . \displaystyle\{1_{\boldsymbol{i}^{\tt t}}\psi_{{\tt t}}1_{\boldsymbol{i}^{\mu}}\mid{\tt t}\in\textup{Std}(\mu)\}. 
 
 
 
 and graded character
 ch ​ 𝗦 ​ ( μ ) = ∑ 𝚝 ∈ Std ​ ( μ ) q deg ⁡ ( ψ 𝚝 ​ 1 𝐢 μ ) ​ 𝐢 𝚝 \textup{ch}\,\boldsymbol{\mathsf{S}}(\mu)=\sum_{{\tt t}\in\textup{Std}(\mu)}q^{\deg(\psi_{\tt t}1_{\boldsymbol{i}^{\mu}})}\boldsymbol{i}^{\tt t} . 









 
 10.4. Cuspidal systems and classification of simple R θ R_{\theta} -modules


 
 Throughout this section we fix i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in\mathbbm{Z}_{2} , and recall that this corresponds to a choice of convex order ≻ i \begin{subarray}{c}{}\\
\textstyle\succ\\
\scriptscriptstyle{}^{i}\end{subarray} from § 4.1 . Following [ 37 , 45 , 33 , 57 , 48 ] , to each β ∈ Φ + re \beta\in\Phi_{+}^{\mathrm{re}} we associate a self-dual simple finite dimensional ≻ i \begin{subarray}{c}{}\\
\textstyle\succ\\
\scriptscriptstyle{}^{i}\end{subarray} -cuspidal module L i , β ∈ R β ​ -mod L_{i,\beta}\in R_{\beta}\textup{-mod} , and to each integer partition λ \lambda we associate a self-dual simple finite dimensional ≻ i \begin{subarray}{c}{}\\
\textstyle\succ\\
\scriptscriptstyle{}^{i}\end{subarray} -semicuspidal module L i , λ ∈ R | λ | ​ δ ​ -mod L_{i,\lambda}\in R_{|\lambda|\delta}\textup{-mod} .
These cuspidal/semicuspidal modules are key building blocks in the representation theory of KLR algebras. We refer the reader to the references above for the precise definition of (semi)cuspidal modules and their properties; for our purposes it will be enough to directly construct these modules in the next subsection.



 
 10.4.1. Cuspidal/semicuspidal modules via Specht modules


 
 Following [ 1 , 48 ] we may explicitly construct the cuspidal/semicuspidal modules via Specht modules associated with certain skew diagrams as follows. Recall that we have fixed i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in\mathbbm{Z}_{2} which determines a choice of convex order ≻ i \begin{subarray}{c}{}\\
\textstyle\succ\\
\scriptscriptstyle{}^{i}\end{subarray} . For j ∈ ℤ 2 j\in\mathbbm{Z}_{2} , k ∈ ℤ > 0 k\in\mathbbm{Z}_{>0} , let ξ i ( α j : k ) \xi_{i}(\alpha_{j:k}) be the unique ribbon skew diagram of content α j : k \alpha_{j:k} such that all boxes of content i i are (southeast)-removable. For a partition λ \lambda , let ξ i ​ ( λ ) \xi_{i}(\lambda) be the skew diagram formed by doubling the length of every column of λ \lambda , shifting the t t th column up t − 1 t-1 units, and choosing residues so that (southeast)-removable boxes have content i i . In Figure  21 we show some example diagrams in the case of the convex order ≻ 1 \begin{subarray}{c}{}\\
\textstyle\succ\\
\scriptscriptstyle{}^{1}\end{subarray} .
