{}^{1}\end{smallmatrix} . 
 
 
 Theorem 10.4 . 

 
 For i , j ∈ ℤ 2 i,j\in\mathbbm{Z}_{2} , k ∈ ℤ > 0 k\in\mathbbm{Z}_{>0} , and λ ∈ 𝒫 \lambda\in\mathcal{P} we have that 

 

 
 
 L i , α j : k ≅ q k − 1 𝗦 ( ξ i ( α j : k ) ) and L i , λ ≅ hd q c ⁡ ( λ ) 𝗦 ( ξ i ( λ ) ) , \displaystyle L_{i,\alpha_{j:k}}\cong q^{k-1}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{S}}(\xi_{i}(\alpha_{j:k}))\qquad\textup{and}\qquad L_{i,\lambda}\cong\textup{hd}\,q^{c(\lambda)}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{S}}(\xi_{i}(\lambda)), 
 
 
 
 where 

 

 
 
 c ⁡ ( λ ) = 2 ​ | λ | − 2 ​ p ​ ( λ ) − 2 ​ ∑ m = 1 p ⁡ ( λ ) min ⁡ { m − 1 , ⌊ λ m − 1 2 ⌋ } . \displaystyle c(\lambda)=2|\lambda|-2p(\lambda)-2\sum_{m=1}^{p(\lambda)}{\rm min}\left\{m-1,\left\lfloor\frac{\lambda_{m}-1}{2}\right\rfloor\right\}. 
 
 
 
 Moreover, [ 𝗦 ( ξ i ( λ ) ) : L i , λ ] = 1 [\boldsymbol{\mathsf{S}}(\xi_{i}(\lambda)):L_{i,\lambda}]=1 and if [ 𝗦 ( ξ i ( λ ) ) : M ] ≠ 0 [\boldsymbol{\mathsf{S}}(\xi_{i}(\lambda)):M]\neq 0 for some simple module M M , then M ≈ L i , μ M\approx L_{i,\mu} for some μ ​ ⊴ ​ λ \mu\trianglelefteq\lambda . 





 
 Proof.

 
 Up to grading shift, these facts are recorded in [ 1 , Theorem 6.13] and [ 48 , Theorem 9.15] . Now write 𝒊 ( α j : k ) = i ^ k − 1 i k ∈ I α j : k \boldsymbol{i}(\alpha_{j:k})=\widehat{i}^{k-1}i^{k}\in I^{\alpha_{j:k}} and 𝒊 ( λ ) = i ^ λ 1 i λ 1 ⋯ i ^ λ p ⁡ ( λ ) i λ p ⁡ ( λ ) ∈ I | λ | ​ δ \boldsymbol{i}(\lambda)=\widehat{i}^{\lambda_{1}}i^{\lambda_{1}}\cdots\widehat{i}^{\lambda_{p(\lambda)}}i^{\lambda_{p(\lambda)}}\in I^{|\lambda|\delta} . Self-duality implies that the graded dimensions of 1 𝒊 ( α j : k ) L i , α j : k 1_{\boldsymbol{i}(\alpha_{j:k})}L_{i,\alpha_{j:k}} and 1 𝒊 ⁡ ( λ ) ​ L i , λ 1_{\boldsymbol{i}(\lambda)}L_{i,\lambda} are invariant under the bar involution q ↔ q − 1 q\leftrightarrow q^{-1} .
Moreover, by [ 1 , Theorem 6.13] and [ 48 , Lemma 5.13, Theorem 9.15] the graded dimensions of 1 𝒊 ( α j : k ) 𝗦 ( ξ i ( α j : k ) ) 1_{\boldsymbol{i}(\alpha_{j:k})}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{S}}(\xi_{i}(\alpha_{j:k})) and 1 𝒊 ⁡ ( λ ) ​ 𝗦 ​ ( ξ i ​ ( λ ) ) 1_{\boldsymbol{i}(\lambda)}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{S}}(\xi_{i}(\lambda)) agree with those of 1 𝒊 ( α j : k ) L i , α j : k 1_{\boldsymbol{i}(\alpha_{j:k})}L_{i,\alpha_{j:k}} and 1 𝒊 ⁡ ( λ ) ​ L i , λ 1_{\boldsymbol{i}(\lambda)}L_{i,\lambda} respectively, up to some explicit shift. By directly calculating the graded dimensions of 1 𝒊 ( α j : k ) 𝗦 ( ξ i ( α j : k ) ) 1_{\boldsymbol{i}(\alpha_{j:k})}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{S}}(\xi_{i}(\alpha_{j:k})) and 1 𝒊 ⁡ ( λ ) ​ 𝗦 ​ ( ξ i ​ ( λ ) ) 1_{\boldsymbol{i}(\lambda)}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{S}}(\xi_{i}(\lambda)) using Theorem  10.3 , and finding the requisite shifts to induce bar invariance, we arrive at the shifts in the theorem statement.
∎ 









 
 10.5. Classification of simple R θ R_{\theta} -modules via cuspidal systems


 
 There is a bilexicographic partial order ≥ \geq on Π ⁡ ( θ ) \Pi(\theta) defined as follows (see [ 48 , Definition 9.17] ). Set π > ϕ \pi>\phi provided that:

 
 (1) 
 
 For j ∈ ℤ 2 j\in\mathbbm{Z}_{2} , there exists k ∈ ℤ > 0 k\in\mathbbm{Z}_{>0} where n k ​ ( π j ) > n k ​ ( ϕ j ) n_{k}(\pi^{j})>n_{k}(\phi^{j}) and n k ′ ​ ( π j ) = n k ′ ​ ( ϕ j ) n_{k^{\prime}}(\pi^{j})=n_{k^{\prime}}(\phi^{j}) for all k ′ < k k^{\prime}<k , or;




 (2) 
 
 For j ∈ ℤ 2 j\in\mathbbm{Z}_{2} , we have π j = ϕ j \pi^{j}=\phi^{j} and π δ ⊳ ϕ δ \pi^{\delta}\triangleright\phi^{\delta} .




 


 
 Let θ ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 ​ I \theta\in\mathbbm{Z}_{\geq 0}I and π = { π 1 , π δ , π 0 } ∈ Π ⁡ ( θ ) \pi=\{\pi^{1},\pi^{\delta},\pi^{0}\}\in\Pi(\theta) . We associate to π \pi a proper standard module Δ ¯ i ​ ( π ) \bar{\Delta}_{i}(\pi) which is the ≻ i \begin{subarray}{c}{}\\
\textstyle\succ\\
\scriptscriptstyle{}^{i}\end{subarray} -ordered induction product of simple cuspidal and semicuspidal modules as defined in [ 37 , (6.5)] :

 

 
 (10.1) 
 
 Δ ¯ i ( π ) := q sh ​ ( π ) L i , α i : 1 ∘ n 1 ​ ( π i ) ∘ L i , α i : 2 ∘ n 2 ​ ( π i ) ∘ ⋯ ∘ L i , π δ ∘ ⋯ ∘ L i , α i ^ : 2 ∘ n 2 ​ ( π i ^ ) ∘ L i , α i ^ : 1 ∘ n 1 ​ ( π i ^ ) , \displaystyle\bar{\Delta}_{i}(\pi):=q^{\textup{sh}(\pi)}L_{i,\alpha_{i:1}}^{\circ\,n_{1}(\pi^{i})}\circ L_{i,\alpha_{i:2}}^{\circ\,n_{2}(\pi^{i})}\circ\cdots\circ L_{i,\pi^{\delta}}\circ\cdots\circ L_{i,\alpha_{\;\widehat{i}:2}}^{\circ\,n_{2}(\pi^{\widehat{i}})}\circ L_{i,\alpha_{\;\widehat{i}:1}}^{\circ\,n_{1}(\pi^{\widehat{i}})}, 
 
 
 
 where the grading shift sh ​ ( π ) \textup{sh}(\pi) is given by

 

 
 (10.2) 
 
 sh ​ ( π ) = 1 2 ​ ∑ j ∈ ℤ 2 t ∈ ℤ > 0 n t ​ ( π j ) ​ [ n t ​ ( π j ) − 1 ] . \displaystyle\textup{sh}(\pi)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in\mathbbm{Z}_{2}\\
t\in\mathbbm{Z}_{>0}\end{subarray}}n_{t}(\pi^{j})[n_{t}(\pi^{j})-1]. 
 
 
 


 
 The key result about cuspidal systems is the following:



 
 Theorem 10.5 . 

 
 Let θ ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 ​ I \theta\in\mathbbm{Z}_{\geq 0}I and i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in\mathbbm{Z}_{2} . 

 
 (1) 
 
 For π ∈ Π ⁡ ( θ ) \pi\in\Pi(\theta) , the proper standard module Δ ¯ i ​ ( π ) \bar{\Delta}_{i}(\pi) has self-dual simple head L i ​ ( π ) := hd ​ Δ ¯ i ​ ( π ) L_{i}(\pi):=\textup{hd}\,\bar{\Delta}_{i}(\pi) . 




 (2) 
 
 The set 

 

 
 (10.3) 
 
 { L i ​ ( π ) ∣ π ∈ Π ⁡ ( θ ) } \displaystyle\{L_{i}(\pi)\mid\pi\in\Pi(\theta)\} 
 
 
 
 is a complete and irredundant list of simple R θ R_{\theta} -modules up to isomorphism and grading shift. 




 (3) 
 
 For all π , ϕ ∈ Π ⁡ ( θ ) \pi,\phi\in\Pi(\theta) we have [ Δ ¯ i ( π ) : L i ( π ) ] = 1 [\bar{\Delta}_{i}(\pi):L_{i}(\pi)]=1 and [ Δ ¯ i ( π ) : L i ( ϕ ) ] ≠ 0 [\bar{\Delta}_{i}(\pi):L_{i}(\phi)]\neq 0 only if π ≥ ϕ \pi\geq\phi . 




 (4) 
 
 For all ( π | ϕ ) ∈ ℳ ​ 𝒱 ​ ( θ ) (\pi|\phi)\in\mathcal{MV}(\theta) , we have that
 L 1 ​ ( π ) ≅ L 0 ​ ( ϕ ) L_{1}(\pi)\cong L_{0}(\phi) , and we may alternatively index the simple modules in ( 10.3 ) as 

 

 
 
 { L ⁡ ( π | ϕ ) := L 1 ​ ( π ) ≅ L 0 ​ ( ϕ ) ∣ ( π | ϕ ) ∈ ℳ ​ 𝒱 ​ ( θ ) } . \displaystyle\{L(\pi|\phi):=L_{1}(\pi)\cong L_{0}(\phi)\mid(\pi|\phi)\in\mathcal{MV}(\theta)\}. 
 
 
 



 (5) 
 
 For ( π | ϕ ) ∈ ℳ ​ 𝒱 (\pi|\phi)\in\mathcal{MV} , the geometric polytope for ( π | ϕ ) (\pi|\phi) is the convex hull of the words of L ⁡ ( π | ϕ ) L(\pi|\phi) considered as paths in the root lattice. 




 




 
 Proof.

 
 (1)–(3) are the content of [ 37 , Theorem 4.1] and [ 57 , Theorem 2.19] . By [ 48 , Lemma 5.13] , our simple semicuspidal modules L i , λ L_{i,\lambda} are uniquely identified by having the Gelfand-Graev word 𝒈 λ = i ^ λ 1 i λ 1 ⋯ i ^ λ p ⁡ ( λ ) i λ p ⁡ ( λ ) \boldsymbol{g}^{\lambda}=\widehat{i}^{\lambda_{1}}i^{\lambda_{1}}\cdots\widehat{i}^{\lambda_{p(\lambda)}}i^{\lambda_{p(\lambda)}} and no word 𝒈 μ \boldsymbol{g}^{\mu} for μ \mu more dominant. Then, as noted in [ 57 , Remark 3.43] , our choice of labelings for the collection of imaginary semicuspidal simple modules agrees with the choice made in [ 57 , Definition 3.38] , so (4) and (5) are the content of [ 57 , Theorem B, Corollary 3.11] .
∎ 





 
 Remark 10.6 . 

 
 The cuspidal system approach to KLR representation theory has proven to be a powerful organizing principle, yielding deep structural results and interactions with other areas of representation theory.
The simple modules L i ​ ( π ) L_{i}(\pi) and proper standard modules Δ ¯ i ​ ( π ) \bar{\Delta}_{i}(\pi) (as well as their infinite dimensional counterparts, the projective covers P i ​ ( π ) P_{i}(\pi) and standard modules Δ i ​ ( π ) \Delta_{i}(\pi) ) are the key ingredients in a complete stratification framework for R θ R_{\theta} -mod, replete with associated filtrations, BGG reciprocity, and other homological results, see [ 33 , 45 , 47 ] . The imaginary strata in this setup are linked via Howe duality to the classical Schur algebra, and through Morita equivalence to RoCK blocks of Hecke algebras and to ‘zigzag deformations’ of affine Hecke and Schur algebras, see [ 12 , 32 , 34 , 44 , 48 ] . Through the lens of categorification, the projective and standard modules (and respectively, simple and proper standard modules) encode higher symmetries in the theory of crystal and PBW bases (and respectively, their duals) for 𝒰 q − ​ ( 𝔰 ​ 𝔩 ^ 2 ) \mathcal{U}^{-}_{q}(\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}) , see [ 27 , 41 , 57 , 59 ] .







 
 10.6. Real root functors


 
 For i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in\mathbbm{Z}_{2} , k ∈ ℤ > 0 k\in\mathbbm{Z}_{>0} , we denote the functors

 

 
 
 
 E i : k : R θ + α i : k -mod → R θ -mod , M ↦ Hom R θ + α i : k ( R θ ∘ L i ^ , α i : k , M ) \displaystyle E_{i:k}\colon R_{\theta+\alpha_{i:k}}\textup{-mod}\rightarrow R_{\theta}\textup{-mod},\quad M\mapsto{\rm Hom}_{R_{\theta+\alpha_{i:k}}}(R_{\theta}\circ L_{\widehat{i},\alpha_{i:k}},M) 
 
 
 
 
 
 F i : k : R θ -mod → R θ + α i : k -mod , M ↦ Ind θ , α i : k θ + α i : k ( M ⊠ L i ^ , α i : k ) \displaystyle F_{i:k}\colon R_{\theta}\textup{-mod}\rightarrow R_{\theta+\alpha_{i:k}}\textup{-mod},\quad M\mapsto{\mathrm{Ind}}_{\theta,\alpha_{i:k}}^{\theta+\alpha_{i:k}}(M\boxtimes L_{\widehat{i},\alpha_{i:k}}) 
 
 
 
 
 
 E i : k ∗ : R θ + α i : k -mod → R θ -mod , M ↦ Hom R θ + α i : k ( L i , α i : k ∘ R θ , M ) \displaystyle E_{i:k}^{*}\colon R_{\theta+\alpha_{i:k}}\textup{-mod}\rightarrow R_{\theta}\textup{-mod},\quad M\mapsto{\rm Hom}_{R_{\theta+\alpha_{i:k}}}(L_{i,\alpha_{i:k}}\circ R_{\theta},M) 
 
 
 
 
 
 F i : k ∗ : R θ -mod → R θ + α i : k -mod , M ↦ Ind α i : k θ + α i : k ( L i , α i : k ⊠ M ) \displaystyle F_{i:k}^{*}\colon R_{\theta}\textup{-mod}\rightarrow R_{\theta+\alpha_{i:k}}\textup{-mod},\quad M\mapsto{\mathrm{Ind}}_{\alpha_{i:k}}^{\theta+\alpha_{i:k}}(L_{i,\alpha_{i:k}}\boxtimes M) 
 
 
 
 These functors generalize the Chevalley functors of § 10.2.1 from simple roots to all real roots; we have E i ≅ E i : 1 , F i ≅ F i : 1 , E i ∗ ≅ E ∗ i : 1 E_{i}\cong E_{i:1},F_{i}\cong F_{i:1},E_{i}^{*}\cong E^{*}_{i:1} and F i ∗ ≅ F ∗ i : 1 F_{i}^{*}\cong F^{*}_{i:1} since L i , α i ≅ L i ^ , α i ≅ 𝕜 α i L_{i,\alpha_{i}}\cong L_{\widehat{i},\alpha_{i}}\cong\mathbbm{k}_{\alpha_{i}} is the unique one-dimensional self-dual simple module for R α i R_{\alpha_{i}} .





 
 10.7. Augmented branching rules for simple modules labeled by affine MV polytopes


 
 It will be useful to define the following statistics on polytopes ( π | ϕ ) ∈ ℳ ​ 𝒱 (\pi|\phi)\in\mathcal{MV} :

 

 
 
 m 1 ​ ( π | ϕ ) = min ⁡ ( ϕ 1 ) ; m 1 ∗ ​ ( π | ϕ ) = min ⁡ ( π 1 ) ; m 0 ​ ( π | ϕ ) = min ⁡ ( π 0 ) ; m 0 ∗ ​ ( π | ϕ ) = min ⁡ ( ϕ 0 ) . n 1 : k ( π | ϕ ) = n k ( ϕ 1 ) ; n ∗ 1 : k ( π | ϕ ) = n k ( π 1 ) ; n 0 : k ( π | ϕ ) = n k ( π 0 ) ; n 0 : k ∗ ( π | ϕ ) = n k ( ϕ 0 ) . \displaystyle\begin{array}[]{lllllllllll}m_{1}(\pi|\phi)={\rm min}(\phi^{1});&m_{1}^{*}(\pi|\phi)={\rm min}(\pi^{1});&m_{0}(\pi|\phi)={\rm min}(\pi^{0});&m_{0}^{*}(\pi|\phi)={\rm min}(\phi^{0}).\\
n_{1:k}(\pi|\phi)=n_{k}(\phi^{1});&n^{*}_{1:k}(\pi|\phi)=n_{k}(\pi^{1});&n_{0:k}(\pi|\phi)=n_{k}(\pi^{0});&n_{0:k}^{*}(\pi|\phi)=n_{k}(\phi^{0}).\end{array} 
 
 
 
 Recall the operators e i : k , e ∗ i : k , f i : k , f ∗ i : k e_{i:k},e^{*}_{i:k},f_{i:k},f^{*}_{i:k} on ℳ ​ 𝒱 \mathcal{MV} defined in § 4.3 .



 
 Proposition 10.7 . 

 
 Let ( π | ϕ ) ∈ ℳ ​ 𝒱 (\pi|\phi)\in\mathcal{MV} , i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in\mathbbm{Z}_{2} , and k ∈ ℤ > 0 k\in\mathbbm{Z}_{>0} . 

 
 (1) 
 
 If m i ​ ( π | ϕ ) > k m_{i}(\pi|\phi)>k then E i : k L ( π | ϕ ) = 0 E_{i:k}L(\pi|\phi)=0 , and if m i ​ ( π | ϕ ) = k m_{i}(\pi|\phi)=k then 

 

 
 
 q 1 − n i : k ( π | ϕ ) soc E i : k L ( π | ϕ ) ≅ L ( e i : k ( π | ϕ ) ) \displaystyle q^{1-n_{i:k}(\pi|\phi)}\textup{soc}\,E_{i:k}L(\pi|\phi)\cong L(e_{i:k}(\pi|\phi)) 
 
 
 



 (2) 
 
 If m i ∗ ​ ( π | ϕ ) > k m^{*}_{i}(\pi|\phi)>k then E i : k ∗ L ( π | ϕ ) = 0 E^{*}_{i:k}L(\pi|\phi)=0 , and if m i ∗ ​ ( π | ϕ ) = k m^{*}_{i}(\pi|\phi)=k then 

 

 
 
 q 1 − n i : k ∗ ( π | ϕ ) soc E i : k ∗ L ( π | ϕ ) ≅ L ( e i : k ∗ ( π | ϕ ) ) . \displaystyle q^{1-n_{i:k}^{*}(\pi|\phi)}\textup{soc}\,E^{*}_{i:k}L(\pi|\phi)\cong L(e^{*}_{i:k}(\pi|\phi)). 
 
 
 



 (3) 
 
 If m i ​ ( π | ϕ ) ≥ k m_{i}(\pi|\phi)\geq k then 

 

 
 
 q n i : k ( π | ϕ ) hd F i : k L ( π | ϕ ) ≅ L ( f i : k ( π | ϕ ) ) . \displaystyle q^{n_{i:k}(\pi|\phi)}\textup{hd}\,F_{i:k}L(\pi|\phi)\cong L(f_{i:k}(\pi|\phi)). 
 
 
 



 (4) 
 
 If m i ∗ ​ ( π | ϕ ) ≥ k m^{*}_{i}(\pi|\phi)\geq k then 

 

 
 
 q n i : k ∗ ( π | ϕ ) hd F i : k ∗ L ( π | ϕ ) ≅ L ( f i : k ∗ ( π | ϕ ) ) . \displaystyle q^{n_{i:k}^{*}(\pi|\phi)}\textup{hd}\,F^{*}_{i:k}L(\pi|\phi)\cong L(f^{*}_{i:k}(\pi|\phi)). 
 
 
 



 




 
 Proof.

 
 For sake of space throughout the proof, write α := α i : k \alpha:=\alpha_{i:k} .
We prove (4) first in the i = 1 i=1 case, noting that the i = 0 i=0 case, as well as (3), is proved in symmetrical fashion. We have then that L ⁡ ( π | ϕ ) = L i ​ ( π ) L(\pi|\phi)=L_{i}(\pi) .
Since m i ∗ ​ ( π | ϕ ) = min ⁡ ( π i ) ≥ k m_{i}^{*}(\pi|\phi)={\rm min}(\pi^{i})\geq k ,
, it follows from ( 10.1 , 10.2 ) that
 Δ ¯ i ( π + i : k ) ≅ q n k ​ ( π i ) L i , α ∘ Δ ¯ i ( π ) . \bar{\Delta}_{i}(\pi_{+i:k})\cong q^{n_{k}(\pi^{i})}L_{i,\alpha}\circ\bar{\Delta}_{i}(\pi). 
By Theorem  10.5 (1) we have a surjection L i , α ⊠ Δ ¯ i ​ ( π ) ↠ L i , α ⊠ L i ​ ( π ) L_{i,\alpha}\boxtimes\bar{\Delta}_{i}(\pi)\twoheadrightarrow L_{i,\alpha}\boxtimes L_{i}(\pi) , and so by exactness of induction we have a surjection 

 

 
 (10.4) 
 
 Δ ¯ i ( π + i : k ) ≅ q n k ​ ( π i ) L i , α ∘ Δ ¯ i ( π ) ↠ q n k ​ ( π i ) L i , α ∘ L i ( π ) = q n k ​ ( π i ) F i : k ∗ L i ( π ) . \displaystyle\bar{\Delta}_{i}(\pi_{+i:k})\cong q^{n_{k}(\pi^{i})}L_{i,\alpha}\circ\bar{\Delta}_{i}(\pi)\twoheadrightarrow q^{n_{k}(\pi^{i})}L_{i,\alpha}\circ L_{i}(\pi)=q^{n_{k}(\pi^{i})}F^{*}_{i:k}L_{i}(\pi). 
 
 
 
 But Δ ¯ i ( π + i : k ) \bar{\Delta}_{i}(\pi_{+i:k}) has simple head L i ( π + i : k ) L_{i}(\pi_{+i:k}) by Theorem  10.5 (1), which yields result (4). 



 
 Now we prove (2) in the i = 1 i=1 case as well, noting that the i = 0 i=0 case, as well as (1), is proved in symmetrical fashion. We again have L ⁡ ( π | ϕ ) = L i ​ ( π ) L(\pi|\phi)=L_{i}(\pi) .
By [ 37 , Theorem 4.1] (v), every simple factor of Res α , θ α + θ ​ L i ​ ( π ) {\mathrm{Res}}_{\alpha,\theta}^{\alpha+\theta}L_{i}(\pi) is of the form L i , α ⊠ L i ​ ( σ ) L_{i,\alpha}\boxtimes L_{i}(\sigma) , where σ ≤ π − i : k \sigma\leq\pi_{-i:k} . Then for such σ \sigma we have: 

 

 
 
 [ soc Res α , θ α + θ L i ( π ) : L i , α ⊠ L i ( σ ) ] \displaystyle[\textup{soc}\,{\mathrm{Res}}_{\alpha,\theta}^{\alpha+\theta}L_{i}(\pi):L_{i,\alpha}\boxtimes L_{i}(\sigma)] 
 = dim q Hom R α ⊗ R θ ​ ( L i , α ⊠ L i ​ ( σ ) , Res α , θ α + θ ​ L i ​ ( π ) ) \displaystyle=\dim_{q}{\rm Hom}_{R_{\alpha}\otimes R_{\theta}}(L_{i,\alpha}\boxtimes L_{i}(\sigma),{\mathrm{Res}}_{\alpha,\theta}^{\alpha+\theta}L_{i}(\pi)) 
 
 
 
 
 
 = dim q Hom R α + θ ​ ( L i , α ∘ L i ​ ( σ ) , L i ​ ( π ) ) \displaystyle=\dim_{q}{\rm Hom}_{R_{\alpha+\theta}}(L_{i,\alpha}\circ L_{i}(\sigma),L_{i}(\pi)) 
 
 
 
 
 
 = dim q Hom R α + θ ( F i : k ∗ L i ( σ ) , L i ( π ) ) \displaystyle=\dim_{q}{\rm Hom}_{R_{\alpha+\theta}}(F^{*}_{i:k}L_{i}(\sigma),L_{i}(\pi)) 
 
 
 
 
 
 = dim q Hom R α + θ ( q − n k ​ ( σ i ) L i ( σ + i : k ) , L i ( π ) ) = δ σ , π − i : k q n k ( π − i : k i ) , \displaystyle=\dim_{q}{\rm Hom}_{R_{\alpha+\theta}}(q^{-n_{k}(\sigma^{i})}L_{i}(\sigma_{+i:k}),L_{i}(\pi))=\delta_{\sigma,\pi_{-i:k}}q^{n_{k}(\pi_{-i:k}^{i})}, 
 
 
 
 where the second to last equality follows since L i ​ ( π ) L_{i}(\pi) is simple and F i : k ∗ L i ( σ ) F^{*}_{i:k}L_{i}(\sigma) has simple head q − n k ​ ( σ i ) L i ( σ + i : k ) q^{-n_{k}(\sigma^{i})}L_{i}(\sigma_{+i:k}) by part (4), and the last equality follows by Theorem  10.5 (2). Since n k ( π − i : k i ) = n k ( π i ) − 1 n_{k}(\pi_{-i:k}^{i})=n_{k}(\pi^{i})-1 , we have soc Res α , θ α + θ L i ( π ) ≅ q n k ​ ( π i ) − 1 L i , α ⊠ L i ( π − i : k ) \textup{soc}\,{\mathrm{Res}}_{\alpha,\theta}^{\alpha+\theta}L_{i}(\pi)\cong q^{n_{k}(\pi^{i})-1}L_{i,\alpha}\boxtimes L_{i}(\pi_{-i:k}) if n k ​ ( π i ) > 0 n_{k}(\pi^{i})>0 , and zero otherwise. Note that, as R θ R_{\theta} -modules, 

 

 
 
 Hom R α ( L i , α , soc Res α , θ α + θ L i ( π ) ) ≅ Hom R α ( L i , α , q n k ​ ( π i ) − 1 L i , α ⊠ L i ( π − i : k ) ) ≅ q n k ​ ( π i ) − 1 L i ( π − i : k ) . \displaystyle{\rm Hom}_{R_{\alpha}}(L_{i,\alpha},\textup{soc}\,{\mathrm{Res}}_{\alpha,\theta}^{\alpha+\theta}L_{i}(\pi))\cong{\rm Hom}_{R_{\alpha}}(L_{i,\alpha},q^{n_{k}(\pi^{i})-1}L_{i,\alpha}\boxtimes L_{i}(\pi_{-i:k}))\cong q^{n_{k}(\pi^{i})-1}L_{i}(\pi_{-i:k}). 
 
 
 


 
 Now let the homomorphism φ ∈ Hom R α ​ ( L i , α , Res α , θ α + θ ​ L i ​ ( π ) ) \varphi\in{\rm Hom}_{R_{\alpha}}(L_{i,\alpha},{\mathrm{Res}}_{\alpha,\theta}^{\alpha+\theta}L_{i}(\pi)) be any generator of a simple R θ R_{\theta} -submodule in Hom R α ​ ( L i , α , Res α , θ α + θ ​ L i ​ ( π ) ) {\rm Hom}_{R_{\alpha}}(L_{i,\alpha},{\mathrm{Res}}_{\alpha,\theta}^{\alpha+\theta}L_{i}(\pi)) . Then there is a nonzero R α ⊗ R θ R_{\alpha}\otimes R_{\theta} -module evaluation map L i , α ⊠ ( R θ ⋅ φ ) → Res α , θ α + θ ​ L i ​ ( π ) L_{i,\alpha}\boxtimes(R_{\theta}\cdot\varphi)\rightarrow{\mathrm{Res}}_{\alpha,\theta}^{\alpha+\theta}L_{i}(\pi) ,
and since L i , α ⊠ ( R θ ⋅ φ ) L_{i,\alpha}\boxtimes(R_{\theta}\cdot\varphi) is a simple R α ⊗ R β R_{\alpha}\otimes R_{\beta} -module, this map must be injective with image in soc ​ Res α , θ α + θ ​ L i ​ ( π ) \textup{soc}\,{\mathrm{Res}}_{\alpha,\theta}^{\alpha+\theta}L_{i}(\pi) . Therefore φ ⁡ ( L i , α ) ⊆ soc ​ Res α , θ α + θ ​ L i ​ ( π ) \varphi(L_{i,\alpha})\subseteq\textup{soc}\,{\mathrm{Res}}_{\alpha,\theta}^{\alpha+\theta}L_{i}(\pi) , and so φ ∈ Hom R α ​ ( L i , α , soc ​ Res α , θ α + θ ​ L i ​ ( π ) ) \varphi\in{\rm Hom}_{R_{\alpha}}(L_{i,\alpha},\textup{soc}\,{\mathrm{Res}}_{\alpha,\theta}^{\alpha+\theta}L_{i}(\pi)) . Thus 

 

 
 
 soc ​ Hom ​ ( L i , α , Res α , θ α + θ ​ L i ​ ( π ) ) ⊆ Hom ⁡ ( L i , α , soc ​ Res α , θ α + θ ​ L i ​ ( π ) ) . \displaystyle\textup{soc}\,{\rm Hom}(L_{i,\alpha},{\mathrm{Res}}_{\alpha,\theta}^{\alpha+\theta}L_{i}(\pi))\subseteq{\rm Hom}(L_{i,\alpha},\textup{soc}\,{\mathrm{Res}}_{\alpha,\theta}^{\alpha+\theta}L_{i}(\pi)). 
 
 
 
 Therefore, as R θ R_{\theta} -modules, we have 

 

 
 
 soc E i : k ∗ L i ( π ) \displaystyle\textup{soc}\,E^{*}_{i:k}L_{i}(\pi) 
 = soc ​ Hom R α + θ ​ ( L i , α ∘ R θ , L i ​ ( π ) ) ≅ soc ​ Hom R α ⊗ R θ ​ ( L i , α ⊠ R θ , Res α , θ α + θ ​ L i ​ ( π ) ) \displaystyle=\textup{soc}\,{\rm Hom}_{R_{\alpha+\theta}}(L_{i,\alpha}\circ R_{\theta},L_{i}(\pi))\cong\textup{soc}\,{\rm Hom}_{R_{\alpha}\otimes R_{\theta}}(L_{i,\alpha}\boxtimes R_{\theta},{\mathrm{Res}}_{\alpha,\theta}^{\alpha+\theta}L_{i}(\pi)) 
 
 
 
 
 
 ≅ soc Hom R α ( L i , α , Res α , θ α + θ L i ( π ) ) ⊆ Hom ( L i , α , soc Res α , θ α + θ L i ( π ) ) ≅ q n k ​ ( π i ) − 1 L i ( π − i : k ) . \displaystyle\cong\textup{soc}\,{\rm Hom}_{R_{\alpha}}(L_{i,\alpha},{\mathrm{Res}}_{\alpha,\theta}^{\alpha+\theta}L_{i}(\pi))\subseteq{\rm Hom}(L_{i,\alpha},\textup{soc}\,{\mathrm{Res}}_{\alpha,\theta}^{\alpha+\theta}L_{i}(\pi))\cong q^{n_{k}(\pi^{i})-1}L_{i}(\pi_{-i:k}). 
 
 
 
 Since Hom ⁡ ( L i , α , soc ​ Res α , θ α + θ ​ L i ​ ( π ) ) ≠ 0 {\rm Hom}(L_{i,\alpha},\textup{soc}\,{\mathrm{Res}}_{\alpha,\theta}^{\alpha+\theta}L_{i}(\pi))\neq 0 , it follows that Hom ⁡ ( L i , α , Res α , θ α + θ ​ L i ​ ( π ) ) ≠ 0 {\rm Hom}(L_{i,\alpha},{\mathrm{Res}}_{\alpha,\theta}^{\alpha+\theta}L_{i}(\pi))\neq 0 , and thus we have that soc ​ Hom R α ​ ( L i , α , Res α , θ α + θ ​ L i ​ ( π ) ) ≠ 0 \textup{soc}\,{\rm Hom}_{R_{\alpha}}(L_{i,\alpha},{\mathrm{Res}}_{\alpha,\theta}^{\alpha+\theta}L_{i}(\pi))\neq 0 . Therefore soc E i : k ∗ L i ( π ) \textup{soc}\,E^{*}_{i:k}L_{i}(\pi) is a nonzero R θ R_{\theta} -submodule of the simple module q n k ​ ( π i ) − 1 L i ( π − i : k ) q^{n_{k}(\pi^{i})-1}L_{i}(\pi_{-i:k}) , and result (2) follows.
∎ 





 
 This yields the immediate corollary (which appears in ungraded form in [ 57 , Proposition 3.13] ):



 
 Corollary 10.8 . 

 
 Let ( π | ϕ ) ∈ ℳ ​ 𝒱 ​ ( θ ) (\pi|\phi)\in\mathcal{MV}(\theta) . Recalling the 𝙰 1 ( 1 ) {\tt A}_{1}^{(1)} -bicrystal structure of ℳ ​ 𝒱 \mathcal{MV} from § 4.3 , we have 

 
 (1) 
 
 If ε i ​ ( π | ϕ ) = 0 \varepsilon_{i}(\pi|\phi)=0 then E i ​ L ​ ( π | ϕ ) = 0 E_{i}L(\pi|\phi)=0 , and if ε i ∗ ​ ( π | ϕ ) = 0 \varepsilon^{*}_{i}(\pi|\phi)=0 then E i ∗ ​ L ​ ( π | ϕ ) = 0 E_{i}^{*}L(\pi|\phi)=0 . Otherwise 

 

 
 
 q 1 − ε i ​ ( π | ϕ ) ​ soc ​ E i ​ L ​ ( π | ϕ ) ≅ L ⁡ ( e i ​ ( π | ϕ ) ) and q 1 − ε i ∗ ​ ( π | ϕ ) ​ soc ​ E i ∗ ​ L ​ ( π | ϕ ) ≅ L ⁡ ( e i ∗ ​ ( π | ϕ ) ) . \displaystyle q^{1-\varepsilon_{i}(\pi|\phi)}\textup{soc}\,E_{i}L(\pi|\phi)\cong L({e}_{i}(\pi|\phi))\quad\textup{and}\quad q^{1-\varepsilon^{*}_{i}(\pi|\phi)}\textup{soc}\,E_{i}^{*}L(\pi|\phi)\cong L({e}^{*}_{i}(\pi|\phi)). 
 
 
 



 (2) 
 
 We have 

 

 
 
 q ε i ​ ( π | ϕ ) ​ hd ​ F i ​ L ​ ( π | ϕ ) ≅ L ⁡ ( f i ​ ( π | ϕ ) ) and q ε i ∗ ​ ( π | ϕ ) ​ hd ​ F i ∗ ​ L ​ ( π | ϕ ) ≅ L ⁡ ( f i ∗ ​ ( π | ϕ ) ) . \displaystyle q^{\varepsilon_{i}(\pi|\phi)}\textup{hd}\,F_{i}L(\pi|\phi)\cong L({f}_{i}(\pi|\phi))\quad\textup{and}\quad q^{\varepsilon^{*}_{i}(\pi|\phi)}\textup{hd}\,F_{i}^{*}L(\pi|\phi)\cong L({f}^{*}_{i}(\pi|\phi)). 
 
 
 



 






 
 10.8. Cyclotomic KLR algebras


 
 Let θ ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 ​ I \theta\in\mathbbm{Z}_{\geq 0}I and Λ = t 0 ​ Λ 0 + t 1 ​ Λ 1 \Lambda=t_{0}\Lambda_{0}+t_{1}\Lambda_{1} be a dominant integral weight. Then we define the cyclotomic KLR algebra R θ Λ R_{\theta}^{\Lambda} to be the quotient of R θ R_{\theta} by the two-sided ideal generated by { y 1 t i 1 ​ 1 𝒊 ∣ 𝒊 ∈ I θ } \{y_{1}^{t_{i_{1}}}1_{\boldsymbol{i}}\mid\boldsymbol{i}\in I^{\theta}\} . We write infl Λ : R θ Λ ​ -mod → R θ ​ -mod \textup{infl}^{\Lambda}:R_{\theta}^{\Lambda}\textup{-mod}\rightarrow R_{\theta}\textup{-mod} and pr Λ : R θ ​ -mod → R θ Λ ​ -mod \textup{pr}^{\Lambda}:R_{\theta}\textup{-mod}\rightarrow R_{\theta}^{\Lambda}\textup{-mod} for the usual inflation and truncation functors. The algebra R θ Λ R_{\theta}^{\Lambda} is always finite dimensional.



 
 Remark 10.9 . 

 
 Cyclotomic KLR algebras occupy a central position in modern representation theory due to their myriad connections to categorification and other algebraic structures. To name a few, the celebrated result [ 10 ] of Brundan-Kleshchev establishes that cyclotomic KLR algebras are isomorphic to blocks of cyclotomic Hecke algebras, allowing the latter—and modular representations of symmetric groups in particular (see [ 38 ] )—to be studied through the (graded) lens of cyclotomic KLR algebras. Furthermore, Kang-Kashiwara proved [ 24 ] that the cyclotomic KLR algebras R θ Λ R^{\Lambda}_{\theta} categorify the highest weight representations V ⁡ ( Λ ) V(\Lambda) for the quantum group U q ​ ( 𝔰 ​ 𝔩 ^ n ) U_{q}(\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{n}) , with the simple R θ Λ R^{\Lambda}_{\theta} -modules categorifying the crystal ℬ ⁡ ( Λ ) \mathcal{B}(\Lambda) , as shown by Varagnolo-Vasserot [ 59 ] .





 
 10.8.1. Specht modules for multipartitions


 
 Let 𝜿 = ( κ 1 , κ 2 , … ) \boldsymbol{\kappa}=(\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2},\ldots) be a multicharge and 𝝀 = ( λ ( 1 ) , λ ( 2 ) , … ) ∈ ℳ ​ 𝒫 𝜿 ​ ( θ ) \boldsymbol{\lambda}=(\lambda^{(1)},\lambda^{(2)},\ldots)\in\mathcal{MP}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\theta) be a multipartition of level ℓ \ell . Then we may consider each constituent partition λ ( t ) \lambda^{(t)} as a (skew) diagram with residues as determined by κ t \kappa_{t} . Following [ 31 ] (and expressly calculating the grading shift in [ 31 , (2.3)] ) we set

 

 
 
 S 𝜿 ( 𝝀 ) := q sh 𝜿 ​ ( 𝝀 ) 𝗦 ( λ ( ℓ ) ) ∘ 𝗦 ( λ ( ℓ − 1 ) ) ∘ ⋯ ∘ 𝗦 ( λ ( 1 ) ) ∈ R θ -mod , \displaystyle S^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}):=q^{\textup{sh}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{S}}(\lambda^{(\ell)})\circ\boldsymbol{\mathsf{S}}(\lambda^{(\ell-1)})\circ\cdots\circ\boldsymbol{\mathsf{S}}(\lambda^{(1)})\in R_{\theta}\textup{-mod}, 
 
 
 
 where the grading shift is given by

 

 
 
 sh 𝜿 ​ ( 𝝀 ) = ∑ 1 ≤ t ≤ ℓ 1 ≤ r ≤ p ⁡ ( λ ( t ) ) ⌊ λ r ( t ) 2 ⌋ + ∑ 1 ≤ s < t ≤ ℓ 1 ≤ r ≤ p ⁡ ( λ ( t ) ) ⌊ λ r ( t ) + ( ( r + δ κ s , κ ^ t ) mod 2 ) 2 ⌋ . \displaystyle\textup{sh}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq t\leq\ell\\
1\leq r\leq p(\lambda^{(t)})\end{subarray}}\left\lfloor\frac{\lambda_{r}^{(t)}}{2}\right\rfloor+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq s<t\leq\ell\\
1\leq r\leq p(\lambda^{(t)})\end{subarray}}\left\lfloor\frac{\lambda_{r}^{(t)}+((r+\delta_{\kappa_{s},\widehat{\kappa}_{t}})\hskip-5.69054pt\mod 2)}{2}\right\rfloor. 
 
 
 
 If 𝝀 ∈ ℳ ​ 𝒫 ( 𝜿 , ℓ ) ​ ( θ ) \boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{MP}^{(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\ell)}(\theta) , then S 𝜿 ​ ( 𝝀 ) S^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}) naturally descends to an R θ Λ ⁡ ( 𝜿 , ℓ ) R^{\Lambda(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\ell)}_{\theta} -module.



 
 Theorem 10.10 . 

 
 [ 11 , Theorem 5.10] [ 18 , Main Theorem] 
Let θ ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 ​ I \theta\in\mathbbm{Z}_{\geq 0}I . Let Λ \Lambda be a dominant weight, and assume that the multicharge
 𝛋 \boldsymbol{\kappa} and ℓ ∈ ℤ > 0 \ell\in\mathbbm{Z}_{>0} are such that Λ = Λ ⁡ ( 𝛋 , ℓ ) \Lambda=\Lambda(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\ell) . Then the algebra R θ Λ R_{\theta}^{\Lambda} is graded cellular, with poset ℳ ​ 𝒫 ( 𝛋 , ℓ ) ​ ( θ ) \mathcal{MP}^{(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\ell)}(\theta) under the dominance order ⊵ \trianglerighteq and cell modules { S 𝛋 ​ ( 𝛌 ) ∣ 𝛌 ∈ ℳ ​ 𝒫 ( 𝛋 , ℓ ) ​ ( θ ) } \{S^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{MP}^{(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\ell)}(\theta)\} . For all Kleshchev multipartitions 𝛌 ∈ ℳ ​ 𝒫 K ( 𝛋 , ℓ ) ​ ( θ ) \boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\ell)}(\theta) , the module S 𝛋 ​ ( 𝛌 ) S^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}) has self-dual simple head D 𝛋 ​ ( 𝛌 ) := hd ​ S 𝛋 ​ ( 𝛌 ) D^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}):=\textup{hd}\,S^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}) , and 

 

 
 
 { D 𝜿 ​ ( 𝝀 ) ∣ 𝝀 ∈ ℳ ​ 𝒫 K ( 𝜿 , ℓ ) ​ ( θ ) } \displaystyle\{D^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\ell)}(\theta)\} 
 
 
 
 is a complete and irredundant list of simple R θ Λ R_{\theta}^{\Lambda} -modules up to isomorphism and grading shift. 







 
 10.8.2. Cyclotomic branching rules for simple modules labeled by Kleshchev multipartitions


 
 Following [ 11 , §4.4] we define (biadjoint, exact) cyclotomic i i -restriction and i i -induction functors

 

 
 
 
 E i Λ : R θ + α i Λ ​ -mod → R θ Λ ​ -mod , M ↦ 1 θ , α i ​ R θ + α i Λ ⊗ R θ + α i Λ M \displaystyle E_{i}^{\Lambda}\colon R^{\Lambda}_{\theta+\alpha_{i}}\textup{-mod}\rightarrow R^{\Lambda}_{\theta}\textup{-mod},\hskip 14.22636ptM\mapsto 1_{\theta,\alpha_{i}}R^{\Lambda}_{\theta+\alpha_{i}}\otimes_{R^{\Lambda}_{\theta+\alpha_{i}}}M 
 
 
 
 
 
 F i Λ : R θ Λ ​ -mod → R θ + α i Λ ​ -mod , M ↦ R θ + α i Λ ​ 1 θ , α i ⊗ R θ Λ M \displaystyle F_{i}^{\Lambda}\colon R^{\Lambda}_{\theta}\textup{-mod}\rightarrow R^{\Lambda}_{\theta+\alpha_{i}}\textup{-mod},\hskip 14.22636ptM\mapsto R_{\theta+\alpha_{i}}^{\Lambda}1_{\theta,\alpha_{i}}\otimes_{R_{\theta}^{\Lambda}}M 
 
 
 


 
 Theorem 10.11 . 

 
 [ 11 , Theorem 4.12] 
Let θ = a 0 ​ α 0 + a 1 ​ α 1 ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 ​ I \theta=a_{0}\alpha_{0}+a_{1}\alpha_{1}\in\mathbbm{Z}_{\geq 0}I . Let Λ = t 0 ​ Λ 0 + t 1 ​ Λ 1 \Lambda=t_{0}\Lambda_{0}+t_{1}\Lambda_{1} be a dominant integral weight, and assume that the multicharge
 𝛋 \boldsymbol{\kappa} and ℓ ∈ ℤ > 0 \ell\in\mathbbm{Z}_{>0} are such that Λ = Λ ⁡ ( 𝛋 , ℓ ) \Lambda=\Lambda(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\ell) . Let i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in\mathbbm{Z}_{2} and 𝛌 ∈ ℳ ​ 𝒫 K ( 𝛋 , ℓ ) ​ ( θ ) \boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\ell)}(\theta) . Then recalling the 𝙰 1 ( 1 ) {\tt A}_{1}^{(1)} -crystal structure of ℳ ​ 𝒫 K ( 𝛋 , ℓ ) \mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\ell)} from § 5.3 , we have 

 
 (1) 
 
 If ε i ( 𝜿 , ℓ ) ​ ( 𝝀 ) = 0 \varepsilon_{i}^{(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\ell)}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})=0 , then E i Λ ​ D 𝜿 ​ ( 𝝀 ) = 0 E_{i}^{\Lambda}D^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})=0 . Otherwise 

 

 
 
 q ε i ​ ( 𝝀 ) − 1 ​ hd ​ ( E i Λ ​ D 𝜿 ​ ( 𝝀 ) ) ≅ D 𝜿 ​ ( e i ( 𝜿 , ℓ ) ​ 𝝀 ) ≅ q 1 − ε i ​ ( 𝝀 ) ​ soc ​ ( E i Λ ​ D 𝜿 ​ ( 𝝀 ) ) \displaystyle q^{\varepsilon_{i}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})-1}\textup{hd}\left(E_{i}^{\Lambda}D^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\right)\cong D^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(e_{i}^{(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\ell)}\boldsymbol{\lambda})\cong q^{1-\varepsilon_{i}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})}\textup{soc}\left(E_{i}^{\Lambda}D^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\right) 
 
 
 



 (2) 
 
 If φ i ( 𝜿 , ℓ ) ​ ( 𝝀 ) = 0 \varphi^{(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\ell)}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})=0 , then F i Λ ​ D 𝜿 ​ ( 𝝀 ) = 0 F_{i}^{\Lambda}D^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})=0 . Otherwise 

 

 
 
 q 2 ​ a i − 2 ​ a i ^ − t i + φ i ( 𝜿 , ℓ ) ​ ( 𝝀 ) ​ hd ​ ( F i Λ ​ D 𝜿 ​ ( 𝝀 ) ) ≅ D 𝜿 ​ ( f i ( 𝜿 , ℓ ) ​ 𝝀 ) ≅ q 2 ​ a i − 2 ​ a i ^ − t i − φ i ( 𝜿 , ℓ ) ​ ( 𝝀 ) + 2 ​ soc ​ ( F i Λ ​ D 𝜿 ​ ( 𝝀 ) ) . \displaystyle q^{2a_{i}-2a_{\widehat{i}}-t_{i}+\varphi_{i}^{(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\ell)}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})}\textup{hd}\left(F_{i}^{\Lambda}D^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\right)\cong D^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(f_{i}^{(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\ell)}\boldsymbol{\lambda})\cong q^{2a_{i}-2a_{\widehat{i}}-t_{i}-\varphi_{i}^{(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\ell)}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})+2}\textup{soc}\left(F_{i}^{\Lambda}D^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\right). 
 
 
 



 








 
 10.9. Classification of simple R θ R_{\theta} -modules via Kleshchev multipartitions


 
 In a limiting procedure, we may lift the classification of simple modules for cyclotomic KLR algebras R θ Λ R^{\Lambda}_{\theta} in Theorem  10.10 to the full affine KLR algebra R θ R_{\theta} .



 
 Theorem 10.12 . 

 
 Let θ ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 ​ I \theta\in\mathbbm{Z}_{\geq 0}I and let 𝛋 \boldsymbol{\kappa} be a multicharge. The set 

 

 
 (10.5) 
 
 { D 𝜿 ​ ( 𝝀 ) = hd ​ S 𝜿 ​ ( 𝝀 ) ∣ 𝝀 ∈ ℳ ​ 𝒫 K 𝜿 ​ ( θ ) } \displaystyle\{D^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})=\textup{hd}\,S^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\theta)\} 
 
 
 
 is a complete and irredundant set of simple R θ R_{\theta} -modules up to isomorphism and grading shift. Moreover, [ S 𝛋 ( 𝛌 ) : D 𝛋 ( 𝛌 ) ] = 1 [S^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}):D^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})]=1 and [ S 𝛋 ( 𝛍 ) : D 𝛋 ( 𝛌 ) ] ≠ 0 [S^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\boldsymbol{\mu}):D^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})]\neq 0 for some 𝛍 ∈ ℳ ​ 𝒫 𝛋 ​ ( θ ) \boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{MP}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\theta) only if 𝛍 ​ ⊵ ​ 𝛌 \boldsymbol{\mu}\trianglerighteq\boldsymbol{\lambda} . 





 
 Proof.

 
 This is essentially [ 42 , Theorem 6.34] ; we provide the proof here since our setup is somewhat different. Simplicity of the modules in ( 10.5 ) follows from Theorem  10.10 . Every self-dual simple module L ∈ R θ ​ -mod L\in R_{\theta}\textup{-mod} is finite dimensional and thus y 1 m ​ L = 0 y_{1}^{m}L=0 for some m ≫ 0 m\gg 0 by degrees. Then, letting M M be large enough that the list κ 1 , … , κ M \kappa_{1},\ldots,\kappa_{M} contains at least m m 0’s and 1’s, it follows that L L descends to a simple R Λ ⁡ ( 𝜿 , M ) R^{\Lambda(\boldsymbol{\kappa},M)} -module, and hence L ≈ D 𝜿 ​ ( 𝝀 ) L\approx D^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}) for some 𝝀 ∈ ℳ ​ 𝒫 K ( 𝜿 , M ) ​ ( θ ) ⊂ ℳ ​ 𝒫 K 𝜿 ​ ( θ ) \boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{(\boldsymbol{\kappa},M)}(\theta)\subset\mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\theta) by Theorem  10.10 . Thus the list ( 10.5 ) is complete. For irredundancy, we note that if D 𝜿 ​ ( 𝝀 ) ≈ D 𝜿 ​ ( 𝝁 ) D^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\approx D^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\boldsymbol{\mu}) for some 𝝀 , 𝝁 ∈ ℳ ​ 𝒫 K 𝜿 ​ ( θ ) \boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\theta) , then again choosing M ≫ 0 M\gg 0 such that λ ( m ) = μ ( m ) = ∅ \lambda^{(m)}=\mu^{(m)}=\varnothing for m > M m>M , we would have that 𝝀 , 𝝁 ∈ ℳ ​ 𝒫 K ( 𝜿 , M ) ​ ( θ ) \boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{(\boldsymbol{\kappa},M)}(\theta) and D 𝜿 ​ ( 𝝀 ) ≈ D 𝜿 ​ ( 𝝁 ) ∈ R θ Λ ⁡ ( 𝜿 , M ) ​ -mod D^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\approx D^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\boldsymbol{\mu})\in R_{\theta}^{\Lambda(\boldsymbol{\kappa},M)}\textup{-mod} and thus 𝝀 = 𝝁 \boldsymbol{\lambda}=\boldsymbol{\mu} by Theorem  10.10 . The final statement in the theorem follows likewise from consideration of these modules in the cyclotomic setting, see [ 11 , Theorem 4.12] .
∎ 





 
 10.9.1. (Affine) branching rules for simple modules labeled by Kleshchev multipartitions


 
 Proposition 10.13 . 

 
 Let θ ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 ​ I \theta\in\mathbbm{Z}_{\geq 0}I and let 𝛋 \boldsymbol{\kappa} be a multicharge. Let i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in\mathbbm{Z}_{2} and 𝛌 ∈ ℳ ​ 𝒫 K 𝛋 ​ ( θ ) \boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\theta) .
Recalling the Chevalley functors from § 10.2.1 and the 𝙰 1 ( 1 ) {\tt A}_{1}^{(1)} -crystal structure of ℳ ​ 𝒫 K 𝛋 \mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}} from § 5.2 , we have: 

 
 (1) 
 
 If ε i ​ ( 𝝀 ) = 0 \varepsilon_{i}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})=0 then E i ​ D 𝜿 ​ ( 𝝀 ) = 0 E_{i}D^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})=0 . Otherwise 

 

 
 
 q ε i ​ ( 𝝀 ) − 1 ​ hd ​ E i ​ D 𝜿 ​ ( 𝝀 ) ≅ D 𝜿 ​ ( e i ​ 𝝀 ) ≅ q 1 − ε i ​ ( 𝝀 ) ​ soc ​ E i ​ D 𝜿 ​ ( 𝝀 ) . \displaystyle q^{\varepsilon_{i}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})-1}\textup{hd}\,E_{i}D^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\cong D^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}({e}_{i}\boldsymbol{\lambda})\cong q^{1-\varepsilon_{i}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})}\textup{soc}\,E_{i}D^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}). 
 
 
 



 (2) 
 
 We have 

 

 
 
 q ε i ​ ( 𝝀 ) ​ hd ​ F i ​ D 𝜿 ​ ( 𝝀 ) ≅ D 𝜿 ​ ( f i ​ 𝝀 ) . \displaystyle q^{\varepsilon_{i}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})}\textup{hd}\,F_{i}D^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\cong D^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}({f}_{i}\boldsymbol{\lambda}). 
 
 
 



 




 
 Proof.

 
 It follows from definitions that infl Λ ⁡ ( 𝜿 , ℓ ) ∘ E i Λ ⁡ ( 𝜿 , ℓ ) = E i ∘ infl Λ ⁡ ( 𝜿 , ℓ ) \textup{infl}^{\Lambda(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\ell)}\circ E_{i}^{\Lambda(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\ell)}=E_{i}\circ\textup{infl}^{\Lambda(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\ell)} , and thus the right side of Theorem  10.11 (1) implies (1) in the proposition. By Theorem  10.12 and Theorem  10.5 (4) there is some bijection B : ℳ ​ 𝒫 K 𝜿 → ℳ ​ 𝒱 B:\mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}\rightarrow\mathcal{MV} such that D 𝜿 ​ ( 𝝀 ) ≅ L ⁡ ( B ⁡ ( 𝝀 ) ) D^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\cong L(B(\boldsymbol{\lambda})) for all 𝝀 ∈ ℳ ​ 𝒫 K 𝜿 \boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}} . Now, note that by Proposition  10.8 we have 

 

 
 
 L ⁡ ( B ⁡ ( 𝝀 ) ) ≈ D 𝜿 ​ ( 𝝀 ) = D 𝜿 ​ ( e i ​ f i ​ 𝝀 ) ≈ hd ​ E i ​ D 𝜿 ​ ( f i ​ 𝝀 ) ≈ hd ​ E i ​ L ​ ( B ⁡ ( f i ​ 𝝀 ) ) ≈ L ⁡ ( e i ​ B ​ ( f i ​ 𝝀 ) ) , \displaystyle L(B(\boldsymbol{\lambda}))\approx D^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})=D^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}({e}_{i}{f}_{i}\boldsymbol{\lambda})\approx\textup{hd}\,E_{i}D^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}({f}_{i}\boldsymbol{\lambda})\approx\textup{hd}\,E_{i}L(B({f}_{i}\boldsymbol{\lambda}))\approx L({e}_{i}B({f}_{i}\boldsymbol{\lambda})), 
 
 
 
 so by Theorem  10.5 (4) we have that e i ​ B ​ ( f i ​ 𝝀 ) = B ⁡ ( 𝝀 ) {e}_{i}B({f}_{i}\boldsymbol{\lambda})=B(\boldsymbol{\lambda}) , and thus B ⁡ ( f i ​ 𝝀 ) = f i ​ e i ​ B ​ ( f i ​ 𝝀 ) = f i ​ B ​ ( 𝝀 ) B({f}_{i}\boldsymbol{\lambda})={f}_{i}{e}_{i}B({f}_{i}\boldsymbol{\lambda})={f}_{i}B(\boldsymbol{\lambda}) , and so B B is a crystal isomorphism. Thus the rest of the claims in the proposition follow from Proposition  10.8 .
∎ 









 
 10.10. Translating between labeling regimes for simple KLR modules


 
 In this subsection we apply the combinatorial results of § 7 to help work with, and translate between, the various classification regimes for simple KLR modules.



 
 10.10.1. Working with cuspidal systems


 
 When studying the representation theory of R θ R_{\theta} from the cuspidal system point of view, it is useful to be able to efficiently switch between labeling systems associated to the two choices of convex order ≻ 1 \begin{subarray}{c}{}\\
\textstyle\succ\\
\scriptscriptstyle{}^{1}\end{subarray} and ≻ 0 \begin{subarray}{c}{}\\
\textstyle\succ\\
\scriptscriptstyle{}^{0}\end{subarray} , particularly for the purposes of calculating with the Chevalley functors of § 10.2.1 . Recall the combinatorial description of the involution ◊ : Π ⁡ ( θ ) → Π ⁡ ( θ ) \lozenge:\Pi(\theta)\rightarrow\Pi(\theta) from Theorem  Theorem B , and the fact that for π ∈ Π ⁡ ( θ ) \pi\in\Pi(\theta) , we have that ( π | π ◊ ) (\pi|\pi_{\lozenge}) and ( π ◊ | π ) (\pi_{\lozenge}|\pi) are affine MV polytopes. Then the next proposition follows from Theorem  10.5 (4).



 
 Proposition 10.14 . 

 
 Let θ ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 ​ I \theta\in\mathbbm{Z}_{\geq 0}I . For all i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in\mathbbm{Z}_{2} and π ∈ Π ⁡ ( θ ) \pi\in\Pi(\theta) we have L i ​ ( π ) ≅ L i ^ ​ ( π ◊ ) L_{i}(\pi)\cong L_{\widehat{i}}(\pi_{\lozenge}) . 





 
 Remark 10.15 . 

 
 In conjunction with Theorem  Theorem B , Proposition  10.7 and Corollary  10.8 , Proposition  10.14 allows one to efficiently and combinatorially compute with the Chevalley functors of § 10.2.1 (and their generalized real root analogues in § 10.6 ) in the root partition setting; for instance we have

 

 
 
 hd F 1 L 1 ( π ) ≅ hd F 1 L 0 ( π ◊ ) ≅ q − n 1 ​ ( π ◊ 1 ) L 0 ( ( π ◊ ) + 1 : 1 ) ≅ q − n 1 ​ ( π ◊ 1 ) L 1 ( ( ( π ◊ ) + 1 : 1 ) ◊ ) . \displaystyle\textup{hd}\,F_{1}L_{1}(\pi)\cong\textup{hd}\,F_{1}L_{0}(\pi_{\lozenge})\cong q^{-n_{1}(\pi_{\lozenge}^{1})}L_{0}((\pi_{\lozenge})_{+1:1})\cong q^{-n_{1}(\pi_{\lozenge}^{1})}L_{1}(((\pi_{\lozenge})_{+1:1})_{\lozenge}). 
 
 
 






 
 10.10.2. Translating between cuspidal system and Kleshchev multipartition labelings


 
 The ability to move efficiently between the cuspidal- and multipartition-theoretic approaches to KLR representations allows us to combine the advantages of each. The former carries with it a stratification theory with significant homological control and reciprocity results, while the latter connects to the cellular structure of cyclotomic quotients and the classical theory of Specht modules and their homomorphisms. Different aspects of representation-theoretic information are encoded in the combinatorics of the labels themselves: we know, for instance, that the character of a simple R θ R_{\theta} -module is bounded by its labeling polytope, while the multiplicity of words in the simple is bounded by their occurrence as residue sequences in the labeling multipartition. The crystal isomorphisms of § 7 let us make this connection directly.



 
 Theorem 10.16 . 

 
 Let θ ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 ​ I \theta\in\mathbbm{Z}_{\geq 0}I , let 𝛋 \boldsymbol{\kappa} be a multicharge and let i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in\mathbbm{Z}_{2} . Then we have 

 

 
 
 L i ​ ( π ) ≅ D 𝜿 ​ ( 𝒲 𝜿 ∘ 𝒳 i ​ ( π ) ) and D 𝜿 ​ ( 𝝀 ) ≅ L i ​ ( 𝒴 i ∘ 𝒵 𝜿 ​ ( 𝝀 ) ) \displaystyle L_{i}(\pi)\cong D^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}\circ\mathcal{X}_{i}(\pi))\qquad\textup{and}\qquad D^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\cong L_{i}(\mathcal{Y}_{i}\circ\mathcal{Z}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})) 
 
 
 
 for all π ∈ Π ⁡ ( θ ) \pi\in\Pi(\theta) and 𝛌 ∈ ℳ ​ 𝒫 K 𝛋 ​ ( θ ) \boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\theta) . 





 
 Proof.

 
 As noted in the proof of Proposition  10.13 , there is a crystal isomorphism B : ℳ ​ 𝒫 K 𝜿 → ∼ ℳ ​ 𝒱 B:\mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{MV} such that L ⁡ ( B ⁡ ( 𝝀 ) ) ≅ D 𝜿 ​ ( 𝝀 ) L(B(\boldsymbol{\lambda}))\cong D^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}) for all 𝝀 ∈ ℳ ​ 𝒫 K 𝜿 \boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}} . Then it follows by Corollary  Corollary I that B = 𝒴 ∘ 𝒵 𝜿 B=\mathcal{Y}\circ\mathcal{Z}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}} and B − 1 = 𝒲 𝜿 ∘ 𝒳 B^{-1}=\mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}\circ\mathcal{X} , so the statement follows from Theorem  Theorem E .
∎ 





 
 Remark 10.17 . 

 
 In view of Theorem  10.16 , we may consider 𝒰 ​ ℒ \mathcal{UL} as yet another index for simple KLR modules, writing
 L ⁡ ( 𝖣 ) := L ⁡ ( 𝒴 ⁡ ( 𝖣 ) ) L(\mathsf{D}):=L(\mathcal{Y}(\mathsf{D})) 
for all 𝖣 ∈ 𝒰 ​ ℒ \mathsf{D}\in\mathcal{UL} , noting then that L ⁡ ( 𝖣 ) ≅ L i ​ ( 𝒴 i ​ ( 𝖣 ) ) ≅ D 𝜿 ​ ( 𝒲 𝜿 ​ 𝖣 ) L(\mathsf{D})\cong L_{i}(\mathcal{Y}_{i}(\mathsf{D}))\cong D^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}\mathsf{D}) for any i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in\mathbbm{Z}_{2} , and multicharge 𝜿 \boldsymbol{\kappa} .









 
 10.11. Translating between multicharges


 
 For a given integral dominant weight Λ = t 0 ​ Λ 0 + t 1 ​ Λ 1 \Lambda=t_{0}\Lambda_{0}+t_{1}\Lambda_{1} , there are in general ( t 0 + t 1 t 0 ) {t_{0}+t_{1}\choose t_{0}} inequivalent choices of multicharges 𝜿 \boldsymbol{\kappa} which define a cellular structure and labeling for the simple modules of R θ Λ R^{\Lambda}_{\theta} . Our next result shows that translating between these choices amounts to ‘gluing-then-splitting’:



 
 Proposition 10.18 . 

 
 Let 𝛋 , 𝛚 \boldsymbol{\kappa},\boldsymbol{\omega} be multicharges. Then D 𝛋 ​ ( 𝛌 ) ≅ D 𝛚 ​ ( 𝒲 𝛚 ∘ 𝒵 𝛋 ​ ( 𝛌 ) ) D^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\cong D^{\boldsymbol{\omega}}(\mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{\omega}}\circ\mathcal{Z}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})) for all 𝛌 ∈ ℳ ​ 𝒫 K 𝛋 \boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}} . 





 
 Proof.

 
 In view of Theorem  10.16 and Theorem  Theorem E we have 

 

 
 
 D 𝜿 ​ ( 𝝀 ) ≅ L 1 ​ ( 𝒴 1 ∘ 𝒵 𝜿 ​ ( 𝝀 ) ) ≅ D 𝝎 ​ ( 𝒲 𝝎 ∘ 𝒳 1 ∘ 𝒴 1 ∘ 𝒵 𝜿 ​ ( 𝝀 ) ) ≅ D 𝝎 ​ ( 𝒲 𝝎 ∘ 𝒵 𝜿 ​ ( 𝝀 ) ) , \displaystyle D^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\cong L_{1}(\mathcal{Y}_{1}\circ\mathcal{Z}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}))\cong D^{\boldsymbol{\omega}}(\mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{\omega}}\circ\mathcal{X}_{1}\circ\mathcal{Y}_{1}\circ\mathcal{Z}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}))\cong D^{\boldsymbol{\omega}}(\mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{\omega}}\circ\mathcal{Z}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})), 
 
 
 
 as desired.
∎ 







 
 10.12. The jump statistic via upper ledge diagrams


 
 For i ∈ ℤ 2 i\in\mathbbm{Z}_{2} , M ∈ R θ ​ -mod M\in R_{\theta}\textup{-mod} , let us set

 

 
 
 F ~ i ​ ( M ) = hd ​ ( F i ​ M ) ; E ~ i ∗ ​ ( M ) = soc ​ ( E i ∗ ​ M ) ; ε ~ i ∗ ​ ( M ) = max ⁡ { m ∈ ℤ > 0 ∣ ( E ~ i ∗ ) m ​ ( M ) ≠ 0 } . \displaystyle\widetilde{F}_{i}(M)=\textup{hd}(F_{i}M);\qquad\widetilde{E}_{i}^{*}(M)=\textup{soc}(E_{i}^{*}M);\qquad\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{i}^{*}(M)=\max\{m\in\mathbbm{Z}_{>0}\mid(\widetilde{E}_{i}^{*})^{m}(M)\neq 0\}. 
 
 
 
 Then the jump statistic on simple modules in R θ ​ -mod R_{\theta}\textup{-mod} defined in [ 41 , §6.1] is given by

 

 
 
 jump i ​ ( M ) := max ⁡ { J ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 ∣ ε i ∗ ​ ( F ~ i J ​ M ) = ε i ∗ ​ ( M ) } . \displaystyle\textup{jump}_{i}(M):=\max\{J\in\mathbbm{Z}_{\geq 0}\mid\varepsilon_{i}^{*}(\widetilde{F}_{i}^{J}M)=\varepsilon_{i}^{*}(M)\}. 
 
 
 
 This statistic was a technical innovation that allowed Lauda–Vazirani to verify the crystal axioms for induction/restriction, and has played a key role in subsequent categorification projects [ 39 , 60 ] . In general computing jump i ​ ( M ) \textup{jump}_{i}(M) is at best an iterative process, even with the labeling crystal data for M M in hand. If one works as in Remark  10.17 with the labeling data of 𝒰 ​ ℒ \mathcal{UL} instead however, it can be computed directly:



 
 Proposition 10.19 . 

 
 Let 𝖣 ∈ 𝒰 ​ ℒ \mathsf{D}\in\mathcal{UL} . Then we have 

 

 
 (10.6) 
 
 jump i ​ ( L ⁡ ( 𝖣 ) ) = # ⁡ { unarced  i ^ -bottom boxes in  ​ 𝖣 ​ [ i ] } + # ⁡ { unarced  i ^ -top boxes in  ​ 𝖣 ​ [ i ] } . \displaystyle\textup{jump}_{i}(L(\mathsf{D}))=\#\{\textup{unarced $\widehat{i}$-bottom boxes in }\mathsf{D}[i]\}+\#\{\textup{unarced $\widehat{i}$-top boxes in }\mathsf{D}[i]\}. 
 
 
 




 
 Proof.

 
 Say 𝖣 \mathsf{D} has u b u_{b} unarced i ^ \widehat{i} -bottom boxes and u t u_{t} unarced i ^ \widehat{i} -top boxes. Then by § 6.2 , f i u b ​ 𝖣 f_{i}^{u_{b}}\mathsf{D} is obtained by placing an i i -box beneath each one of these i ^ \widehat{i} -bottom boxes, so that ( f i u b ​ 𝖣 ) ​ [ i ] (f_{i}^{u_{b}}\mathsf{D})[i] has no unarced i ^ \widehat{i} -bottom boxes, has a peak row of color i i , and the top boxes in f i u b ​ 𝖣 f_{i}^{u_{b}}\mathsf{D} are unchanged from 𝖣 \mathsf{D} . Then by § 6.2 again, f i u b + m ​ 𝖣 f_{i}^{u_{b}+m}\mathsf{D} is obtained by adding m m boxes of color i i —which are both i i -top and i i -bottom—to the uppermost i i -peak row of f i u b ​ 𝖣 f_{i}^{u_{b}}\mathsf{D} . Each of these m m new i i -colored boxes will be top-arced in ( f i u b + m ​ 𝖣 ) ​ [ i ] (f_{i}^{u_{b}+m}\mathsf{D})[i] if and only if u t ≥ m u_{t}\geq m , so we have ε i ∗ ​ ( f i u b + m ​ 𝖣 ) = ε i ∗ ​ ( 𝖣 ) \varepsilon_{i}^{*}(f_{i}^{u_{b}+m}\mathsf{D})=\varepsilon_{i}^{*}(\mathsf{D}) if and only if u t ≥ m u_{t}\geq m . Then the result follows from Theorems  9.6 and Corollary  10.8 .
∎ 







 
 10.13. Augmented branching rules for simple modules labeled by Kleshchev multipartitions


 
 Thanks to Theorem  10.16 and Proposition  10.7 , we can describe the action of root functors of § 10.6 on simple modules labeled by Kleshchev multipartitions—we simply translate from D 𝜿 ​ ( 𝝀 ) D^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}) to the isomorphic module L ⁡ ( π | ϕ ) L(\pi|\phi) via the map 𝒴 ∘ 𝒵 𝜿 \mathcal{Y}\circ\mathcal{Z}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}} , utilize Proposition  10.7 and then translate back via the map 𝒲 𝜿 ∘ 𝒳 \mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}\circ\mathcal{X} . For reader convenience we detail an example of this computation now.



 
 Example 10.20 . 

 
 Assume 𝜿 = ( 1 , 0 , … ) \boldsymbol{\kappa}=(1,0,\ldots) , and 𝝀 = ( ( 5 , 4 2 , 3 4 , 2 , 1 ) , ( 4 2 , 3 , 2 2 , 1 ) ) \boldsymbol{\lambda}=((5,4^{2},3^{4},2,1),(4^{2},3,2^{2},1)) , shown at left in Figure  22 . Consider the application of the functor F 0 : 3 F_{0:3} to D 𝜿 ​ ( 𝝀 ) D^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}) .

 (10.7) 
 
 q n 3 ​ ( π 0 ) hd F 0 : 3 D 𝜿 ( 𝝀 ) ≅ D 𝜿 ( 𝝁 ) ; 𝝁 := 𝒲 𝜿 ∘ 𝒳 1 ( ( 𝒴 1 ∘ 𝒵 𝜿 ( 𝝀 ) ) + 0 : 3 ) , \displaystyle q^{n_{3}(\pi^{0})}\textup{hd}F_{0:3}D^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\cong D^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\boldsymbol{\mu});\qquad\boldsymbol{\mu}:=\mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}\circ\mathcal{X}_{1}((\mathcal{Y}_{1}\circ\mathcal{Z}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}))_{+0:3}),

 (2) 
 
 Separate into the 1 1 -triple decomposition trip 1 ​ ( 𝖣 ) = ( 𝖣 1 ↑ , 𝖣 1 δ , 𝖣 1 ↓ ) \mathrm{trip}_{1}(\mathsf{D})=(\mathsf{D}^{\uparrow}_{1},\mathsf{D}^{\delta}_{1},\mathsf{D}^{\downarrow}_{1}) .

 (4)

 (5) 
 
 Replace 𝖣 1 ↓ \mathsf{D}^{\downarrow}_{1} with the new diagram 𝖣 ~ 1 ↓ = color 0 ∘ ord ( π + 0 : 3 0 ) \widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{\downarrow}_{1}=\textup{color}_{0}\circ\mathrm{ord}(\pi^{0}_{+0:3}) .

 Then we arrive at

 10.14. A tile factor theorem


 
 James’s celebrated regularization theorem (see § 10.15.1 ) gives, for any level one Specht module, a purely combinatorial rule for describing a unique factor of this module, and a dominance bound on all of its other simple factors.
In [ 48 , Theorem 9.23] a cuspidal-flavored analogue of this result was provided for Specht modules. In this section we can provide a streamlined version of this analogue in level one thanks to the isomorphisms in § 7 .



 
 Recalling § 10.5 , the partial order ≥ \geq on Π ⁡ ( θ ) \Pi(\theta) induces a partial order ≥ i \geq_{i} on ℳ ​ 𝒫 K 𝜿 ​ ( θ ) \mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\theta) ; we write 𝝀 ≥ i 𝝁 \boldsymbol{\lambda}\geq_{i}\boldsymbol{\mu} provided 𝒴 i ∘ 𝒵 𝜿 ​ ( 𝝀 ) ≥ 𝒴 i ∘ 𝒵 𝜿 ​ ( 𝝁 ) \mathcal{Y}_{i}\circ\mathcal{Z}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\geq\mathcal{Y}_{i}\circ\mathcal{Z}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\boldsymbol{\mu}) .
Let λ ∈ 𝒫 κ \lambda\in\mathcal{P}^{\kappa} be a (level one) partition, and let σ \sigma be the largest staircase partition (i.e., of the form ( m , m − 1 , … , 1 ) (m,m-1,\ldots,1) for some m m ) contained in λ \lambda . If σ \sigma contains the rightmost i i -residue box in the top row of λ \lambda , then we say λ \lambda is i i -quasirestricted .



 
 Assume λ ∈ 𝒫 κ \lambda\in\mathcal{P}^{\kappa} is i i -quasirestricted. Then there is a unique ‘ i i -tiling’ of λ \lambda , which we compute in a greedy fashion; first tile all the southeast-removable ξ 1 ( α i ^ : 1 ) \xi_{1}(\alpha_{\widehat{i}:1}) ribbons in λ \lambda , then (ignoring these boxes) tile all the southeast-removable ξ 1 ( α i ^ : 2 ) \xi_{1}(\alpha_{\widehat{i}:2}) ’s, and then ξ 1 ( α i ^ : 3 ) \xi_{1}(\alpha_{\widehat{i}:3}) ’s, and so on until the remaining diagram has only removable boxes of residue i i (and hence no more such tiles can be found). Call the remaining partition λ ¯ \bar{\lambda} . Let χ i \chi^{i} be the partition such that n k ​ ( χ i ) n_{k}(\chi^{i}) is the number of ξ i ( α i ^ : k ) \xi_{i}(\alpha_{\widehat{i}:k}) tiles in the i i -tiling of λ \lambda . Then we construct a partition λ 𝖳 ⁡ ( i ) ∈ 𝒫 2 ​ r ​ e ​ s κ \lambda^{\mathsf{T}(i)}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{2res}}^{\kappa} by appending c r ​ ( ord ⁡ ( χ i ) ) c_{r}(\mathrm{ord}(\chi^{i})) boxes to the r r th column of λ ¯ \bar{\lambda} for all r ∈ ℤ > 0 r\in\mathbbm{Z}_{>0} (in essence, shifting the columns of ord ⁡ ( χ i ) \mathrm{ord}(\chi^{i}) up to meet λ ¯ \bar{\lambda} ). See Figure  23 for an example.



 
 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 Figure 23. In the top center, the two i i -tilings of an i i -quasirestricted partition λ ∈ 𝒫 0 \lambda\in\mathcal{P}^{0} . Below them are the partitions χ i \chi^{i} which record the ribbons used in the tiling, and their spar tableaux 𝚜𝚙𝚊𝚛 ⁡ ( χ i ) {\tt spar}(\chi^{i}) . To the sides, the associated 2-restricted partition λ 𝖳 ⁡ ( i ) \lambda^{\mathsf{T}(i)} . 
 
 
 Proposition 10.21 . 

 
 Let i , κ ∈ ℤ 2 i,\kappa\in\mathbbm{Z}_{2} , and assume λ ∈ 𝒫 κ \lambda\in\mathcal{P}^{\kappa} is an i i -quasirestricted partition. Then D κ ​ ( λ 𝖳 ⁡ ( i ) ) D^{\kappa}(\lambda^{\mathsf{T}(i)}) arises exactly once as a simple factor in S κ ​ ( λ ) S^{\kappa}(\lambda) up to some shift, and every simple factor of S κ ​ ( λ ) S^{\kappa}(\lambda) is of the form D κ ​ ( μ ) D^{\kappa}(\mu) up to shift, where μ ∈ 𝒫 2 ​ r ​ e ​ s κ \mu\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{2res}}^{\kappa} and μ ≤ i λ 𝖳 ⁡ ( i ) \mu\leq_{i}\lambda^{\mathsf{T}(i)} . 





 
 Proof.

 
 The condition that λ \lambda is i i -quasirestricted ensures that λ \lambda has a ξ ⁡ ( π ) \xi(\pi) -tiling for ≻ i \begin{subarray}{c}{}\\
\textstyle\succ\\
\scriptscriptstyle{}^{i}\end{subarray} in the language of [ 48 , §9] , for some π ∈ Π ⁡ ( θ ) \pi\in\Pi(\theta) . By [ 48 , Theorem 9.23] the simple module L i ​ ( π ) L_{i}(\pi) arises once as a factor of S κ ​ ( λ ) S^{\kappa}(\lambda) , and ϕ ≤ i π \phi\leq_{i}\pi for every factor L i ​ ( ϕ ) L_{i}(\phi) of S κ ​ ( λ ) S^{\kappa}(\lambda) (up to shifts). By [ 48 , Proposition 4.12] , the tiles in ξ i ​ ( π ) \xi_{i}(\pi) of content ⪰ i ​ δ \begin{subarray}{c}{}\\
\textstyle\succeq\\
\scriptscriptstyle{}^{i}\end{subarray}\delta compose the partition λ ¯ \bar{\lambda} , and it follows by Corollary  Corollary I and consideration of the associated combinatorial maps that λ 𝖳 ⁡ ( i ) = 𝒲 𝜿 ∘ 𝒳 i ​ ( π ) \lambda^{\mathsf{T}(i)}=\mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}\circ\mathcal{X}_{i}(\pi) . Then the result follows from Theorem  10.16 .
∎ 





 
 Remark 10.22 . 

 
 When λ \lambda is itself 2-restricted, the partition λ 𝖳 ⁡ ( i ) \lambda^{\mathsf{T}(i)} is recovered simply by removing all removable i ^ \widehat{i} boxes and then adding them back on in the lowest possible i ^ \widehat{i} -addable positions. In this setting Proposition  10.21 is covered by [ 56 , Theorem 4.4] .





 
 Remark 10.23 . 

 
 While Proposition  10.21 is of a similar flavor to James’s regularization theorem, we note that in general the information offered by each is distinct; the simple factor guaranteed by our tiling theorem is not generally the same as the one coming from James’s theorem (as may be seen by considering the Figure  23 example), and James’s dominance partial order ⊵ \trianglerighteq and our cuspidal-flavored partial order ≥ i \geq_{i} do not coincide.







 
 10.15. A level two analogue of James’s regularization theorem (conjecturally)


 
 10.15.1. Restrictization


 
 Let λ ∈ 𝒫 \lambda\in\mathcal{P} . The restrictization λ R \lambda_{\textup{R}} is defined by sliding boxes in λ \lambda as far southwest as possible along the northeast/southwest antidiagonals (or ladders ) they belong to.
James’s celebrated regularization theorem (better called in our setting a restrictization theorem), extended by Fayers–Lyle–Martin, establishes the following powerful result (presented here for 𝙰 1 ( 1 ) {\tt A}_{1}^{(1)} but holding analogously for all types 𝙰 ≥ 1 ( 1 ) {\tt A}_{\geq 1}^{(1)} ), which describes a specific composition factor appearing in any level one Specht module, and gives a dominance bound on other factors.



 
 Theorem 10.24 . 

 
 [ 21 , Theorem A] , [ 13 , Theorem 1.7] 
Let κ \kappa be a (level one) charge, and let λ ∈ 𝒫 κ \lambda\in\mathcal{P}^{\kappa} . Then: 

 
 (1) 
 
 There exists a nonzero homomorphism S κ ​ ( λ R ) → S κ ​ ( λ ) S^{\kappa}(\lambda_{\textup{R}})\rightarrow S^{\kappa}(\lambda) ; 




 (2) 
 
 We have [ S κ ( λ ) : D κ ( λ R ) ] = 1 [S^{\kappa}(\lambda):D^{\kappa}(\lambda_{\textup{R}})]=1 ; 




 (3) 
 
 If [ S κ ( λ ) : D κ ( μ ) ] > 0 [S^{\kappa}(\lambda):D^{\kappa}(\mu)]>0 for μ ∈ 𝒫 2 ​ r ​ e ​ s κ \mu\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{2res}}^{\kappa} , then μ ​ ⊴ ​ λ R \mu\trianglelefteq\lambda_{\textup{R}} . 




 




 
 It is beguiling to attempt to extend James’s theorem to higher levels. In type 𝙰 1 ( 1 ) {\tt A}_{1}^{(1)} level three and higher, a completist’s dream generalization will be impossible—it is easy to find Specht modules S 𝜿 ​ ( 𝝀 ) S^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}) which have multiple maximally dominant factors, so a result along the lines of Theorem  10.24 (3) cannot be hoped for. In types 𝙰 > 1 ( 1 ) {\tt A}_{>1}^{(1)} matters are even worse, as similar issues begin popping up even in level two. Thus if one wants a full-strength generalization of Theorem  10.24 , the only setting where it seems possible to accomplish is in type 𝙰 1 ( 1 ) {\tt A}_{1}^{(1)} level two. We give a conjecture in this direction below.





 
 10.15.2. Kleshchevization


 
 Let 𝜿 = ( κ 1 , κ 2 ) \boldsymbol{\kappa}=(\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}) be a bicharge, and 𝝀 = ( λ ( 1 ) , λ ( 2 ) ) ∈ ℳ ​ 𝒫 𝜿 \boldsymbol{\lambda}=(\lambda^{(1)},\lambda^{(2)})\in\mathcal{MP}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}} . We define the Kleshchevization 𝝀 K ∈ ℳ ​ 𝒫 K 𝜿 \boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\textup{K}}\in\mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}} of 𝝀 \boldsymbol{\lambda} by setting 𝝀 K = 𝒵 𝜿 ∘ 𝒲 𝜿 ​ ( 𝝀 ) \boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\textup{K}}=\mathcal{Z}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}\circ\mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}) . Note that by Theorem  Theorem G we have 𝝀 K = 𝝀 \boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\textup{K}}=\boldsymbol{\lambda} if and only if 𝝀 ∈ ℳ ​ 𝒫 K 𝜿 \boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}} .



 
 Unpacking the particulars of the maps 𝒲 𝜿 \mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}} and 𝒵 𝜿 \mathcal{Z}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}} , one sees that Kleshchevization amounts to “greedy ladder-filling” as follows.
First position λ ( 2 ) \lambda^{(2)} to the northeast of λ ( 1 ) \lambda^{(1)} , so that the northwesternmost box(es) of residue κ 1 \kappa_{1} in λ ( 2 ) \lambda^{(2)} lie along the same antidiagonal as the northwesternmost ( κ 1 \kappa_{1} -residue) box in λ ( 1 ) \lambda^{(1)} .
Then 𝝀 K \boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\textup{K}} is the 𝜿 \boldsymbol{\kappa} -bipartition obtained by sliding boxes directly to the southwest along antidiagonals in this arrangement as far as possible. Note that this procedure specializes to the usual restrictization of λ ( 1 ) \lambda^{(1)} when λ ( 2 ) = ∅ \lambda^{(2)}=\varnothing . See Figure  24 for an illustration.



 
 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 Figure 24. Let 𝜿 = ( 1 , 0 ) \boldsymbol{\kappa}=(1,0) and 𝝀 = ( ( 5 2 , 2 , 1 3 ) , ( 5 2 , 4 3 , 2 3 ) ) \boldsymbol{\lambda}=((5^{2},2,1^{3}),(5^{2},4^{3},2^{3})) . We arrange λ ( 1 ) , λ ( 2 ) \lambda^{(1)},\lambda^{(2)} so that the first antidiagonal of 1 1 -residue boxes in λ ( 2 ) \lambda^{(2)} is aligned with the top left (residue 1) box in in λ ( 1 ) \lambda^{(1)} , and then slide boxes all the way down antidiagonals (passing through but not landing in the gray zone) to get 𝝀 K = ( ( 6 , 5 , 4 , 3 2 , 2 , 1 2 ) , ( 5 , 4 , 3 2 , 2 , 1 ) ) \boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\textup{K}}=((6,5,4,3^{2},2,1^{2}),(5,4,3^{2},2,1)) . 
 
 
 Conjecture 10.25 . 

 
 Let 𝜿 = ( κ 1 , κ 2 ) \boldsymbol{\kappa}=(\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}) be a bicharge, and let 𝝀 ∈ ℳ ​ 𝒫 𝜿 \boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{MP}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}} . Then:

 
 (1) 
 
 There exists a nonzero homomorphism S 𝜿 ​ ( 𝝀 K ) → S 𝜿 ​ ( 𝝀 ) S^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\textup{K}})\rightarrow S^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}) ;




 (2) 
 
 We have [ S κ ( 𝝀 ) : D κ ( 𝝀 K ) ] = 1 [S^{\kappa}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}):D^{\kappa}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\textup{K}})]=1 ;




 (3) 
 
 If [ S κ ( 𝝀 ) : D κ ( 𝝁 ) ] > 0 [S^{\kappa}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}):D^{\kappa}(\boldsymbol{\mu})]>0 for 𝝁 ∈ ℳ ​ 𝒫 K 𝜿 \boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}} , then 𝝁 ​ ⊴ ​ 𝝀 K \boldsymbol{\mu}\trianglelefteq\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\textup{K}} .




 




 
 We have computationally verified that this result holds in small ranks. An intrepid mathematician may hypothesize that the Kleshchevization process can reasonably be extended to levels greater than two (at the expense of weakening part (3) of the conjecture statement). Naively defining 𝝀 K = 𝒵 𝜿 ∘ 𝒲 𝜿 ​ ( 𝝀 ) \boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\textup{K}}=\mathcal{Z}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}\circ\mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}) in higher levels does not succeed without some adjustment, however, and we are currently unable to compute with large enough ranks in higher levels to make any concrete conjectures in this direction.







 
 10.16. An alternate classification regime for KLR algebras (conjecturally)


 
 In § 10.5 , 10.9 we recalled how simple modules for the KLR algebra R θ R_{\theta} may be constructed as heads of explicit (skew) Specht modules directly associated to the combinatorial aspects of vertices in the 𝙰 1 ( 1 ) {\tt A}_{1}^{(1)} -crystals ℳ ​ 𝒱 \mathcal{MV} and ℳ ​ 𝒫 K 𝜿 \mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}} . We expect that there should be such a construction associated to our ‘intermediary’ 𝙰 1 ( 1 ) {\tt A}_{1}^{(1)} -crystal 𝒰 ​ ℒ \mathcal{UL} as well, and make a conjecture in this direction now.



 
 We associate a skew diagram ξ ⁡ ( 𝖣 ) \xi(\mathsf{D}) to any upper ledge diagram 𝖣 ∈ 𝒰 ​ ℒ \mathsf{D}\in\mathcal{UL} as follows. Let u u be the rightmost box in the uppermost peak row of 𝖣 \mathsf{D} , and extend a ray r r from the centroid of u u directly to the northwest (i.e., at a 135 ∘ 135^{\circ} angle). Now form a diagram 𝖣 ′ \mathsf{D}^{\prime} by shifting every row above the uppermost peak row in 𝖣 \mathsf{D} to the right until the rightmost box in each of these rows lies along r r (forming a staircase along the northeast side of 𝖣 ′ \mathsf{D}^{\prime} ). Next, construct ξ ⁡ ( 𝖣 ) \xi(\mathsf{D}) by shifting the k k th column in 𝖣 ′ \mathsf{D}^{\prime} up k − 1 k-1 steps. See Figure  25 for an example.



 
 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 Figure 25. Computing the skew diagram ξ ⁡ ( 𝖣 ) \xi(\mathsf{D}) associated to an upper ledge diagram 𝖣 \mathsf{D} . Note ξ ⁡ ( 𝖣 ) \xi(\mathsf{D}) is equal to λ \ μ \lambda\backslash\mu , where λ = ( 8 , 7 2 , 6 3 , 5 , 4 , 3 2 , 2 , 1 2 ) \lambda=(8,7^{2},6^{3},5,4,3^{2},2,1^{2}) , μ = ( 4 , 3 3 , 2 2 , 1 ) ∈ 𝒫 2 ​ r ​ e ​ s 0 \mu=(4,3^{3},2^{2},1)\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{2res}}^{0} . 
 
 
 Conjecture 10.26 . 

 
 Let θ ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 ​ I \theta\in\mathbbm{Z}_{\geq 0}I . For 𝖣 ∈ 𝒰 ​ ℒ ​ ( θ ) \mathsf{D}\in\mathcal{UL}(\theta) , the Specht module 𝗦 ⁡ ( ξ ⁡ ( 𝖣 ) ) \boldsymbol{\mathsf{S}}(\xi(\mathsf{D})) has simple head isomorphic to L ⁡ ( 𝖣 ) L(\mathsf{D}) up to some shift.





 
 As in the ℳ ​ 𝒱 \mathcal{MV} , ℳ ​ 𝒫 K 𝜿 \mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\boldsymbol{\kappa}} settings, one would also expect some control over the other simple factors of 𝗦 ⁡ ( ξ ⁡ ( 𝖣 ) ) \boldsymbol{\mathsf{S}}(\xi(\mathsf{D})) and related homological results.
Considering the combinatorics at work, one sees (as in Figure  25 ) that the skew diagrams ξ ⁡ ( 𝖣 ) \xi(\mathsf{D}) are exactly those realized by set differences of properly nested 2-restricted partitions. Defining the set of skew diagrams:

 

 
 
 𝒩 𝒫 2res ( θ ) = { λ \ μ ∣ κ ∈ ℤ 2 , λ , μ ∈ 𝒫 2 ​ r ​ e ​ s κ , λ 1 > μ 1 ′ , cont ( λ \ μ ) = θ } \displaystyle\mathcal{NP}_{\textup{2res}}(\theta)=\{\lambda\backslash\mu\mid\kappa\in\mathbbm{Z}_{2},\,\lambda,\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{2res}}^{\kappa},\,\lambda_{1}>\mu^{\prime}_{1},\,\textup{cont}(\lambda\backslash\mu)=\theta\} 
 
 
 
 allows a reformulation of Conjecture  10.26 :



 
 Conjecture 10.27 . 

 
 Let θ ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 ​ I \theta\in\mathbbm{Z}_{\geq 0}I .
For λ \ μ ∈ 𝒩 ​ 𝒫 2res ​ ( θ ) \lambda\backslash\mu\in\mathcal{NP}_{\textup{2res}}(\theta) , the Specht module 𝗦 ⁡ ( λ \ μ ) \boldsymbol{\mathsf{S}}(\lambda\backslash\mu) has simple head and

 

 
 
 { L ⁡ ( λ \ μ ) := hd ​ 𝗦 ​ ( λ \ μ ) ∣ λ \ μ ∈ 𝒩 ​ 𝒫 2res ​ ( θ ) } \displaystyle\{L(\lambda\backslash\mu):=\textup{hd}\,\boldsymbol{\mathsf{S}}(\lambda\backslash\mu)\mid\lambda\backslash\mu\in\mathcal{NP}_{\textup{2res}}(\theta)\} 
 
 
 
 is a complete and irredundant list of simple R θ R_{\theta} -modules up to isomorphism and grading shift.





 
 Theorem  10.10 shows that 𝗦 ⁡ ( λ \ ∅ ) \boldsymbol{\mathsf{S}}(\lambda\backslash\varnothing) has simple head for every λ ∈ 𝒫 2 ​ r ​ e ​ s κ \lambda\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{2res}}^{\kappa} , and it was shown in [ 48 ] that deleting a core (staircase in this 𝙰 1 ( 1 ) {\tt A}_{1}^{(1)} setting) partition ρ \rho from λ \lambda has nice enough functorial behavior that 𝗦 ⁡ ( λ \ ρ ) \boldsymbol{\mathsf{S}}(\lambda\backslash\rho) should maintain simple head as well. One cannot push this too far; it can be seen for instance that 𝗦 ⁡ ( λ \ μ ) \boldsymbol{\mathsf{S}}(\lambda\backslash\mu) is decomposable over a field of characteristic zero when we take λ = ( 2 2 , 1 3 ) , μ = ( 1 3 ) ∈ 𝒫 2 ​ r ​ e ​ s κ \lambda=(2^{2},1^{3}),\mu=(1^{3})\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{2res}}^{\kappa} . Our hope is that the condition λ 1 > μ 1 ′ \lambda_{1}>\mu^{\prime}_{1} for λ , μ ∈ 𝒫 2 ​ r ​ e ​ s κ \lambda,\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{2res}}^{\kappa} is restrictive enough to dissuade this rude behavior.



 
 10.16.1. A conjecture in general affine type A 


 
 For readers familiar with the setup of KLR algebras and associated combinatorics in general type 𝙰 e − 1 ( 1 ) {\tt A}^{(1)}_{e-1} , we offer one final conjecture, motivated by small-rank computations and large-rank optimism. Let e ≥ 2 e\geq 2 , and consider the associated KLR algebra R θ ​ ( 𝙰 e − 1 ( 1 ) ) R_{\theta}({\tt A}_{e-1}^{(1)}) for some θ \theta in the root lattice of type 𝙰 e − 1 ( 1 ) {\tt A}_{e-1}^{(1)} , over an arbitrary field. For i ∈ ℤ e i\in\mathbbm{Z}_{e} , let ℳ ​ 𝒫 K ( i , i ) ​ ( θ ) \mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{(i,i)}(\theta) be the set of Kleshchev 𝙰 e − 1 ( 1 ) {\tt A}_{e-1}^{(1)} -bipartitions of bicharge ( i , i ) (i,i) , with cont ​ ( λ ( 1 ) \ λ ( 2 ) ) = θ \textup{cont}(\lambda^{(1)}\backslash\lambda^{(2)})=\theta (see [ 5 , 18 ] ).



 
 Conjecture 10.28 . 

 
 For i ∈ ℤ e i\in\mathbbm{Z}_{e} , ( λ ( 1 ) , λ ( 2 ) ) ∈ ℳ ​ 𝒫 K ( i , i ) (\lambda^{(1)},\lambda^{(2)})\in\mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{(i,i)} , the Specht module 𝗦 ⁡ ( λ ( 1 ) \ λ ( 2 ) ) \boldsymbol{\mathsf{S}}(\lambda^{(1)}\backslash\lambda^{(2)}) has simple head and

 

 
 
 { hd ​ 𝗦 ​ ( λ ( 1 ) \ λ ( 2 ) ) ∣ ( λ ( 1 ) , λ ( 2 ) ) ∈ ℳ ​ 𝒫 K ( i , i ) ​ ( θ ) } \displaystyle\{\textup{hd}\,\boldsymbol{\mathsf{S}}(\lambda^{(1)}\backslash\lambda^{(2)})\mid(\lambda^{(1)},\lambda^{(2)})\in\mathcal{MP}_{\mathrm{K}}^{(i,i)}(\theta)\} 
 
 
 
 is a complete (albeit redundant) list of simple R θ ​ ( 𝙰 e − 1 ( 1 ) ) R_{\theta}({\tt A}_{e-1}^{(1)}) -modules up to isomorphism and grading shift.

 Instructions for reporting errors

 We are continuing to improve HTML versions of papers, and your feedback helps enhance accessibility and mobile
 support. To report errors in the HTML that will help us improve conversion and rendering, choose any of the
 methods listed below:

 
 Click the "Report Issue" ( 
 
 ) button, located in the page header.

 
 Tip: You can select the relevant text first, to include it in your report.

 Our team has already identified the following issues . We appreciate your time reviewing and reporting rendering errors we
 may not have found yet. Your efforts will help us improve the HTML versions for all readers, because disability
 should not be a barrier to accessing research. Thank you for your continued support in championing open access for
 all.

 Have a free development cycle? Help support accessibility at arXiv! Our collaborators at LaTeXML maintain a list of packages that need conversion , and welcome developer contributions .
